\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}|\phi^{\prime}(t)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{L}(\phi)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi T}}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2T}|x-y|^{2}}\cdot\mathrm{d}\mathbb{W}^{y,x}_{[0,T]}(\phi),

(1.2.77)

where
\mathbb{W}^{y,x}_{[0,T]}
denotes the
Brownian bridge measure
on
C([0,T])
with boundary conditions
\phi(0)=y
,
\phi(T)=x
.

Wick Rotation

In this subsection we consider
\mathsf{H}:=\mathbf{H}_{1,1}-E_{0}
, the “shifted Hamiltonian” of the harmonic oscillator, appeared in (
1.2.51
). The main concern of this subsection is to relate the unitary group
\textrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t\mathsf{H}}
to the semi-group
\textrm{e}^{-t\mathsf{H}}
by “analytic continuation”. Formally, the former becomes the latter upon a substitution
t\mapsto-\mathrm{i}t
which is usually called the
Wick rotation
. Rigorously, the question could be asked at different levels. Denote
\mathcal{H}:=L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}})
.

1.

For which vectors
\psi\in\mathcal{H}
(and which
z\in\mathbb{C}
) does
z\mapsto\textrm{e}^{-z\mathsf{H}}\psi
make sense as an
\mathcal{H}
-valued analytic function of
z
? Clearly, if
\psi
is a finite linear combination of the eigenstates (
1.2.19
), then
\sum_{n}\frac{|z|^{n}}{n!}\left\lVert\mathsf{H}^{n}\psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}<\infty
for all
z\in\mathbb{C}
and
z\mapsto\textrm{e}^{-z\mathsf{H}}\psi
is entire. In particular these
\psi
are
analytic vectors
of
\mathsf{H}
, see
[
RS75
]
section X.6;

2.

For which
z\in\mathbb{C}
does
z\mapsto\textrm{e}^{-z\mathsf{H}}
define an
analytic family
of bounded operators (that is, an
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
-valued analytic function of
z
), which then necessarily satisfies
\textrm{e}^{-(z+z^{\prime})\mathsf{H}}=\textrm{e}^{-z\mathsf{H}}\textrm{e}^{-z^{\prime}\mathsf{H}}
for admissible
z
and
z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}
? This leads to the notion of a
bounded holomorphic semi-group
(
[
RS75
]
pp. 248), typically defined on a “right opening” sector in
\mathbb{C}
centered at
0
. Now since
\mathsf{H}
is self-adjoint and positive, it is possible to define
\textrm{e}^{-z\mathsf{H}}
as such for
\mathfrak{Re}(z)>0
by holomorphic functional calculus (Cauchy integral), see
[
RS75
]
theorem X.52. However we barely miss the imaginary axis.

3.

The “path measure” of the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process can more generally be seen as a probability measure on
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
, so that
\omega\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
gives a typical path and the “evaluation”
\omega\mapsto\omega(t)
gives the random variable
X_{t}
(see example
1.3.2
). Can we characterize more generally properties of such a measure which, like our case, enables one to define from it a Hilbert space based on the “law of
\omega(0)
” (cf.
L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\gamma_{1,1})
), a “stochastic evolution” on this Hilbert space, and extract from it a positive self-adjoint generator? In this way, we say that we have
reconstructed
quantum mechanics out of the given probability measure on
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
.

More could be said about question (ii). As the imaginary axis is only “barely missed” by the region of analyticity of
\textrm{e}^{-z\mathsf{H}}
, it is still reasonable that for (general) fixed
\varphi
,
\psi\in\mathcal{H}
,
\langle\varphi,\textrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t\mathsf{H}}\psi\rangle
can be taken as a “boundary value” of the analytic function
\langle\varphi,\textrm{e}^{-z\mathsf{H}}\psi\rangle
. Let
z=\mathrm{i}w
so that
w\mapsto\textrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}w\mathsf{H}}
is analytic on the lower half-plane
\mathbb{C}_{-}
. Note

\langle\varphi,\textrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}(t-\mathrm{i}\eta)\mathsf{H}}\psi\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\textrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}(t-\mathrm{i}\eta)\lambda}\langle\varphi,\mathrm{d}E_{\mathsf{H}}(\lambda)\psi\rangle,

(1.2.78)

where
E_{\mathsf{H}}
is spectral measure of
\mathsf{H}
. In our case, since
\left\langle\varphi,\mathrm{d}E_{\mathsf{H}}(\bullet)\psi\right\rangle
is a complex measure (with finite total variation) and
\mathsf{H}
is
positive
(
a fortiori
bounded from below),
\langle\varphi,\textrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t\mathsf{H}}\psi\rangle
is indeed the limit of
\langle\varphi,\textrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}(t-\mathrm{i}\eta)\mathsf{H}}\psi\rangle
as
\eta\to 0^{+}
by dominated convergence. More generally, the Fourier transform of any tempered distribution supported on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
is the boundary value of an analytic function on
\mathbb{C}_{-}
(
[
RS75
]
theorem IX.16). For example,

\mathcal{F}(1_{(0,\infty)})=\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}(x-\mathrm{i}0)}=\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}}\mathrm{p.v.}\Big(\frac{1}{x}\Big)+\pi\delta_{0},

(1.2.79)

that is,
\mathcal{F}(1_{(0,\infty)})
is the boundary value of
1/\mathrm{i}w
on
\mathbb{C}_{-}
. In QFT, it is important to make sense of the (higher dimensional) analogue of

W_{n}(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\big\langle\Omega_{0},\mathbf{X}\textrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}(t_{n}-t_{n-1})\mathsf{H}}\mathbf{X}\cdots\textrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}(t_{2}-t_{1})\mathsf{H}}\mathbf{X}\Omega_{0}\big\rangle,

(1.2.80)

the so-called
Wightman distributions
, as the boundary value of an analytic function of
several variables
defined on the
forward tube
(
[
RS75
]
theorem IX.32,
[
Sim74
]
pp. 60). Here
\mathbf{X}
is the position operator (cf. (
1.2.20
)). In that case,
W_{n}
must be taken as a distribution and (
1.2.80
) is understood formally. Generally, the possibility of doing the analytic continuation comes from the requirement that the
joint spectrum
(
[
Sim15
]
section 5.6) of the generators of space-time translations
12
12

12

from the unitary representation of the Poincaré group.
lie in the
closed forward light cone
(
[
RS75
]
pp. 63), in particular, that the Hamiltonian be positive. That
W_{n}
could be analytically continued to the forward tube means if
s_{1}<\cdots<s_{n}
then

W_{n}(-\mathrm{i}s_{1},\dots,-\mathrm{i}s_{n})=\big\langle\Omega_{0},\mathbf{X}\textrm{e}^{-(s_{n}-s_{n-1})\mathsf{H}}\mathbf{X}\cdots\textrm{e}^{-(s_{2}-s_{1})\mathsf{H}}\mathbf{X}\Omega_{0}\big\rangle.

(1.2.81)

The function
S_{n}(s_{1},\dots,s_{n}):=W_{n}(-\mathrm{i}s_{1},\dots,-\mathrm{i}s_{n})
is usually called a
Schwinger function
13
13

13

This definition only works for
s_{1}<\cdots<s_{n}
by the requirements of analytic continuation. It could be extended symmetrically to larger domains, see
[
Sim74
]
theorem II.12.
. We observe a peculiarity of the Schwinger function: let
s_{2}>s_{1}>0
, since the semi-group
\textrm{e}^{-s\mathsf{H}}
is now self-adjoint we have

\boxed{S_{4}(-s_{2},-s_{1},s_{1},s_{2})=\big\langle\textrm{e}^{-s_{1}\mathsf{H}}\mathbf{X}\textrm{e}^{-(s_{2}-s_{1})\mathsf{H}}\mathbf{X}\Omega_{0},\textrm{e}^{-s_{1}\mathsf{H}}\mathbf{X}\textrm{e}^{-(s_{2}-s_{1})\mathsf{H}}\mathbf{X}\Omega_{0}\big\rangle\geqslant 0.}

(1.2.82)

This is an instance of a property called
reflection positivity
which we discuss briefly below.

Question (iii) underlies the so-called
Euclidean approach
to constructive QFT. It goes the other way round: start with the Euclidean, or probabilistic, objects, in what circumstances can we recover from them a “genuine” Minkowski QFT? This leads to the
Osterwalder-Schrader (OS) axioms
. Let
\mu
be a probability measure on
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
. Assume that the random variables
(X_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
are well-defined by evaluation
\omega\mapsto\omega(t)
, giving accordingly the
\sigma
-algebras
\mathcal{O}_{\geqslant t}=\sigma(X_{s}|s\geqslant t)
,
\mathcal{O}_{\leqslant t}=\sigma(X_{s}|s\leqslant t)
.
14
14

14

We will use the notations
X_{t}
and
\omega(t)
interchangeably to avoid heavy subscripts.
We present an incomplete and simplified version for
\mu
, referring to
[
GJ87
]
chapter 6 for the complete version:

(OS0)

The variables
X_{t}
have finite joint moments.

(OS2)

\mu
is Euclidean (translation and reflection) invariant (e.g.
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[X_{s}X_{t}]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[X_{s-a}X_{t-a}]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[X_{-s}X_{-t}]
).

(OS3)

Reflection Positivity (RP)
. Let
F=F(\omega(t_{1}),\dots,\omega(t_{N}))
be a polynomial functional of finitely many
X_{t}
’s. Denote by
\Theta F
the “reflection across
t=0
” of
F
, namely
(\Theta F)(\omega)=F(\omega(-t_{1}),\dots,\omega(-t_{N}))
. Then we have

\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\overline{F}(\Theta F)]\geqslant 0,\quad\textrm{provided}\quad F=F(\omega(t_{1}),\dots,\omega(t_{N}))\textrm{ with }t_{j}\geqslant 0.

(1.2.83)

Example 1.2.10

(Ornstein-Uhlenbeck, following
1.2.5
)
.

We can characterize the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R},(\tau_{0})_{*}\mu)
of the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process in this new framework. Here
(\tau_{0})_{*}\mu=\gamma_{1,1}
is the law of
X_{0}
. Consider the map
J_{0}:L^{2}(\mathbb{R},(\tau_{0})_{*}\mu)\longrightarrow L^{2}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}),\mu)
defined by
(J_{0}F)(\omega):=F(\omega(0))
. Then by definition the functionals in
\ran J_{0}
are
\sigma(X_{0})
-measurable. In fact, by the Gaussianity of the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process and the Wiener Chaos decomposition (proposition
1.3.1
),
\ran J_{0}
are
precisely
the
\sigma(X_{0})
-measurable functionals in
L^{2}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}),\mu)
. Next we note that the latter condition in (
1.2.83
) says
F
is
\mathcal{O}_{\geqslant 0}
-measurable. Accordingly
\Theta F
is
\mathcal{O}_{\leqslant 0}
-measurable. Applying the
Markov property
and reflection invariance of the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process we have

\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\overline{F}(\Theta F)]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\penalty\ \mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\overline{F}|\mathcal{O}_{\leqslant 0}]\cdot\Theta F\penalty\ ]\overset{\mathrm{Markov}}{=}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\penalty\ \mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\overline{F}|\sigma(X_{0})]\cdot\Theta F\penalty\ ]\overset{\begin{subarray}{c}\textrm{reflection}\\
\mathrm{invariance}\end{subarray}}{=}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\bigl|\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[F|\sigma(X_{0})]\bigr|^{2}\geqslant 0.

(1.2.84)

This shows that the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process is reflection positive and more importantly, that for an
\mathcal{O}_{\geqslant 0}
-measurable functional
F
,
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\overline{F}(\Theta F)]=0
iff
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[F|\sigma(X_{0})]=0
almost surely and these are precisely the functionals in
(\ran J_{0})^{\perp}
. From the above discussions we conclude

L^{2}(\mathbb{R},(\tau_{0})_{*}\mu)\cong\overline{\mathcal{E}_{+}/\mathcal{N}},

(1.2.85)

where
\mathcal{E}_{+}:=L^{2}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}),\mathcal{O}_{\geqslant 0},\mu)
,
\mathcal{N}
is the kernel of the semi-definite inner product (
1.2.83
), and the closure taken w.r.t. (
1.2.83
) as well.

The recipe on the r.h.s. of (
1.2.85
) applies more generally when neither Gaussianity nor Markov property is available. It gives a general reconstruction of the “quantum mechanical” Hilbert space of a QFT out of a probability measure on
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})
satisfying (OS2-3) (
[
GJ87
]
propositions 6.1.1-2). Now following
[
GJ87
]
section 6.1, we indicate how to reconstruct time evolution again with
d=0
. The crucial fact to note is that if a Euclidean transformation
\mathscr{T}
15
15

15

Like
\Theta
, we define the action of
\mathscr{T}
on
L^{2}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}))
by
(\mathscr{T}F)(\phi)=F(\mathscr{T}_{*}\phi)
for
\phi\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})
, and
(\mathscr{T}_{*}\phi)(f)=\phi(\mathscr{T}^{*}f)
.
maps
\mathcal{E}_{+}\longrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{+}
and
\mathcal{N}\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}
, then it descends to
\mathcal{H}:=\overline{\mathcal{E}_{+}/\mathcal{N}}
. A
positive
time translation
a\mapsto a+t
, denoted
\mathscr{T}(t)
for
t\geqslant 0
, has this property. We denote by
U(t):\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}
the operator induced by
\mathscr{T}(t)
. Since
\mathscr{T}(t)\mathscr{T}(s)=\mathscr{T}(t+s)
,
(U(t))_{t\geqslant 0}
defines a semi-group and more precisely it will be strongly continuous and self-adjoint (
[
GJ87
]
theorem 6.1.3). There will be an extra, most noticeable feature: each
U(t)
will be a
contraction
(i.e.
\left\lVert U(t)\right\rVert\leqslant 1
).
Indeed, by semi-group property, self-adjointness, and Cauchy-Schwarz on
\mathcal{H}
,

\left\lVert U(t)\psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}=\left\langle\psi,U(2t)\psi\right\rangle^{1/2}_{\mathcal{H}}\leqslant\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert^{1/2}_{\mathcal{H}}\left\lVert U(2t)\psi\right\rVert^{1/2}_{\mathcal{H}}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert^{1-2^{-n}}_{\mathcal{H}}\left\lVert U(2^{n}t)\psi\right\rVert^{2^{-n}}_{\mathcal{H}}.

(1.2.86)

Now observe (i) the projection
\Pi:\mathcal{E}_{+}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}
is itself a contraction, (ii) the Euclidean transformations act unitarily on
L^{2}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}))
, and (iii) by definition of
U(t)
, if
\psi=\Pi(F)
then
U(2^{n}t)\psi=\Pi(\mathscr{T}(2^{n}t)F)
. Therefore
\left\lVert U(2^{n}t)\psi\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\leqslant\left\lVert F\right\rVert_{\mathcal{E}_{+}}
and we obtain the result by sending
n\to\infty
. This contraction property of
U(t)
, with self-adjointness, tells that its generator
\mathsf{H}
, the reconstructed Hamiltonian, must be
positive
. As discussed above, this is a key fact which suggests that one may proceed to reconstruct a “genuine” quantum theory out of
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathsf{H}
.

Fock Space and Gaussian Measures

We start by recalling the formula for a Gaussian density on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. That is, given a real positive symmetric
N\times N
matrix
C^{-1}
on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, one has

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathbf{x},C^{-1}\mathbf{x}\rangle}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(\mathbf{x})=(2\pi)^{\frac{N}{2}}(\det C)^{\frac{1}{2}},

(1.3.1)

and the measure

\mathrm{d}\mu_{C}(\mathbf{x})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(2\pi)^{-\frac{N}{2}}(\det C^{-1})^{\frac{1}{2}}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathbf{x},C^{-1}\mathbf{x}\rangle}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(\mathbf{x})

(1.3.2)

is a Gaussian probability measure with
C
being its covariance matrix, namely
C_{ij}=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{C}}[x_{i}x_{j}]
. In the case where
C^{-1}
is diagonal with entries (eigenvalues)
\lambda_{1}
, …,
\lambda_{N}
, the expression becomes

\mathrm{d}\mu_{C}(\mathbf{x})=\bigg[\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Big(\frac{\lambda_{i}}{2\pi}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg]\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}x_{i}^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}(\mathbf{x}).

(1.3.3)

The purpose of this section is two-fold. In subsection
1.3.1
we motivate the notion of a
Gaussian random field
which is the infinite dimensional generalization of a
Gaussian random vector
, i.e. a random vector in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
with law (
1.3.2
). Examples
1.3.1
and
1.3.2
show that these are ubiquitous and natural. Then in subsection
1.3.4
we explain the general characterization of (infinite dimensional) Gaussian measures, i.e. laws of Gaussian random fields, by their
covariance structure
and indicate one of their general constructions. They show up naturally in considerations of both the Quantum and Euclidean free fields discussed in subsections
1.4.2
and
1.4.3
. These are the first focus. Subsections
1.3.2
and
1.3.3
constitute the second focus devoted to explaining one fact, that is, informally, any probability
L^{2}
space containing a Gaussian variable automatically “contains” infinitely many Bosons. The precise meaning of this statement is propositions
1.3.1
,
1.3.5
and definition
1.3.3
combined.

From the Brownian Motion to the GFF

The Brownian motion (on
\mathbb{R}
) is a family
(B_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
of Gaussian random variables defined on some probability space
(Q,\mathcal{O},\mathbb{P})
such that
\mathbb{E}[B_{t}]\equiv 0
and
\mathbb{E}[B_{t}B_{s}]=\min\{s,t\}
for
s
,
t\geqslant 0
. To
construct
the Brownian motion means to find a suitable probability space
(Q,\mathcal{O},\mathbb{P})
and to define the random variables
B_{t}
. A natural choice for
Q
is the space of
all possible trajectories
. Below, we construct
B_{t}
up to
t=\pi/2
.

Example 1.3.1

(construction of Wiener measure on
C({[}0,\pi/2{]},\mathbb{R})
)
.

We start with the Laplacian
\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}=-\mathrm{d}^{2}/\mathrm{d}x^{2}
on the open interval
(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
, initially acting on
C_{c}^{\infty}((0,\frac{\pi}{2}))
. Consider its self-adjoint extension
\Delta_{\mathrm{W}}
with domain

\dom(\Delta_{\mathrm{W}})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\big\{f\in W^{2}((0,\textstyle\frac{\pi}{2}))\penalty\ \big|\penalty\ f(0)=f^{\prime}(\frac{\pi}{2})=0\big\},

(1.3.4)

where
W^{2}
denotes the second order Sobolev space (
[
Lew22
]
section 2.8.3). This
\Delta_{\mathrm{W}}
is then positive, has compact resolvent (
[
Lew22
]
theorem 5.25), and
L^{2}
-diagonalized by the complete eigenbasis
\{e_{n}\}_{n=0}^{\infty}
where

e_{n}(t)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\sin((2n+1)t),\quad\quad x\in(0,\textstyle\frac{\pi}{2}),

(1.3.5)

corresponding to eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}=(2n+1)^{2}
. We now define a random distribution
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}((0,\frac{\pi}{2}))
by writing formally a Fourier series

B\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\xi_{n}}{2n+1}e_{n},

(1.3.6)

with the random coefficients
\{\xi_{n}\}
i.i.d. following the standard Gaussian
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. What does
\varphi
look like? We could compute the covariances. Indeed, since
\{\xi_{n}\}
are i.i.d. we have formally

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[B(t)B(s)]

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{e_{n}(t)e_{n}(s)}{(2n+1)^{2}}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{4}{\pi}\frac{\sin((2n+1)t)\sin((2n+1)s)}{(2n+1)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{4}{\pi}\int_{0}^{t}\cos((2n+1)x)\,\mathrm{d}x\int_{0}^{s}\cos((2n+1)y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\left\langle 1_{(0,t)},1_{(0,s)}\right\rangle_{L^{2}((0,\pi/2))}=\min\{t,s\},

where the second last step is true by Plancherel’s theorem and realizing that
\widetilde{e}_{n}:=2\pi^{-1/2}\cos((2n+1)x)
form another basis
16
16

16

In fact, a closer scrutiny shows that
\sum_{n}\int_{0}^{t}e_{n}^{\prime}\int_{0}^{s}e_{n}^{\prime}=\min\{t,s\}
for any o.n. basis
\{e_{n}^{\prime}\}
of
L^{2}((0,\pi/2))
. This reflects the fact that the
Gaussian white noise

B^{\prime}=\sum_{n}\xi_{n}e_{n}^{\prime}
is “intrinsically defined”, whose law being independent of the basis, and accordingly so is
B
when written as
\sum_{n}\xi_{n}\int_{0}^{t}e_{n}^{\prime}
. However, our basis
\{e_{n}\}
is special in giving the equality (
1.3.7
).

of
L^{2}((0,\frac{\pi}{2}))
.
Therefore we recovered the covariance structure of Brownian motion. In fact, the expression (
1.3.6
) also shows that
\mathbb{E}\lVert\Delta_{\mathrm{W}}^{s/2}B\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}=\sum_{n}(2n+1)^{-2+s}<\infty
if
s<1
. Since
\Delta_{\mathrm{W}}
is an elliptic positive differential operator,
\lVert\Delta_{\mathrm{W}}^{s/2}B\rVert_{L^{2}}\approx\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{W^{s}}
, and we see that
B
is almost surely in
W^{s}
for all
s<1
. By the Sobolev embedding theorem, therefore,
B
is almost surely in
C^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}
for all
\varepsilon>0
. We have refound Brownian motion!
We remark that the construction above is adapted form an original construction by Paul Lévy (1948). See
[
CM20
]
section 2.3 and also
[
Kah85
]
chapter 16.

Pick
f\in L^{2}((0,\frac{\pi}{2}))
. The expression (
1.3.6
) also enables us to work more generally with the random variables
\left\langle B,f\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
, and
B(t)
could be thought of as taking
f=\delta_{t}
. Indeed, its variance can be computed as

\mathbb{E}\bigl|\big\langle B,f\big\rangle\bigr|^{2}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\left|\left\langle f,e_{n}\right\rangle\right|^{2}}{(2n+1)^{2}}=\big\langle f,\Delta_{\mathrm{W}}^{-1}f\big\rangle_{L^{2}}.

(1.3.7)

This
a posteriori
shows that
\left\langle B,f\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
is also the probability
L^{2}
-limit of the Gaussian variables
\sum_{n=1}^{N}\xi_{n}f_{n}(2n+1)^{-1}
where
f_{n}:=\left\langle f,e_{n}\right\rangle
, and hence is also
Gaussian
. Since
\langle f,\Delta_{\mathrm{W}}^{-1}f\rangle\leqslant\left\lVert f\right\rVert^{2}
,
\left\langle B,f\right\rangle
is as well the limit of
\left\langle B,\varphi_{n}\right\rangle
for some sequence of test functions
\varphi_{n}\in C_{c}^{\infty}((0,\frac{\pi}{2}))
approaching
f
in
L^{2}
. We will see in subsection
1.3.4
that this perspective of characterizing
Gaussian
random distributions, by looking at the random variables produced by testing against test functions, generalizes.

It is now not difficult to generalize the recipe (
1.3.6
) and construct Gaussian random distributions on an arbitrary closed Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
with the help of the discrete spectrum of
\Delta_{g}
. Indeed, if
\{e_{n}\}_{n=0}^{\infty}
is the o.n. basis (abusing notation) of
L^{2}(M)
consisting of eigenfunctions of
\Delta_{g}
with eigenvalues
0=\lambda_{0}<\lambda_{1}\leqslant\lambda_{2}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant\lambda_{n}\leqslant\cdots
counted with multiplicity, we define

\phi\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\xi_{n}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{n}}}e_{n},

(1.3.8)

with
\{\xi_{n}\}
again being i.i.d. Gaussians following
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. One could then show that
\phi
defines a Gaussian random distribution with
\int_{M}\phi=0
almost surely (since we removed the zero eigenvalue), and the random variables
\left\langle\phi,f\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
,
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
, satisfy

\mathbb{E}\bigl|\big\langle\phi,f\big\rangle_{L^{2}}\bigr|^{2}=\big\langle f,\Delta_{g,0}^{-1}f\big\rangle_{L^{2}},\quad\quad\mathbb{E}\big[\big\langle\phi,f\big\rangle_{L^{2}}\big]=0,

(1.3.9)

where
\Delta_{g,0}^{-1}=\Delta_{g}^{-1}(\mathbbm{1}-P_{\ker\Delta})
denotes the Green operator. This random distribution, or random
field
(and accordingly the measure on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
), is called the
(massless) Gaussian Free Field
(GFF). The bottom eigenvalue may also be included if we consider
\Delta_{g}+m^{2}
instead for some
m>0
. The result will be called the
(massive) Gaussian Free Field with mass

m>0
. Moreover, the almost sure Sobolev regularity of
\phi
could also be deduced from the same argument as in example
1.3.1
by resorting to the Weyl asymptotics.

Example 1.3.2

(Ornstein-Uhlenbeck, continued)
.

We notice the following workhorse formula:

\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\textrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\rho s}}{\rho^{2}+a^{2}}\mathrm{d}\rho=\frac{1}{2a}\textrm{e}^{-a|s|},\quad\quad a>0,\penalty\ s\in\mathbb{R}.

(1.3.10)

Comparing with (
1.2.44
), we notice that
\mathbb{E}[X_{s}X_{t}]=\langle\delta_{s},(-\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}x^{2}}+1)^{-1}\delta_{t}\rangle
. Indeed, (
1.2.44
) already suggests that unlike the Brownian motion, the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process is allowed to go “backwards in time”. By Minlos’ theorem, one can indeed define a (unique) global Gaussian measure on
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
with covariance
(-\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}t^{2}}+1)^{-1}
(playing the role of
\Delta_{\mathrm{W}}^{-1}
above), and the random variables
X_{t}
realized as evaluation at
t
.

Fock Space and Wiener Chaos

In this subsection we show how Gaussian random variables could be related to a construction in mathematical physics called the (Boson)
Fock space
.
This elegant relation will be exploited in the next subsection and then in subsection
1.4.3
to obtain the semi-group of the Euclidean free field as well as its Markov property.
We mention that, among the other references cited below, a detailed development that parallels this subsection and the next could be found in
[
Mal97
]
chapter I, but in a different language.

Any closed linear subspace of
L^{2}(Q,\mathcal{O},\mathbb{P})
of a probability space
(Q,\mathcal{O},\mathbb{P})
, consisting of centered
Gaussian
variables will be called a
Gaussian Hilbert space
.

Definition 1.3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space. Then the
Boson Fock space
\Gamma(\mathcal{H})
associated to
\mathcal{H}
is defined to be the (completed) Hilbert space direct sum

\Gamma(\mathcal{H})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}\sym^{\otimes n}(\mathcal{H}),\quad\quad\left\lVert(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\dots)\right\rVert_{\Gamma(\mathcal{H})}^{2}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\left\lVert\alpha_{i}\right\rVert_{\odot i}^{2},

(1.3.11)

where
\sym^{\otimes n}(\mathcal{H})
denotes the
n
-fold (completed) symmetric tensor product of
\mathcal{H}
with norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\odot n}
, and we put
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes 0}:=\mathbb{C}
(or
\mathbb{R}
), and the unit length generator of
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes 0}
is called the
vacuum
, denoted
|0\rangle
or
\Omega_{0}
.

In concrete situations,
\mathcal{H}
is the Hilbert space of wave functions describing
1 particle
. If the particle is a
Boson
, then
\sym^{\otimes n}(\mathcal{H})
is the Hilbert space of the joint wave functions of
n
indistinguishable particles of the same type. The next two propositions suggest that each probability
L^{2}
space having Gaussian variables automatically “contains” infinitely many Bosons.

Proposition 1.3.1

(Wiener Chaos decomposition,
[
Jan97
]
theorem 2.6)
.

Let
(Q,\mathcal{O},\mathbb{P})
be a probability space and
\mathcal{H}\subset L^{2}(Q,\mathcal{O},\mathbb{P})
a Gaussian Hilbert space. Then there is an orthogonal decomposition

L^{2}(Q,\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H}),\mathbb{P})\cong\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{H}^{{:}n{:}},

(1.3.12)

where
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H})
is the
\sigma
-algebra generated by variables in
\mathcal{H}
,
\mathcal{H}^{{:}n{:}}=\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}(\mathcal{H})\cap\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n-1}(\mathcal{H})^{\perp}
, where
\mathcal{P}_{j}(\mathcal{H})
denotes the span of polynomials of random variables in
\mathcal{H}
of degree
\leqslant j
; in particular
\mathcal{H}^{{:}0{:}}
denotes the constants.
\Box

Proposition 1.3.2

(Itô-Wiener-Segal isomorphism)
.

Let
(Q,\mathcal{O},\mathbb{P})
be a probability space and
\mathcal{H}\subset L^{2}(Q,\mathcal{O},\mathbb{P})
a Gaussian Hilbert space. Then the map, denoting by
\odot
the symmetric tensor product,

X_{1}\odot\dots\odot X_{n}\longmapsto{:}X_{1}\cdots X_{n}{:}

(1.3.13)

gives a Hilbert space isomorphism
\sym^{\otimes n}(\mathcal{H})\cong\mathcal{H}^{{:}n{:}}
. Moreover, the direct sum of these maps for each
n
extends to a Hilbert space isomorphism

\Gamma(\mathcal{H})\cong L^{2}(Q,\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H}),\mathbb{P}).

(1.3.14)

Consequently for
X_{1}
, …,
X_{n}\in\mathcal{H}
,
X_{1}\odot\dots\odot X_{n}
and
{:}X_{1}\cdots X_{n}{:}
are indistinguishable.

Proof.

It follows from the observation that the
L^{2}(Q)
inner product restricted to
\mathcal{H}^{{:}n{:}}
coincides with the symmetric one, by Wick’s theorem (
1.3.16
) which we discuss below.
∎

If
F\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}(\mathcal{H})
, denote by
{:}F{:}
the projection of
F
onto
\mathcal{H}^{{:}n{:}}
, and is called a
Wick ordered polynomial
; for
F\in L^{2}(Q,\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H}),\mathbb{P})
, denote by
I_{n}(F)
its projection onto
\mathcal{H}^{{:}n{:}}
. Computation rules for Wick ordering generally go under the name
Wick’s theorem
or
Feynman rules
. The terms in those formulas can be systematically represented by
Feynman diagrams
, and are generally expressed in terms of the
Hermite polynomials

h_{n}(x)
, defined by

\exp\Big(zx-\frac{1}{2}z^{2}\Big)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{z^{n}}{n!}h_{n}(x).

(1.3.15)

We have
h_{0}(x)=1
,
h_{2}(x)=x^{2}-1
,
h_{4}(x)=x^{4}-6x^{2}+3
, etc. Useful formulas include

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[{:}X_{1}\dots X_{n}{:}\penalty\ {:}Y_{1}\dots Y_{n}{:}\big]=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[X_{i}Y_{\sigma(i)}],

(1.3.16)

\displaystyle{:}X^{n}{:}=\mathbb{E}\big[X^{2}\big]^{\frac{n}{2}}h_{n}\big(X\big/\mathbb{E}[X^{2}]^{\frac{1}{2}}\big),

(1.3.17)

\displaystyle Y{:}X_{1}\cdots X_{n}{:}={:}YX_{1}\cdots X_{n}{:}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\big[YX_{j}\big]{:}X_{1}\cdots\widehat{X_{j}}\cdots X_{n}{:}.

(1.3.18)

Here
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
is the symmetric group. Next we indicate how
operators
correspond under (
1.3.14
).

Proposition 1.3.3

(
[
Jan97
]
theorem 4.5)
.

Let
(Q,\mathcal{O},\mathbb{P})
be a probability space and
\mathcal{H}
,
\mathcal{K}\subset L^{2}(Q,\mathcal{O},\mathbb{P})
two Gaussian Hilbert spaces. Denote respectively by
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H})
and
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{K})
the sub-
\sigma
-algebra generated by variables in
\mathcal{H}
and in
\mathcal{K}
. Let
A:\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow\mathcal{K}
be an operator which is a
contraction
, namely
\left\lVert A\right\rVert\leqslant 1
. Then

\left.\begin{array}[]{rcl}\Gamma(A):L^{2}(Q,\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H}),\mathbb{P})&\longrightarrow&L^{2}(Q,\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{K}),\mathbb{P}),\\
{:}X_{1}\cdots X_{n}{:}&\longmapsto&{:}A(X_{1})\cdots A(X_{n}){:},\end{array}\right.

(1.3.19)

for any
X_{1}
, …,
X_{n}\in\mathcal{H}
, is a bounded operator with norm
1
.

Lastly we point out that the operation “
\Gamma
” has a nice functoriality property:

Proposition 1.3.4

(
[
Jan97
]
page 45)
.

Following the previous proposition let
\mathcal{L}\subset L^{2}(Q,\mathcal{O},\mathbb{P})
be another Gaussian Hilbert space and
B:\mathcal{K}\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}
another operator with
\left\lVert B\right\rVert\leqslant 1
. Then

1.

\Gamma(BA)=\Gamma(B)\Gamma(A)
as operators
\Gamma(\mathcal{H})\to\Gamma(\mathcal{L})
;

2.

\Gamma(A^{*})=\Gamma(A)^{*}:\Gamma(\mathcal{K})\to\Gamma(\mathcal{H})
,
(\bullet)^{*}
being the adjoint;

3.

\Gamma(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{H}})=\mathbbm{1}_{\Gamma(\mathcal{H})}
for any
\mathcal{H}
.

Fock Representation of the CCR

Let
(Q,\mathcal{O},\mathbb{P})
be a probability space and
\mathcal{H}\subset L^{2}(Q,\mathcal{O},\mathbb{P})
a Gaussian Hilbert space. We have seen above that in the case
\mathcal{H}
generates
\mathcal{O}
,
L^{2}(Q,\mathcal{O},\mathbb{P})
is naturally identified with the (Boson) Fock space
\Gamma(\mathcal{H})
over
\mathcal{H}
, and algebraic operations on Fock spaces are thus carried over to
L^{2}(Q,\mathcal{O},\mathbb{P})
. In this subsection, we present one of the most important operations. We construct (Boson)
creation
and
annihilation operators
acting on
L^{2}(Q)
, satisfying a set of
canonical commutation relations
(CCR), that generalizes (
1.2.28
) and (
1.2.29
).
In example
1.3.3
we apply the construction to the 1D case, rebuilding the operators (
1.2.15
, with
m=\omega=1
) of the harmonic oscillator from the invariant measure
\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{1}{2})
of the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process. This provides one more link between the two objects, furnishing (
1.2.51
).

Denote
\Omega_{0}:=1_{Q}\in L^{2}(Q,\mathcal{O},\mathbb{P})
, and the (abstract) random variable corresponding to
f\in\mathcal{H}
by
\phi(f)
.

Definition 1.3.2

(Fock representation on
L^{2}(Q)
)
.

For
f\in\mathcal{H}
and each
n\geqslant 0
define
17
17

17

Our conventions on the symmetrization operator
\sym^{\otimes n}
and inner product on
\sym^{\otimes n}(\mathcal{H})
are such as to make (
1.3.13
) an isometry and the following definitions coincide with the usual one on Fock space. This convention is notably adopted in
[
Jaf05
]
.

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}(f)\big({:}\phi(f_{1})\cdots\phi(f_{n}){:}\big)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left\langle f,f_{j}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}{:}\phi(f_{1})\cdots\widehat{\phi(f_{j})}\cdots\phi(f_{n}){:},

(1.3.20)

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}^{\dagger}(f)\big({:}\phi(f_{1})\cdots\phi(f_{n}){:}\big)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}{:}\phi(f)\phi(f_{1})\cdots\phi(f_{n}){:}.

(1.3.21)

Moreover,
\mathbf{a}^{\dagger}(f)\Omega_{0}:=\phi(f)={:}\phi(f){:}
and
\mathbf{a}(f)\Omega_{0}:=0
.

Example 1.3.3
.

We will consider
Q=\mathbb{R}
with the Borel
\sigma
-algebra and
\mathbb{P}=\gamma_{1,1}=\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{1}{2})
, that is

\mathrm{d}\gamma_{1,1}(x)=\sqrt{\frac{1}{\pi}}\textrm{e}^{-x^{2}}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}}(x),

(1.3.22)

here
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}}
being the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}
. Set
\mathcal{H}=\spn\{x\}
.
Since
x
generates the Borel
\sigma
-algebra, we have
L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\gamma_{1,1})=\Gamma(\mathcal{H})
. The normalized basis of
\mathcal{H}
consists of
X=\sqrt{2}x
and thus by (
1.3.17
),

{:}X^{n}{:}=h_{n}(X)=h_{n}(\sqrt{2}x).

(1.3.23)

In this setting, let

A\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathbf{a}(X)=\mathbf{a}(\sqrt{2}x),\quad\quad A^{\dagger}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathbf{a}^{\dagger}(X)=\mathbf{a}^{\dagger}(\sqrt{2}x),

(1.3.24)

then
[A,A^{\dagger}]=\mathbbm{1}
by computing with Hermite polynomials. Indeed, from (
1.3.15
) we have recurrence formulas

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}h_{n}^{\prime}(\theta)=nh_{n-1}(\theta),\\
h_{n+1}(\theta)=\theta h_{n}(\theta)-h_{n}^{\prime}(\theta).\end{array}\right.

(1.3.25)

This, via the prescriptions (
1.3.20
) and (
1.3.21
), gives

Ag=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}g}{\mathrm{d}x},\quad\quad A^{\dagger}g=\sqrt{2}xg-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}g}{\mathrm{d}x},

(1.3.26)

with
g
being any finite linear combination of
\{h_{n}(\sqrt{2}x)\}
. To fully recover (
1.2.15
), we need to conjugate by the ground state transform defined in (
1.2.48
) since the natural Hilbert space for quantum mechanics is
L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}})
. Doing this, we find

\Omega_{0}A\Omega_{0}^{-1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Big(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}+x\Big)=\mathbf{A}_{1,1},\quad\quad\Omega_{0}A^{\dagger}\Omega_{0}^{-1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Big(-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}+x\Big)=\mathbf{A}_{1,1}^{\dagger},

(1.3.27)

acting on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}})
.

In general, by using (
1.3.16
) and the definitions one can show the following.

Proposition 1.3.5
.

With the above definitions (
1.3.20
), (
1.3.21
) we have

\big\langle\mathbf{a}^{\dagger}(f)F,G\big\rangle_{L^{2}(Q)}=\big\langle F,\mathbf{a}(f)G\big\rangle_{L^{2}(Q)}

(1.3.28)

for
F
,
G\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{N}(\mathcal{H})
for some
N<\infty
, and

\displaystyle[\mathbf{a}(f),\mathbf{a}(h)]

\displaystyle=[\mathbf{a}^{\dagger}(f),\mathbf{a}^{\dagger}(h)]=0,

(1.3.29)

\displaystyle[\mathbf{a}(f),\mathbf{a}^{\dagger}(h)]

\displaystyle=\left\langle f,h\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},

(1.3.30)

for
f
,
h\in\mathcal{H}
.
\Box

By (
1.3.18
) the random variable
\phi(f)
acting as a multiplication operator on each
\mathcal{H}^{{:}n{:}}
satisfies

\phi(f)=\mathbf{a}^{\dagger}(f)+\mathbf{a}(f).

(1.3.31)

Using further Wick formulas, one can show (
[
Sim74
]
pp. 24) that the multiplication by
{:}\phi(f)^{n}{:}
is accordingly

{:}\phi(f)^{n}{:}=\sum_{p=0}^{n}\binom{n}{p}\mathbf{a}^{\dagger}(f)^{n-p}\mathbf{a}(f)^{p}.

(1.3.32)

Therefore, the Wick ordering
{:}\bullet{:}
amounts to
moving annihilation operators to the right
, and
creation operators to the left
. In regard to the important remark
1.2.1
, we notice that a common domain for all the
\mathbf{a}
,
\mathbf{a}^{\dagger}
’s is

\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{Fk}}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\spn\big\{f\in L^{2}(Q)\penalty\ \big|\penalty\ f\in\mathcal{H}^{{:}N{:}}\textrm{ for some }N<\infty\big\},

(1.3.33)

which by proposition
1.3.1
is
dense
in
L^{2}(Q)
. We end our discussion with the following definition.

Definition 1.3.3

(
[
GJ87
]
definition 6.3.2)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a real pre-Hilbert space with inner product
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathcal{S}}
and
\mathscr{H}
a complex Hilbert space. Then a
Fock representation of the CCR
over
\mathcal{S}
on
\mathscr{H}
is a pair of linear maps

\left.\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathbf{a},\mathbf{a}^{\dagger}:\mathcal{S}&\longrightarrow&\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}(\textrm{\small Unbounded})\\
\textrm{\small Operators on }\mathscr{H}\end{array}\right\}\\[15.0pt]
f&\longmapsto&\mathbf{a}(f),\mathbf{a}^{\dagger}(f).\end{array}\right.

(1.3.34)

such that

1.

there is a unit vector
\Omega\in\mathscr{H}
with
\mathbf{a}(f)\Omega=0
for all
f\in\mathcal{S}
;

2.

all of
\mathbf{a}(f)
and
\mathbf{a}^{\dagger}(f)
,
f\in\mathcal{S}
, have the common domain

\mathscr{D}_{\mathrm{Fk}}=\spn\big\{\mathbf{a}^{\dagger}(f_{1})\cdots\mathbf{a}^{\dagger}(f_{n})\Omega\penalty\ \big|\penalty\ f_{1},\cdots,f_{n}\in\mathcal{S},\penalty\ n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}\big\}

(1.3.35)

which is dense in
\mathscr{H}
;

3.

we have
\mathbf{a}(f)\mathscr{D}_{\mathrm{Fk}}\subset\mathscr{D}_{\mathrm{Fk}}
,
\mathbf{a}^{\dagger}(f)\mathscr{D}_{\mathrm{Fk}}\subset\mathscr{D}_{\mathrm{Fk}}
, (
1.3.28
), (
1.3.29
), and (
1.3.30
) for all
f
,
h\in\mathcal{S}
,
F
,
G\in\mathscr{D}_{\mathrm{Fk}}
, with
\mathscr{H}
in place of
L^{2}(Q)
and
\mathcal{S}
in place of
\mathcal{H}
.

Remark 1.3.1
.

The Fock representation over the same real pre-Hilbert space is unique up to unitary equivalence (
[
GJ87
]
theorem 6.3.4).

Remark 1.3.2
.

For parallel results on Fermions, see
[
CHP23
]
section 2. These are carried out in the context of
non-commutative
probability theory.

General Gaussian Measures

In this subsection we discuss general Gaussian measures on (real) separable Fréchet spaces: they are uniquely determined by their
covariance structures
(cf. (
1.3.9
), assuming they are all centered), and on the other hand, on a special class of spaces we could always construct a Gaussian measure with a given covariance structure (Minlos’ theorem). Readers focused on quantization may take these two statements for granted and skip this subsection. The main reference is
[
Bog98
]
, but see also
[
Hai23
]
section 4 for a shorter and lucid account.

Definition 1.3.4
.

A Borel probability measure
\gamma
on a locally convex topological vector space
\mathcal{X}
is called (centered)
Gaussian
if every linear functional
f\in\mathcal{X}^{*}
,
f:\mathcal{X}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
, is a (centered) Gaussian random variable on
\mathcal{X}
.

In the general situation we will also denote the random variable represented by the linear functional
f
by
\phi(f)
or
\left\langle\phi,f\right\rangle
, thinking of
\phi
as a random vector following
\gamma
in
\mathcal{X}
. An important tool for identifying Gaussian measures is the (inverse) Fourier transform, more frequently called the
charateristic function
,
\widehat{\gamma}:\mathcal{X}^{*}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
, defined as

\widehat{\gamma}(f)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\int_{\mathcal{X}}\textrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\phi(f)}\mathrm{d}\gamma(\phi).

(1.3.36)

This is useful because of the following property.

Lemma 1.3.6

(
[
Bog98
]
theorem 2.2.4, proposition A.3.18)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a real separable Fréchet (or Banach) space, then any two Borel measures on
\mathcal{X}
with equal characteristic functions coincide. In particular, a Borel probability measure
\gamma
on
\mathcal{X}
is a centered Gaussian measure if and only if

\widehat{\gamma}(f)=\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\phi(f)^{2}]}

(1.3.37)

for all
f\in\mathcal{X}^{*}
, where
\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}
is the expectation with respect to
\gamma
.

Corollary 1.3.7
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a real separable Fréchet (or Banach) space, then any two centered Borel Gaussian measures on
\mathcal{X}
with the same
covariance structure
must coincide; that is, if

\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\phi(f)\phi(h)]=\mathbb{E}_{\widetilde{\gamma}}[\phi(f)\phi(h)]

(1.3.38)

for all
f
,
h\in\mathcal{X}^{*}
, then
\gamma=\widetilde{\gamma}
.
\Box

Now we turn to the existence result which applies to a class of Fréchet topological vector spaces called
nuclear spaces
. While postponing their definition to appendix
B.3
, we point out that the two most typical examples of nuclear spaces are
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the Schwartz functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
C^{\infty}(M)
, the smooth functions on a closed smooth manifold. Their topological duals are accordingly
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
. The main reference is
[
GV64
]
section IV.3.2 theorem 2 (chapter I for nuclear space). Helpful information can be found in
[
HL20
]
section 1.4.2,
[
BS18
]
sections 5.10-11,
[
GJ87
]
appendix A.4.

Proposition 1.3.8

(Minlos)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{\infty}
be a nuclear space and
\mathcal{H}_{-\infty}
its topological dual space. Then given a continuous positive definite Hermitian bilinear form (indeed, an
inner product
)
\mathcal{C}
on
\mathcal{H}_{\infty}\times\mathcal{H}_{\infty}
, there exists a unique (centered) Borel Gaussian probability measure
\mu_{\mathcal{C}}
on
\mathcal{H}_{-\infty}
such that

\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\phi(f)\phi(h)]=\mathcal{C}(f,h)

(1.3.39)

for every
f
,
h\in\mathcal{H}_{\infty}
.

Proof.

See
[
HL20
]
section 1.4.2,
[
GJ87
]
appendix A.4.
∎

Quantization on the Cylinder
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}

In this section we traverse through the second line of development in the map
1.2
, for the free real scalar field over the circle, taken as space, and the cylinder, taken as space-time. This is the simplest and most fundamental type of field theory. Most of subsections
1.4.1
and
1.4.2
are standard introductory graduate-level materials in the physics department, see e.g.
[
PS95
]
and
[
CDG+18
]
. Our presentation, however, will have slightly different flavor and emphasis, oriented towards the aspects that could be mathematically “rigorized”. In addition, in subsection
1.4.2
(cf. (
1.4.22
)) we point out a precise relationship between the “ground state” of the free field on the Minkowski cylinder with a Gaussian measure on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
analogous to the relationship (
1.2.46
) in the 1D case.

From Particles to Fields

In this subsection we describe the classical field theory of a free real scalar field over the circle
\mathbb{S}^{1}
. We shall say that a typical
field configuration

\varphi
belongs to
\mathrm{Map}(\mathbb{S}^{1},\mathbb{R})
, where by writing “Map” we mean functions on which we deliberately do not specify the regularity, as the purpose of this subsection is rather heuristic. More precisely, we will proceed by the following analogy with the point particle in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
described in subsection
1.2.1
.

real scalar field

particle in
\mathbb{R}^{d}

configuration space

\mathrm{Map}(\mathbb{S}^{1},\mathbb{R})

\mathbb{R}^{d}

“position-velocity”

space

T\mathrm{Map}(\mathbb{S}^{1},\mathbb{R})\cong\mathrm{Map}(\mathbb{S}^{1},\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{Map}(\mathbb{S}^{1},\mathbb{R})

T\mathbb{R}^{d}

phase space

T^{*}\mathrm{Map}(\mathbb{S}^{1},\mathbb{R})\cong\mathrm{Map}(\mathbb{S}^{1},\mathbb{R})\times\mathrm{Map}(\mathbb{S}^{1},\mathbb{R})

T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{d}

index for

“coordinate”

\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
or
n\in\mathbb{N}
(see example
1.4.2
)

1\leqslant i\leqslant d

Remark 1.4.1
.

The real scalar field is a simplest type of field that one can consider. The “configuration” spaces of other types of fields (over some manifold
M
) include the section space of a complex vector bundle over
M
(e.g. for
spinor fields
), the space
\mathrm{Map}(M,N)
of maps into a certain Riemannian manifold
N
(
\sigma
-models
), or space of connections on a principal bundle associated to a compact Lie group
G
over
M
(
gauge fields
).
Moreover, different types of fields can be “coupled” (e.g. spinor field to a gauge field). We refer to
[
RS17
]
chapter 7 for some detailed discussion of modern field theory models in mathematical language.

Now we give the defining data of the real scalar free field as follows.

\displaystyle\textrm{{Lagrangian density}}:

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}(\varphi,\dot{\varphi})(\theta)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\frac{\beta}{4\pi}\big(\dot{\varphi}(\theta)^{2}-(\partial_{\theta}\varphi)(\theta)^{2}-m^{2}\varphi(\theta)^{2}\big),

(1.4.1)

\displaystyle\textrm{{Lagrangian}}:

\displaystyle L(\varphi,\dot{\varphi})

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\mathscr{L}(\varphi,\dot{\varphi})(\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\theta=\frac{\beta}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\big(\dot{\varphi}(\theta)^{2}-(\partial_{\theta}\varphi)(\theta)^{2}-m^{2}\varphi(\theta)^{2}\big)\,\mathrm{d}\theta

(1.4.2)

\displaystyle\textrm{{Action over }}[0,T]:

\displaystyle S_{[0,T]}^{\mathrm{free}}(\phi)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\frac{\beta}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\big((\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}-(\partial_{\theta}\phi)^{2}-m^{2}\phi^{2}\big)\,\mathrm{d}\theta\,\mathrm{d}t.

(1.4.3)

Here
m\geqslant 0
,
\beta>0
are some fixed parameters, and
\phi:[0,T]\longrightarrow\mathrm{Map}(\mathbb{S}^{1},\mathbb{R})
or equivalently
[0,T]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
, is the trajectory through which a configuration in
\mathrm{Map}(\mathbb{S}^{1},\mathbb{R})
evolves over
[0,T]
.

Below, we discuss this model for two choices of
m
,
\beta
with different purposes: in example
1.4.1
we let
m>0
,
\beta=2\pi
and obtain the Hamiltonian picture and Newtonian equation of motion, which results in an analogue of the
Klein-Gordon
equation on the circle; then in example
1.4.2
we describe this model in a special set of coordinates on
\mathrm{Map}(\mathbb{S}^{1},\mathbb{R})
, in the case
m=0
, which prepares for the next subsection.

Example 1.4.1

(
m>0
,
\beta=2\pi
, Klein-Gordon)
.

To transit to the Hamiltonian picture, the first step is to find an analogue of the “canonical momentum” of the point particle in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Before doing that, it would be beneficial to go back and make the analogy more precise. The convenient point of view here is to treat
\varphi(\theta)
as an ordinary coordinate for
\varphi
just as
x^{i}
is for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and taking the
\theta
-derivative on
\mathrm{Map}(\mathbb{S}^{1},\mathbb{R})
could be compared to a linear map
T
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Now a precise analogy of our Lagrangian for a particle in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
would be

L(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{v})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}\big[(v^{i})^{2}-((T\mathbf{x})^{i})^{2}-m^{2}(x^{i})^{2}\big].

(1.4.4)

The good news is that whatever
T
may be,
T\mathbf{x}
does not involve any
v^{i}
. So the canonical momentum conjugate to the coordinate
x^{i}
is as usual
p_{i}=\partial L/\partial(v^{i})=v^{i}
. Going back to our scalar field, we obtain by direct analogy the
momentum density

\pi(\theta)=\dot{\varphi}(\theta)
. Now by comparing (
1.2.6
) with (
1.2.9
), and then with our case, we also write down by direct analogy the
Hamiltonian

H(\varphi,\pi)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\big(|\pi(\theta)|^{2}+|\partial_{\theta}\varphi(\theta)|^{2}+m^{2}|\varphi(\theta)|^{2}\big)\,\mathrm{d}\theta.

(1.4.5)

Next, what is the Hamiltonian equation of motion? To continue with our analogy, we now need to find the infinite dimensional gradient of the potential

V(\varphi)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}(|\partial_{\theta}\varphi(\theta)|^{2}+m^{2}\varphi^{2}(\theta))\,\mathrm{d}\theta.

(1.4.6)

To proceed, we notice that the
L^{2}
inner product on
\mathrm{Map}(\mathbb{S}^{1},\mathbb{R})
now plays the role of the standard inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(more precisely, the Riemannian metric on the corresponding tangent spaces). Therefore, by definition of the “gradient”,

\left\langle\nabla V(\varphi),h\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(\mathrm{D}_{h}V)(\varphi)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}(V(\varphi+\varepsilon h)-V(\varphi)),

(1.4.7)

where
\mathrm{D}_{h}V
denotes the “directional derivative” in the direction of the “test function”
h\in\mathrm{Map}(\mathbb{S}^{1},\mathbb{R})
. Applying the Green-Stokes formula we get

V(\varphi+\varepsilon h)-V(\varphi)=\varepsilon\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\big((-\partial_{\theta}^{2}\varphi)h+m^{2}\varphi h\big)\,\mathrm{d}\theta+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2}).

(1.4.8)

Therefore,
\nabla V(\varphi)=(-\partial_{\theta}^{2}+m^{2})\varphi
. We could then proceed to write down the Hamiltonian as well as Newtonian equations. The Newtonian equation is

\Big(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}-\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\theta^{2}}+m^{2}\Big)\varphi=0,

(1.4.9)

which is an analogue of the
Klein-Gordon
equation on the circle. Lastly, we mention that the “value” of
\nabla V(\varphi)
at
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
corresponds heuristically to the case
h=\delta_{\theta}
, the delta function supported at
\theta
. The commonly used notation is

\frac{\delta V}{\delta\varphi(\theta)}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(\mathrm{D}_{\delta_{\theta}}V)(\varphi).

(1.4.10)

The
\mathrm{D}_{h}V
and
\delta V/\delta\varphi(\theta)
are usually called
functional derivatives
and could be made rigorous under various assumptions. See
[
GJ87
]
section 9.1,
[
Dim11
]
section 12.1.3 or
[
Fol08
]
pp. 268-269 for details.

Example 1.4.2

(
m=0
, Fourier coordinates)
.

In the previous example we exploited an analogy with particle mechanics to analyze the real scalar field, by treating
\varphi(\theta)
as a “coordinate” for
\varphi\in\mathrm{Map}(\mathbb{S}^{1},\mathbb{R})
. For the purpose of quantization which we do in the next subsection, another set of coordinates will prove convenient. These are given by the Fourier decomposition. For precision we now stress that our circle has perimeter
2\pi
, and
\theta
is the arc length parameter. Given the trajectory
\phi:[0,T]\longrightarrow\mathrm{Map}(\mathbb{S}^{1},\mathbb{R})
, we write

\phi(t,\theta)=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\phi_{n}(t)\textrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}n\theta},\quad\quad\textrm{with }\phi_{-n}=\overline{\phi_{n}},

(1.4.11)

as
\phi
is real-valued
18
18

18

Alternatively, we could also write
\phi(t,\theta)=\phi_{0}(t)+2\sum_{n>0}(x_{n}(t)\cos(n\theta)-y_{n}(t)\sin(n\theta))
with
\phi_{0}(t)\in\mathbb{R}
,
x_{n}(t)=\mathfrak{Re}(\phi_{n}(t))
and
y_{n}(t)=\mathfrak{Im}(\phi_{n}(t))
.
. Now we make an important observation: thanks to the fact that
\{\textrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}n\theta}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is orthogonal
19
19

19

Note
\lVert\textrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}n\theta}\rVert_{L^{2}}=2\pi
.
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
, the
action
now also decomposes into a sum:

\displaystyle S_{[0,T]}^{\mathrm{free}}(\phi)

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta}{2}\int_{0}^{T}|\phi_{0}^{\prime}(t)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t+\beta\sum_{n>0}\int_{0}^{T}\big(|\phi_{n}^{\prime}(t)|^{2}-n^{2}|\phi_{n}(t)|^{2}\big)\,\mathrm{d}t

(1.4.12)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}S_{[0,T]}^{(0)}(\phi_{0})+\sum_{n>0}S_{[0,T]}^{(n)}(\phi_{n}),

(1.4.13)

where by the latter relation in (
1.4.11
) the two terms involving the modes
\phi_{-n}
and
\phi_{n}
are combined into one in (
1.4.12
). Therefore,
\phi
minimizes
S_{[0,T]}^{\mathrm{free}}
if and only if each
\phi_{k}
,
k\geqslant 0
, minimizes
S_{[0,T]}^{(k)}
individually. This means that for the
classical
field
\phi
, the evolution over time of the space-slice configuration
\phi(t,\bullet)
under
S^{\mathrm{free}}
is equivalent to the evolution of the sequence of numbers
\{\phi_{n}(t)\}_{n\geqslant 0}
in which each
\phi_{n}(t)
evolves under
S^{(n)}
separately.
Note, in comparing with (
1.2.9
), that
S^{(0)}
describes a free particle with mass
\beta
moving in
\mathbb{R}
, and
S^{(n)}
,
n\geqslant 1
, the harmonic oscillator with mass
2\beta
and angular frequency
\omega_{n}=n
on
\mathbb{C}=\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Free Field on the Cylinder = Infinitely Many Harmonic Oscillators

Having described the classical mechanics of the (massless) real scalar free field
\phi
on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
, we will now try to quantize it. To begin with, we emphasize that we have identified heuristically
20
20

20

We remark here that
\varphi_{n}
are not the actual Fourier coefficients,
\sqrt{2\pi}\varphi_{n}
are, and we have
\left\langle\varphi,f\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}=(2\pi)[\varphi_{0}f_{0}+2\sum_{n\geqslant 1}\mathfrak{Re}(\overline{\varphi_{n}}f_{n})]
for another real “test function”
f
written in the form (
1.4.11
).

\left.\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{R}}:\mathrm{Map}(\mathbb{S}^{1},\mathbb{R})&\xlongrightarrow{\sim}&\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{N}},\\
\varphi&\longmapsto&(\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\dots),\end{array}\right.

(1.4.14)

where
\varphi_{i}
are the Fourier coefficients of
\varphi
as in (
1.4.11
). To quantize a system, two basic ingredients are the Hilbert space and the operators of time evolution. In analogy with the 1D case, the Hilbert space should be

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}L^{2}(\mathrm{Map}(\mathbb{S}^{1},\mathbb{R}),\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Map}(\mathbb{S}^{1},\mathbb{R})})\overset{\mathrm{heu}}{=}L^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{N}},\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{N}}})

(1.4.15)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{heu}}{=}L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}})\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\infty}L^{2}(\mathbb{C},\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{C}})_{i}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathcal{H}_{0}\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{H}_{i}

(1.4.16)

where
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{N}}}
denotes the non-existent Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{N}}
, and
L^{2}(\mathbb{C},\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{C}})_{i}
is the individual Hilbert space associated to the component
\varphi_{i}
. The recipe for time evolution, in one way, is given by the so-called
canonical quantization
:

In analogy with (
1.2.25
), we find that the
Hamiltonian
of
\phi
on
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}
should be

\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{fr},\beta}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathbf{H}_{\beta,0}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbf{H}_{2\beta,n},

(1.4.17)

where each
\mathbf{H}_{2\beta,n}
is defined as in (
1.2.12
), and its action extended to
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}
by imposing

\mathbf{H}_{2\beta,n}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{n-1}}\otimes(\mathbf{H}_{2\beta,n}|_{\mathcal{H}_{n}})\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{n+1}}\otimes\cdots,

(1.4.18)

and likewise for
\mathbf{H}_{\beta,0}
.

Next, we try to postulate a “ground state” for
\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{fr},\beta}
and relate it to a Gaussian measure on
\mathrm{Map}(\mathbb{S}^{1},\mathbb{R})
, preliminarily, in the spirit of (
1.2.46
). First we consider the components
\mathbf{H}_{2\beta,n}
with
n>0
. From example
1.2.3
we know that
\mathbf{H}_{2\beta,n}
has the ground state

\Omega_{(n)}(x,y)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\Big(\frac{2n\beta}{\pi}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\textrm{e}^{-n\beta(x^{2}+y^{2})}.

(1.4.19)

In analogy with the case of finite tensor product, the reasonable “ground state” for
\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{fr},\beta}
restricted to
\bigotimes_{i\geqslant 1}\mathcal{H}_{i}
is therefore

\displaystyle\Omega_{0,\beta}^{\mathrm{os}}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\cdots)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\Big(\frac{2n\beta}{\pi}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\textrm{e}^{-n\beta(x_{n}^{2}+y_{n}^{2})}=\bigg[\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\Big(\frac{2n\beta}{\pi}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg]\exp\Big(-\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}|n|\cdot|\varphi_{n}|^{2}\Big)

(1.4.20)

\displaystyle=\bigg[\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\Big(\frac{2n\beta}{\pi}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg]\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{\beta}{2\pi}\langle\varphi,\mathbf{D}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\varphi\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}}

(1.4.21)

where
\mathbf{D}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}=(\Delta_{\mathbb{S}^{1}})^{\frac{1}{2}}
, and
\varphi_{n}=x_{n}+\mathrm{i}y_{n}
. This suggests the following expression for a Gaussian probability measure on
\mathrm{Map}(\mathbb{S}^{1},\mathbb{R})
:

\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathbf{D},\mathbb{S}^{1}}(\varphi)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathrm{d}\Big[\bigotimes_{n=1}^{\infty}\gamma^{\mathbb{C}}_{2\beta,n}\Big](\varphi)=\big(\textrm{``}\det\textrm{''}(4\beta\mathbf{D}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}})\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{\beta}{2\pi}\langle\varphi,2\mathbf{D}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\varphi\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{C}}(\varphi_{n})}{2\pi}.

(1.4.22)

where we write in short hand
\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{C}}(0,(4n\beta)^{-1}):=\gamma^{\mathbb{C}}_{2\beta,n}
.
This measure has covariance
21
21

21

The discrepancy of a factor of 2 between what’s in the exponential of (
1.4.22
) and (
1.4.23
) as compared to the finite dimensional version (
1.3.2
) comes from the fact that the quadratic form
\left\langle\varphi,2\mathbf{D}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}
is a priori defined on
\mathrm{Map}(\mathbb{S}^{1},\mathbb{C})
, but only restricted to
\mathrm{Map}(\mathbb{S}^{1},\mathbb{R})
.

\mathbb{E}\bigl|\left\langle\varphi,f\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}\bigr|^{2}=\frac{4\pi}{\beta}\big\langle f,(2\mathbf{D}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}})^{-1}f\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})},

(1.4.23)

for real test functions
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
. Accordingly from example
1.2.2
we know that the “ground state” of the free Hamiltonian
\mathbf{H}_{\beta,0}
on the zero mode is simply the function
1
. Thus
|1|^{2}
gives nothing but the Lebesgue measure. Therefore an infinite dimensional analogue of the
ground state transform
(cf. (
1.2.48
)) gives a formal isomorphic representation

\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\cong L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}})\otimes\bigotimes_{n=1}^{\infty}L^{2}(\mathbb{C},\gamma^{\mathbb{C}}_{2\beta,n}).

(1.4.24)

Note that unlike (
1.4.16
), (
1.4.24
) can actually be given a rigorous meaning since the second component makes sense as the
L^{2}
space over
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{N}}
equipped with a Gaussian measure.

Each
L^{2}(\mathbb{C},\gamma^{\mathbb{C}}_{2\beta,n})
now carries a Fock representation of the CCR as suggested by example
1.3.3
in the 1D case. For conformal purposes it turns out more convenient to write the representation in complex coordinates as introduced in example
1.2.3
. We thus define the (ground state conjugated) operators

\mathbf{a}_{2\beta}(n)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\beta n}}\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{\varphi}_{n}}+n\beta\varphi_{n}\Big),\quad\quad\overline{\mathbf{a}}_{2\beta}(n)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\beta n}}\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial\varphi_{n}}+n\beta\overline{\varphi}_{n}\Big).

(1.4.25)

Then we would have
\mathbf{H}_{2\beta,n}=n(\mathbf{a}^{\dagger}_{2\beta}(n)\mathbf{a}_{2\beta}(n)+\overline{\mathbf{a}}^{\dagger}_{2\beta}(n)\overline{\mathbf{a}}_{2\beta}(n)+1)
and

\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{fr},\beta}=-\frac{1}{2\beta}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\varphi_{0}^{2}}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n(\mathbf{a}^{\dagger}_{2\beta}(n)\mathbf{a}_{2\beta}(n)+\overline{\mathbf{a}}^{\dagger}_{2\beta}(n)\overline{\mathbf{a}}_{2\beta}(n)+1).

(1.4.26)

Note here that we have an infinite ground energy
E_{0}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n
, where it is customary to adopt
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n=:\zeta(-1)=-\frac{1}{12}
(
[
FMS97
]
pp. 172).
Given a smooth real test function
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
written in the form
f=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}f_{n}\textrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}n\theta}
again with
f_{-n}=\overline{f}_{n}
we have from (
1.4.25
) the following representation of the multiplication operator
\boldsymbol{\varphi}(f):F\mapsto\left\langle\varphi,f\right\rangle_{L^{2}}F
on
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}
:

\boldsymbol{\varphi}(f)=2\pi\varphi_{0}f_{0}+\frac{2\pi}{\sqrt{2\beta}}\sum_{n>0}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\big[f_{-n}(\mathbf{a}_{2\beta}(n)+\overline{\mathbf{a}}^{\dagger}_{2\beta}(n))+f_{n}(\mathbf{a}^{\dagger}_{2\beta}(n)+\overline{\mathbf{a}}_{2\beta}(n))\big].

(1.4.27)

Actually, since
\varphi_{-n}=\overline{\varphi}_{n}
, namely
x_{-n}=x_{n}
,
y_{-n}=-y_{n}
, we have
\overline{\mathbf{a}}_{2\beta}(n)=\mathbf{a}_{2\beta,-n}
. Thus (
1.4.27
) shows the operator valued distribution
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
is also given formally by

\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\theta)=\varphi_{0}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\beta}}\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{1}{\sqrt{|n|}}\big(\textrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}n\theta}\mathbf{a}_{2\beta}(n)+\textrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}n\theta}\mathbf{a}^{\dagger}_{2\beta}(n)\big).

(1.4.28)

Here
\{\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\theta)\}_{\theta}
could be thought of as an
\mathbb{S}^{1}
-indexed family of “position operators”
\mathbf{X}_{\theta}:F\mapsto\varphi(\theta)\cdot F
on
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}
.

Dynamics of the Massive Gaussian Free Field

In this subsection we are interested in describing the “slice dynamics” of the massive Gaussian Free Field (GFF) on the infinite cylinder
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
. In the previous subsection we have seen that the quantum (massless) free field on the cylinder consists of infinitely many harmonic oscillators and a free particle; here we will see that the Euclidean (probabilistic) free field accordingly consists of infinitely many Ornstein-Uhlenbeck processes, while the counterpart of the free particle disappears due to the mass.

Let
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1})
denote the space of smooth functions on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
which are rapidly decaying (Schwartz) in the
\mathbb{R}
-direction, and accordingly
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1})
the dual space of distributions tempered in the
\mathbb{R}
-direction. Let
\Delta:=\Delta_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}}
denote the positive Laplacian. We have by Fourier transform
22
22

22

Now we use
\widehat{\chi}(k):=(2\pi)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\int\textrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}k\theta}\chi(\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\theta
on
\mathbb{S}^{1}
and
\widehat{f}(E)=\int\textrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}Et}f(t)\,\mathrm{d}t
on
\mathbb{R}
.

\left\langle f,(\Delta+\mathbbm{1})^{-1}f\right\rangle_{L^{2}}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{k=-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{|\widehat{f}(k,E)|^{2}}{k^{2}+E^{2}+1}\,\mathrm{d}E

(1.4.29)

for
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1})
and one sees that
(\Delta+\mathbbm{1})^{-1}
extends to a bounded positive self-adjoint operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1})
. By Minlos’ theorem (proposition
1.3.8
) there exists a unique Gaussian measure
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}}
(abb.
\mu
) on
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1})
with

\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\big[\phi(f)\phi(h)\big]=\big\langle f,(\Delta+\mathbbm{1})^{-1}h\big\rangle_{L^{2}},\quad\quad\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\big[\phi(f)\big]\equiv 0,

(1.4.30)

for all
f
,
h\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1})
,
\phi
being the random distribution following
\mu
, called the
massive GFF
with mass
=1
. We will proceed by the following (heuristic) analogy with the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process:

Ornstein-Uhlenbeck on
\mathbb{R}

massive GFF on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}

sample path

\omega\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})

\phi\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1})

configuration at time
t

X_{t}=\omega(t)\in\mathbb{R}

\phi|_{\{t\}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{S}^{1})

covariance

\mathbb{E}[X_{s}X_{t}]=\langle\delta_{s},(-\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}x^{2}}+1)^{-1}\delta_{t}\rangle

(
1.4.30
)

invariant measure

\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{1}{2})=\gamma_{1,1}

?

Hilbert space

L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\gamma_{1,1})

L^{2}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{S}^{1}),\penalty\ ?\penalty\ )

Feller semi-group

(
1.2.45
) or (
1.2.51
)

?

Our goal now is to find the question marks. Recall that due to the Gaussianity of the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process, we were able to read-off the fact that the law of each
X_{t}
is invariant for all
t
from the covariance structure, as all
X_{t}
’s have the same variance (and are centered). In the GFF case, we are led naturally to ask: pretend that the restriction map
\tau_{t}:\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1})\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
,
\phi\mapsto\phi|_{\{t\}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}}
is well-defined, what would be the image measure
(\tau_{t})_{*}\mu
living on
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
? Is it still Gaussian? If so, what is the covariance? Is it also invariant for all
t
?

Many of these questions have simple answers intuitively. Since
\tau_{t}
is
linear
, it follows from the definition
1.3.4
that
(\tau_{t})_{*}\mu
, which is the linear image of a Gaussian measure, should also be Gaussian, provided that we are allowed to apply the ‘‘change of variables’’ formula
23
23

23

That is,
\int F(\varphi)\,\mathrm{d}(\tau_{t})_{*}\mu=\int F(\tau_{t}\phi)\,\mathrm{d}\mu
.
. Moreover, since the covariance operator
(\Delta+\mathbbm{1})^{-1}
commutes with time translations,
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}
is time translation invariant, and therefore
(\tau_{t})_{*}\mu
should indeed be the same for all
t
, once we are able to define it. Along these lines, the main technical point thus boils down to making sense of the restriction map
\tau_{t}
in our low regularity scenario where it is ill-defined ordinarily. Note that in the probabilistic setting we need not make
\tau_{t}

continuous
of any sort; all we need is
measurability
. This will turn out to allow much more freedom; we leave the details to more specialized discussions (e.g.
[
Hai23
]
section 4.3).

(a)

GFF on a finite piece of Cylinder

(b)

Sharp-time Test Function

Figure 1.4
:
GFF on the Cylinder

To actually “know” the measure
(\tau_{t})_{*}\mu
, we still need to find the covariance. This, in other words, is to determine the bilinear form
24
24

24

Again having applied the “change of variables” formula above.

\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[(\tau_{t}\phi)(\chi)(\tau_{t}\phi)(\eta)]
for two (real) test functions
\chi
,
\eta\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
. In the first place, we need to have a good definition of the random variable
(\tau_{t}\phi)(\chi)
, and again, the problem is the low regularity of
\phi
. However, we see from the covariance structure (
1.4.30
) that as long as
f\in W^{-1}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1})
, the Sobolev space of order
-1
, we would be able to define the random variable
\phi(f)
uniquely as a limit in
L^{2}(\mu)
. Now observe that (by definition)

\left\langle\tau_{t}\phi,\chi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}=\left\langle\phi,J_{t}\chi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1})},\quad\quad\textrm{with }J_{t}(\chi)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\delta_{t}\otimes\chi,

(1.4.31)

and by Fourier transform,
\delta_{t}\otimes\chi
is actually in
W^{-1}
(see figure
1.4(b)
)! We thus find by (
1.3.10
)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\big[(\tau_{t}\phi)(\chi)(\tau_{t}\phi)(\eta)\big]

\displaystyle=\big\langle J_{t}\chi,(\Delta+\mathbbm{1})^{-1}J_{t}\eta\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1})}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{k}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\overline{\widehat{\chi}}(k)\widehat{\eta}(k)}{k^{2}+E^{2}+1}\,\mathrm{d}E

(1.4.32)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\frac{\overline{\widehat{\chi}}(k)\widehat{\eta}(k)}{2\sqrt{k^{2}+1}}=\big\langle\chi,(2(\Delta_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}+1)^{\frac{1}{2}})^{-1}\eta\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}.

(1.4.33)

This shows the covariance operator of
(\tau_{t})_{*}\mu
is
(2\mathbf{D}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}+1})^{-1}
setting
\mathbf{D}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}+1}:=(\Delta_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}+1)^{\frac{1}{2}}
(cf. (
1.4.23
)). Moreover, we are reassured that
(\tau_{t})_{*}\mu
is indeed independent of
t
. Note that the above calculations does not prevent us from taking two
different
time instants
s
and
t
for
\chi
and
\eta
respectively. Doing that, we find

\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\big[(\tau_{s}\phi)(\chi)(\tau_{t}\phi)(\eta)\big]=\big\langle\chi,\frac{\textrm{e}^{-|s-t|\cdot\mathbf{D}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}+1}}}{2\mathbf{D}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}+1}}\eta\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}.

(1.4.34)

This is the “1-particle” analogue of (
1.2.45
). If we switch to
L^{2}((\tau_{0})_{*}\mu)
on the r.h.s., that is, we regard
\chi
and
\eta
as random variables defined by pairing with
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
, then we get

\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\big[(\tau_{s}\phi)(\chi)(\tau_{t}\phi)(\eta)\big]=\big\langle\chi,\textrm{e}^{-|s-t|\cdot\mathbf{D}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}+1}}\,\eta\big\rangle_{L^{2}((\tau_{0})_{*}\mu)}.

(1.4.35)

The inner product (
1.4.33
) suggests that the
Gaussian Hilbert space
of
(\tau_{0})_{*}\mu
is
W^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
which corresponds to the “1-particle” Hilbert space. By proposition
1.3.1
its Fock space is exactly
L^{2}((\tau_{0})_{*}\mu)
and we can further promote (
1.4.35
) to general functionals
F
,
G\in L^{2}((\tau_{0})_{*}\mu)
by applying the “Fock functor”
\Gamma
:

\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\big[\overline{F(\tau_{s}\phi)}G(\tau_{t}\phi)\big]=\big\langle F,\Gamma(\textrm{e}^{-|s-t|\cdot\mathbf{D}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}+1}})\,G\big\rangle_{L^{2}((\tau_{0})_{*}\mu)}.

(1.4.36)

Since
\textrm{e}^{-|s-t|\cdot\mathbf{D}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}+1}}
acting on
W^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
is
contracting
,
further Fock space theory tells that

\Gamma(\textrm{e}^{-|s-t|\cdot\mathbf{D}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}+1}})=\textrm{e}^{-|s-t|\cdot\mathrm{d}\Gamma(\mathbf{D}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}+1})},

(1.4.37)

with
\mathrm{d}\Gamma(\mathbf{D}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}+1})|_{\sym^{\otimes n}}:=\mathbf{D}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}+1}\otimes\mathbbm{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbbm{1}+\cdots+\mathbbm{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbbm{1}\otimes\mathbf{D}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}+1}
called the
second quantization
of
\mathbf{D}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}+1}
(see
[
Sim74
]
pp. 32). It
seems
that we have found the Feller semi-group of the massive GFF, generated by
\mathbf{H}_{0}:=\mathrm{d}\Gamma(\mathbf{D}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}+1})
, and completed the program of determining the question marks!

Actually, we missed the
most important
point. Namely, we did not show that the GFF has a
Markov
property analogous to (
1.2.31
) and hence we do not yet have the Chapman-Kolmogorov lemma! One way to peek into this issue is to put
\chi=\eta=\textrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}k\theta}/\sqrt{2\pi}
and see from (
1.4.34
) that the Fourier coefficients
\widehat{\phi}_{k}(t)
of
\tau_{t}\phi
follow pairwise independent Ornstein-Uhlenbeck processes with
\mathbb{E}|\widehat{\phi}_{k}(t)|^{2}=(2\sqrt{k^{2}+1})^{-1}
. As the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process is Markov, it is reasonable that the “distribution valued” process
t\mapsto\tau_{t}\phi
is also Markov in time. Nelson
[
Nel73
]
, nevertheless, found the following beautiful generalization of the Markov property that is
covariant
and does not depend on the choice of a time coordinate, or a “foliation” of our space-time by timelike Cauchy hypersurfaces.

One important ingredient is to define “localized”
\sigma
-algebras
\mathcal{O}_{A}
associated to
closed subsets

A\subset\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
which generalizes the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{O}_{\leqslant t}=\sigma(X_{s}|s\leqslant t)
in 1D. Restricting to the GFF case, we exploit the fact that the random variable
\phi(f)
is well-defined for
f\in W^{-1}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1})
and put
\mathcal{O}_{A}:=\sigma(\phi(f)|\supp f\subset A,f\in W^{-1})
.

Proposition 1.4.1

(Nelson’s Markov property)
.

Let
A
,
B\subset\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
be closed subsets such that
A^{\circ}\cap B=\varnothing
. Then

\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[F|\mathcal{O}_{A}]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[F|\mathcal{O}_{\partial A}]

(1.4.38)

for all functionals
F\in L^{2}(\mu)
which are
\mathcal{O}_{B}
-measurable. Here
\partial A
denotes the boundary points of
A
.

Idea of proof.

First recall that for
L^{2}
random variables conditional expectation corresponds to orthogonal projection. For
Gaussian
variables, we can use the Fock functor
\Gamma
and see that (
1.4.38
) boils down to a fact about projections in the (space-time) “1-particle” Hilbert space, that is,
W^{-1}
. This is to say, if
f\in W^{-1}
and
\supp f\subset B
then
\supp(P_{A}f)\subset\partial A
and hence
P_{A}f=P_{\partial A}f
. Here
P_{A}
denotes the orthogonal projection in
W^{-1}
onto the closed subspace supported in
A
. This latter property of
W^{-1}
in turn boils down to the fact that
\Delta+\mathbbm{1}
, inverse of the covariance which gives the
action

\int|\nabla\phi|^{2}+\phi^{2}
of the GFF, is a
local
operator.
∎

With this Markov property in hand, it would be possible to proceed exactly as in the 1D case (using the Lie-Trotter formula) to derive a generalized “Feynman-Kac” type formula in our setting, modulo technicalities in defining rigorously the interaction potential, a version of which will be treated in section
2.3
. Heuristically, for a (real) test function
\chi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
we define, with
\varphi
following
(\tau_{0})_{*}\mu
, the random variable

V_{\mathrm{int}}(\chi)(\varphi)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\chi(\theta){:}\varphi^{2n}(\theta){:}\,\mathrm{d}\theta.

(1.4.39)

Acting on
\mathcal{H}^{{:}m{:}}
by multiplication, we have heuristically (
[
GRS75
]
pp. 142), by the slogan below (
1.3.32
),

V_{\mathrm{int}}(\chi)=\sum_{k_{1},\cdots,k_{2n}}\overline{\widehat{\chi}}(k_{1}+\cdots+k_{2n})\sum_{j=0}^{n}\binom{2n}{j}\frac{\mathbf{a}^{\dagger}(-k_{1})\cdots\mathbf{a}^{\dagger}(-k_{j})\mathbf{a}(k_{j+1})\cdots\mathbf{a}(k_{2n})}{2^{\frac{n}{2}}(k_{1}^{2}+1)^{\frac{1}{4}}\cdots(k_{2n}^{2}+1)^{\frac{1}{4}}},

(1.4.40)

where
\mathbf{a}^{\dagger}(k)
and
\mathbf{a}(k)
, like (
1.4.25
), are the ground state conjugated complex creation and annihilation operators corresponding to the
k
-th Fourier mode following the measure
\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{C}}(0,\frac{1}{2}(k^{2}+1)^{-\frac{1}{2}})
.
We conclude the present subsection with the 2D analogue of (
1.2.76
) which summarizes in a sense the probabilistic interpretation of the Euclidean field theory.

Proposition 1.4.2

(Feynman-Kac-Nelson,
[
GRS75
]
Theorem II.14)
.

Let
\mu=\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}
, and
\chi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
as above. Then

\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\Big[\overline{F(\tau_{0}\phi)}\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\chi(\theta){:}\phi^{2n}(s,\theta){:}\,\mathrm{d}\theta\,\mathrm{d}s\Big)G(\tau_{t}\phi)\Big]=\big\langle F,\textrm{e}^{-t(\mathbf{H}_{0}+V_{\mathrm{int}}(\chi))}G\big\rangle_{L^{2}((\tau_{0})_{*}\mu)},

(1.4.41)

for any
F
,
G\in L^{2}((\tau_{0})_{*}\mu)
and
t>0
.
\Box

The Functorial Framework

In the previous sections we have been describing quantum evolution over space-times where there is always a distinguished time coordinate (space-time
=
space
\times
time). For
general
curved space-times this is no longer the case. To deal with the more general situation Atiyah and Segal
[
Seg04
]
proposed the following
functorial
framework, motivated partly by gauge theories as well as string theory. In this subsection we describe a very rough version of this framework (as multiple precise versions exist oriented towards different purposes), and finally relate it to the more traditional point of view (Feynman-Kac) established in the previous sections.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{d+1}^{\mathfrak{s}}
denote the category whose objects are closed
d
-dimensional manifolds with a specified “structure” denoted
\mathfrak{s}
(e.g. topological, smooth, Riemannian, conformal, …), and for two such objects
\Sigma_{1}
,
\Sigma_{2}
a morphism from
\Sigma_{1}
to
\Sigma_{2}
given by an
oriented cobordism
from
\Sigma_{1}
to
\Sigma_{2}
(that is, a compact
(d+1)
-manifold
\Omega
with boundary
\partial\Omega=\Sigma_{1}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}
, with
\Sigma_{1}
labelled as “in”, and
\Sigma_{2}
“out”) also with the structure
\mathfrak{s}
. We shall write
\Omega:\Sigma_{1}\rightsquigarrow\Sigma_{2}
. The composition of morphisms is concatenation (or sewing/gluing) of cobordisms
25
25

25

If
\mathfrak{s}
is “topological”, then the identities are finite cylinders; however in other cases the identity might be something degenerate, e.g. an “empty” cobordism.
. We denote very loosely by
\mathsf{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
the category of (topological) complex vector spaces and linear maps (different subcategories would be considered for different
\mathfrak{s}
).

Definition 1.5.1

(Atiyah
[
Ati88
]
, Segal
[
KS21
,
Seg04
]
, Kontsevich
[
KS21
]
)
.

A
(d+1)
-dimensional QFT (with structure
\mathfrak{s}
) is a monoidal
26
26

26

Loosely speaking, a
monoidal category
is a category where tensor products of objects make sense. In
\mathcal{C}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{d+1}
the “tensor product” is given by disjoint union. Moreover, there is a “unit” with respect to the tensor product (
\varnothing
and
\mathbb{C}
respectively in
\mathcal{C}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{d+1}
and
\mathsf{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
). Therefore, rule (i) is saying simply that our functor respects the monoidal structure, i.e. a
monoidal functor
. See
[
Mac78
]
chapter XI.
functor
\mathcal{C}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{d+1}\longrightarrow\mathsf{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}
. This means effectively that it is a rule which associates

1.

to each closed
d
-dimensional manifold
\Sigma
a complex vector space
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
, the “space of states”;

2.

to each
\Omega:\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}\rightsquigarrow\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
a linear map
U_{\Omega}:\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}
, the “evolution”;

satisfying the following
axioms
:

1.

(finite) disjoint unions are taken to (finite) tensor products (e.g.
\Sigma_{1}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}
to
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma_{2}}
), and the empty
d
-manifold
\varnothing
is taken to
\mathcal{H}_{\varnothing}:=\mathbb{C}
;

2.

let
\Omega_{12}:\Sigma_{1}\rightsquigarrow\Sigma_{2}
and
\Omega_{23}:\Sigma_{2}\rightsquigarrow\Sigma_{3}
be two cobordisms, and consider
\Omega_{23}\circ\Omega_{12}:=\Omega_{12}\cup_{\Sigma_{2}}\Omega_{23}
the cobordism obtained by gluing
\Omega_{12}
and
\Omega_{23}
along
\Sigma_{2}
. Then we have

U_{\Omega_{23}\circ\Omega_{12}}=U_{\Omega_{23}}\circ U_{\Omega_{12}}:\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma_{1}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma_{3}}

(1.5.1)

as linear maps.

Figure 1.5
:
Segal Gluing

Unfortunately, much more needs to be said to make the above definition precise (and complete, in many situations). We list a few (far from exhaustive) of these technical points in remark
1.5.1
below, and refer to
[
KS21
]
section 3,
[
Mne19
]
section 1.2,
[
Seg04
]
and the introduction of
[
KMW21
]
for further discussions.

In chapter
2
we work with
a
2D QFT over the Riemannian category. For us objects of
\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{riem}}_{1+1}
are finite disjoint unions of Riemannian circles characterized by perimeters (moreover they need to be “enhanced” by two-sided collars), morphisms are Riemannian surfaces with geodesic boundaries,
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
are Hilbert spaces and
U_{\Omega}
are
Hilbert-Schmidt
operators. See section
2.6.1
for a precise re-statement.

Remark 1.5.1
.

1.

Regarding rule (ii), when one glues
\Omega_{12}
and
\Omega_{23}
one must at the same time glue the geometric structure
\mathfrak{s}
.
27
27

27

A succinct way to say this is that one takes the
fiber product

\Omega_{12}\times_{\Sigma_{2}}\Omega_{23}
in the corresponding category of manifolds with structure
\mathfrak{s}
(
[
Mne19
]
section 1.2.2).
Also, one should appropriately identify cobordisms
\Omega
,
\Omega^{\prime}:\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}\longrightarrow\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
which are “isomorphic” via a boundary-preserving
\mathfrak{s}
-isomorphism; equivalently, one requires a “naturality” condition in terms of a commutative diagram (
[
Mne19
]
section 1.2.2).

2.

As
d
-manifolds are not
a priori
embedded in
(d+1)
-manifolds, a careful definition of the cobordism requires the data of the two
\mathfrak{s}
-embeddings (a.k.a. parametrizations)
\xi_{\mathrm{in},\mathrm{out}}:\Sigma_{\mathrm{in,out}}\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow\partial\Omega
, and gluing cobordisms as in rule (ii) essentially glues the parametrizations of
\Sigma_{2}
. With this data, the “in” and “out” labels on the boundaries are identified by checking whether the induced orientations by the embeddings agree with the co-orientations coming from
\Omega
. The parametrizations naturally go into the definition of
U_{\Sigma}
as we need them to relate things “happening on
\Omega
” to the “model” vector spaces
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}
,
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}
. They are especially important in (2D) CFT regarding the next point below.

3.

The vector space
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
is usually
constructed out of

\Sigma
(see path-integral representation below) and hence carries a natural representation of
\mathrm{Diff}(\Sigma)
, assuming
\mathfrak{s}
at least smooth. In 2D CFT the operators
U_{\Omega}
interplay with the representations of
\mathrm{Diff}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
on
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}
,
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}
through the parametrizations of
\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
and
\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
by copies of
\mathbb{S}^{1}
.

4.

(
unitarity assumption
) For
unitary
QFTs we will make the following assumptions
in this thesis
: we let
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
be real Hilbert spaces;
28
28

28

Equivalently, complex Hilbert spaces equipped with an antilinear involution making it the complexification of an underlying real Hilbert space.

if
\Omega:\Sigma_{1}\rightsquigarrow\Sigma_{2}
is a cobordism, it is seen equally as a cobordism
\Omega^{*}:\Sigma_{2}^{*}\rightsquigarrow\Sigma_{1}^{*}
, and also
\Omega:\varnothing\rightsquigarrow\Sigma_{1}^{*}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}
(abusing notation), where
\Sigma_{j}^{*}
denotes
\Sigma_{j}
with an oppositely oriented parametrization. In this case, we require
