U_{\Omega^{*}}=U_{\Omega}^{\dagger}
, the
real adjoint
(or transpose) of
U_{\Omega}
. This is not yet entirely precise as
\Sigma
needs to be enhanced by symmetric 2-sided collars, see
[
KS21
]
section 3 under “unitarity”.

5.

A
closed

(d+1)
-manifold is considered as a cobordism
\varnothing\longrightarrow\varnothing
. By rule (i) we get in this case an operator
U_{M}:\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
identified with a single complex number. We denote this number otherwise by
\mathcal{Z}_{M}
, usually called the
partition function
. Similarly for a “1-sided” cobordism
\Omega
with
\partial\Omega=\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
we get a vector
U_{\Omega}
in
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}
(
\partial\Omega=\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
, a dual vector in
\mathcal{H}^{*}_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}
). Often a third axiom is mentioned, that if we glue a cobordism
\Omega
with itself by identifying
\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
with
\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
, getting the closed manifold
\check{\Omega}
, then
\ttr(U_{\Omega})=\mathcal{Z}_{\check{\Omega}}
. Technically, this could be implied by rule (ii) using the aforementioned interpretations.
29
29

29

If one glues two oppositely 1-sided cobordisms with boundaries of both identified with
\Sigma
(one labelled “in”, the other “out”), then one effectively pairs a vector in
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
with a dual vector in
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}^{*}
, and gets the number
\mathcal{Z}_{M}
,
M
being the resulting closed manifold. One could then recover the “trace axiom” by taking one of these cobordisms to be a very thin cylinder and taking the length of the cylinder to zero, through an appropriate limiting procedure. See “foreword and postscript” to section 4 in
[
Seg04
]
.

On the other hand, rule (ii) is also a special case of a “partial” version of this trace axiom, namely, composition of linear maps
U\longrightarrow V
,
V\longrightarrow W
of vector spaces corresponds to contracting (tracing)
V
with
V^{*}
in
W\otimes V^{*}\otimes V\otimes U^{*}
.

As mentioned by the authors in
[
KS21
]
section 3, “the guiding principle of this approach is to preserve as much as possible of the path-integral intuition”. Let us see now that the path-integral formulation introduced in subsection
1.2.5
applies without essential modification to this new setting.

A cobordism
\Omega:\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}\rightsquigarrow\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
is considered as a piece of space-time connecting the two “time slices”
\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
and
\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
. As explained in section
1.4.1
, each field theory comes with specified
field configuration spaces
over space and space-time, denoted
\conf(\Sigma)
and
\conf(\Omega)
. Typically
\conf(X)=\mathrm{Map}(X,\mathscr{V})
, the space of
maps
from
X
to a
spin value space
/
target space

\mathscr{V}
.
The path-integral formulation gives a recipe for
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
and
U_{\Omega}
as follows. We define

\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}L^{2}(\conf(\Sigma),\mathcal{L}),

(1.5.2)

where
\mathcal{L}
denotes the non-existent
Lebesgue measure
on the configuration space
\conf(\Sigma)
. Now suppose our field theory (over Euclidean signature, for definiteness) comes with the action
S_{\Omega}:\conf(\Omega)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
. Define

\left.\begin{array}[]{rcl}U_{\Omega}:\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}},\\
F&\longmapsto&(U_{\Omega}F)(\psi)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\displaystyle\int_{\conf(\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}})}\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}(\psi,\varphi)F(\varphi)\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{L}(\varphi),\end{array}\right.

(1.5.3)

with the integral kernel

\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}(\psi,\varphi)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\int_{\left\{\phi\in\conf(\Omega)\middle|\begin{subarray}{c}\phi|_{\mathrm{in}}=\varphi,\\
\phi|_{\mathrm{out}}=\psi\end{subarray}\right\}}\textrm{e}^{-S_{\Omega}(\phi)}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{L}(\phi),

(1.5.4)

where now we integrate against the still non-existent Lebesgue measure on
\conf(\Sigma)
(with the indicated boundary conditions). At this level, (
1.5.1
) reads

\mathcal{A}_{\Omega_{13}}(\psi,\varphi)=\int_{\conf(\Sigma_{2})}\mathcal{A}_{\Omega_{23}}(\psi,\eta)\mathcal{A}_{\Omega_{12}}(\eta,\varphi)\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{L}(\eta),

(1.5.5)

and corresponds to a “formal Fubini theorem”. It requires
S_{\Omega_{13}}(\phi)=S_{\Omega_{12}}(\phi|_{\Omega_{12}})+S_{\Omega_{23}}(\phi|_{\Omega_{23}})
, namely, the action is
local
.

Example 1.5.1

(connection with Feynman-Kac for cylinders)
.

Now we try to connect the Atiyah-Segal picture to the traditional Feynman-Kac-Nelson picture introduced previously. We consider the massive GFF in 2D discussed in subsection
1.4.3
. This is a unitary QFT so we work under (4) of remark
1.5.1
, and since the massive GFF depends on a metric we work in the Riemannian category. Here the configuration space for a circle
\mathbb{S}^{1}
is
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
and the formal Hilbert space (
1.5.2
) can be replaced by the rigorous probabilistic Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{S}^{1}),(\tau_{0})_{*}\mu)
obtained in subsection
1.4.3
(cf. (
1.4.35
)).
On a naive level, for a flat cylinder
\Omega=[0,t]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
, it is natural to impose
U_{\Omega}:=\textrm{e}^{-t\mathbf{H}_{0}}
with
\mathbf{H}_{0}
defined below (
1.4.37
). On this level (
1.5.1
) holds trivially between standard cylinders. Going one step further, the fact that
\textrm{e}^{-t\mathbf{H}_{0}}
is the “Feller semi-group” of the GFF means that it comes from the underlying path-integral representation for the GFF in the spirit of (
1.2.76
).
On this level (
1.5.1
), also for cylinders, is the Chapman-Kolmogorov lemma for GFF which is a Markov process in the sense of (
1.4.38
). However, more questions remain to be asked:

1.

How is this definition of
U_{\Omega}
for cylinders compatible with gluing other general curved surfaces?

2.

Postulating a definition for
U_{\Omega}
that satisfies (
1.5.1
) gives one
possibility
for an Atiyah-Segal model for the (massive) GFF. In general this does not imply
necessity
. How
necessary
is this definition of
U_{\Omega}
, even for cylinders?

As an immature attempt at answering these questions (especially (b)), we single out the following assumptions which would be made for the GFF. In this more general setting the data of the GFF includes a probability measure
\mu_{M}
defined on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
for every closed manifold
M
following (
1.3.8
), which has a Markov property in the sense of (
1.4.38
) due to the locality of its action. Moreover denote by
\mu
the measure for the infinite flat cylinder defined in (
1.4.30
). We postulate that

1.

for a 1-sided cobordism
\Omega:\varnothing\rightsquigarrow\Sigma
, the vector or state
U_{\Omega}
is represented by a nonnegative
L^{2}
function
30
30

30

This would mean that
U_{\Omega}
could be interpreted as a “perturbed ground state” in view of the Perron-Frobenius theorem (cf. proposition
2.7.1
and
[
GJ87
]
theorem 3.3.2).
, note that under our unitarity assumption (remark
1.5.1
(4)),
U_{\Omega}
is identified with
U_{\Omega^{*}}
, the dual vector representing
\Omega^{*}:\Sigma\rightsquigarrow\varnothing
;

2.

for 2 oppositely 1-sided cobordisms
\Omega_{1}:\varnothing\rightsquigarrow\Sigma
and
\Omega_{2}:\Sigma\rightsquigarrow\varnothing
such that
\Omega_{2}\circ\Omega_{1}=M
with
\Sigma\subset M
embedded as a hypersurface, then
U_{\Omega_{1}}U_{\Omega_{2}}\cdot(\tau_{0})_{*}\mu=(\tau_{\Sigma})_{*}\mu_{M}
. Here
U_{\Omega_{1}}U_{\Omega_{2}}
is the product of the 2 functions in
L^{2}
space, and
\tau_{\Sigma}:\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma)
denotes the restriction.

Figure 1.6
:
The Cylinder Case

From (i) and (ii) one can deduce that for an infinite half-cylinder
(-\infty,0]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
, the associated state
U_{(-\infty,0]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}}
is the function
1
, the “ground state”, somehow tautologically since we used
(\tau_{0})_{*}\mu
to define the Hilbert space. Accordingly the operator
U_{[0,t]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}}
associated to the finite cylinder
[0,t]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
is the one represented by the Radon-Nikodym density

U_{[0,t]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}}=\frac{\mathrm{d}(\tau_{0\sqcup t})_{*}\mu}{\mathrm{d}[(\tau_{0})_{*}\mu\otimes(\tau_{0})_{*}\mu]}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}},

(1.5.6)

where
\tau_{0\sqcup t}
is the 2-component restriction map
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1})\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\{0\}\times\mathbb{S}^{1})\times\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\{t\}\times\mathbb{S}^{1})
. By (
1.4.36
), we justify that
U_{[0,t]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}}
given by (
1.5.6
) indeed coincides with
\textrm{e}^{-t\mathbf{H}_{0}}
. Moreover, one can proceed to show that (
1.5.6
) is compatible with gluing other surfaces under assumption (ii), hence answering question (i). For example, let
\Omega_{1}:\varnothing\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{S}^{1}
and
\Omega_{2}:\mathbb{S}^{1}\rightsquigarrow\varnothing
again be two 1-sided cobordism. Put
\widetilde{M}:=\Omega_{2}\circ[0,t]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\circ\Omega_{1}
. Then, following essentially the arguments in section
2.6
using the Markov property, one may show that

U_{[0,t]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}}(U_{\Omega_{1}}\otimes U_{\Omega_{2}})\cdot(\tau_{0})_{*}\mu\otimes(\tau_{0})_{*}\mu=(\tau_{0\sqcup t})_{*}\mu_{\widetilde{M}},

(1.5.7)

where we abused the notation
\tau_{0\sqcup t}
now to denote the restriction map onto the two boundaries of
[0,t]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
seen as embedded in
\widetilde{M}
. Last but not least, we mention that postulate (ii) could be formulated in a “ground-state-free” manner using the infinite dimensional half-densities introduced by Pickrell
[
Pic08
]
.

To end this section, we mention that
[
KS21
]
has recently proposed a framework for doing Wick rotation that fits into the functorial picture, based on the idea of working with complex metrics on curved surfaces. A remarkable feature of this setting is that for any fixed surface with boundary there’s a space of admissible metrics where Lorentzian ones are on the boundary and Euclidean ones in the interior. Remembering from subsection
1.2.6
that Minkowski evolution operators were the boundary value of (complex) Euclidean ones, one plausibly imagines that Wick rotation may also be made “functorial”, accounting for the “boundary-bulk” correspondence both on the metric side and on the operator side. However, making this statement precise lies far beyond the scope of the present thesis. We also mention that complex metrics have also been considered in
[
Wro20
]
and
[
Gér21
]
. Lastly, see also
[
Wit22
]
.

Chapter 2
Segal Axioms and Periodic Covers

This Chapter is adapted from
[
Lin24
]
.

Introduction

In the classical approach to Quantum Field Theory (QFT),
a central role is played by the representations of the Poincaré group. For example, the Wightman axioms postulate such a representation on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
and assign operators on
\mathcal{H}
to test functions on Minkowski space-time respecting the representation (
[
RS75
]
X.7).
It was later realized by Feynman, Symanzik
[
Sym69
]
, Nelson
[
Nel73a
]
in the 60’s that one could also describe many models of QFT using the
functional integral
, and considering it in
imaginary time
leads to the
Euclidean approach
to QFT (EQFT). This approach allowed many interesting models to be constructed rigorously in Minkowski space-time, for which the Wightman axioms were explicitly checked. They constitute a significant part of the subject of Constructive Quantum Field Theory (CQFT). However, the traditional techniques relied heavily on the space-time being flat and the results were likewise limited.

In the 80’s, motivated by works of Atiyah and Witten on the relation of QFT with geometry and topology, there was an attempt to give an axiomatic definition of QFT that would incorporate arbitrary curved space-times, while still keeping the main properties of the
functional integral representation. Atiyah, Segal, Kontsevich
[
Seg04
,
KS21
]
then arrived at a
functorial framework
. Loosely speaking, this framework defines a QFT as a linear representation of a certain bordism category of “space-times”. At a preliminary level, a manifest difference between the traditional axiomatics and the functorial approach is the way they view time evolution. Traditionally time evolution is represented by a symmetry transformation of space-time (i.e. a Poincaré group element), whereas in the functorial picture it is represented by the piece of space-time cobordism connecting the initial and final “time-slices”.
One important postulate, for example, says that
composition
of time evolutions corresponds to
gluing
of space-time cobordisms.

This paper is an attempt to reconstruct in the new functorial framework a celebrated interacting QFT model (the
P(\phi)_{2}
-model) previously constructed using traditional CQFT techniques. We give the precise sense and a rigorous proof that the
P(\phi)_{2}
-model satisfies Segal’s axioms (Section
2.6
). Then we apply the functorial QFT framework to certain purely mathematical questions on surface geometry (Section
2.7
).

Example of 1D Spin Chain and its Transfer Operator

Our example illustrates the gluing properties by the transfer operator for discrete spin systems.
Consider
\mathbb{Z}_{N}:=\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}
as a
1
-dimensional chain of size
N
, consider the space
\mathbb{R}^{N}
now as the space of maps
\mathbb{Z}_{N}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
, namely a discrete
path space
. The lattice site is denoted by
i\in\{1,\dots,N\}
and call
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
a
spin configuration
, whose value at the site
i
reads
\sigma(i)\in\mathbb{R}
. We impose
periodic boundary condition
, which means
\sigma(N+1)\equiv\sigma(1)
. Thus the chain could be considered as circular.
We are given an action functional on the configuration space
\mathbb{R}^{N}
that reads

\displaystyle S_{N}(\sigma)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\sigma(i+1)-\sigma(i)|^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}P(\sigma(i))

(2.1.1)

where
P
is a polynomial bounded from below, the interaction is nearest neighbour.
Given a configuration
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
,
S_{N}(\sigma)
may be thought of as its “energy” and the statistical behaviour of the system is described by the probability measure called
Gibbs measure
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\mu_{P(\sigma)}(\sigma)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}(N)}\textrm{e}^{-S_{N}(\sigma)}\mathrm{d}^{N}\sigma,\quad\textrm{with}\quad\mathcal{Z}(N)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\textrm{e}^{-S_{N}(\sigma)}\mathrm{d}^{N}\sigma

(2.1.2)

called the
partition function
of the system.

Now we would like to express this partition function
\mathcal{Z}(N)
in terms of elementary building blocks.
The main idea is to slice the action functional as

\displaystyle S_{N}(\sigma)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Big[|\sigma(i+1)-\sigma(i)|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(P(\sigma(i+1))+P(\sigma(i)))\Big]

so exponentiating gives

\displaystyle\exp\left(-S_{N}(\sigma)\right)=\prod_{i=1}^{N}K(\sigma(i+1),\sigma(i))

(2.1.3)

where

K(x,y)=\textrm{e}^{-|x-y|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}(P(x)+P(y))}

(2.1.4)

which is the Schwartz kernel of an operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
that is smoothing.

Definition 2.1.1
.

Define the
transfer operator

T
to be exactly the operator with kernel
K(x,y)
, that is,

(TF)(x)=\int K(x,y)F(y)\mathrm{d}y,

(2.1.5)

for any function(al)
F\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

Then we see immediately from (
2.1.3
) that

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(N)=\int\prod_{i=1}^{N}\big[K(\sigma(i+1),\sigma(i))\mathrm{d}\sigma(i)\big]=\ttr_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}(T^{N}),

(2.1.6)

remembering that
\sigma(N+1)\equiv\sigma(1)
, since for a smoothing operator
A:L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
we have
\ttr_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}(A)=\int K_{A}(x,x)\mathrm{d}x
with
K_{A}
being the integral kernel.

More generally we would like to express the kernel of
T^{N}
in terms of
\exp(-S(\sigma))
using the relation (
2.1.3
). This means instead of letting the boundary condition be periodic we let
\sigma(1)=\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
,
\sigma(N+1)=\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
given two boundary conditions
(\sigma_{\mathrm{in}},\sigma_{\mathrm{out}})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Then the kernel
K_{N}
of
T^{N}
is
1
1

1

Formula (
2.1.7
) below is an analogue of what is called in the quantum information context the “
replica trick
”.

\displaystyle K_{N}(\sigma_{\mathrm{out}},\sigma_{\mathrm{in}})

\displaystyle=\int K(\sigma_{\mathrm{out}},\sigma(N))\cdots K(\sigma(2),\sigma_{\mathrm{in}})\prod_{i=2}^{N}\mathrm{d}\sigma(i)

(2.1.7)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N-1}}\textrm{e}^{-S_{N}(\sigma|\sigma_{\mathrm{in}},\sigma_{\mathrm{out}})}\prod_{i=2}^{N}\mathrm{d}\sigma(i),

(2.1.8)

with the
conditioned
interaction
S_{N}(\sigma|\sigma_{\mathrm{in}},\sigma_{\mathrm{out}})
defined as

S_{N}(\sigma|\sigma_{\mathrm{in}},\sigma_{\mathrm{out}})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\sigma(i+1)-\sigma(i)|^{2}+\sum_{i=2}^{N}P(\sigma(i))+\frac{1}{2}(P(\sigma_{\mathrm{in}})+P(\sigma_{\mathrm{out}})),

(2.1.9)

with
\sigma(1)\equiv\sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
and
\sigma(N+1)\equiv\sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
. Now if
N_{1}
,
N_{2}
are two integers and we define the kernels
K_{N_{1}}
,
K_{N_{2}}
using (
2.1.8
) and (
2.1.9
) with
N
replaced respectively by
N_{1}
,
N_{2}
, then it follows “trivially” from the composition property
T^{N_{2}}\circ T^{N_{1}}=T^{N_{2}+N_{1}}
that

K_{N_{2}+N_{1}}(\sigma_{\mathrm{out}},\sigma_{\mathrm{in}})=\int K_{N_{2}}(\sigma_{\mathrm{out}},\sigma)K_{N_{1}}(\sigma,\sigma_{\mathrm{in}})\mathrm{d}\sigma.

(2.1.10)

However, such a relation becomes remarkable (rather than trivial) if we do not have the “unit” transfer operator
T
to start with; that is, if we do not have (
2.1.7
) but define
K_{N_{1}}
and
K_{N_{2}}

directly
with an expression of the form (
2.1.8
) and (
2.1.9
).
This corresponds to the idea of a
path integral
in quantum mechanics and quantum field theory. Alternatively one could consider the Gibbs measure (
2.1.2
) and take
K_{N}
as the
transition probability
of a certain stochastic process. Then (
2.1.10
) is the
Chapman-Kolmogorov
equation which relies heavily on the fact that the underlying process is
Markovian
. In a sense, for both interpretations a crucial condition is that the interaction
S(\sigma)
be
local
; that is, very roughly speaking, if one chops the sites
[1,N_{1}+N_{2}]:=\{1,2,\dots,N_{1}+N_{2}\}
into
[1,N_{1}]\sqcup[N_{1}+1,N_{1}+N_{2}]
then
S_{[1,N_{1}+N_{2}]}(\sigma)\approx S_{[1,N_{1}]}(\sigma|_{[1,N_{1}]})+S_{[N_{1}+1,N_{1}+N_{2}]}(\sigma|_{[N_{1}+1,N_{1}+N_{2}]})
.

The main result of this article concerns a 2-dimensional “continuum” version of this story where lattice sites are replaced by the continuum of points on a 2D surface (considered as space-time) and a configuration is replaced by a distribution. See the section below for a more precise description. In the final section, we also show that when the space-time admits a periodic translation symmetry then a more precise analogy with the spin chain described above can be restored, in particular, there exists a
Gibbs state
in the
thermodynamic limit
. Further discussion of the above example continues in section
2.7.2
.

Main Results

This article will prove three main results whose preliminary versions are stated as theorems
2.1
,
2.2
, and
2.3
below, among which the first is the main theorem and the second and third are two main consequences.

Theorem 2.1

(Segal Axioms for
P(\phi)_{2}
, partial statement)
.

For each finite disjoint union
\Sigma
of Riemannian circles (each component circle characterised by its radius) there exists a finite measure
\mu_{\Sigma}
on the space
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma)
of real distributions
2
2

2

By a
real distribution

\phi
we mean that if we pair
\phi
with a potentially complex test function
f
, then
\overline{\phi(f)}=\phi(\overline{f})
,
the bar denoting complex conjugation.
over
\Sigma
, giving the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}:=L^{2}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma),\mu_{\Sigma})
, such that the following holds:
if
\Omega
is a Riemannian surface whose boundary has two components
\partial\Omega=\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}\sqcup\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
, denote by
\mu_{\mathrm{in}}
,
\mu_{\mathrm{out}}
respectively the measures on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}})
,
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}})
and put
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{in}}:=L^{2}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}),\mu_{\mathrm{in}})
,
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{out}}:=L^{2}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}),\mu_{\mathrm{out}})
, then there exists an operator
U_{\Omega}:\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{in}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{out}}
given by

(U_{\Omega}F)(\varphi_{\mathrm{out}})=\int\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}(\varphi_{\mathrm{in}},\varphi_{\mathrm{out}})F(\varphi_{\mathrm{in}})\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathrm{in}}(\varphi_{\mathrm{in}}),\quad F\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{in}},

(2.1.11)

where
\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}(\varphi_{\mathrm{in}},\varphi_{\mathrm{out}})
is given by the Radon-Nikodym density between two mutually absolutely continuous finite measures on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}})\times\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}})
defined rigorously in Eqn. (
2.6.11
), Subsection
2.6.3
.

Moreover, the correspondence
\Omega\longmapsto U_{\Omega}
between surfaces and operators satisfy the following
composition/functorial property
. Let
\Omega_{1}
,
\Omega_{2}
be two Riemannian surfaces of the kind as above where the “out” boundary of
\Omega_{1}
is isometric to the “in” boundary of
\Omega_{2}
via an isometry
\rho
, we glue them along
\rho
and obtain the surface
\Omega_{2}\cup_{\rho}\Omega_{1}
. Then

U_{\Omega_{2}\cup_{\rho}\Omega_{1}}=U_{\Omega_{2}}\circ U_{\Omega_{1}},

(2.1.12)

for the operators
U_{\Omega_{2}\cup_{\rho}\Omega_{1}}
,
U_{\Omega_{2}}
and
U_{\Omega_{1}}
defined as above.

See Subsection
2.6.1
, Theorem
1
′
for a full statement.

The first application of Theorem
2.1
generalizes to the interacting cases, certain results of Naud
[
Nau23
]
on the asymptotics of the free energy of free bosons on certain large degree covers.

Theorem 2.2

(asymptotics of the partition function on large degree cyclic covers)
.

Let
(M,g)
denotes a compact Riemannian surface,
M_{\infty}\longrightarrow M
a Riemannian
\mathbb{Z}
-cover of
M
and
\gamma
the generator of the deck group.
We can think of
M_{\infty}
as the infinite composition of some given cobordism
\Omega
, which serves as the “fundamental domain” of the deck transformations on
M_{\infty}
.
For every
N\in\mathbb{N}
, denote by
M_{N}:=M_{\infty}/\gamma^{N}
the cyclic cover of degree
N
of
M
and
\Delta_{N}
is the Laplace-Beltrami operator on
M_{N}
.
Let
Z_{N}
be the partition function of the
P(\phi)_{2}
theory on
M_{N}
obtained in Theorem
2.4
.

Then the renormalized sequence of free energies

\frac{1}{N}\log\left(Z_{N}\right)
has a limit
\lambda_{0}
when
N\rightarrow+\infty
, moreover this limit
\lambda_{0}
can be interpreted as the leading eigenvalue of some transfer operator
U_{\Omega}
which is the quantization of the cobordism
\Omega
mentioned in Theorem
2.1
.

In subsection
2.7.3
, we use the above
P(\phi)_{2}
measures defined on the towers of cyclic covers
M_{N}
to produce a
P(\phi)_{2}
-Gibbs state on the periodic surface
M_{\infty}
of infinite volume.

Theorem 2.3

(Mass gap for
P(\phi)_{2}
on
M_{\infty}
)
.

In the notations from Theorem
2.2
,
for compactly supported bounded observables
F
on
M_{\infty}
, one can define expectations with respect to the
P(\phi)_{2}
Gibbs state as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[F\right]\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{Z_{N}\cdot{\textstyle\det_{\zeta}}(\Delta_{N}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}}\int_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M_{N})}F(\phi)\textrm{e}^{-\int_{M_{N}}{:}P(\phi(x)){:}\mathrm{d}V(x)}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M_{N}}(\phi),

where the construction of
Z_{N}
and the sense of the latter integral is made in Theorem
2.4
. The generator of the deck group
\gamma
acts on
M_{\infty}
by diffeomorphism which induces a shift map
\tau
on observables which moves the support by
\gamma
.
The Gibbs state defined above is exponentially mixing under the shift map, for compactly supported
L^{2}
observables :

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\tau^{k}FG]=\mathbb{E}[F]\mathbb{E}[G]+\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{k})

for some
\alpha<1
(see corollary
2.7.3
).

Main Novelties, Organization

We would like to point out
four main novelties
of this article, regarding the earlier work
[
Pic08
]
on the subject as well as the related work
[
GKRV21
]
. See the published version of
[
Lin24
]
for a more detailed comparison with literature.

Segal Gluing Gives a Gapped Theory.

Firstly, the relationship between theorems
2.2
,
2.3
and
2.1
above offers a new perspective that certain asymptotic results in pure geometry can be viewed as a consequence of an underlying Segal Quantum Field Theory whose “transfer operators”
U_{\Omega}
satisfy certain nice properties. Besides, of course, theorem
2.2
itself is new in that the numbers
Z_{N}
are now partition functions of
interacting
field theories instead of regularized determinants of geometric operators, which were studied previously and in our setting corresponds to free field theories. Moreover, in the process of proving theorem
2.2
one also obtains an infinite-volume
P(\phi)_{2}
-theory (in one direction) over a curved periodic surface which has never been considered before in the literature. Moreover, the Segal view point gives a simple conceptual proof of the mass gap for the
P(\phi)_{2}
state for any polynomial
P
of degree
\geqslant 4
bounded from below on a space of infinite volume, infinite genus. Theorem
2.3
seems to be one of the first mass gap results for interacting QFT on curved spaces.

A Conceptual Proof for Gluing GFFs.

We also offer a novel treatment on Segal gluing of the GFF (
P=0
), which is natural and based on first principles, but nevertheless has not been followed by existing literature. We describe it briefly via a finite dimensional analogy: suppose two real random variables
X
and
Y
have joint probability law
\mathbb{P}_{(X,Y)}
; then we have two equal expressions for their joint density
p(x,y)=(\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{(X,Y)}/\mathrm{d}\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}}^{\otimes 2})(x,y)
, namely

p(x,y)=\frac{\mathrm{d}(\pi^{x}_{*}\mathbb{P}_{(X,Y)})}{\mathrm{d}\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}}}(x)\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{Y|X=x}}{\mathrm{d}\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}}}(y)=\frac{\mathrm{d}(\pi^{y}_{*}\mathbb{P}_{(X,Y)})}{\mathrm{d}\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}}}(y)\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{X|Y=y}}{\mathrm{d}\mathcal{L}_{\mathbb{R}}}(x),

(2.1.13)

where
\pi^{x}
,
\pi^{y}
are respectively the projections onto the
x
- and
y
-axes,
\mathbb{P}_{Y|X=x}
denotes the conditional law of
Y
knowing “
X=x
”, and vice versa for
\mathbb{P}_{X|Y=y}
. In other words, one could evaluate
p(x,y)
by
conditioning
in two alternative ways: on
X
or on
Y
.
In Proposition
2.5.12
we prove a version of (
2.1.13
) for the GFF measure on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
for
M
a closed Riemannian surface, where the role of
\pi^{x,y}
are played by trace maps (restrictions) onto embedded Riemannian circles.
Since the Segal amplitudes (Eqn. (
2.6.11
)) are related in a simple way to the trace-image measures on distributions over the circles (corresponding to
p(x,y)
above), we may prove Segal’s gluing for the GFF essentially from just the definitions, the Markov property, and symmetry considerations (Proposition
2.6.10
). Then by the
locality
of the
P(\phi)_{2}
-interaction, the result extends to the interacting case.

A Transparent Treatment of Locality.

To go from
P=0
to
P\neq 0
, a key ingredient is to show that the
P(\phi)
-interaction functional of the field —
\int_{M}P(\phi(x))\,\mathrm{d}x
heuristically — is
local
, which in fact enables the amplitude (cf. Eqn. (
2.6.11
)) to be defined rigorously in the first place. No precise description of this locality existed in the literature and no explicit construction was given to prove it except for some remarks in
[
Abd18
,
DDJ23
]
. The present article fills this gap in Subsection
2.5.4
, based on a strengthening of Nelson’s argument presented in Subsection
2.3.2
that allows a freedom in choosing the regulators in the renormalization process, producing the same measure in the limit (see also the introduction of Section
2.3
). This latter result is also new by itself.

Dirichlet-to-Neumann Operator and Reflection Positivity.

As a tool in our treatment of the law of the restriction of GFF on circles, we exhibit an elementary relation between the operators
\tau_{\Sigma}
,
\Delta_{M}+m^{2}
,
\PI_{M}^{\Sigma}
and
\DN_{M}^{\Sigma}
(see “notations” below):

j_{\Sigma}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\tau_{\Sigma}^{*}=(\Delta_{M}+m^{2})\PI_{M}^{\Sigma}(\DN_{M}^{\Sigma})^{-1},

(2.1.14)

where “
*
” denotes the distributional adjoint. In Lemma
2.4.4
we derive this from integration by parts (Green-Stokes theorem). Though elementary, we do not find this formula elsewhere. Via similar considerations, one also has (see Appendix
2.4.4
)

\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega}(\DN_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega})^{-1}(\PI_{\Omega}^{\partial\Omega})^{*}=C_{N}-C_{D},

(2.1.15)

where
C_{D}
,
C_{N}
respectively are the Green operators (of the massive Laplacian) with Dirichlet and Neumann conditions. This gives a
direct relation
between the positivity of the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map and the reflection positivity (RP) of the GFF on closed Riemannian manifolds with reflection symmetry. It has never been made explicit previously.

Organisation.

In Section
2.2
we recall the preliminaries on which our construction will be based; these include the definition and properties of the (massive) Gaussian Free Field (GFF) measure on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
(Subsection
2.2.1
), the functional determinants (Subsection
2.2.2
) and a formula computing the Radon-Nikodym densities between mutually absolutely continuous Gaussian measures on spaces of distributions (Subsection
2.2.3
), of which we include a full proof based on the method of
[
GJ87
]
section 9.3.

In Section
2.3
we retrace the classical argument of Nelson
[
Nel66
]
and construct the interacting
P(\phi)
functional measure on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
for
M
a closed Riemannian surface, giving the partition function
Z_{M}
; the new result which says the interaction functional (and hence the limiting measure) is independent of the regulator for the renormalisation process, when the regulator is picked from a specific class, is Proposition
2.3.5
.

Section
2.4
aims to derive the behavior of a Gaussian field under the trace map (restriction to hypersurface); our method is based on elementary relations between several geometric operators as mentioned above (Lemma
2.4.4
), and as above it leads to the somewhat new perspective on reflection positivity (Subsection
2.4.4
). Section
2.5
discusses the Markov decomposition of the GFF (Subsection
2.5
) that culminates in a “Bayes formula” (Proposition
2.5.12
) for the GFF and proving locality of the
P(\phi)
interaction (Subsection
2.5.4
).

Section
2.6
formulates precisely and proves theorem
2.1
while Section
2.7
does it for theorem
2.2
and
2.3
.

Notations

\overset{\mathrm{heu}}{=}
” means “heuristically equal to”;

(M,g)=
closed Riemannian surface with metric
g
;

, boundary
\partial\Omega

;

Laplacian on
(M,g)
,

, therefore it is defined to be

, the same applies below;

;

(Q,\mathcal{O})=

\mu_{C}^{\mathrm{cond}}=\mu_{C^{-1}}^{\mathrm{cond}}=

,

, or

, equipped with their Fréchet Borel

and

, or between

and

\phi(f)=

,

or

,

,

;

;

,

with covariance

;

\mu_{2\DN}^{\partial\Omega,\Omega}=

;

\mathcal{M}_{M,2}^{1}=\tau_{\Sigma_{2}}\PI_{M}^{\Sigma_{1}}
is the

defined in section

.

Gaussian Fields on a Riemannian Manifold

In this and the next section
we define rigorously a probability measure on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
, as well as certain variants on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega^{\circ})
, which heuristically bears the form

\mathrm{d}\mu_{P}(\phi)\overset{\mathrm{heu}}{=}\frac{1}{Z}\overbrace{\textrm{e}^{-\int_{M}P(\phi(x))\mathrm{d}V_{M}(x)}}^{A}\underbrace{\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}(|\nabla\phi|_{g}^{2}+m^{2}\phi^{2})\mathrm{d}V_{M}}[\mathcal{L}\phi]}_{B},

(2.2.1)

where
P
is a polynomial bounded from below, such as
P(\phi)=\phi^{4}-\phi^{2}
,
[\mathcal{L}\phi]
denotes the nonexistent Lebesgue measure on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
and

Z_{M}\overset{\mathrm{heu}}{=}\int_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)}\textrm{e}^{-\int_{M}P(\phi(x))\mathrm{d}V_{M}(x)}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}(|\nabla\phi|_{g}^{2}+m^{2}\phi^{2})\mathrm{d}V_{M}}[\mathcal{L}\phi]

(2.2.2)

is the normalization factor, also called the
partition function
.

The idea is that while
[\mathcal{L}\phi]
is nonexistent, together with the factor
\exp(-\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}(|\nabla\phi|_{g}^{2}+m^{2}\phi^{2})\mathrm{d}V_{M})
the expression
B
can be given a rigorous meaning as a Gaussian probability measure scaled by a (finite) volume constant, and the measure (
2.2.1
) can be constructed if, after defining part
A
rigorously, one proves that it is
L^{1}
with respect to the measure
B
.

In this section
we construct the measure
B
. In analogy with the expression of Gaussian measures on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, one should define heuristically

\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}(|\nabla\phi|_{g}^{2}+m^{2}\phi^{2})\mathrm{d}V_{M}}[\mathcal{L}\phi]\overset{\mathrm{heu}}{=}\textrm{``}\det\textrm{''}(\Delta+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}(\phi)

(2.2.3)

where
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}
is a Gaussian measure on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
with
covariance operator

(\Delta+m^{2})^{-1}
, and “
\det
” is an infinite dimensional generalization of the determinant of a matrix. The Gaussian measure and the determinant are two issues to be treated separately (in sections
2.2.1
and
2.2.2
), both being rather classical, the former called
Gaussian Free Field (measure)
and the latter called the
\zeta
-regularized determinant
.

The Massive Gaussian Free Field

Definition and Representations

In this subsection we explain the point of view adopted in this article of the massive Gaussian Free Field (GFF), and refer to Sheffield
[
She07
]
and Powell and Werner
[
PW21
]
for more information.

Let
(M,g)
be a closed Riemannian manifold
of dimension
d
with metric
g
, and
\Omega\subset M
an open domain with smooth boundary
\partial\Omega
, both equipped with the metric induced from
g
(same notation). Fix
m>0
as the mass parameter. We say that
the
massive Gaussian Free Field (GFF)
with mass
m
on
M
is the
Gaussian random process indexed by

C^{\infty}(M)
, consisting of random variables
\{\phi(f)\penalty\ |\penalty\ f\in C^{\infty}(M)\}
such that

\mathbb{E}[\phi(f)\phi(h)]=\left\langle f,(\Delta+m^{2})^{-1}h\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M)},\quad\mathbb{E}[\phi(f)]\equiv 0,

(2.2.4)

for any
f
,
h\in C^{\infty}(M)
. Similarly, the
Dirichlet massive Gaussian Free Field
with mass
m
on
\Omega
is the
Gaussian random process indexed by

C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega^{\circ})
, consisting of random variables
\{\phi(f)\penalty\ |\penalty\ f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega^{\circ})\}
such that

\mathbb{E}[\phi(f)\phi(h)]=\big\langle f,(\Delta_{\Omega,D}+m^{2})^{-1}h\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=\big\langle P_{M\setminus\Omega^{\circ}}^{\perp}f,P_{M\setminus\Omega^{\circ}}^{\perp}h\big\rangle_{W^{-1}},\quad\mathbb{E}[\phi(f)]\equiv 0,

(2.2.5)

for any
f
,
h\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega^{\circ})
. See appendix
C.1
and in particular lemma
C.1.4
.

There exist many choices of sample spaces (“
Q
-spaces” in the terminology of
[
Sim74
]
) on which to realize those Gaussian processes. These realizations are all equivalent in the sense of
isomorphism of measure algebras
(see
[
Sim74
]
section I.2). For the sake of concreteness, we point out that one choice for the sample space is
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
(or
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega^{\circ})
), as formulated in the following proposition, whose proof parallels the case on Euclidean space with minor modification.

Proposition 2.2.1

(Bochner-Minlos,
[
BHL11
]
theorem 5.11, page 266)
.

There exists a Borel probability measure
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}
on the Fréchet space
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
such that
\phi\mapsto\left\langle\phi,f\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M)}=:\phi(f)
,
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
, realizes the random variable
\phi(f)
of the massive GFF on
M
.

Similarly, there exists a Borel probability measure
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}
on the Fréchet space
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega^{\circ})
such that
\phi\mapsto\left\langle\phi,f\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)}
,
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega^{\circ})
, realizes the random variable
\phi(f)
of the Dirichlet massive GFF on
\Omega
.
\Box

Notation
.

Denote by
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}
and
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}
the expectations under
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}
and
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}
respectively.

Remark 2.2.1
.

Alternatively, based on the spectral theory of
\Delta
, let
\left\{\varphi_{j}\right\}_{j=0}^{\infty}
be its complete orthonormal eigenfunctions with (real nonnegative) eigenvalues
\left\{\lambda_{j}\right\}_{j=0}^{\infty}
,
0=\lambda_{0}<\lambda_{1}\leqslant\lambda_{2}\leqslant\dots\leqslant\lambda_{j}\leqslant\cdots
, counted with multiplicity. Then the
GFF with mass
m
on
M
could also be represented as the random formal series

\phi=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\xi_{j}\varphi_{j}

(2.2.6)

where the sequence
(\xi_{j})_{j}
consists of i.i.d. real-valued random variables with
\xi_{j}
being the standard centered Gaussian on
\mathbb{R}
with variance
(\lambda_{j}+m^{2})^{-1}
. Similarly, the Dirichlet GFF on
\Omega
could also be so represented using eigenfunctions of
\Delta_{\Omega,D}
, which are complete for
L^{2}(\Omega)
. One way to see that this construction is equivalent to the previous one is by appealing to
[
Shu01
]
page 92 proposition 10.2.

We also consider a slightly more general situation where
(\Delta+m^{2})^{-1}
is replaced by an operator
C
.

Definition 2.2.1
.

We say that a bounded self-adjoint positive elliptic pseudodifferential operator
C
of order
-s
on
M
(
s>0
) is a
Gaussian covariance operator
of order
-s
.

Following similar reasoning as proposition
2.2.1
or remark
2.2.1
, one obtains a measure
\mu_{C}
on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
.

Definition 2.2.2
.

The
Gaussian Field
on
M
with
covariance operator

C
is the
Gaussian random process indexed by

C^{\infty}(M)
, consisting of random variables
\{\phi(f)\penalty\ |\penalty\ f\in C^{\infty}(M)\}
such that

\mathbb{E}[\phi(f)\phi(h)]=\left\langle f,Ch\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M)},\quad\mathbb{E}[\phi(f)]\equiv 0,

(2.2.7)

for any
f
,
h\in C^{\infty}(M)
. We denote the corresponding measure on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
by
\mu_{C}
and the expectation with respect to this measure by
\mathbb{E}_{C}
.

Remark 2.2.2
.

For
C
satisfying the assumptions, the inner product
(f,h)\mapsto\left\langle f,Ch\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M)}
defines an equivalent norm for
W^{-s}(M)
.

Essential Properties

Now we collect some properties of the Gaussian random fields constructed above. We start with the following classical fact (the proofs are in parallel with the Euclidean case for which one could refer to
[
She07
]
).

Lemma 2.2.2
.

The
Gaussian Hilbert space
of
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}
is
W^{-1}(M)
, and that of
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}
is
W^{-1}(\Omega)
. The
Cameron-Martin space
of
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}
is
W^{1}(M)
, and that of
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}
is
W^{1}_{\Omega}(M)
.
\Box

Remark 2.2.3
.

There is a tacit assumption in the way we defined our fields: we expected the random variable
\phi(f)
to come from the distributional pairing (
L^{2}
-pairing) between
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
and
C^{\infty}(M)
(respectively,
\Omega
). Other pairings may also be used. For example, let
\phi(f)
be
\left\langle-,f\right\rangle_{W^{1}(M)}
instead of
\left\langle-,f\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M)}
. One then needs to alter the covariances accordingly. Indeed, they are related by

\mathbb{E}\big[\big\langle\phi,f\big\rangle_{W^{1}}\big\langle\phi,h\big\rangle_{W^{1}}\big]=\mathbb{E}\big[\big\langle\phi,(\Delta+m^{2})f\big\rangle_{L^{2}}\big\langle\phi,(\Delta+m^{2})h\big\rangle_{L^{2}}\big]=\big\langle f,h\big\rangle_{W^{1}(M)}.

(2.2.8)

This way of definition is noticeably used by Sheffield
[
She07
]
. The Gaussian Hilbert space in this case is
W^{1}(M)
(respectively,
W^{1}_{\Omega}(M)
), and the Cameron-Martin spaces are the same. See also remark
C.1.4
.

Similarly,

Lemma 2.2.3
.

The Gaussian Hilbert space of
\mu_{C}
is
W^{-s}(M)
, equipped with
\left\langle-,C-\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
, and the Cameron-Martin space is
W^{s}(M)
, equipped with
\langle-,C^{-1}-\rangle_{L^{2}}
.
\Box

Finally we say a word about the supports of the measures in the closed manifold case. This can be proved either using lemma
2.2.14
and evaluating the expectation of
\lVert\phi\rVert_{W^{s}(M)}^{2}
or a spectral representation like in remark
2.2.1
.

Lemma 2.2.4
.

We have
\mu_{C}(W^{-\delta}(M))=1
for any
\delta>\frac{1}{2}(d-s)
.
\Box

Remark 2.2.4
.

We point out that
\mu_{C}(W^{-\delta}(M))=1
for any
\delta>0
in the following two cases:

1.

\dim M=2
and
\mu_{C}=\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}
;

2.

\dim M=1
and
C
has order
-1
.

Last but not least, we make the following innocent but useful observation.

Lemma 2.2.5
.

If
\Omega=\Omega_{1}\sqcup\Omega_{2}
(possibility of non-empty boundary in either or both components), then GFFs (indeed, Gaussian fields) over
\Omega_{1}
and
\Omega_{2}
are independent and
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,B}=\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega_{1},B}\otimes\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega_{2},B}
where
B=D
when the corresponding
\Omega
,
\Omega_{1}
or
\Omega_{2}
has boundary and
B=\varnothing
when either is closed.
\Box

Determinants

In this section we discuss (two) generalizations of the notion of the determinant (of a matrix) to infinite dimensional operators. General references include Kontsevich and Vishik
[
KV94
]
, Shubin
[
Shu01
]
sections 9-13, Simon
[
Sim05
]
chapter 3 and finally Gohberg, Goldberg and Krupnik
[
GGK00
]
. See also Dang
[
Dan22
]
for a quick acquaintance of the physical-geometric context and Quine, Heydari and Song
[
QHS93
]
for an interesting discussion of zeta-regularization of infinite products.

Zeta-regularized and Fredholm Determinants

The zeta-regularized determinant was first introduced by Ray and Singer
[
RS71
]
. The first step is to define the
zeta function
of a (rather special) pseudodifferential operator
A
over a manifold
M
with or without boundary,

\zeta_{A}(z)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\ttr_{L^{2}}(A^{-z})

(2.2.9)

as a function of the complex variable
z
and study its meromorphic extension over a region that includes
z=0
. For our purposes
A
is
\Delta+m^{2}
,
(\Delta+m^{2})^{1/2}
or a Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator. These are positive elliptic
\Psi
DOs of positive order such that the principal symbol
\sigma_{A}(x,\xi)
is
strictly positive
whenever
\xi\neq 0
. In particular their spectra are in
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and does not intersect
\mathbb{B}_{\rho}(0)\subset\mathbb{C}
for some
\rho>0
. This enables one to define the complex power
A^{-z}
using the Cauchy integral representation

A^{-z}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2\pi}\int_{\gamma}\textrm{e}^{-z\log\lambda}(A-\lambda)^{-1}\mathrm{d}\lambda,

(2.2.10)

where
\gamma
is the contour (with parametrization traversing in order)

\gamma=\{r\textrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\pi}\penalty\ |\penalty\ r>\rho\}\cup\{\rho\textrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta}\penalty\ |\penalty\ -\pi<\theta<\pi\}\cup\{r\textrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\pi}\penalty\ |\penalty\ r>\rho\},

(2.2.11)

and
\log\lambda
taken to be the principal branch defined on
\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,0]
with
\log 1=0
.

Proposition 2.2.6

(
[
Shu01
]
proposition 10.1, theorems 10.1, 13.1, 13.2, also
[
See67
]
)
.

We have

1.

For the operators
A
under consideration, we have the bound

\left\lVert(A-\lambda)^{-1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\leqslant c|\lambda|^{-1}

(2.2.12)

for
\lambda\in\gamma
, and the integral (
2.2.10
) defines
A^{-z}
as a holomorphic function valued in bounded
L^{2}
operators, for
\mathfrak{Re}(z)>0
. It continues as such a holomorphic operator function to all
z\in\mathbb{C}
via

A^{-z}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}A^{k}A^{-z-k},

(2.2.13)

where
k
is any integer with
\mathfrak{Re}(z)>-k
so that
A^{-z-k}
is defined by (
2.2.10
), and the definition (
2.2.13
) does not depend on
k
.

2.

If
A
has order
s
then
A^{-z}
defined as above is a classical
\Psi
DO of order
-zs
. In particular it is trace class on
L^{2}(M)
when
\mathfrak{Re}(z)>d/s
,
d=\dim M
, for which
\zeta_{A}(z)
is well-defined by (
2.2.9
). Moreover, for these
z
,

\zeta_{A}(z)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\lambda_{j}^{-z},

(2.2.14)

where
\{\lambda_{j}\}_{j=0}^{\infty}
are the eigenvalues of
A
, and the sum converges absolutely, and uniformly in
z
over
\{\mathfrak{Re}(z)>d/s+\varepsilon\}
for any
\varepsilon>0
.

3.

Finally,
\zeta_{A}(z)
can be meromorphically continued over
\mathbb{C}
with simple poles possible at
\{\frac{d}{s},\frac{d-1}{s},\frac{d-2}{s},\cdots\}\setminus\mathbb{Z}_{\leqslant 0}
, and holomorphic elsewhere. In particular, it is holomorphic at
z=0
.
\Box

Definition 2.2.3
.

For an operator
A
under consideration, we define its
zeta-regularized determinant
as

\detz A\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\exp\left(-\partial_{z}\zeta_{A}(0)\right),

(2.2.15)

where
\zeta_{A}(0)
is the
zeta function
of
A
given by (
2.2.9
) and (
2.2.10
).

Remark 2.2.5
.

An alternative way of defining the zeta function and its meromorphic continuation is to use the heat kernel and the Mellin transform. See Gilkey
[
Gil95
]
section 1.12. This way of definition also gives (
2.2.14
) over the same region, defining therefore the same function as ours.

Remark 2.2.6
.

From (ii) of proposition
2.2.6
we see that if
A
is self-adjoint and strictly positive, then
\zeta_{A}(z)
is real-valued for
z\in(d/s,+\infty)
. But
\mathfrak{Im}(\zeta_{A})
is real analytic and hence
\zeta_{A}
remains real-valued on
\mathbb{R}
before crossing a pole, and by (iii) it is in particular real-valued on an interval around
0
. Thus
\partial_{z}\zeta_{A}(0)
is real and
\detz A
is positive.

Now we move on to the second notion of determinant. Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space and
A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
. Denote by
\Lambda^{k}\mathcal{H}
and
\Lambda^{k}A
, respectively, the
k
-th exterior product of
\mathcal{H}
and
A
(see Simon
[
Sim05
]
section 1.5).

Proposition 2.2.7

(
[
Sim05
]
lemma 3.3)
.

If
A
is trace class on
\mathcal{H}
, then
\Lambda^{k}A
is also trace class on
\Lambda^{k}\mathcal{H}
with bound on trace norm

\big\lVert\Lambda^{k}A\big\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}\leqslant\frac{1}{k!}\big\lVert A\big\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}^{k}.

(2.2.16)

In particular, putting

\detf(\mathbbm{1}+zA)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}z^{k}\ttr_{\Lambda^{k}\mathcal{H}}(\Lambda^{k}A)

(2.2.17)

for
z\in\mathbb{C}
defines an entire function, and

|\detf(\mathbbm{1}+zA)|\leqslant\exp(|z|\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}).\quad\Box

(2.2.18)

Definition 2.2.4
.

Let
A
be a trace class operator on the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. Then the determinant
\detf(\mathbbm{1}+A)
given by (
2.2.17
) for
z=1
is called the
Fredholm determinant
of
\mathbbm{1}+A
.

Lemma 2.2.8

(
[
Sim05
]
theorems 3.4, 3.7, 3.8)
.

1.

The map
A\longmapsto\detf(\mathbbm{1}+A)
defines a continuous function on the trace ideal
\mathcal{J}_{1}
with
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}
. More precisely,

|\detf(\mathbbm{1}+A)-\detf(\mathbbm{1}+B)|\leqslant\left\lVert A-B\right\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}\exp(\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}+\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}+1).

(2.2.19)

2.

If
A
,
B\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
are such that both
AB
and
BA
are of trace class, then we have

\detf(\mathbbm{1}+AB)=\detf(\mathbbm{1}+BA).\quad\penalty\ \Box

(2.2.20)

Factorization Lemma

Lemma 2.2.9
.

Suppose
A
and
K
are
\Psi
DOs such that both
A
and
A(\mathbbm{1}+K)
satisfy the assumptions of proposition
2.2.6
and that
\detz(A)
and
\detz(A(\mathbbm{1}+K))
are defined. Suppose moreover
K
is trace class and there exists smoothing operators
\{K_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
such that
AK_{i}\to AK
in
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}
(in particular,
AK
is also trace class). Then

\detz(A(\mathbbm{1}+K))=\detz(A)\detf(\mathbbm{1}+K).

(2.2.21)

Proof.

We start from Kontsevich and Vishik
[
KV94
]
proposition 6.4 and take for granted that (
2.2.21
) holds with
K_{i}
in place of
K
. Our assumptions are tailor-made so that as
i\to\infty
,

\detz(A(\mathbbm{1}+K_{i}))\longrightarrow\detz(A(\mathbbm{1}+K)).

(2.2.22)

Indeed, by (
2.2.12
) we have

\left\lVert(A(\mathbbm{1}+K)-\lambda)^{-1}A(K_{i}-K)(A(\mathbbm{1}+K_{i})-\lambda)^{-1}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}\leqslant c|\lambda|^{-2}\big\lVert A(K_{i}-K)\big\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}},

(2.2.23)

with
c
independent of
i
since a fortiori
AK_{i}\to AK
under
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{L^{2}}
. This in particular shows when
\mathfrak{Re}(z)>-1
the integral expression for
A(\mathbbm{1}+K)^{-z}-A(\mathbbm{1}+K_{i})^{-z}
is a converging Bochner integral valued in the trace class ideal
\mathcal{J}_{1}
(note
|\lambda^{-z}|\asymp|\lambda|^{-\mathfrak{Re}(z)}
as
\lambda\to-\infty
) and since
\ttr_{L^{2}}
is a continuous functional on
\mathcal{J}_{1}
,

\big|\ttr_{L^{2}}(A(\mathbbm{1}+K)^{-z}-A(\mathbbm{1}+K_{i})^{-z})\big|\lesssim\int_{\gamma}|\lambda|^{-\mathfrak{Re}(z)-2}\big\lVert A(K_{i}-K)\big\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}\mathrm{d}\lambda.

(2.2.24)

Now by (iii) of proposition
2.2.6
there is
\frac{1}{2}>\delta>0
so that
\zeta_{A(\mathbbm{1}+K_{i})}
,
\zeta_{A(\mathbbm{1}+K)}
are both holomorphic over
\overline{\mathbb{B}}_{\delta}(0)
(for example,
\delta<1/|s|
where
s
is the order of
A
). Thus by (
2.2.24
) and Cauchy’s estimate

\displaystyle\big|\zeta_{A(\mathbbm{1}+K)}^{\prime}(0)-\zeta_{A(\mathbbm{1}+K_{i})}^{\prime}(0)\big|

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{\delta}\sup_{|z|=\delta}\big|\zeta_{A(\mathbbm{1}+K)}(z)-\zeta_{A(\mathbbm{1}+K_{i})}(z)\big|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\delta}\sup_{|z|=\delta}\int_{\gamma}|\lambda|^{-\mathfrak{Re}(z)-2}\big\lVert A(K_{i}-K)\big\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}\mathrm{d}\lambda

\displaystyle\lesssim\big\lVert A(K_{i}-K)\big\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}\int_{\gamma}|\lambda|^{-3/2}\mathrm{d}\lambda

\displaystyle\lesssim\big\lVert A(K_{i}-K)\big\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}.

This shows
|\zeta_{A(\mathbbm{1}+K)}^{\prime}(0)-\zeta_{A(\mathbbm{1}+K_{i})}^{\prime}(0)|\to 0
, as we have desired.
∎

The Gluing Formula of Burghelea-Friedlander-Kappeler

Let
(M,g)
is a closed Riemannian manifold and
\Sigma\subset M
an embedded closed hypersurface with induced metric. Assume proposition
2.5.7
and decompose
\phi=\phi_{\Sigma}+\phi_{M\setminus\Sigma}^{D}
corresponding to
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}=\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M\setminus\Sigma,D}\otimes\mu_{\DN}^{\Sigma,M}
. In view of equation (
2.2.3
), and in parallel

\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\varphi,\DN\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}}}[\mathcal{L}\varphi]\overset{\mathrm{heu}}{=}\detz(\DN_{M}^{\Sigma})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\DN}^{\Sigma,M},

(2.2.25)

if we assume a “formal Fubini theorem” with respect to the heuristic expressions involving
\mathcal{L}
, namely

\int\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\langle\phi,(\Delta+m^{2})\phi\rangle_{L^{2}}}[\mathcal{L}\phi]\overset{\mathrm{heu}}{=}\iint\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\langle\phi_{\Sigma},(\Delta+m^{2})\phi_{\Sigma}\rangle_{L^{2}}}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\langle\phi_{M\setminus\Sigma}^{D},(\Delta+m^{2})\phi_{M\setminus\Sigma}^{D}\rangle_{L^{2}}}[\mathcal{L}\phi_{\Sigma}]\otimes[\mathcal{L}\phi_{M\setminus\Sigma}^{D}],

(2.2.26)

then we are led to the following relation of the corresponding determinants (volumes) which were first rigorously proved by Burghelea, Friedlander and Kappeler
[
BFK92
]
.

Proposition 2.2.10

(
[
BFK92
]
theorem B,
[
Lee97
]
theorem 1.1)
.

Let
(M,g)
is a closed Riemannian surface and
\Sigma\subset M
an embedded closed hypersurface with induced metric. Then

\detz(\Delta_{M}+m^{2})=\detz(\Delta_{M\setminus\Sigma,D}+m^{2})\detz(\DN_{M}^{\Sigma}).\quad\Box

(2.2.27)

The following version where
\Sigma
dissects
M
such that
M\setminus\Sigma=M^{\circ}_{+}\sqcup M^{\circ}_{-}
is also useful.

Corollary 2.2.11
.

In the situation as above, we have

\detz(\Delta_{M}+m^{2})=\detz(\Delta_{M_{+},D}+m^{2})\detz(\Delta_{M_{-},D}+m^{2})\detz(\DN_{M}^{\Sigma}).\quad\Box

(2.2.28)

As we base our analysis on background Gaussian probability measures, the formulae (
2.2.27
) and (
2.2.28
) constitute separate ingredients (constants) that needs to be “tuned” for the final gluing result to hold exactly. In fact, it is also reasonable to consider “projective gluing” which allows the freedom for an arbitrary (nonzero) constant to appear in the equation (see Segal
[
Seg04
]
page 460).

Quadratic Perturbation = Radon-Nikodym Density

Let
C
be a Gaussian covariance operator of order
-s
on a closed Riemannian manifold
\Sigma
, and denote by
\mu_{C}
the Gaussian measure on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma)
with covariance
\left\langle-,C-\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}
. Let
V
be another bounded formally self-adjoint operator on
L^{2}(\Sigma)
(it could be given by a real symmetric Schwartz kernel). In this section we look at the Gibbs measure

\mathrm{d}\mu(\varphi)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\frac{\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\varphi,V\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}}}\mathrm{d}\mu_{C}(\varphi)}{\int\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\varphi,V\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}}}\mathrm{d}\mu_{C}},

(2.2.29)

which is a Gaussian measure (see proposition
2.2.13
).

From another perspective we consider Radon-Nikodym densities between mutually absolutely continuous Gaussian measures on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma)
. See Bogachev
[
Bog98
]
section 6.4 for a general treatment from this perspective. We shall reproduce a proof following Glimm and Jaffe
[
GJ87
]
section 9.3 for reader’s convenience and adaptation to the current situation.

A principal corollary of the results of this section is the following.

Corollary 2.2.12
.

Let
\Sigma
be the disjoint union of Riemannian circles, embedded in an ambient Riemannian surface
M
(with or without boundary). Let
\mu_{\mathrm{DN}}^{\Sigma,M}
and
\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}
be the two Gaussian measures constructed on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma)
with covariance operators
(\DN_{M}^{\Sigma})^{-1}
and
(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma})^{-1}
(if
M
has boundary, specify the boundary condition to be
B
as in section
2.4.1
). Then
\mu_{\mathrm{DN}}^{\Sigma,M}
and
\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}
are mutually absolutely continuous with Radon-Nikodym density given by

\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathrm{DN}}^{\Sigma,M}}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}}(\varphi)=({\textstyle\det_{\zeta}}(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma}))^{-\frac{1}{2}}({\textstyle\det_{\zeta}}\DN_{M}^{\Sigma})^{\frac{1}{2}}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\langle\varphi,(\DN_{M}^{\Sigma}-2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma})\varphi\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}}.

(2.2.30)

The proof is at the end of this section. First we come back to the general case.

Proposition 2.2.13
.

Let
V:L^{2}(\Sigma)\longrightarrow L^{2}(\Sigma)
be as above and moreover assume

C^{-1}+V\textrm{ is positive,}

(2.2.31)

and that

\widehat{V}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}C^{\frac{1}{2}}VC^{\frac{1}{2}}\textrm{ is trace class.}

(2.2.32)

Then

1.

the random variable
\left\langle\varphi,V\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
can be defined in
L^{1}(\mu_{C})
and
\mathbb{E}_{C}[\left\langle\varphi,V\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}}]=\ttr(\widehat{V})
,

2.

Z:=\mathbb{E}_{C}[\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\varphi,V\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}}}]={\textstyle\det_{\mathrm{Fr}}}(\mathbbm{1}+\widehat{V})^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, and

3.

the Gibbs measure (
2.2.29
) is Gaussian with covariance
(C^{-1}+V)^{-1}
.

Note that since
C^{-1}+V
is positive and
C
is also positive,
\mathbbm{1}+\widehat{V}=C^{\frac{1}{2}}(C^{-1}+V)C^{\frac{1}{2}}
is positive.

Lemma 2.2.14
.

There exist an orthonormal basis
\{f_{j}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
of the Gaussian Hilbert space
W^{-s}(\Sigma)
of
\mu_{C}
equipped with
\left\langle-,C-\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
, such that

\left\langle\varphi,V\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{j}\varphi(f_{j})^{2},

(2.2.33)

for all
\varphi
belonging to the Cameron-Martin space
W^{s}(\Sigma)
, where
\{\lambda_{j}\}
are the eigenvalues of
\widehat{V}
on
L^{2}(\Sigma)
, and the series converges absolutely in
L^{1}(\mu_{C})
. Thus we
define
the random variable
\left\langle\varphi,V\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
with this converging series. Consequently, (i) of proposition
2.2.13
holds.

Proof.

The key is to seek
\{C^{\frac{1}{2}}f_{j}\}
as complete
L^{2}
-orthonormal eigenfunctions of
\widehat{V}
with eigenvalues
\{\lambda_{j}\}
, which exist since
\widehat{V}
is self-adjoint and trace class on
L^{2}(\Sigma)
. Now
\{f_{j}\}\subset W^{-s}(\Sigma)
and is complete orthonormal with respect to
\left\langle-,C-\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
. This also means the random variables
\{\varphi(f_{j})\}
are mutually independent. Note for
\varphi\in W^{s}(\Sigma)
,
C^{-\frac{1}{2}}\varphi\in L^{2}(\Sigma)
, and

\displaystyle\big\langle\varphi,V\varphi\big\rangle_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\big\langle C^{-\frac{1}{2}}\varphi,\widehat{V}C^{-\frac{1}{2}}\varphi\big\rangle_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{j}\big|\big\langle C^{-\frac{1}{2}}\varphi,C^{\frac{1}{2}}f_{j}\big\rangle_{L^{2}}\big|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{j}\varphi(f_{j})^{2}.

Since
\widehat{V}
is trace class,

\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{C}\big[|\lambda_{j}|\varphi(f_{j})^{2}\big]=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}|\lambda_{j}|<\infty,

(2.2.34)

and we obtain the result.
∎

Remark 2.2.7
.

We thus defined
\left\langle\varphi,V\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
as a
Wiener quadratic form
and it lies in the second Wiener chaos of
\mu_{C}
, a fortiori in
L^{2}(\mu_{C})
. See Bogachev
[
Bog98
]
pages 257-261 for more information and in particular proposition 5.10.16 for the same result in the context of Malliavin calculus. In fact,

VCf_{j}=C^{-\frac{1}{2}}\widehat{V}C^{\frac{1}{2}}f_{j}=\lambda_{j}f_{j},

(2.2.35)

and hence
V
is trace class on
W^{s}(\Sigma)
with eigenbasis
\{Cf_{j}\}
.

Remark 2.2.8
.

We have
\lambda_{j}>-1
by the assumption (
2.2.31
).

To treat (ii) and (iii) of proposition
2.2.13
we adopt some approximations.

Lemma 2.2.15
.

Proposition
2.2.13
(ii) and (iii) is true in the case
V
has finite rank.

Proof.

In this case the series in (
2.2.33
) is finite with
\{f_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N}
for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Thus

\displaystyle Z

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{N/2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}x_{j}^{2}}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}x_{j}^{2}}\mathrm{d}^{N}x

\displaystyle=\prod_{j}(1+\lambda_{j})^{-\frac{1}{2}}=\det(\mathbbm{1}+\widehat{V})^{-\frac{1}{2}},

by projecting onto
\mathbb{R}^{N}
via
\varphi\mapsto(\varphi(f_{1}),\dots,\varphi(f_{N}))=:(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})
.
For the covariance, we orthogonally decompose
W^{-s}(\Sigma)
as

W^{-s}(\Sigma)=\spn\{f_{j}\penalty\ |\penalty\ 1\leqslant j\leqslant N\}\oplus\spn\{f_{j}\penalty\ |\penalty\ 1\leqslant j\leqslant N\}^{\perp},

(2.2.36)

let
\Pi_{0}
and
\Pi_{1}
be the corresponding orthogonal projections (in order), and for any
f\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
write

\varphi(f)=\alpha_{1}\varphi(f_{1})+\cdots+\alpha_{N}\varphi(f_{N})+\varphi(\Pi_{1}f),

(2.2.37)

then
\varphi(\Pi_{1}f)
is independent of both
\varphi(f_{j})
,
1\leqslant j\leqslant N
, and
\left\langle\varphi,V\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
. It is now clear that (
2.2.29
) is Gaussian because we could now express
\mu(\{\phi(f)\in A\})
for any Borel set
A\subset\mathbb{R}
as a Gaussian integral over
\mathbb{R}^{N+1}
. Apply
C^{\frac{1}{2}}
to (
2.2.36
) we get the
L^{2}
-orthogonal decomposition

L^{2}(\Sigma)=\underbrace{C^{\frac{1}{2}}(\spn\{f_{j}\penalty\ |\penalty\ 1\leqslant j\leqslant N\})}_{=:L^{2}(\Sigma)_{0}}\oplus\underbrace{C^{\frac{1}{2}}(\spn\{f_{j}\penalty\ |\penalty\ 1\leqslant j\leqslant N\}^{\perp})}_{=:L^{2}(\Sigma)_{1}}.

(2.2.38)

Clearly
\widehat{V}
leaves this decomposition invariant and is zero on
L^{2}(\Sigma)_{1}
. Hence
(\mathbbm{1}+\widehat{V})^{-1}
is block-diagonal,

(\mathbbm{1}+\widehat{V})^{-1}=\begin{array}[]{ccl}\{C^{\frac{1}{2}}f_{j}\}_{1}^{N}&L^{2}(\Sigma)_{1}&\\
\diag_{j=1}^{N}\{(\lambda_{j}+1)^{-1}\}&0&\{C^{\frac{1}{2}}f_{j}\}_{1}^{N}\\
0&\mathbbm{1}&L^{2}(\Sigma)_{1}\\
\end{array}

(2.2.39)

Thus

\displaystyle Z^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{C}[\varphi(f)^{2}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\varphi,V\varphi\right\rangle}]

\displaystyle=Z^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{C}[\varphi(\Pi_{0}f)^{2}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}\varphi(f_{j})^{2}}]+Z^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{C}[\varphi(\Pi_{1}f)^{2}]\mathbb{E}_{C}[\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\varphi,V\varphi\right\rangle}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}\alpha_{j}^{2}(\lambda_{j}+1)^{-1}+\big\langle\Pi_{1}f,C\Pi_{1}f\big\rangle_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\big\langle C^{\frac{1}{2}}\Pi_{0}f,(\mathbbm{1}+\widehat{V})^{-1}C^{\frac{1}{2}}\Pi_{0}f\big\rangle_{L^{2}}+\big\langle\Pi_{1}f,C^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbbm{1}+\widehat{V})^{-1}C^{\frac{1}{2}}\Pi_{1}f\big\rangle_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle f,(C^{-1}+V)^{-1}f\right\rangle_{L^{2}},

where we perform again a Gaussian integral on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
in the second line.
∎

Proof of proposition
2.2.13
(ii) and (iii).

For general
V
we impose spectral cut-off at
N
,

V_{N}(\varphi)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\lambda_{j}\left\langle\varphi,f_{j}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}f_{j}.

(2.2.40)

Then
C^{\frac{1}{2}}V_{N}C^{\frac{1}{2}}=:\widehat{V}_{N}\to\widehat{V}
under the trace norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\ttr}
(acting on
L^{2}(\Sigma)
). Hence
\left\langle\varphi,V_{N}\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\to\left\langle\varphi,V\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
in
L^{1}(\mu_{C})
by lemma
2.2.14
and after passing to a subsequence

\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\varphi,V_{N}\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}}}\to\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\varphi,V\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}}}

(2.2.41)

in
L^{1}(\mu_{C})
and (ii) for
V
follows, since
\detf(\mathbbm{1}+\widehat{V}_{N})\to\detf(\mathbbm{1}+\widehat{V})
by lemma
2.2.8
.
To prove (iii), we note that
\widehat{V_{N}}\to\widehat{V}
a fortiori under the operator norm, then
C^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbbm{1}+\widehat{V}_{N})^{-1}C^{\frac{1}{2}}\to C^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbbm{1}+\widehat{V})^{-1}C^{\frac{1}{2}}
in norm and

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}_{C}[\textrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(f)}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\varphi,V\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}}}]}{\mathbb{E}_{C}[\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\varphi,V\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}}}]}

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{C}[\textrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\varphi(f)}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\varphi,V_{N}\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}}}]}{\mathbb{E}_{C}[\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\varphi,V_{N}\varphi\right\rangle_{L^{2}}}]}

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle f,(C^{-1}+V_{N})^{-1}f\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle f,(C^{-1}+V)^{-1}f\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\Big),

for
f\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
, showing that (
2.2.29
) is Gaussian with the right covariance.
∎

Proof of corollary
2.2.12
.

Remember now that
\dim\Sigma=1
.
Write for short
\mathbf{D}:=2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma}
and
\DN:=\DN_{M}^{\Sigma}
. Setting
C=\mathbf{D}^{-1}
and
V=\DN-\mathbf{D}
in proposition
2.2.13
, we are left to prove the determinant identity

\detz(\mathbf{D})\detf(\mathbbm{1}+\mathbf{D}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\DN-\mathbf{D})\mathbf{D}^{-\frac{1}{2}})=\detz(\DN).

(2.2.42)

Indeed, this is now immediate as

\textrm{LHS}=\detz(\mathbf{D})\detf(\mathbbm{1}+\mathbf{D}^{-1}(\DN-\mathbf{D}))=\textrm{RHS}

(2.2.43)

by lemma
2.2.8
(ii) and lemma
2.2.9
. We point out
\mathbf{D}\mathbf{D}^{-1}(\DN-\mathbf{D})=V
can be approximated in
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}
by smoothing operators (by lemma
2.4.3
(iv),
V=\DN-\mathbf{D}
is
L^{2}
-trace class), so the conditions of lemma
2.2.9
is satisfied. Indeed, as above,
\{Cf_{j}\}\subset W^{1}(\Sigma)
are
W^{1}
-complete eigenfunctions of
V
. If the corresponding eigenvalue
\lambda_{j}\neq 0
, then the bootstrap argument shows
Cf_{j}\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
since
V\in\Psi^{<0}(\Sigma)
. Thus
V_{N}
, where
V_{N}
is as in (
2.2.40
), approximates
V
in
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}
and is smoothing. This concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 2.2.9
.

Though it is probably true, we do not claim
\DN-\mathbf{D}
is elliptic.

Variants of Nelson’s Argument

The goal of this section is to define part
A
of (
2.2.1
) which culminates in
Nelson’s theorem
. The principal obstacle in achieving this is the fact that powers of a distribution such as
\phi^{2}
,
\phi^{4}
etc., are generally not defined. Even as a random variable under
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}
, we have (formally)
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}[\phi(x)\phi(y)]\overset{\mathrm{heu}}{=}G_{(\Delta+m^{2})}(x,y)
from (
2.2.4
) for
x\neq y
but this implies
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}[\phi(x)\phi(x)]=G_{(\Delta+m^{2})}(x,x)=\infty
. This necessitates a procedure of
renormalization
which
subtracts away

\infty
and makes
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}[\phi(x)\phi(x)]<\infty
.

Here the natural renormalization strategy is provided by the Gaussian probability theory. Let
\{K_{\varepsilon}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \varepsilon>0\}
be a family of
smoothing
operators
3
3

3

that is, each
K_{\varepsilon}
maps
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)\longrightarrow C^{\infty}(M)
.
on
M
, for which
K_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbbm{1}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
(in a sense to be specified later), and consider the mollified random field
\phi_{\varepsilon}:=K_{\varepsilon}\phi
. Instead of
\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)^{4}
, say, we look at

\displaystyle{:}\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)^{4}{:}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)^{4}-6\mathbb{E}[\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)^{2}]\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)^{2}+3\mathbb{E}[\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)^{2}]^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)^{4}-6C_{\varepsilon}(x)\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)^{2}+3C_{\varepsilon}(x)^{2}.

It happens that for any
\chi\in C^{\infty}(M)
, the integral
\int_{M}\chi(x){:}\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)^{4}{:}\mathrm{d}V_{M}(x)
converges as a random variable in
L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M})
to a definitive limit, and defines
\int_{M}\chi(x){:}\phi(x)^{4}{:}\mathrm{d}V_{M}(x)
as a random variable in
L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M})
. Note that
C_{\varepsilon}(x)\to\infty
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, so we have subtracted “infinities”.

Remark 2.3.1
.

The crucial point
in our adaptation of Nelson’s argument is to realize the
locality
of the interaction
\int_{M}\chi(x){:}P(\phi(x)){:}\mathrm{d}V_{M}(x)
(see section
2.5.4
). To this end we must allow a sufficiently large class of
regulators

K_{\varepsilon}
(in particular,
local
ones) and show that they define the same interaction (proposition
2.3.5
). In addition, the
Wick ordering
also needs to be local (see section
2.3.4
), so that the interaction on a domain with boundary could be defined without reference to the ambient closed manifold where this domain “caps”. See Brunetti, Fredenhagen, Verch
[
BFV03
]
and Guo, Paycha, Zhang
[
GPZ23
]
for more information and perspective on locality.

Remark 2.3.2
.

The method adopted here is restricted to dimension two. In three dimensions, the target measure bearing the heuristic form (
2.2.1
) becomes mutually singular with respect to
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}
and hence cannot be expressed as an integrable function multiplied by
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}
. A recent phenomenal method to treat this case is developed in the framework of stochastic PDEs, called
stochastic quantization
, providing an alternative to older results outlined in
[
GJ87
]
section 23.1. See the introductions in
[
GH21
]
,
[
HS22
]
,
[
AK20
]
,
[
MWX17
]
and
[
BDFT24
]
for reviews of the literature and pedagogical discussions.

Regularizations

In this subsection we describe an admissible class of regulators
K_{\varepsilon}
which would eventually produce the same random variable
\int_{M}\chi(x){:}\phi(x)^{4}{:}\mathrm{d}V_{M}(x)
as will be proved in the next subsection. Basically, they are smoothing operators such that
K_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbbm{1}
in
\Psi^{\delta}(M)
in the
symbol sense
for any
\delta>0
(see definition below). A compact notation is to say
K_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbbm{1}
in
\Psi^{0+}(M)
.

Definition 2.3.1
.

Let
r\in\mathbb{R}
we say that operators
K_{\varepsilon}\to K
in
\Psi^{r}(M)

in the symbol sense
if for any coordinate chart
\kappa:U\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
and cut-off
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(U)
, the full symbol of
\chi K_{\varepsilon}\chi
(considered acting on
C_{c}^{\infty}(\kappa(U))
), converges to that of
\chi K\chi
in the
\mathcal{S}^{r}_{1,0}(\kappa(U)\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
topology.

Now we describe the first candidate for
K_{\varepsilon}
satisfying the above assumption (the proof is in appendix
C.2
). This was introduced in Dyatlov and Zworski
[
DZ16
]
and has the advantage of being
local
, realizing the locality of the
P(\phi)
interaction eventually in section
2.5.4
. Consider
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}((-1,1))
with
0\leqslant\psi\leqslant 1
and equal to
1
near
0
. For
\varepsilon>0
we define the operator

E_{\varepsilon}u(x)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\int_{M}E_{\varepsilon}(x,y)u(y)\mathrm{d}V_{g}(y),\quad\textrm{with }E_{\varepsilon}(x,y)=\frac{1}{F_{\varepsilon}(x)}\psi\left(\frac{d_{g}(x,y)}{\varepsilon}\right).

(2.3.1)

Here
F_{\varepsilon}(x)=\int\psi(d(x,y)/\varepsilon)\mathrm{d}y
so that
\int E_{\varepsilon}(x,y)\mathrm{d}y=1
, and
d_{g}
denotes the Riemannian distance. One observes that
E_{\varepsilon}(x,y)
is smooth for each
\varepsilon>0
so
E_{\varepsilon}:\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)\longrightarrow C^{\infty}(M)
. Observe also that
\varepsilon^{d}/C\leqslant F_{\varepsilon}(x)\leqslant C\varepsilon^{d}
for some
C>0
, and this
C
could be made dependent neither on
\varepsilon
nor on
x
as
M
is compact.

Lemma 2.3.1
.

For any
\delta>0
we have
E_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbbm{1}
in
\Psi^{\delta}(M)
in the symbol sense.

Proof.

See appendix
C.2
.
∎

Note that
K^{*}_{\varepsilon}(x,y)=K_{\varepsilon}(y,x)
for real smoothing operators (and their symbols are related in a simple manner),
K_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbbm{1}
in the symbol sense is equivalent to
K_{\varepsilon}^{*}\to\mathbbm{1}
in the symbol sense.

Lemma 2.3.2
.

Let
\{E^{\prime}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}\}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}>0}
be another family of smoothing operators such that
E^{\prime}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}\to\mathbbm{1}
in
\Psi^{\delta^{\prime}}(M)
in the symbol sense for any
\delta^{\prime}>0
. Then the net
E_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\Delta+m^{2})^{-1}(E^{\prime}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}-E_{\varepsilon})
,
(\varepsilon^{\prime},\varepsilon)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
(we say
(\varepsilon^{\prime},\varepsilon)\prec(\varepsilon^{\prime}_{1},\varepsilon_{1})
iff
\varepsilon^{\prime}>\varepsilon^{\prime}_{1}
and
\varepsilon>\varepsilon_{1}
), converges to zero in
\Psi^{-2+\delta}(M)
in the symbol sense for any
\delta>0
.

Proof.

Note that following essentially the same arguments as above the
\Psi^{\delta/2}(M)
seminorms of
E_{\varepsilon}
can be bounded uniformly in
\varepsilon
. This said, the result follows essentially from the continuity of the twisted product (composition product) of symbols as a map
\mathcal{S}^{r}_{1,0}\times\mathcal{S}^{r^{\prime}}_{1,0}\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}^{r+r^{\prime}}_{1,0}
with respect to the symbol topologies (see Folland
[
Fol89
]
page 105 theorem 2.47).
∎

We shall consider another set of seminorms on
\Psi^{r}(M)
in the case
-d<r<0
which suits better our purposes. They are defined as follows. Let
\mathcal{M}\subset C^{\infty}(M\times M,T(M\times M))
denote the
C^{\infty}(M\times M)
-module of smooth vector fields tangent to the diagonal in
M\times M
. We fix a finite coordinate cover
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in 1}^{N}
of
M
, with charts
\kappa_{i}:U_{i}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and a partition of unity
\{\chi_{i}\}
subordinate to this cover.

Definition 2.3.2
.

For any
K\in C^{\infty}(M\times M\setminus\mathrm{diag})
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant N
and
L_{1}
, …,
L_{p}\in\mathcal{M}
, we define the seminorms

p_{i,L_{1},\dots,L_{p}}(K)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sup_{(x,y)\in U_{i}\times U_{i}}\big|(\kappa_{i}\times\kappa_{i})_{*}\left((\chi_{i}\otimes\chi_{i})L_{1}\cdots L_{p}K\right)(x,y)\big|\cdot d_{g}(x,y)^{d+r},

(2.3.2)

while on
U_{i}\times U_{j}
,
i\neq j
, which does not touch the diagonal, we use the
C^{\infty}(U_{i}\times U_{j})
seminorms. By the
kernel topology
on
\Psi^{r}(M)
,
-d<r<0
, we mean the topology induced by these seminorms on the Schwartz kernels
K_{A}
of
A\in\Psi^{r}(M)
. Here
d_{g}
is the distance function.

Proposition 2.3.3
.

In the case
-d<r<0
, the above kernel topology is equivalent to the topology induced by symbols
\mathcal{S}^{r}_{1,0}(T^{*}M)
on
\Psi^{r}(M)
. In particular, if
A_{\varepsilon}\to A
as
\varepsilon\to 0
in
\Psi^{r}(M)
in the symbol sense then
A_{\varepsilon}\to A
also in the above kernel topology.

Proof.

Essentially in Taylor
[
Tay23a
]
page 6, proposition 2.2, page 7, proposition 2.4 and page 10, proposition 2.7. See also Bailleul, Dang, Ferdinand and Tô
[
BDFT24a
]
proposition 6.9 for a more detailed treatment.
∎

Finally, we observe that the heat operator
K_{\varepsilon}=\textrm{e}^{-\varepsilon(\Delta+m^{2})}
is also a valid candidate:

Lemma 2.3.4

(
[
Dan22
]
lemma 4.15)
.

We have
\textrm{e}^{-\varepsilon(\Delta+m^{2})}\to\mathbbm{1}
in
\Psi^{\delta}(M)
in the symbol sense for any
\delta>0
.
\Box

Some properties of the heat operator is summed up in appendix
C.2
.

Integrability of Interaction and Regularization Independence

Proposition 2.3.5
.

Let
(K_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
be any family of real smoothing operators such that
K_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbbm{1}
in
\Psi^{\delta}(M)
in the symbol sense for any
\delta>0
. Define
\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)
and
{:}P(\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)){:}
as above and let
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(M)
be a test function. Put

S_{M,\varepsilon,\chi}(\phi):=\int_{M}\chi(x){:}P(\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)){:}\mathrm{d}V_{M}.

(2.3.3)

This is a random variable on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
equipped with
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}
. Then
\{S_{M,\chi,\varepsilon}\}
converges in
L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M})
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, and the limit is independent of the specific smoothing
(K_{\varepsilon})
chosen, provided they have the convergence property described above.

More precisely, for any other smoothing family
(\widetilde{K}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}})
satisfying the same condition and defining the random variable
\widetilde{S}_{M,\varepsilon^{\prime},\chi}
, we have a quantitative bound of the form

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\big|S_{M,\varepsilon,\chi}-\widetilde{S}_{M,\varepsilon^{\prime},\chi}\big|^{2}\big]\leqslant\mathfrak{O}(|\varepsilon-\varepsilon^{\prime}|)C_{n}\|\chi\|_{L^{4}}^{2},

where
\mathfrak{O}(|\varepsilon-\varepsilon^{\prime}|)
is a function going to zero as
|\varepsilon-\varepsilon^{\prime}|\to 0
, depending only on
M
.

A particularity of the dimension two is seen in the following elementary lemma.

Lemma 2.3.6
.

If
\dim M=2
and
g
is a smooth Riemannian metric on
M
then

\iint_{M\times M}|\log(d_{g}(x,y))|^{p}\mathrm{d}V_{M\times M}\leqslant C_{p}<\infty

(2.3.4)

for any
1\leqslant p<\infty
and

\iint_{M\times M}d_{g}(x,y)^{-\delta}\mathrm{d}V_{M\times M}\leqslant C_{\delta}<\infty

(2.3.5)

for any
0<\delta<1
,
d_{g}
denoting the distance function.
\Box

Consequently, since in two dimensions
G(x,y)=\mathcal{O}(\log(d(x,y)))
as
y\to x
(see lemma
2.3.12
), a simple argument with partition of unity shows

\iint_{M\times M}\chi(x)\chi(y)|G(x,y)|^{p}\mathrm{d}V_{M\times M}\leqslant C_{p}\|\chi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\vol(\text{supp}(\chi))^{2}<\infty

(2.3.6)

for some
C_{p}>0
, for all
1\leqslant p<\infty
. When
p=2
this is the familiar fact that in two dimensions the Green operator
(\Delta+m^{2})^{-1}
is Hilbert-Schmidt, which may also be shown using Weyl’s law.

We will denote

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}\big[\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)\phi_{\varepsilon}(y)\big]

\displaystyle=\big\langle\delta_{x},K_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\Delta+m^{2})^{-1}K_{\varepsilon}\delta_{y}\big\rangle_{L^{2}}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon}(x,y),

(2.3.7)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}\big[\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)\phi_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}(y)\big]

\displaystyle=\big\langle\delta_{x},K_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\Delta+m^{2})^{-1}\widetilde{K}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}\delta_{y}\big\rangle_{L^{2}}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x,y),

(2.3.8)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}\big[\phi_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x)\phi_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}(y)\big]

\displaystyle=\big\langle\delta_{x},\tilde{K}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}^{*}(\Delta+m^{2})^{-1}\widetilde{K}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}\delta_{y}\big\rangle_{L^{2}}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}G_{\varepsilon^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x,y).

(2.3.9)

Note that
G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}
has a
different
regulator on the second variable! For fixed
\varepsilon>0
, we have by lemma
A.3.1
,

{:}\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)^{2n}{:}=G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon}(x,x)^{n}h_{2n}\big(\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)\big/G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon}(x,x)^{\frac{1}{2}}\big)\geqslant-b_{1}G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon}(x,x)^{n},

(2.3.10)

for some constant
b_{1}>0
independent of
\varepsilon
, since the even-degree Hermite polynomial
h_{2n}
is bounded below.

Proof of proposition
2.3.5
.

We shall prove the proposition for the case
P(\theta)=\theta^{2n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the general case is similar. For fixed
\varepsilon
,
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
, we compute

\displaystyle\left\lVert S_{M,\varepsilon}-S_{M,\varepsilon^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\left|\int_{M}\chi{:}\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)^{2n}{:}\mathrm{d}V_{M}-\int_{M}\chi{:}\phi_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x)^{2n}{:}\mathrm{d}V_{M}\right|^{2}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\iint_{M\times M}{:}\chi(x)\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)^{2n}{:}\penalty\ {:}\phi_{\varepsilon}(y)^{2n}{:}\chi(y)\mathrm{d}V_{M}\otimes\mathrm{d}V_{M}\right]

\displaystyle\quad\quad-2\mathbb{E}\left[\iint_{M\times M}\chi(x){:}\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)^{2n}{:}\penalty\ {:}\phi_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}(y)^{2n}{:}\chi(y)\mathrm{d}V_{M}\otimes\mathrm{d}V_{M}\right]

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\mathbb{E}\left[\iint_{M\times M}\chi(x){:}\phi_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x)^{2n}{:}\penalty\ {:}\phi_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}(y)^{2n}{:}\chi(y)\mathrm{d}V_{M}\otimes\mathrm{d}V_{M}\right]

\displaystyle=\iint_{M\times M}\Big(\mathbb{E}\left[\chi(x){:}\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)^{2n}{:}\penalty\ {:}\phi_{\varepsilon}(y)^{2n}{:}\chi(y)\right]-2\mathbb{E}\left[\chi(x){:}\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)^{2n}{:}\penalty\ {:}\phi_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}(y)^{2n}{:}\chi(y)\right]

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\mathbb{E}\left[\chi(x){:}\phi_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x)^{2n}{:}\penalty\ {:}\phi_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}(y)^{2n}{:}\chi(y)\right]\Big)\mathrm{d}V_{M\times M}

(Tonelli)

\displaystyle=(2n)!\iint_{M\times M}\chi(x)\chi(y)\left(G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon}(x,y)^{2n}-2G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x,y)^{2n}+G_{\varepsilon^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x,y)^{2n}\right)\mathrm{d}V_{M\times M}

(lemma
A.3.1
)

We will control the integral
\iint_{M\times M}\chi(x)\chi(y)\left(G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon}(x,y)^{2n}-G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x,y)^{2n}\right)\mathrm{d}V_{M\times M}
for
\chi\in C^{\infty}(M)
and
\chi\geqslant 0
.
Indeed,

\displaystyle|\textrm{this integral}|

\displaystyle\leqslant\iint_{M\times M}\chi(x)\chi(y)\left|G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon}(x,y)-G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x,y)\right|\cdot\big|G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon}(x,y)^{2n-1}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon}(x,y)^{2n-2}G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x,y)+\dots+G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x,y)^{2n-1}\big|\mathrm{d}V_{M\times M}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2n\|\chi\|^{2}_{L^{4}}\left(C_{8n-4}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}\times\left(\iint_{M\times M}\left|G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon}(x,y)-G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x,y)\right|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

Remember that
G_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(x,y)
is the kernel of
