\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma_{1}\sqcup\Sigma_{2})=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma_{1})\oplus\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma_{2})

(2.6.10)

and the operator
\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma}
acts diagonally, and clearly the Gaussian fields on
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
are independent, that is,
\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{1}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}}=\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{1}}\otimes\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{2}}
. The real
L^{2}
space is hence the tensor product (
[
RS72
]
page 52).

Finally, if
\rho:\Sigma\longrightarrow\Sigma^{\prime}
is an isometry (of Riemannian 1-manifolds), then define
\rho_{*}:\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}
to be the obvious map induced by the correspondence between
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma)
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma^{\prime})
. Up to this point we have verified (i) of Definition
2.6.1
. By the nature of our definitions, in the sequel we do not distinguish
\mathcal{H}_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
from
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
.

Amplitudes = Schwartz Kernels

Let
\Omega\in\Mor(\varnothing,\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
, now we define
\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}\in\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
. Consider
\Omega^{*}\in\Mor(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{*},\varnothing)
as described in the paragraph “crossing and reflections” of subsection
2.6.1
, and let
|\Omega|^{2}:=\Omega^{*}\circ\Omega
, a closed surface. Clearly
|\Omega|^{2}
is also the
isometric double
of
\Omega
, equipped with a
reflection

\Theta_{|\Omega|^{2}}
with
\Theta_{|\Omega|^{2}}|_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}=\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
(
[
JR07a
]
Section 2). Now suppose we have defined
\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}
satisfying Definition
2.6.1
, then (iii) would imply

\displaystyle Z_{|\Omega|^{2}}

\displaystyle=\int|\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}(\varphi)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}(\varphi)\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma})^{-\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)}\textrm{e}^{-S_{|\Omega|^{2}}(\phi)}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}}(\phi)\detz(\Delta_{|\Omega|^{2}}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma)}\mathrm{d}\tau_{\Sigma}\big(\textrm{e}^{-S_{|\Omega|^{2}}}\cdot\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}}\big)(\varphi)\detz(\Delta_{|\Omega|^{2}}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

In the case
\Omega\in\Mor(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2})
we have in other words
Z_{|\Omega|^{2}}=\ttr_{\rho}(U_{\Omega^{*}}U_{\Omega})
. This motivates the following definition which is also inspired by related considerations in
[
Pic08
]
.

Definition 2.6.2
.

Let
\Omega\in\Mor(\varnothing,\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
and
P
the polynomial defining the interaction. We define the
amplitude
associated to
\Omega
to be the quantity

\mathcal{A}^{P}_{\Omega}(\varphi)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\bigg[\frac{\detz(\Delta_{|\Omega|^{2}}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma})^{-\frac{1}{2}}}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{d}\big[\tau_{\Sigma}\big(\textrm{e}^{-S_{|\Omega|^{2}}}\cdot\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}}\big)\big]}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}}(\varphi)\bigg]^{\frac{1}{2}},

(2.6.11)

where the second ratio denotes the Radon-Nikodym density.

This is well-defined since
\tau_{\Sigma}(\textrm{e}^{-S_{|\Omega|^{2}}}\cdot\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}})\ll\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}
as
\tau_{\Sigma}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}})=\mu_{2\DN}^{\Sigma,\Omega}\ll\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}
by Corollary
2.2.12
, and both are finite positive measures. We see also that
\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}>0
almost surely with respect to
\widehat{\mu}_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}
. We have automatically
\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}\in L^{2}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma),\widehat{\mu}_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma})=\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
since
|\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}|^{2}\in L^{1}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma),\widehat{\mu}_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma})
by definition.

Lemma 2.6.2
.

Let
\Omega
,
\Omega^{\prime}\in\Mor(\varnothing,\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
and
\kappa:\Omega\longrightarrow\Omega^{\prime}
an isometry of morphisms in the sense of Subsection
2.6.1
. Then
\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{P}=\mathcal{A}_{\Omega^{\prime}}^{P}
.

Proof.

The isometry
\kappa
may be extended to an isometry of the double
|\Omega|^{2}
,
\kappa^{*}\sqcup\kappa
, which fixes a collar of
\Sigma
. The lemma follows because all objects involved in the definition (
2.6.11
) are isometry invariant. In particular the interaction
S_{|\Omega|^{2}}
is so as there exist isometry invariant regularization procedures (e.g. heat or (
2.3.1
)).
∎

Example 2.6.1
.

Let us derive the amplitude
\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{0}
for the free field, that is, with
S_{|\Omega|^{2}}=0
. Indeed,

|\mathcal{A}^{0}_{\Omega}(\varphi)|^{2}=\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{\Sigma}\big(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}}\detz(\Delta_{|\Omega|^{2}}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big)}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}\detz(2\mathbf{D})^{-\frac{1}{2}}}(\varphi).

(2.6.12)

Since

\tau_{\Sigma}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}})=\mu_{2\DN}^{\Sigma,\Omega},

(2.6.13)

namely the Gaussian measure on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma)
with covariance
\frac{1}{2}(\DN_{\Omega}^{\Sigma})^{-1}
, taking into account the BFK formula

\detz(\Delta_{|\Omega|^{2}}+m^{2})=[\detz(\Delta_{\Omega,D}+m^{2})]^{2}\detz(2\DN_{\Omega}^{\Sigma}),

(2.6.14)

we obtain, by Corollary
2.2.12
,

\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{0}(\varphi)=\detz(\Delta_{\Omega,D}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\langle\varphi,(\DN_{\Omega}^{\Sigma}-\mathbf{D})\varphi\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}}.

(2.6.15)

Lemma 2.6.3
.

We have

\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{P}(\varphi)=\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{0}(\varphi)\int\textrm{e}^{-S_{\Omega}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}|\varphi)}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}),

(2.6.16)

for almost every
\varphi
under
\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}
. Here
S_{\Omega}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}|\varphi)
is as defined in Definition
2.5.2
.

Proof.

Indeed, by definition of the measure image,

\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{P}(\varphi)^{2}=\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{0}(\varphi)^{2}\iint\textrm{e}^{-S_{|\Omega|^{2}}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}+\phi^{D}_{\Omega^{*}}+\PI_{|\Omega|^{2}}^{\Sigma}\varphi)}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega})\otimes\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega^{*},D}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega^{*}}).

(2.6.17)

However, by Proposition
2.5.15
which decouples the interaction into a sum over complementary regions,

\textrm{e}^{-S_{|\Omega|^{2}}(\phi)}=\textrm{e}^{-S_{\Omega}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}|\varphi)}\textrm{e}^{-S_{\Omega^{*}}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega^{*}}|\varphi)},

(2.6.18)

almost surely againt
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}\otimes\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega^{*},D}\otimes\tau_{\Sigma}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}})
, and hence also against
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}\otimes\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega^{*},D}\otimes\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}
. Thus by reflection symmetry between
\Omega
and
\Omega^{*}
and independence between
\phi^{D}_{\Omega}
and
\phi^{D}_{\Omega^{*}}
we obtain the result.
∎

Trace Axiom and its Consequences

In this subsection we treat separately (ii) of Lemma
2.6.1
as part of (iii) of Definition
2.6.1
. Let
\Omega\in\Mor(\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
,
not necessarily connected
. We will consider two closed surfaces:
\check{\Omega}
(from Lemma
2.6.1
(ii)) and
|\Omega|^{2}:=\Omega^{*}\circ\Omega
.

Proposition 2.6.4

(pre-trace)
.

We have

\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{\Sigma}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\check{\Omega}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}}(\varphi)=\frac{\detz(\DN_{\check{\Omega}}^{\Sigma})^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\detz(2\DN_{\Omega}^{\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma})^{\frac{1}{4}}}\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma}}(\varphi,\varphi)\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(2.6.19)

Proof.

This boils down to comparing the explicit expressions for the densities as given by Corollary
2.2.12
. Indeed, the relation that we need is

\Big\langle\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{\varphi}{\varphi},(2\DN_{\Omega}^{\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma}-2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma})\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{\varphi}{\varphi}\Big\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma)}=2\big\langle\varphi,(\DN_{\check{\Omega}}^{\Sigma}-2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma})\varphi\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}.

(2.6.20)

This is true for
\varphi\in W^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma)
because
\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma}=\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma}\oplus\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma}
and
\langle[\begin{smallmatrix}\varphi\\
\varphi\end{smallmatrix}],\DN_{\Omega}^{\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma}[\begin{smallmatrix}\varphi\\
\varphi\end{smallmatrix}]\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma)}
and
\langle\varphi,\DN_{\check{\Omega}}^{\Sigma}\varphi\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}
are both the Dirichlet energy of the harmonic extension over
\Omega
with boundary condition
(\varphi,\varphi)
. The equality then extends to
\varphi\in W^{-\delta}(\Sigma)
for small enough
\delta
by continuity (see Lemma
2.2.2
(iii)).
∎

Corollary 2.6.5

(trace for free field)
.

In the situation as above, we have

\int\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{0}(\varphi,\varphi)\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}(\varphi)\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma})^{-\frac{1}{2}}=\detz(\Delta_{\check{\Omega}}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

(2.6.21)

Proof.

Indeed,

LHS

\displaystyle=\frac{\detz(\Delta_{|\Omega|^{2}}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{4}}}{\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma}\oplus 2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma})^{-\frac{1}{4}}}\int\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma}}(\varphi,\varphi)\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}(\varphi)\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma})^{-\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\detz(2\DN_{\Omega}^{\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma})^{\frac{1}{4}}}{\detz(\DN_{\check{\Omega}}^{\Sigma})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{\detz(\Delta_{|\Omega|^{2}}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{4}}}{\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma})^{-\frac{1}{2}}}\underbrace{\int\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{\Sigma}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\check{\Omega}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}}(\varphi)\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}(\varphi)}_{=\penalty\ 1}\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma})^{-\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\detz(\DN_{\check{\Omega}}^{\Sigma})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\detz(\Delta_{\Omega,D}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}

(BFK for
|\Omega|^{2}
)

\displaystyle=\textrm{RHS},

(BFK for
\check{\Omega}
)

finishing the proof.
∎

Note that we do not insist that
\Omega
be connected. This leads to the following important consequence of Proposition
2.6.4
.

Corollary 2.6.6
.

Let
\Omega_{1}\in\Mor(\varnothing,\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
,
\Omega_{2}\in\Mor(\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\varnothing)
. Denote
M:=\Omega_{2}\circ\Omega_{1}\in\Mor(\varnothing,\varnothing)
. Then

\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{\Sigma}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}}(\varphi)=\frac{\detz(\DN_{M}^{\Sigma})^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\detz(2\DN_{\Omega_{1}}^{\Sigma})^{\frac{1}{4}}\detz(2\DN_{\Omega_{2}}^{\Sigma})^{\frac{1}{4}}}\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{\Sigma}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega_{1}|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}}(\varphi)\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{\Sigma}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega_{2}|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}}(\varphi)\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(2.6.22)

Proof.

Indeed, in this case
\Omega:=\Omega_{1}\sqcup\Omega_{2}=\Omega_{1}\circ\Omega_{2}
can be seen as an element of
\Mor(\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
. Then
|\Omega|^{2}=|\Omega_{1}|^{2}\sqcup|\Omega_{2}|^{2}
and the GFFs on the two components are independent. In addition,
\DN_{\Omega}^{\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma}
is the direct sum
\DN_{\Omega_{1}}^{\Sigma}\oplus\DN_{\Omega_{2}}^{\Sigma}
. Thus (
2.6.19
) gives (
2.6.22
).
∎

Corollary 2.6.7

(dissection gluing)
.

In the same situation as Corollary
2.6.6
, we have

\int\mathcal{A}_{\Omega_{1}}^{0}(\varphi)\mathcal{A}_{\Omega_{2}}^{0}(\varphi)\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}(\varphi)\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma})^{-\frac{1}{2}}=\detz(\Delta_{M}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

(2.6.23)

Proof.

Again, we apply Corollary
2.6.5
directly to the case
\Omega=\Omega_{1}\sqcup\Omega_{2}
and
|\Omega|^{2}=|\Omega_{1}|^{2}\sqcup|\Omega_{2}|^{2}
. We note that
\mathcal{A}_{\Omega_{1}}^{0}(\varphi)\mathcal{A}_{\Omega_{2}}^{0}(\varphi)=\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{0}(\varphi,\varphi)
because

\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma}}(\varphi,\varphi)=\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{\Sigma}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega_{1}|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}}(\varphi)\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{\Sigma}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega_{2}|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}}(\varphi)

(2.6.24)

and
\detz(\Delta_{|\Omega|^{2}}+m^{2})=\detz(\Delta_{|\Omega_{1}|^{2}}+m^{2})\detz(\Delta_{|\Omega_{2}|^{2}}+m^{2})
.
∎

Next we deal with the trace axiom in the interacting case. Again let
\Omega\in\Mor(\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
and consider
\check{\Omega}
. One has in this case the decomposition

\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\check{\Omega}}=\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}\otimes\tau_{\Sigma}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\check{\Omega}})

(2.6.25)

and against which

S_{\check{\Omega}}(\phi)=S_{\check{\Omega}}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}+\PI_{\check{\Omega}}^{\Sigma}\varphi)=S_{\Omega}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}|\varphi,\varphi)\quad\textrm{and}\quad\textrm{e}^{-S_{\check{\Omega}}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}+\PI_{\check{\Omega}}^{\Sigma}\varphi)}=\textrm{e}^{-S_{\Omega}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}|\varphi,\varphi)}

(2.6.26)

for
\phi^{D}_{\Omega}\sim\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}
and
\varphi\sim\tau_{\Sigma}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\check{\Omega}})
almost surely.

Corollary 2.6.8

(trace for
P(\phi)
field)
.

In the situation as above, we have

\int\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{P}(\varphi,\varphi)\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}(\varphi)\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma})^{-\frac{1}{2}}=Z_{\check{\Omega}}.

(2.6.27)

Proof.

Indeed,

\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{P}(\varphi,\varphi)=\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{0}(\varphi,\varphi)\int\textrm{e}^{-S_{\Omega}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}|\varphi,\varphi)}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}),

(2.6.28)

therefore, with the constants involving determinants working out in exactly the same way as Corollary
2.6.5
, one has

LHS

\displaystyle=\detz(\Delta_{\check{\Omega}}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{\Sigma}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\check{\Omega}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}}(\varphi)\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}(\varphi)\int\textrm{e}^{-S_{\Omega}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}|\varphi,\varphi)}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega})

\displaystyle=\detz(\Delta_{\check{\Omega}}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\int\textrm{e}^{-S_{\Omega}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}|\varphi,\varphi)}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}\otimes\mathrm{d}\tau_{\Sigma}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\check{\Omega}})(\phi^{D}_{\Omega},\varphi)

\displaystyle=\detz(\Delta_{\check{\Omega}}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\check{\Omega}}[\textrm{e}^{-S_{\check{\Omega}}(\phi)}]=\textrm{RHS}.

We arrive at the proof.
∎

Corollary 2.6.9

(dissection gluing for
P(\phi)
field)
.

In the same situation as Corollary
2.6.6
, we have

\int\mathcal{A}_{\Omega_{1}}^{P}(\varphi)\mathcal{A}_{\Omega_{2}}^{P}(\varphi)\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}(\varphi)\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma})^{-\frac{1}{2}}=Z_{M}.

(2.6.29)

Proof.

One verifies that for
\Omega=\Omega_{1}\sqcup\Omega_{2}
,

\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{P}(\varphi,\varphi)=\mathcal{A}_{\Omega_{1}}^{P}(\varphi)\mathcal{A}_{\Omega_{2}}^{P}(\varphi).

(2.6.30)

This in fact also comes directly from the definition as

S_{|\Omega|^{2}}(\phi_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}})=S_{|\Omega_{1}|^{2}}(\phi_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega_{1}|^{2}})+S_{|\Omega_{2}|^{2}}(\phi_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega_{2}|^{2}})

(2.6.31)

pointwise almost surely with
\phi_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}}=\phi_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega_{1}|^{2}}+\phi_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega_{2}|^{2}}
. One then follows a verbatim reasoning as for Corollary
2.6.7
.
∎

Gluing in the General Case

Now let us put ourselves in the general situation of (iii) of Definition
2.6.1
. Suppose
\Omega\in\Mor(\varnothing,\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}\sqcup\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{*}\sqcup\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1})
and that
\rho:\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}\longrightarrow\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{*}
is an orientation reversing isometry. Recall
\Omega/\rho
from the paragraph “composition” from subsection
2.6.1
. Denote by
\Sigma_{4}
the reflected copy of
\Sigma_{2}
(core) in
|\Omega/\rho|^{2}
, see top-right picture in figure
2.2
.

Notation
.

In general, for
M
a closed manifold and
\Sigma_{1}
, …,
\Sigma_{\ell}\subset M
finitely many nonintersecting closed embedded hypersurfaces, we denote

\displaystyle\tau_{i\sqcup j\sqcup\cdots}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\textrm{the trace map }C^{\infty}(M)\longrightarrow C^{\infty}(\Sigma_{i})\times C^{\infty}(\Sigma_{j})\times\cdots,\textrm{ and its extensions,}

\displaystyle\PI_{M}^{i\sqcup j\sqcup\cdots}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\textrm{the Poisson integral operator }W^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma_{i})\times W^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma_{j})\times\cdots\longrightarrow W^{1}(M),

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{M,k\sqcup\cdots}^{i\sqcup j\sqcup\cdots}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\tau_{k\sqcup\cdots}\PI_{M}^{i\sqcup j\sqcup\cdots},\textrm{ the transition operator }\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma_{i})\times\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma_{j})\times\cdots\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma_{k})\times\cdots\textrm{ as in (\ref{eqn-trans-op-def}).}

We also remind the reader of the notations set up in definitions
2.4.4
and
2.4.1
.

Proposition 2.6.10

(Bayes with free ends)
.

We have

\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{1}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega/\rho|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{1}}}(\varphi_{1})\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{d}\big((\mathcal{M}_{|\Omega/\rho|^{2},2}^{1}\varphi_{1})_{*}\mu_{\DN}^{\Sigma_{2},\Omega/\rho,D}\big)}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{2}}}(x)\bigg)^{2}=\frac{\detz(\DN_{|\Omega/\rho|^{2}}^{\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{4}})^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\detz(\DN_{|\Omega|^{2}}^{\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}^{*}})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{2\sqcup 2^{*}\sqcup 1}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{2\sqcup 2^{*}\sqcup 1}}(x,x,\varphi_{1})

(2.6.32)

Figure 2.2
:
two ways of taking trace

Proof.

Indeed, by reflection symmetry across
\Sigma_{1}
where
\Sigma_{2}
gets identified with
\Sigma_{4}
, we have

\mathcal{M}_{|\Omega/\rho|^{2},2}^{1}\varphi_{1}=\mathcal{M}_{|\Omega/\rho|^{2},4}^{1}\varphi_{1},\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mu_{\DN}^{\Sigma_{2},\Omega/\rho,D}=\mu_{\DN}^{\Sigma_{4},\Omega/\rho,D},

(2.6.33)

under the symmetry. Also by symmetry (method of images)
, we have

\mathcal{M}_{|\Omega/\rho|^{2},1}^{2\sqcup 4}\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{x}{ x}=\mathcal{M}_{|\Omega|^{2},1}^{2\sqcup 2^{*}}\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{x}{ x},

(2.6.34)

because both of them expresses the associated Dirichlet data on
\Sigma_{1}
of the solution of the Helmholtz boundary value problem over
\Omega
with Dirichlet data equal to
x
on both
\Sigma_{2}
,
\Sigma_{2}^{*}
and Neumann condition (zero normal derivative) on
\Sigma_{1}
. Thus,

LHS

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{2\sqcup 4\sqcup 1}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega/\rho|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{2\sqcup 4\sqcup 1}}(x,x,\varphi_{1})

(formula (
2.5.25
) backward)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{2\sqcup 4}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega/\rho|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{4}}}(x,x)\frac{\mathrm{d}\big((\mathcal{M}_{|\Omega/\rho|^{2},1}^{2\sqcup 4}[\begin{smallmatrix}x\\
x\end{smallmatrix}])_{*}\mu_{2\DN}^{\Sigma_{1},\Omega,D}\big)}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{1}}}(\varphi_{1})

(formula (
2.5.26
) forward)

\displaystyle=\frac{\detz(\DN_{|\Omega/\rho|^{2}}^{\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{4}})^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\detz(\DN_{|\Omega|^{2}}^{\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}^{*}})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{2\sqcup 2^{*}}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}^{*}}}(x,x)\frac{\mathrm{d}\big((\mathcal{M}_{|\Omega|^{2},1}^{2\sqcup 2^{*}}[\begin{smallmatrix}x\\
x\end{smallmatrix}])_{*}\mu_{2\DN}^{\Sigma_{1},\Omega,D}\big)}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{1}}}(\varphi_{1})

(#)

\displaystyle=\textrm{RHS}.

We see that with the help of Bayes principle we “glued away” the free end and we are reduced to the situation of Proposition
2.6.4
. Indeed, at step (#) we use Proposition
2.6.4
twice with respect to
|\Omega\cup_{1}\Omega^{*}|^{2}
which is
\Omega
“reflected twice” by first gluing
\Omega
with a reflected copy along
\Sigma_{1}
to get
\Omega\cup_{1}\Omega^{*}
, and then reflect and glue again along
\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}^{*}\sqcup\Sigma_{4}\sqcup\Sigma_{4}^{*}
, with
\Sigma_{4}
,
\Sigma_{4}^{*}
being the reflected copies of
\Sigma_{2}
,
\Sigma_{2}^{*}
across
\Sigma_{1}
. From this we get

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{2\sqcup 4}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega/\rho|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{4}}}(x,x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\detz(\DN_{|\Omega/\rho|^{2}}^{\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{4}})^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\detz(2\DN_{|\Omega\cup_{1}\Omega^{*}|^{2}}^{2\sqcup 2^{*}\sqcup 4\sqcup 4^{*}})^{\frac{1}{4}}}\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{2\sqcup 2^{*}\sqcup 4\sqcup 4^{*}}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega\cup_{1}\Omega^{*}|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{2\sqcup 2^{*}\sqcup 4\sqcup 4^{*}}}(x,x,x,x)\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\detz(\DN_{|\Omega/\rho|^{2}}^{\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{4}})^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\detz(\DN_{|\Omega|^{2}}^{\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}^{*}})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{2\sqcup 2^{*}}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}^{*}}}(x,x),

finishing the proof.
∎

Corollary 2.6.11

(free-end gluing for free field)
.

We have

\int\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{0}(\varphi,\varphi,\psi)\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{2}}(\varphi)\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma_{2}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}=\mathcal{A}_{\Omega/\rho}^{0}(\psi),

(2.6.35)

for almost every
\psi\sim\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{1}}
.

Proof.

The key point is to disintegrate
\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{0}(\varphi,\varphi,\psi)
into a part involving only
\varphi
, which one could “integrate out” cleanly, multiplied by a part independent of
\varphi
, using Proposition
2.6.10
. Indeed,

LHS

\displaystyle=\frac{\detz(\Delta_{|\Omega|^{2}}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{4}}}{\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma_{2}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma_{1}})^{-\frac{1}{4}}}\int\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{2\sqcup 2^{*}\sqcup 1}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{2\sqcup 2^{*}\sqcup 1}}(\varphi,\varphi,\psi)\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{2}}(\varphi)\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma_{2}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\detz(\DN_{|\Omega|^{2}}^{\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}^{*}})^{\frac{1}{4}}}{\detz(\DN_{|\Omega/\rho|^{2}}^{\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{4}})^{\frac{1}{4}}}\frac{\detz(\Delta_{|\Omega|^{2}}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{4}}}{\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma_{1}})^{-\frac{1}{4}}}\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{1}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega/\rho|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{1}}}(\psi)\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\underbrace{\int\frac{\mathrm{d}\big((\mathcal{M}_{|\Omega/\rho|^{2},2}^{1}\psi)_{*}\mu_{\DN}^{\Sigma_{2},\Omega/\rho,D}\big)}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{2}}}(\varphi)\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{2}}(\varphi)}_{=\penalty\ 1}

\displaystyle=\detz(\DN_{|\Omega/\rho|^{2}}^{\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{4}})^{-\frac{1}{4}}\detz(\Delta_{|\Omega|^{2}\setminus\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}^{*},D}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{4}}\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma_{1}})^{\frac{1}{4}}\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{1}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega/\rho|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{1}}}(\psi)\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}

(BFK for
|\Omega|^{2}
and
\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}^{*}
)

\displaystyle=\textrm{RHS},

(BFK for
|\Omega/\rho|^{2}
and
\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{4}
)

finishing the proof.
∎

We proceed to the interacting case.

Lemma 2.6.12
.

For fixed
\psi\in W^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma_{1})
, then the random fields

\phi\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\PI_{\Omega/\rho}^{1}\psi+\PI_{\Omega/\rho}^{2,D}\tau_{2}\phi_{\Omega/\rho}^{D},\quad\textrm{and}\quad\widetilde{\phi}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\PI_{\Omega}^{2\sqcup 2^{*}\sqcup 1}[\varphi,\varphi,\psi]

(2.6.36)

with
\phi_{\Omega/\rho}^{D}\sim\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega/\rho,D}
and
\varphi\sim(\mathcal{M}_{|\Omega/\rho|^{2},2}^{1}\psi)_{*}\mu_{\DN}^{\Sigma_{2},\Omega/\rho,D}
, follow the same law on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega^{\circ})
.

Proof.

Here
\Omega^{\circ}
is also
(\Omega/\rho)\setminus\Sigma_{2}
. Indeed, both
\phi
and
\widetilde{\phi}
solves the stochastic boundary value problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\Delta+m^{2})u=0&\textrm{in }\Omega^{\circ},\\
u|_{\Sigma_{2}}=u|_{\Sigma_{2}^{*}}\sim(\mathcal{M}_{|\Omega/\rho|^{2},2}^{1}\psi)_{*}\mu_{\DN}^{\Sigma_{2},\Omega/\rho,D},&\textrm{on }\Sigma_{2}\textrm{ or }\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}^{*}\\
u|_{\Sigma_{1}}=\psi,&\textrm{on }\Sigma_{1},\end{array}\right.

(2.6.37)

with equalities holding almost surely.
∎

Corollary 2.6.13

(free-end gluing for
P(\phi)
field)
.

In the situation as above, we have

\int\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{P}(\varphi,\varphi,\psi)\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{2}}(\varphi)\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma_{2}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}=\mathcal{A}_{\Omega/\rho}^{P}(\psi),

(2.6.38)

for almost every
\psi\sim\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{1}}
.

Proof.

Indeed,

\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{P}(\varphi,\varphi,\psi)=\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{0}(\varphi,\varphi,\psi)\int\textrm{e}^{-S_{\Omega}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}|\varphi,\varphi,\psi)}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}),

(2.6.39)

therefore, with the constants involving determinants working out in exactly the same way as Corollary
2.6.11
, one has

LHS

\displaystyle=\frac{\detz(\Delta_{|\Omega/\rho|^{2}}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{4}}}{\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma_{1}})^{\frac{1}{4}}}\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{1}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega/\rho|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{1}}}(\psi)\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\int\mathrm{d}\big((\mathcal{M}_{|\Omega/\rho|^{2},2}^{1}\psi)_{*}\mu_{\DN}^{\Sigma_{2},\Omega/\rho,D}\big)(\varphi)\int\textrm{e}^{-S_{\Omega}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}|\varphi,\varphi,\psi)}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}_{\Omega/\rho}^{0}(\psi)\int\textrm{e}^{-S_{\Omega/\rho}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}+(\phi_{\Omega/\rho}^{D})_{\Sigma_{2}}|\psi)}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}\otimes\mathrm{d}\big[(\PI_{\Omega/\rho}^{2,D}\tau_{2})_{*}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega/\rho,D})\big](\phi^{D}_{\Omega},(\phi_{\Omega/\rho}^{D})_{\Sigma_{2}})

(Lemma
2.6.12
)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}_{\Omega/\rho}^{0}(\psi)\int\textrm{e}^{-S_{\Omega/\rho}(\phi_{\Omega/\rho}^{D}|\psi)}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega/\rho,D}(\phi_{\Omega/\rho}^{D})=\textrm{RHS},

where the notation
(\phi_{\Omega/\rho}^{D})_{\Sigma_{2}}
is as in Proposition
2.5.8
. We arrive at the proof.
∎

Now let
\Omega_{12}\in\Mor(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2})
,
\Omega_{23}\in\Mor(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{3})
and consider the double
|\Omega_{23}\circ\Omega_{12}|^{2}=:|\Omega_{23}\Omega_{12}|^{2}
. In this case the result could be seen as a special case of the previous one, where
\Omega=\Omega_{12}\sqcup\Omega_{23}\in\Mor(\varnothing,\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}^{*}\sqcup\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}\sqcup\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}^{*}\sqcup\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{3})
has two disjoint components. We shall re-state these results without proofs.

Corollary 2.6.14
.

We have

\displaystyle\quad\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{1\sqcup 3}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega_{23}\Omega_{12}|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{1}\sqcup\Sigma_{3}}}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{3})\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\big((\mathcal{M}_{|\Omega_{23}\Omega_{12}|^{2},2}^{1\sqcup 3}[\begin{smallmatrix}\varphi_{1}\\
\varphi_{3}\end{smallmatrix}])_{*}\mu_{2\DN}^{\Sigma_{2},\Omega_{23}\Omega_{12},D}\big)}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{2}}}(x)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\quad\frac{\detz(\DN_{|\Omega_{23}\Omega_{12}|^{2}}^{\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{4}})^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\detz(2\DN_{\Omega_{12}}^{\Sigma_{2},N})^{\frac{1}{2}}\detz(2\DN_{\Omega_{23}}^{\Sigma_{2},N})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{\Sigma_{1}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega_{12}|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{1}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}}}(\varphi_{1},x)\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau_{\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{3}}(\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{|\Omega_{23}|^{2}})}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\Sigma_{3}}}(x,\varphi_{3}).\quad\Box

Corollary 2.6.15

(composition for
P(\phi)
field)
.

In the situation as above, we have

\int\mathcal{A}_{\Omega_{12}}^{P}(\psi_{1},\varphi)\mathcal{A}_{\Omega_{23}}^{P}(\varphi,\psi_{3})\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{2}}(\varphi)\detz(2\mathbf{D}_{\Sigma_{2}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}=\mathcal{A}_{\Omega_{23}\circ\Omega_{12}}^{P}(\psi_{1},\psi_{3}),

(2.6.40)

for almost every
(\psi_{1},\psi_{3})\sim\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma_{1}\sqcup\Sigma_{3}}
.
\Box

Periodic Cover = Spin Chain

Geometric setting

Our set-up corresponds to example 1 in Bergeron
[
Ber17
]
. Let
M
be a closed oriented Riemannian surface
5
5

5

In particular its
\mathbb{Z}
-homology groups are non-torsion.
of genus
g\geqslant 1
and
\Sigma\subset M
an embedded
primitive
closed geodesic whose
\mathbb{Z}
-homology class is non-trivial (exists by a classical theorem of E. Cartan). Necessarily,
\Sigma
is nondissecting.
6
6

6

If
\Sigma
dissects
M
into
M_{+}^{\circ}\sqcup M_{-}^{\circ}
, then
\Sigma=\partial M_{+}
; now closed 1-forms integrate to zero over
\Sigma
by Stokes theorem, thus
\Sigma
is null-homologous via de Rham’s theorem. Alternatively, note
\partial:H_{2}(M_{+},\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow H_{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})
in the exact sequence of the pair
(M_{+},\Sigma)
is surjective, producing the fundamental class.
We consider the covering space
M_{\infty}^{\Sigma}\longrightarrow M
over
M
given by a (normal) subgroup
\ker\rho
of the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(M)
where
\rho
is the map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho:\pi_{1}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Ab}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I(-{,}{[}\Sigma{]})}

(2.7.1)

where the first is Abelianization and
I(-,[\Sigma])
is the
oriented intersection number
7
7

7

I([\gamma],[\Sigma])=D(D^{-1}[\gamma]\smile D^{-1}[\Sigma])=\int_{\gamma}\eta_{\Sigma}\in\mathbb{Z}
, where
D^{-1}
is the Poincaré dual map and
\eta_{\Sigma}
is a smooth bump 1-form supported in a tubular neighborhood of
\Sigma
such that
\int_{\Sigma}\alpha=\int_{M}\alpha\wedge\eta_{\Sigma}
for any 1-form
\alpha
.
with
\Sigma
, which is surjective (since
\Sigma
is primitive). In other words, we put
M_{\infty}^{\Sigma}=\ker\rho\setminus\widetilde{M}
where
\widetilde{M}
is the universal cover of
M
and
\ker\rho
acts on
\widetilde{M}
as deck transformations. Equip
M_{\infty}^{\Sigma}
with the covering metric (thus deck transformations act by isometries).

Geometrically,
M_{\infty}^{\Sigma}
can be understood as first cutting
M
along
\Sigma
and obtaining the surface
\Omega:=M\setminus\Sigma
with boundaries
\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}\sqcup\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
where
\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}\cong\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}\cong\Sigma
, and gluing
\Omega
periodically where each
\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
is glued to the “next”
\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
. Indeed, the class
[\gamma]\in\pi_{1}(M)
of a loop
\gamma
is in
\ker\rho
iff
I([\gamma],[\Sigma])=0
; in other words, these loops are exactly those which are “not cut”, i.e. lifts to a loop on
M_{\infty}^{\Sigma}
, and loops which do intersect
\Sigma
are lifted to segments whose end points are related by a deck transformation, i.e. they are “cut”.

Now, for
N\in\mathbb{N}
, compose
\rho
further with the mod
N
map
\mathbb{Z}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}=:\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}
and denote it by
\rho_{N}
, and let the covering space of
M
corresponding to
\ker\rho_{N}
be
M_{N}^{\Sigma}
. Since
\ker\rho\subset\ker\rho_{N}
,
M_{\infty}^{\Sigma}
also covers
M_{N}^{\Sigma}
. Geometrically, this corresponds to closing the surface after gluing
N
copies of
\Omega
— loops that intersect
\Sigma

N
-times are now lifted to a “big loop” in
M_{N}^{\Sigma}
.

Figure 2.3
:
periodic covering and cyclic covering

Remark 2.7.1
.

We also say that the sequence of covers
(M_{N}^{\Sigma})_{N}

converges
to
M_{\infty}^{\Sigma}
.

That
\Omega:=M\setminus\Sigma
should be understood for what follows.

Continued Introduction of Spin Chain Example

Here we continue our discussion of the circular spin chain proposed in section
2.1.1
, in particular the equation (
2.1.6
). More generally, one can insert “nice” functionals
F_{1}
, …,
F_{k}
in between at the sites
1\leqslant i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\leqslant N
, then

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}F_{k}(\sigma(i_{k}))\cdots F_{1}(\sigma(i_{1}))\textrm{e}^{-S(\sigma)}\mathrm{d}^{N}\sigma=\ttr_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\left(T^{N+1-i_{k}}F_{k}T^{i_{k}-i_{k-1}}\cdots F_{1}T^{i_{1}-1}\right),

(2.7.2)

where
F_{j}
are thought of as multiplication operators. The evaluation

\left.\begin{array}[]{rcl}F_{k}\otimes\cdots\otimes F_{1}&\longmapsto&\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}(N)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}F_{k}(\sigma(i_{k}))\cdots F_{1}(\sigma(i_{1}))\textrm{e}^{-S(\sigma)}\mathrm{d}^{N}\sigma\\
&=&\displaystyle\frac{\ttr_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\left(T^{N+1-i_{k}}F_{k}T^{i_{k}-i_{k-1}}\cdots F_{1}T^{i_{1}-1}\right)}{\ttr_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}(T^{N})}\end{array}\right.

(2.7.3)

is said to define a
Gibbs state
of our spin chain on
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
. Alternatively this is the expected value functional under the discrete Gibbs measure (
2.1.2
).

Definition 2.7.1
.

We say that a Gibbs state exists in the
thermodynamic limit
if the second expression in (
2.7.3
) tends to a limit as
N\to\infty
for bounded functionals
F_{1}
, …,
F_{k}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
.

We can see from (
2.1.4
) that the operator
T
is not just bounded, smoothing, but its kernel is strictly positive. Such an operator has a special property which we call the
Perron-Frobenius
property, referring to the consequence of proposition
2.7.1
below. In particular, this would ensure that the thermodynamic limit does exist, as we shall see in corollaries
2.7.3
,
2.7.4
, and
2.7.5
.

As announced in the introduction,
Corollary
2.7.3
could be equivalently understood as saying that for compactly supported observables
(F,G)
, and
(\tau\sigma)(i):=\sigma(i+1)
the shift operator, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[F(\tau^{k}\sigma)G(\sigma)]=\mathbb{E}[F]\mathbb{E}[G]+\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{k})

with
\alpha<1
the same as in corollary
2.7.3
. Here the expected value should be thought of as coming from a Gibbs measure on the infinite path space
\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{Z}}
over
\mathbb{Z}
. Indeed, by definition
2.7.1
, this is exactly the vague limit of the finite dimensional (periodic) Gibbs measures over
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
. We say that with respect to this Gibbs measure the shift operator is
exponentially mixing
which is exactly saying the
P(\phi)_{2}
Gibbs state constructed has a
mass gap
.

With transfer operator being Perron-Frobenius, the partition functions as in (
2.1.2
) also enjoy explicit asymptotics. We will explore a consequence in the case of periodic surfaces in the last section.

Example 2.7.1
.

In the case
P(\sigma)=m^{2}\sigma^{2}
, the spin chain is the discrete massive GFF. We have an exact formula for the partition function
\mathcal{Z}(N)=\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}(1+m^{2}-\cos(2\pi\frac{k}{N}))^{-\frac{1}{2}}

hence

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{N}\log\left(\mathcal{Z}(N)\right)=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\log(1+m^{2}-\cos(2\pi x))\mathrm{d}x.

Perron-Frobenius Property and Gibbs State

A large part of this section follow from the “properties of a Perron-Frobenius operator”. But we include them here as they form an integral part of the discussion of physical phenomena.

Definition 2.7.2
.

An operator
A
on
L^{2}(Q,\mu)
of some measure space
(Q,\mu)
has
strictly positive kernel
if for any nonnegative
F\in L^{2}(Q,\mu)
such that
\left\lVert F\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\neq 0
we have
AF>0
almost surely.

Proposition 2.7.1

(Perron-Frobenius,
[
GJ87
]
page 51)
.

If
A
on
L^{2}(Q,\mu)
has strictly positive kernel, and
\lambda=\left\lVert A\right\rVert
is an eigenvalue of
A
, then
\lambda
is simple, and the corresponding eigenvector can be chosen to be strictly positive almost surely.
\Box

By our definition of the amplitudes
\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}
as the square-root of the Radon-Nikodym density between two mutually absolutely continuous positive finite measures (
\textrm{e}^{-S_{\Omega}}
is almost surely positive since
S_{\Omega}
is a real-valued random variable, recall also remark
2.2.6
that the zeta-determinants are positive), we get immediately

Lemma 2.7.2
.

For any traceable cobordism
\Omega\in\Mor(\Sigma,\Sigma)
, the Segal transfer operator
U_{\Omega}:L^{2}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma),\mu_{2\mathbf{D}})\longrightarrow L^{2}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma),\mu_{2\mathbf{D}})
has strictly positive kernel.
\Box

We deduce that
U_{\Omega}
has a simple top eigenvalue
\lambda_{0}=\left\lVert U_{\Omega}\right\rVert
spanned by a normalized, almost surely strictly positive eigenvector
\Omega_{0}\in L^{2}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma),\mu_{2\mathbf{D}})
. Alternatively speaking
U_{\Omega}
has a
spectral gap
. We get

Corollary 2.7.3
.

Denote
\widehat{U}_{\Omega}:=\lambda_{0}^{-1}U_{\Omega}
, let
\lambda_{1}
be the eigenvalue of
U_{\Omega}
with next largest modulus, thus
\lambda_{0}>|\lambda_{1}|
, and put
\alpha:=|\lambda_{1}|/\lambda_{0}<1
. Then for any
F
,
G\in L^{2}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma),\mu_{2\mathbf{D}})
, we have

\big|\big\langle F,\widehat{U}_{\Omega}^{N}G\big\rangle-\big\langle F,\Omega_{0}\big\rangle\big\langle\Omega_{0},G\big\rangle\big|\leqslant\alpha^{N}\left\lVert F\right\rVert\left\lVert G\right\rVert.

(2.7.4)

Proof.

Note
\langle F,\widehat{U}_{\Omega}^{N}G\rangle-\left\langle F,\Omega_{0}\right\rangle\left\langle\Omega_{0},G\right\rangle=\langle\Pi_{0}^{\perp}F,\widehat{U}_{\Omega}^{N}\Pi_{0}^{\perp}G\rangle
where
\Pi_{0}^{\perp}
is the orthogonal projection onto the complement of
\spn\{\Omega_{0}\}
, where
\widehat{U}_{\Omega}
has norm
\alpha<1
.
∎

The above corollary is exactly stating that the Gibbs state we defined has a mass gap, in perfect analogy with
1
D spin chains with local interactions which are well known to have no phase transitions.

Corollary 2.7.4
.

We have

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\log\ttr(U_{\Omega}^{N})=\log\lambda_{0}.

(2.7.5)

Proof.

Without loss of generality let
N\geqslant 2
so each
U_{\Omega}^{N}
is trace class. On one hand

\ttr(U_{\Omega}^{N})\leqslant\big\lVert U_{\Omega}^{2}\big\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}\big\lVert U_{\Omega}^{N-2}\big\rVert\leqslant\big\lVert U_{\Omega}^{2}\big\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}\lambda_{0}^{N-2}.

(2.7.6)

On the other, we decompose
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}=\spn\{\Omega_{0}\}\oplus\spn\{\Omega_{0}\}^{\perp}
where

\displaystyle\ttr(U_{\Omega}^{N})

\displaystyle=\lambda_{0}^{N}+\ttr(U_{\Omega}^{N}|_{\spn\{\Omega_{0}\}^{\perp}})

\displaystyle\geqslant\lambda_{0}^{N}-\big\lVert U_{\Omega}^{2}|_{\spn\{\Omega_{0}\}^{\perp}}\big\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}|\lambda_{1}|^{N-2}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{0}^{N}\left(1-C_{1}\alpha^{N-2}\lambda_{0}^{-2}\right).

This and (
2.7.6
) gives the result after taking
N\to\infty
.
∎

Corollary
2.7.3
and the proof of corollary
2.7.4
implies

Corollary 2.7.5
.

For any bounded operator
F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma})
we have

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\ttr(U_{\Omega}^{N-L}F)}{\ttr(U_{\Omega}^{N})}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{0}^{L}}\left\langle\Omega_{0},F\Omega_{0}\right\rangle=\frac{\left\langle\Omega_{0},F\Omega_{0}\right\rangle}{\left\langle\Omega_{0},U_{\Omega}^{L}\Omega_{0}\right\rangle}.

(2.7.7)

Proof.

Remember that
N\gg L
. Thus by
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}=\spn\{\Omega_{0}\}\oplus\spn\{\Omega_{0}\}^{\perp}
and
[
Sim05
]
theorem 2.14 we have

\lambda_{0}^{-N+L}\ttr\big(U_{\Omega}^{N-L}F\big)=\big\langle\Omega_{0},\widehat{U}_{\Omega}^{N-L}F\Omega_{0}\big\rangle+\ttr\big(\widehat{U}_{\Omega}^{N-L}F|_{\spn\{\Omega_{0}\}^{\perp}}\big)

(2.7.8)

with

\big|\ttr\big(\widehat{U}_{\Omega}^{N-L}F|_{\spn\{\Omega_{0}\}^{\perp}}\big)\big|\leqslant\left\lVert F\right\rVert\big\lVert\widehat{U}_{\Omega}^{N-L}|_{\spn\{\Omega_{0}\}^{\perp}}\big\rVert\to 0.

(2.7.9)

Since
\langle\Omega_{0},\widehat{U}_{\Omega}^{N-L}F\Omega_{0}\rangle\to\langle\Omega_{0},F\Omega_{0}\rangle
by corollary
2.7.3
and
\ttr(U_{\Omega}^{N})\asymp\lambda_{0}^{N}
by corollary
2.7.4
, we obtain the result.
∎

We arrive at the conclusion that, for functionals
F_{1}
, …,
F_{k}\in L^{\infty}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma),\mu_{2\mathbf{D}})
and integers
1\leqslant i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}
, the evaluation

\left.\begin{array}[]{rcl}F_{k}\otimes\cdots\otimes F_{1}&\longmapsto&\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\ttr\big(U_{\Omega}^{N+1-i_{k}}F_{k}U_{\Omega}^{i_{k}-i_{k-1}}\cdots F_{1}U_{\Omega}^{i_{1}-1}\big)}{\ttr\big(U_{\Omega}^{N}\big)}\\
&=&\displaystyle\frac{\big\langle\Omega_{0},F_{k}U_{\Omega}^{i_{k}-i_{k-1}}\cdots F_{1}U_{\Omega}^{i_{1}-1}\Omega_{0}\big\rangle}{\big\langle\Omega_{0},U_{\Omega}^{i_{k}-1}\Omega_{0}\big\rangle}\end{array}\right.

(2.7.10)

defines a Gibbs state in the thermodynamic limit on a
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma)
-valued
\mathbb{Z}
-spin chain.

Remark 2.7.2
.

In fact, a valid functional
F\in L^{\infty}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma),\mu_{2\mathbf{D}})
could be given by

F(\psi)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\int\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}^{0}(\psi,\varphi)\mathrm{d}\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma}(\varphi)\int\widetilde{F}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega},\psi,\varphi)\textrm{e}^{-S_{\Omega}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}|\psi,\varphi)}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}(\phi^{D}_{\Omega}),

(2.7.11)

for
\widetilde{F}\in L^{\infty}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega^{\circ}),\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Omega,D}\otimes\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma})
, with
\mu_{2\mathbf{D}}^{\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma}
considered as the induced measure on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega^{\circ})
via
\PI_{\Omega}^{\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma}
. Hence the Gibbs state actually extends to the continuum
M_{\infty}^{\Sigma}
.

Asymptotic of Partition Function

Now we assume the surface
\Omega\in\Mor(\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
be
reflection symmetric
, which means
\Omega=\widetilde{\Omega}^{*}\circ\widetilde{\Omega}
for some
\widetilde{\Omega}\in\Mor(\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\boldsymbol{\Sigma^{\prime}})
, in the notations of Lemma
2.6.1
. In other words there is an isometric involution
\Theta:\Omega\longrightarrow\Omega
whose fixed point set is exactly
\Sigma^{\prime}
, and exchanges the two components of
\partial\Omega
. In this case
U_{\Omega}=U_{\widetilde{\Omega}^{*}}\circ U_{\widetilde{\Omega}}
and
[
Sim15
]
theorem 3.8.5 applies, namely
\ttr(U_{\Omega})=\ttr_{\rho}(U_{\Omega})
, as well as for
U_{\Omega}^{N}
. From Corollary
2.6.8
and Corollary
2.7.4
we then deduce that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\log(Z_{M_{N}^{\Sigma}})=\log\lambda_{0}.

(2.7.12)

Thus we have proved theorem
2.2
. This applies in particular to the free case where
Z_{M_{N}^{\Sigma}}=\detz(\Delta_{M_{N}^{\Sigma}}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}
.

Remark 2.7.3
.

There arises the interesting question of how
\lambda_{0}
(or
\log\lambda_{0}
) would depend on the geometry of
\Omega
. Very crudely one would expect
\log\lambda_{0}\propto\vol(\Omega)
, since in our case the corresponding
\widetilde{\lambda}_{0}
for
N\Omega
(
N
copies of
\Omega
glued) is just
\lambda_{0}^{N}
. A more precise formula in the general, non-periodic case seems desirable.

Chapter 3
Spectral Cut-off, Locality, and Reflection Positivity

This Chapter is adapted form
[
BDF+23
]
joint with Ismael Bailleul, Nguyen Viet Dang, Léonard Ferdinand, Gaëtan Leclerc.

Introduction and context

Markov property and reflection positivity of a random field

Let
(M,g)
be a smooth, closed, compact Riemannian manifold or
M=\mathbb{R}\times\Sigma
where
\Sigma
is a complete Riemannian manifold endowed with the product metric
\mathrm{d}t^{2}+g_{\Sigma}
. We denote by
\Delta_{g}
the positive Laplace-Beltrami operator acting on
C^{\infty}(M)
. In case
M
is compact, we write
(e_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\mathrm{spec}(\Delta_{g})}
for the
L^{2}
basis of eigenfunctions of
\Delta_{g}
and

\mathrm{E}_{\leqslant\Lambda}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathrm{span}(e_{\lambda})_{\lambda\leqslant\Lambda}\subset C^{\infty}(M)\,.

The massive Gaussian Free Field (GFF in the sequel)
\Phi
of law
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}
is defined
on
M
compact as
the random series

\Phi\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sum_{\lambda\in\mathrm{spec}(\Delta_{g})}\frac{c_{\lambda}}{\sqrt{1+\lambda}}e_{\lambda}\,,

where the coefficients
c_{\lambda}
are independent, identically distributed, random variables with Gaussian distribution
\CB{N}(0,1)
. On a cylinder
M=\mathbb{R}\times\Sigma
the massive Gaussian free field is defined as the unique Gaussian process
\Phi
indexed by
H^{-1}(M)
with covariance

\mathbb{E}\big[\Phi(f)\Phi(h)\big]=\left\langle f,h\right\rangle_{H^{-1}(M)}.

For every
\varepsilon>0
, one can realize the Gaussian process
\Phi
as a random variable with values in
H^{({2-d})/{2}-\varepsilon}(M)
.
This follows essentially from the Weyl law by an argument analogous to
[
BP25
]
Theorem 1.45.
In the sequel, we thus fix
\varepsilon>0
and work with the canonical probability space
\big(H^{(2-d)/2-\varepsilon}(M),\CB{O},\mu_{\text{GFF}}\big)
, where
\CB{O}
stands for the Borel
\sigma
-algebra of
\Omega=H^{(2-d)/2-\varepsilon}(M)
. One can take
\Phi(\omega)=\omega
for every
\omega\in\Omega
.

Definition 3.1.1
.

In case of
M
compact or a cylinder,
\Delta_{g}:C^{\infty}_{c}(M)\subset L^{2}(M)\rightarrow L^{2}(M)
is essentially self-adjoint, and therefore admits a well-defined functional calculus.
For
\Lambda>0
we write

\Pi_{\Lambda}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}1_{[0,\Lambda]}(\Delta_{g})

for the sharp spectral projector. In particular, when
M
is compact, one has
\Pi_{\Lambda}:\CB D^{\prime}(M)\rightarrow\mathrm{E}_{\leqslant\Lambda}
. Note that
\Pi_{\Lambda}
is self-adjoint. Finally, we define the spectrally cut-off GFF
\Phi_{\Lambda}
GFF
by

\Phi_{\Lambda}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\Pi_{\Lambda}(\Phi)\,,

which is a random smooth function.

Except in Section
3.3.1
, throughout the rest of the article, without loss of generality, in our choice of cut-off free field we could replace
\Pi_{\Lambda}
by
\Psi(\Delta_{g}/\Lambda)
where
\Psi
is smooth and compactly supported.

We will use the notion of smooth functionals on a locally convex space such as
\CB{D}^{\prime}(M),C^{\infty}(M)
or on some Sobolev space
H^{s}(M)
as discussed in
[
BDLR21
]
, along with the notion of support of a smooth functional. Both notions are recalled hereafter, we start with smooth functionals :

Definition 3.1.2
.

Let
E
be a locally convex space and
U\subset E
an open subset. A function
F:U\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is
smooth
if for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
x\in U
, every
(h_{1},\dots,h_{k})\in E^{k}
, the limit

\frac{\partial^{k}F\big(x+t_{1}h_{1}+\dots+t_{k}h_{k}\big)}{\partial t_{1}\cdots\partial t_{k}}\Big|_{(t_{1},\dots,t_{k})=0}=\mathrm{D}^{k}_{x}F\big(h_{1},\cdots,h_{k}\big)

exists and
\mathrm{D}^{k}F:U\times E^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is jointly continuous as a function of its arguments and linear in
h_{1},\dots,h_{k}
.

Next, we discuss the notion of support:

Definition 3.1.3
.

Take
E
to be
C^{\infty}(M)
,
\CB{D}^{\prime}(M)
, or some Sobolev space
H^{s}(M)
, and let
U\subset E
be an open subset.
The
support of some smooth functional

F:U\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, denoted by
\mathrm{supp}(F)
, is the set containing those points
x\in M
such that for every neighbourhood
A_{x}\subset M
of
x
, there exists
\phi,\psi\in U
that verify
F(\phi)\neq F(\psi)
and
\mathrm{supp}(\phi-\psi)\subset A_{x}
.

We next introduce the notion of reflection positive manifold following the work of Jaffe & Ritter
[
JR07
,
JR07a
,
JR08
]
:

Definition 3.1.4
.

We say that the
d
dimensional manifold
M
is
reflection positive
if there exists an
isometric involution

\Theta\colon M\rightarrow M
which admits as invariant subset a submanifold
\Sigma
of dimension
d-1
such that
M\setminus\Sigma
is the disjoint union of two open manifolds
M_{+}
and
M_{-}
that both verify
\partial\overline{M_{+}}=\partial\overline{M_{-}}=\Sigma
. One can therefore think of
M
as the disjoint union

\displaystyle M=M_{+}\cup\Sigma\cup M_{-}\,,\,\Theta\upharpoonright\Sigma=\mathrm{Id}_{\Sigma}\,,\,\Theta(M_{\pm})=M_{\mp}\,.

\Sigma
M_{-}
M_{+}
\Sigma
\mathbb{R}
\Theta

Figure 3.1
:
A cylinder
\mathbb{R}\times\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\Sigma}
and a reflection positive manifold
M=\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{M_{+}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\;\cup\;}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\Sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\;\cup\;}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{M_{-}}
.

For clarity we write below
\overline{M}_{\pm}
for
\overline{M_{\pm}}
and refer to Figure
3.1
. The spaces of constant curvature
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\mathbb{S}^{d}
, and
\mathbb{H}^{d}
are reflection positive. In the sequel, we will mostly be interested in the cases
M=\mathbb{R}\times\Sigma
and
M=\mathbb{S}^{d}
, for which we precise the setting. The cylinder
\mathbb{R}\times\Sigma
is reflection positive with respect to
\{0\}\times\Sigma
. The sphere
\mathbb{S}^{d}
is reflection positive with respect to any of its equators.

Whenever a manifold
M
is reflection positive with isometric involution
\Theta
, the map
\Theta
acts via pull-back on
C^{\infty}(M)
and
\CB{D}^{\prime}(M)
and hence on functionals on
M
and
\CB{D}^{\prime}(M)
-valued random variables. Note that for any distribution
\omega\in\CB D^{\prime}(M)
,

\Pi_{\Lambda}(\omega)\upharpoonright{\overline{M}_{-}}=\big(\Pi_{\Lambda}(\omega)\circ\Theta\big)\upharpoonright{\overline{M}_{+}}=\Pi_{\Lambda}(\omega\circ\Theta)\upharpoonright{\overline{M}_{+}}\,.

(3.1.1)

Let
A
be a closed subset of
M
. We denote by
W^{-1}_{A}(M)
the subset of elements in the Sobolev space
W^{-1}(M)
which are supported in
A
. Let
\Psi
be a random distribution defined on our probability space
\Omega
, with law
\mu_{\Psi}
and associated expectation operator
\mathbb{E}_{\Psi}[\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to5.74991pt{\hfil\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{\lower 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\hfil}}{\hbox to5.74991pt{\hfil\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{\lower 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\hfil}}{\hbox to5.28671pt{\hfil\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{\lower 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\hfil}}{\hbox to5.1909pt{\hfil\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{\lower 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\hfil}}}]
. We introduce a sub
\sigma
-algebra of
\CB{O}

\displaystyle\sigma(\Psi;A)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sigma\big(\Psi(f);f\in W^{-1}_{A}(M)\big)\,.

We implicitly assume here that we only consider those functions
f\in W^{-1}_{A}(M)
for which
\Psi(f)
is well-defined. One can think of
\sigma(\Psi;A)
as the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the observables that are only sensitive to the fluctuation of the field
\Psi
on the set
A
.

Definition 3.1.5
.

A random process
\Psi
as above has the
Markov property
if for
any
closed subsets
A,B
of
M
such that
A^{\circ}\cap B=\varnothing
, namely
B
does not intersect the interior of
A
(Figure
3.2
below), and any random variable
F\in L^{2}
that is
\sigma(\Psi;B)
-measurable, one has

\mathbb{E}\big[F|\sigma(\Psi;A)\big]=\mathbb{E}\big[F|\sigma(\Psi;\partial A)\big].

A
B
\partial A

Figure 3.2
:
Two closed subsets
A
and
B
of
M
such that
A^{\circ}\cap B=\varnothing
.

The Markov property was introduced by Nelson in
[
Nel73b
]
,
[
Nel73
]
(pp. 224-225). He also proved that
\mu_{\text{GFF}}
verifies this property
[
Nel73
]
(pp. 225).

Definition 3.1.6
.

A random process
\Psi
on a reflection positive manifold
M
is
reflection positive
if for any
\mathbb{C}
-valued function
F
in
L^{2}\big(\CB{D}^{\prime}(M),\sigma(\Psi;\overline{M}_{+}),\mu_{\Psi}\big)
it holds

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Psi}\big[(\overline{\Theta F})F\big]\geqslant 0\,.

Reflection positivity is one of the axioms introduced by Osterwalder and Schrader
[
OS73
]
; it is a crucial condition to recover a Lorentzian Quantum Field Theory from a Euclidean Quantum Field Theory.

Lastly we indicate the relation between the Markov property and reflection positivity. Let
M
be a reflection positive manifold with reflection
\Theta
and decomposition
M=M_{+}\cup\Sigma\cup M_{-}
as in Definition
3.1.4
. The random process
\Psi
as above with law
\mu_{\Psi}
is called
reflection invariant
if
\mathbb{E}_{\Psi}[\overline{F}G]=\mathbb{E}_{\Psi}[\overline{\Theta F}\Theta G]
for any
F
,
G\in L^{2}(\CB{D}^{\prime}(M),\mu_{\Psi})
.

Lemma 3.1.1

(
[
Dim04
]
, Theorem 2)
.

On a reflection positive Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
, a random field
\Psi
indexed by
W^{-1}(M)
that is reflection invariant and has the Markov property is reflection positive.

Proof.

First observe that if
G\in L^{2}\big(\CB{D}^{\prime}(M),\sigma(\Psi;\Sigma),\mu_{\Psi}\big)
then
\Theta G=G
, since functionals of the form
\varphi(\Psi(f_{1}),\cdots,\Psi(f_{n}))
, for
f_{j}\in W^{-1}_{\Sigma}(M)
,
\varphi\in\CB{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, are dense in
L^{2}\big(\CB{D}^{\prime}(M),\sigma(\Psi;\Sigma),\mu_{\Psi}\big)
(
[
Sim74
]
Lemma I.5), and,

\Theta\cdot\varphi(\Psi(f_{1}),\cdots)=\varphi(\Theta\Psi(f_{1}),\cdots)=\varphi(\Psi(\Theta f_{1}),\cdots)=\varphi(\Psi(f_{1}),\cdots)\,,

(3.1.2)

as each
f_{j}\in W^{-1}_{\Sigma}(M)
is invariant by
\Theta
. This with reflection invariance tells that
\mathbb{E}_{\Psi}[H|\sigma(\Psi;\Sigma)]=\mathbb{E}_{\Psi}[\Theta H|\sigma(\Psi;\Sigma)]
for any
H\in L^{2}
. Indeed, for any
G\in L^{2}\big(\CB{D}^{\prime}(M),\sigma(\Psi;\Sigma),\mu_{\Psi}\big)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Psi}[\mathbb{E}_{\Psi}[\Theta H|\sigma(\Psi;\Sigma)]\cdot G]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\Psi}[\mathbb{E}_{\Psi}[\Theta H\cdot\Theta G|\sigma(\Psi;\Sigma)]]=\mathbb{E}_{\Psi}[\Theta H\cdot\Theta G]

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{reflection}}{=}\mathbb{E}_{\Psi}[HG]=\mathbb{E}_{\Psi}[\mathbb{E}_{\Psi}[H|\sigma(\Psi;\Sigma)]\cdot G].

Finally we proceed with the main argument: pick
F\in L^{2}\big(\CB{D}^{\prime}(M),\sigma(\Psi;\overline{M}_{+}),\mu_{\Psi}\big)
. It is clear that the functional
\Theta F
is then
\sigma(\Psi;\overline{M}_{-})
-measurable. Hence we have

\mathbb{E}_{\Psi}[\overline{\Theta F}\cdot F]=\mathbb{E}_{\Psi}[\mathbb{E}_{\Psi}[\overline{\Theta F}|\sigma(\Psi;\overline{M}_{+})]\cdot F]\overset{\mathrm{Markov}}{=}\mathbb{E}_{\Psi}[\mathbb{E}_{\Psi}[\overline{\Theta F}|\sigma(\Psi;\Sigma)]\cdot F]=\mathbb{E}_{\Psi}\big|\mathbb{E}_{\Psi}[F|\sigma(\Psi;\Sigma)]\big|^{2}\geqslant 0,

proving the result.
∎

The regularized
\Phi^{4}_{3}
measure built from the cut-off interaction

The
\Phi^{4}_{3}
measure
\nu
is a probability measure on
\CB D^{\prime}(M)
with
(M,g,\Theta)
a closed three dimensional reflection positive manifold that heuristically reads as

\displaystyle\nu(\mathrm{d}\Phi)\propto e^{-c\|\Phi\|^{4}_{L^{4}(M)}}\,\mu_{\text{GFF}}(\mathrm{d}\Phi)\,.

(3.1.3)

We use the non-conventional notation
c
for the coupling constant. Since the dimension of
M
is greater then or equal to 2 the GFF is not supported on
L^{p}
functions and the
L^{4}
norm of
\Phi
is almost surely infinite. The formal expression (
3.1.3
) is thus meaningless. The probability measure
\nu
can therefore only be defined as the weak limit of a sequence of approximations
(\nu_{\rho,\Lambda})_{\Lambda\geqslant 0}
. A choice which is often made is to consider

\displaystyle\nu_{\rho,\Lambda}(\mathrm{d}\Phi)\propto e^{-c\|\rho\Phi_{\Lambda}\|^{4}_{L^{4}(M)}-ca_{\Lambda}\|\rho\Phi_{\Lambda}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(M)}}\,\mu_{\text{GFF}}(\mathrm{d}\Phi)\,,

(3.1.4)

where
\rho\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M)
is identically equal to
1
on a large compact set of
M
and
(a_{\Lambda})_{\Lambda\geqslant 0}
is a suitably chosen sequence of real number that is divergent at large
\Lambda
.

On
M
, the
\Phi^{4}_{3}
measure
\nu
is a good starting point to construct a EQFT, provided
M
is reflection positive and
\nu
is proved to be reflection positive too. When
\nu
is constructed as a limit of a sequence of approximations, reflection positivity of
\nu
is in general obtained by showing that the measures
\nu_{\rho,\Lambda}
are reflection positive for every
\Lambda
, in which case
\nu
inherits the reflection positivity property of its approximations.

However, in any attempt to prove that property for
\nu_{\rho,\Lambda}
, we quickly face a problem that comes from the following fact, which is proved in Section
3.2
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
M
be a smooth complete Riemannian manifold of dimension
d
. Let
A\subsetneq M
be a closed subset with non-empty interior, and
G
a smooth functional on
C^{\infty}(M)
such that the random variable
G\circ\Pi_{\Lambda}(\Phi)
is
\sigma(\Phi;A)
-measurable. Then the function
G\circ\Pi_{\Lambda}
on
H^{{(2-d)/}{2}-\varepsilon}(M)
is constant.

This fact prevents us from transfering the reflection positivity property of the GFF to its spectrally cutoff version
\Phi_{\Lambda}
. As a matter of fact, we prove in Theorem
3.4
in Section
3.3.3
that the regularized
\Phi^{4}_{3}
measures
\nu_{\rho,\Lambda}
are not reflection positive for a small enough coupling constant
c>0
in (
3.1.4
). Moreover we give hereafter three arguments that show that the cut-off free field
\Phi_{\Lambda}
can by no means be neither Markov nor reflection positive, neither on cylinders like
\mathbb{R}^{d}
nor on compact manifolds. This also confirms that any approach involving spectral cut-offs is unlikely to be efficient in order to prove the reflection positivity of the
\Phi^{4}_{3}
measure.

To conclude this introduction, we would like to mention several works
where the authors are able to construct reflection positive interacting EQFT measures.
In the seminal work of Glimm-Jaffe, RP of the regularized theory comes from the RP property of the lattice approximations
[
GJ87
]
. The Wick rotation of the
P(\Phi)_{2}
model on the cylinder and the sphere was first treated in the seminal articles
[
Hoe74
,
FHN75
]
. We also refer to
[
GJ05
,
GJ05a
,
BJM23
]
for detailed constructions of some quantum fields in 2 space-time dimensions.

In
[
Abd18
]
(pp. 237), the author suggests to mollify the field by convolution with some compactly supported function and put a position dependent coupling in front of the interaction
\widetilde{g}_{r}\phi^{4}
where the coupling function
\widetilde{g}_{r}
, which depends on the cut–off
r>0
, vanishes in some neighborhood of the reflection hyperplane. This ensures that the regularized measures are RP. To implement this rigorously on say the
\Phi^{4}_{3}
model, the difficulty would be to show that one recovers the same EQFT measure as limit EQFT measure where the coupling function is constant.
In the very interesting
recent preprint
[
DDJ23
]
, the authors use a very similar strategy to construct a reflection positive
P(\Phi)_{2}
measure on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Finally, a similar strategy is used by the last author
[
Lin24
]
to prove that the
P(\Phi)_{2}
theory on every Riemannian surface satisfies both the spatial Markov property and the Segal gluing axioms which is stronger than just RP.

Organization and Comments

We prove Theorem
3.1
in Section
3.2
. This somehow shows that the spatial Markov property for the cut-off GFF is not well-posed since we prove that the only
\sigma(\Phi;\overline{M}_{+})
-measurable random variables of the form
G(\Phi_{\Lambda})
where
G
is smooth are constants. In Section
3.3.1
we give a counter-example showing that the cut-off free field on the cylinder
\mathbb{R}\times\Sigma
is not reflection positive, by constructing a function on which the bilinear form associated to the cut-off covariance acts negatively, see Theorem
3.2
below. In Section
3.3.2
we show that the cut-off free field on compact manifolds also has a covariance which is not reflection positive, by constructing a counter-example, see Theorem
3.3
below. Given Lemma
3.1.1
the results of these sections show that the cut-off regularized Gaussian free field does not have the Markov property in these settings.

Remark 3.1.1
.

We point out that we may also see that the cut-off GFF cannot be reflection positive non-constructi- vely, using the Källén–Lehmann representation (
[
GJ87
]
Theorem 6.2.4), which states that the covariance must be a weighted sum of inverse massive laplacians of different masses. The cut-off GFF cannot have a Källén–Lehmann representation since, if so, it would have non-zero covariance at arbitrarily high frequency, i.e. one would have
\mathbb{E}|\Phi_{\Lambda}(e_{\lambda})|^{2}>0
for
\lambda
arbitrarily large. This comment was suggested to us by an anonymous referee.

Note that in the flat case
M=\mathbb{R}^{3}
, it is a well-established fact that the
\Phi^{4}_{3}
measure is reflection positive, since it can be constructed as the limit of a sequence of Gibbs measure on the lattice
[
GH21
]
, where regularized measures are reflection positive. However this construction does not generalize easily to the case of a compact Riemannian manifold
M
where lattice regularization is a more difficult option. On the other hand, we prove in Section
3.3.3
that the regularized
\Phi^{4}_{3}
measures
\nu_{\rho,\Lambda}
are not reflection positive for a small enough coupling constant
c>0
. Hence the proof of the reflection positivity of the
\Phi^{4}_{3}
measure on the sphere
\mathbb{S}^{3}
is still an open problem.

Regarding the Markov property, contrary to the two dimensional case, not much is known about the
\Phi^{4}_{3}
measure. Note that the original construction of the
\Phi^{4}_{3}
QFT by Glimm and Jaffe relies on the study of the Hamiltonian
[
Gli68
,
GJ73
]
, which indicates that the
\Phi^{4}_{3}
measure should have a semi-group property on the plane.

Triviality of Smooth Localized Observables under Cut-off

We prove Theorem
3.1
in this section. Our proof is based on the following result due to Lebeau-Robbiano
[
LR95
]
, Jerison-Lebeau
[
JL99
]
, Lebeau-Zuazua
[
LZ98
]
which asserts that the zero set (also called
nodal set
) of linear combinations of Laplace eigenfunctions always has empty interior:

Lemma 3.2.1

(Nodal sets of linear combinations of eigenfunctions)
.

Let
(M,g)
be a smooth closed compact Riemannian manifold.
Then for any
\Lambda\in(0,\infty)
and any non-trivial finite
linear combination of the eigenfunctions of
\Delta_{g}
that we denote by
f\in\mathrm{E}_{\leqslant\Lambda}
, the zero set
Z_{f}=\{x\in M|f(x)=0\}
of the function
f
has
empty interior
.

Proof.

Assume there exists
f\in\mathrm{E}_{\leqslant\Lambda}
such that
\|f\|_{L^{2}(M)}=1
and
Z_{f}
contains an open subset
U
.
Then the Lebeau-Robbiano spectral inequality
[
JL99
]
Theorem 14.6,
states that given
U\subset M
, there exists constants
C,K>0
such that for all
\varphi\in\mathrm{E}_{\leqslant\Lambda}
, we have an inequality of the form:

\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}(M)}\leqslant Ce^{K\Lambda}\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}(U)}\,,

where
C,K
do not depend on
\varphi
.
Setting
\varphi=f
yields

\|f\|_{L^{2}(M)}\leqslant Ce^{K\Lambda}\|f\|_{L^{2}(U)}=0\,,

since we assumed
f\upharpoonright U=0
. This yields a contradiction with the assumption
\|f\|_{L^{2}(M)}=1
.
∎

Corollary 3.2.2
.

We assume
(M,g)
is a smooth closed compact Riemannian manifold or
M=\mathbb{R}^{d}
with the flat metric and
B
is some open subset
B\subsetneq M
.
Let
T\in\CB{D}^{\prime}(M)
be a distribution such that for every
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(B)
,
T(\Pi_{\Lambda}f)=0
. Then
\Pi_{\Lambda}T=0
.

Proof.

Since
\Pi_{\Lambda}
is self-adjoint, we have

\displaystyle T(\Pi_{\Lambda}f)=\langle\Pi_{\Lambda}T,f\rangle_{L^{2}(M)}.

Therefore,
\Pi_{\Lambda}T
is a smooth function in
\mathrm{E}_{\leqslant\Lambda}
which vanishes on the interior of
B
. Hence, in view of Lemma
3.2.1
, it is identically equal to zero.

In the case
M=\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we repeat the exact same argument and conclude using the fact that
\Pi_{\Lambda}T
is analytic by the Paley-Wiener Theorem hence null if it vanishes on the open set
B
.
∎

We are now ready to give the proof of Theorem
3.1
.

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.

We work by contradiction. Set
F=G\circ\Pi_{\Lambda}
which is a smooth functional on
H^{{(2-d)/}{2}-\varepsilon}(M)
. Assuming that
F
is
\sigma(\Phi;A)
-measurable, for every direction
h\in C^{\infty}_{c}(A^{c})
, we have the identity

F(\Phi+h)=F(\Phi)

for
\Phi
in a set
\Omega_{h}\subset\Omega
of probability
1
that depends on
h
. Now we would like to go from an
h
-dependent almost sure statement to a deterministic statement, namely we aim to prove that

\tau_{h}F(\Phi)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}F(\Phi+h)-F(\Phi)=0

for every distribution
\Phi\in H^{{(2-d)/}{2}-\varepsilon}(M)
.

Assume by contradiction that there exists some
\varphi\in H^{{(2-d)/}{2}-\varepsilon}(M)
such that
\tau_{h}F(\varphi)\neq 0
, and assume without loss of generality that
\tau_{h}F(\varphi)=L>0
. The functional

H^{{(2-d)/}{2}-\varepsilon}(M)\ni\Phi\mapsto\tau_{h}F(\Phi)

is smooth. Hence, by continuity, there is some open subset
U_{\varphi}\ni\varphi
of
H^{{(2-d)/}{2}-\varepsilon}(M)
such that
0<\frac{L}{2}\leqslant F\upharpoonright{U_{\varphi}}\leqslant\frac{3L}{2}
. There, there exists
R>0
such that
B_{\varphi}(R)\subset U_{\varphi}
for the
H^{{(2-d)/}{2}-\varepsilon}(M)
topology. One then has

0=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}\left[\tau_{h}F(\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to5.74991pt{\hfil\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{\lower 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\hfil}}{\hbox to5.74991pt{\hfil\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{\lower 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\hfil}}{\hbox to5.28671pt{\hfil\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{\lower 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\hfil}}{\hbox to5.1909pt{\hfil\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{\lower 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\hfil}}})1_{B_{\varphi}(R)}\right]\geqslant\frac{L}{2}\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}\left(B_{\varphi}(R)\right)\,.

The first equality follows from the almost sure vanishing of
\tau_{h}F(\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to5.74991pt{\hfil\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{\lower 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\hfil}}{\hbox to5.74991pt{\hfil\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{\lower 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\hfil}}{\hbox to5.28671pt{\hfil\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{\lower 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\hfil}}{\hbox to5.1909pt{\hfil\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{\lower 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\hfil}}})
, which implies that

\mu\big(B_{\varphi}(R)\big)=0\,.

This is a contradiction, because
\mu_{\text{GFF}}
has
full support
in
H^{{(2-d)}{/2}-\varepsilon}(M)
since the Cameron-Martin space
H^{1}(M)
is everywhere dense in
H^{{(2-d)/}{2}-\varepsilon}(M)
for the
H^{{(2-d)/}{2}-\varepsilon}(M)
topology (
[
Bog98
]
Theorem 3.6.1,
[
Hai23
]
Proposition 3.68). Therefore,
F(\Phi+h)=F(\Phi)
for every distributions
\Phi\in H^{{(2-d)/}{2}-\varepsilon}(M)
and every
h\in C^{\infty}_{c}(A^{c})
which is much stronger than the almost sure statement. It means that
G\circ\Pi_{\Lambda}
should not depend on
h\in C_{c}^{\infty}(A^{c})
. More precisely, by definition of the support of a functional (see Definition
3.1.3
), for any such
h
we have
G(\Phi_{\Lambda}+\Pi_{\Lambda}h)=G(\Phi_{\Lambda})
, which implies that
\mathrm{D}_{\Phi}(G\circ\Pi_{\Lambda})(h)=0
. Observing that

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\Phi}(G\circ\Pi_{\Lambda})(h)=\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\frac{G(\Phi_{\Lambda}+t\Pi_{\Lambda}h)-G(\Phi_{\Lambda})}{t}=\mathrm{D}_{\Phi_{\Lambda}}(G)(\Pi_{\Lambda}h)\,,

we conclude that
\mathrm{D}_{\Phi_{\Lambda}}(G)(\Pi_{\Lambda}h)=0
. In view of Corollary
3.2.2
taking
B=A^{c}
and
T=\mathrm{D}_{\Phi_{\Lambda}}(G)
, we thus have that
\Pi_{\Lambda}\mathrm{D}_{\Phi_{\Lambda}}(G)=0
. In particular, for all
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
,

\displaystyle\Pi_{\Lambda}\mathrm{D}_{\Phi_{\Lambda}}(G)(f)=\mathrm{D}_{\Phi_{\Lambda}}(G)(\Pi_{\Lambda}f)=\mathrm{D}_{\Phi}(G\circ\Pi_{\Lambda})(f)=0\,,

so
G\circ\Pi_{\Lambda}
is indeed constant.
∎

Non-Reflection Positivity

The cut-off GFF on Cylinders

In this section, we give an explicit counter-example contradicting the assertion that
\Phi_{\Lambda}
could be reflection positive on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and more generally on any Riemannian cylinder
M
of the form
M=\mathbb{R}\times\Sigma
where
\Sigma
is complete Riemannian and the cylinder is endowed with the split metric
\mathrm{d}t^{2}+g_{\Sigma}
. This is based on the following lemma.

Lemma 3.3.1

(
[
GJ87
]
, Theorem 6.2.2)
.

A Gaussian random field
\Psi
with covariance
C
is reflection positive if and only if its covariance is reflection positive, in the sense that for any
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}\times\Sigma)
,

\displaystyle\langle\Theta f,Cf\rangle_{L^{2}(M)}\geqslant 0\,.

We construct hereafter such a function
f
such that
\langle\Theta f,\frac{\Pi_{\Lambda}}{\Delta_{g}+1}f\rangle_{L^{2}(M)}<0
, which implies the following result.

Theorem 3.2
.

The spectrally cut-off massive Gaussian free field on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
or on any Riemannian cylinder
\Phi_{\Lambda}
is not reflection positive. In particular, it is not Markov either.

To construct our counter-example, we need the following intermediate lemma.

Lemma 3.3.2
.

Fix
\kappa>0
. There exists a function
h\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0})
such that, denoting by
\CB Fh(\xi)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\int_{\mathbb{R}}h(x)e^{\mathrm{i}\xi x}\mathrm{d}x
the Fourier transform of
h
, it holds

\int_{-1}^{1}\overline{\CB F{h}(-\xi)}\,\CB F{h}(\xi)\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\xi}{\xi^{2}+\kappa}<0\,.

Proof.

Note that because
h
is real, one has
\overline{\CB F{h}(-\xi)}=\CB F{h}(\xi)
. Thus,
h
needs to satisfy

\int_{-1}^{1}\CB F{h}^{2}(\xi)\frac{\mathrm{d}\xi}{\xi^{2}+\kappa}<0\,.

Now write

\CB F{h}(\xi)=A_{h}(\xi)+\mathrm{i}B_{h}(\xi)\,,

where

A_{h}(\xi)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\int_{\mathbb{R}}h(x)\cos(\xi x)\mathrm{d}x\,,\quad B_{h}(\xi)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\int_{\mathbb{R}}h(x)\sin(\xi x)\mathrm{d}x\,,

so that
A_{h}
is even and
B_{h}
is odd. This gives

\CB F{h}^{2}(\xi)=A^{2}_{h}(\xi)-B^{2}_{h}(\xi)+C_{h}(\xi)\,,\quad\text{where}\quad C_{h}(\xi)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}2\mathrm{i}{A_{h}(\xi)B_{h}(\xi)}\,.

Here, observe that
C_{h}(\xi)
is an odd function, which does not contribute to the integral.

Moreover, the important idea is that since
\CB F{h}^{2}(0)=\big(\int_{\mathbb{R}}h(x)\mathrm{d}x\big)^{2}\geqslant 0
, one needs to shift the weight of integration
(\xi^{2}+\kappa)^{-1}
“away from zero”. This can be done by taking derivatives: setting
h=\varphi^{(2n)}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}\big(\CB F{\varphi^{(2n)}}(\xi)\big)^{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}\xi}{\xi^{2}+\kappa}

\displaystyle=2\int_{0}^{1}\frac{(\mathrm{i}\xi)^{4n}}{\xi^{2}+\kappa}\big(A^{2}_{\varphi}(\xi)-B^{2}_{\varphi}(\xi)\big)\mathrm{d}\xi\,.

Note that we have taken care of choosing a number of derivatives that enforces
\mathrm{i}^{4n}=1
. Now, observe that

\frac{\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\xi^{4n}}{\xi^{2}+\kappa}\big(A^{2}_{\varphi}(\xi)-B^{2}_{\varphi}(\xi)\big)\mathrm{d}\xi}{\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\xi^{4n}}{\xi^{2}+\kappa}\mathrm{d}\xi}\longrightarrow A^{2}_{\varphi}(1)-B^{2}_{\varphi}(1)\,\,\,\textrm{as }n\uparrow\infty\,.

Choosing
\varphi
supported near
\frac{\pi}{2}
ensures that
\cos(x)\approx 0
and
\sin(x)\approx 1
, so that
A^{2}_{\varphi}(1)-B^{2}_{\varphi}(1)<0
. Taking
n
large enough but finite and rescaling
\varphi
, one finally obtains the desired result. Moreover, since
\varphi
is compactly supported on
\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
, so is
h=\varphi^{(2n)}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.2
.

As alluded to, the proof follows from the fact that we provide a counter-example of the positivity of the covariance, that is to say, we construct
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}\times\Sigma)
such that
\langle\Theta f,\frac{\Pi_{\Lambda}}{\Delta_{g}+1}f\rangle_{L^{2}(M)}<0
.

We denote by
\CB E_{\lambda}\mathrm{d}\lambda
the projection valued measure of the slice Laplacian
\Delta_{\Sigma}
which is well–known to be self-adjoint by the completeness of
\Sigma
. For any function
f\in L^{2}(M)

on the cylinder
M=\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\Sigma_{x}
, we denote by

\CB T_{(\tau,\lambda)}f(\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to5.74991pt{\hfil\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{\lower 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\hfil}}{\hbox to5.74991pt{\hfil\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{\lower 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\hfil}}{\hbox to5.28671pt{\hfil\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{\lower 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\hfil}}{\hbox to5.1909pt{\hfil\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{\lower 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\hfil}}})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-\mathrm{i}t\tau}\CB E_{\lambda}f(t,\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to5.74991pt{\hfil\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{\lower 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}\hfil}}{\hbox to5.74991pt{\hfil\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{\lower 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}\hfil}}{\hbox to5.28671pt{\hfil\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{\lower 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}\hfil}}{\hbox to5.1909pt{\hfil\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{\lower 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}\hfil}}})\mathrm{d}t

its Fourier transform w.r.t. the time variable
t
and its spectral transform with respect to the spectral measure of
\Delta_{\Sigma}
. It is a convenient expression because we know that the action of the Laplacian on
\CB T_{(\tau,\lambda)}f
boils down to the multiplication by
\tau^{2}+\lambda
.

Then, denoting by
\mathrm{v}_{\Sigma}
the volume form on
\Sigma
, we rewrite the pairing as

\displaystyle\big\langle\Theta f,\frac{\Pi_{\Lambda}}{\Delta_{g}+1}f\big\rangle_{L^{2}(M)}

\displaystyle=\int_{\{\tau^{2}\vee\lambda\leqslant\Lambda\}\subset\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}}\Big(\int_{\Sigma}\overline{\CB T_{(-\tau,\lambda)}{f}}\CB T_{(\tau,\lambda)}{f}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{v}_{\Sigma}\Big)\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\tau^{2}+\lambda+1}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\Lambda}\int_{-\sqrt{\Lambda}}^{\sqrt{\Lambda}}\Big(\int_{\Sigma}\overline{\CB T_{(-\tau,\lambda)}{f}}\CB T_{(\tau,\lambda)}{f}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{v}_{\Sigma}\Big)\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\tau^{2}+\lambda+1}\,.

(3.3.1)

We will reduce to the one variable case by a scaling argument. First, fix
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0})
such that

\int_{-1}^{1}\CB F{\varphi}^{2}(\tau)(\tau^{2}+\lambda+\Lambda^{-1})^{-1}\mathrm{d}\tau<0
for every
\lambda\in[0,1]
. The existence
of such
\varphi
comes from Lemma
3.3.2
.
Then, choose
\chi\in L^{2}(\Sigma)
such that
\|\chi\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}^{2}=1
and
\chi
has non trivial spectral measure in the interval
[0,1]
, that is to say

\int_{0}^{1}\|\CB E_{\lambda}\chi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}\mathrm{d}\lambda>0\,.

Finally, we set

f_{\varphi,\chi}(t,x)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\Lambda}\varphi(\sqrt{\Lambda}t)\CB E_{{\lambda}/{\Lambda}}\chi(x)\mathrm{d}\lambda\,,

which verifies

\displaystyle\CB T_{(\tau,\lambda)}f_{\varphi,\chi}(x)=\CB F\varphi(\tau/\sqrt{\Lambda})\CB E_{\lambda/\Lambda}\chi(x)\,.

Plugging this expression inside (
3.3.1
), we obtain

\displaystyle\big\langle\Theta f_{\varphi,\chi},\frac{\Pi_{\Lambda}}{\Delta_{g}+1}f_{\varphi,\chi}\big\rangle_{L^{2}(M)}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\Lambda}\int_{-\sqrt{\Lambda}}^{\sqrt{\Lambda}}\|\CB E_{\lambda/\Lambda}\chi\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}^{2}\CB F{\varphi}^{2}({\tau}/{\sqrt{\Lambda}})\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\tau^{2}+\lambda+1}

\displaystyle=\Lambda^{3/2}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}\|\CB E_{\lambda}\chi\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}^{2}\CB F{\varphi}^{2}(\tau)\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\Lambda(\tau^{2}+\lambda)+1}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\Lambda}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}\|\CB E_{\lambda}\chi\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}^{2}\CB F{\varphi}^{2}(\tau)\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\tau^{2}+\lambda+\frac{1}{\Lambda}}

\displaystyle<0.

We have thus proven that the cut-off covariance is not reflection positive, and by Lemma
3.3.1
,
\Phi_{\Lambda}
is therefore not reflection positive.
∎

The cut-off GFF on Compact Manifolds

We now turn to the proof that the cut-off GFF on compact manifolds is not reflection positive. This will also imply that it is not Markov.

Theorem 3.3
.

Assume that
(M,g)
is a smooth compact Riemannian manifold which is reflection positive.
Then there exists
L>0
such that for any
\Lambda\geqslant L
, there exists a function
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M_{+})

verifying

\displaystyle\big\langle\Theta f,\frac{\Pi_{\Lambda}}{\Delta_{g}+1}f\big\rangle_{L^{2}(M)}<0\,.

Proof.

First, because
\Delta_{g}
and
\Theta
commute, we can choose the
L^{2}
basis of
\Delta_{g}
in such a way that any eigenfunction
e_{\lambda}
of the Laplacian
\Delta_{g}
satisfies either

\Theta e_{\lambda}=e_{\lambda}
(we say that
e_{\lambda}
is even) or
\Theta e_{\lambda}=-e_{\lambda}
(we say that it is odd). Observe that the set of odd eigenfunctions is not
empty, since the span of eigenfunctions is dense in
C^{\infty}(M)
, which contains
odd functions. We choose
L
large enough so that there exists
\lambda\leqslant L
such that
e_{\lambda}
is odd. Given
\Lambda\geqslant L
, we denote by
\lambda_{*}
the largest eigenvalue smaller than
\Lambda
with odd eigenfunction
e_{\lambda_{*}}
.

Next, we consider the linear map

\displaystyle\CB P_{\Lambda}\colon C^{\infty}(M_{+})\ni f\mapsto\left(\left\langle f,e_{\lambda}\right\rangle\right)_{\lambda\leqslant\Lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{\dim(\mathrm{E}_{\leqslant\Lambda})}\,.

We have to show that
\CB P_{\Lambda}
is onto. Assume by contradiction that it is not onto. Then
\CB P_{\Lambda}(C^{\infty}(M_{+}))
is a strict vector subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{\dim(\mathrm{E}_{\leqslant\Lambda})}
, and we can choose some vector
(c_{\lambda})_{\lambda\leqslant\Lambda}
in
\CB P_{\Lambda}(C^{\infty}(M_{+}))^{\perp}
. In other words there exists a non-trivial linear combination
\varphi=\sum_{\lambda\leqslant\Lambda}c_{\lambda}e_{\lambda}\in\mathrm{E}_{\leqslant\Lambda}
such that for every
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M_{+})
it holds

\sum_{\lambda\leqslant\Lambda}c_{\lambda}\left\langle e_{\lambda},f\right\rangle=0\,.

This entails that
\varphi\in\mathrm{E}_{\leqslant\Lambda}
vanishes on
M_{+}
. However, since
M_{+}
has non-empty interior, this would contradict Lemma
3.2.1
, which concludes the proof that the map
\CB P_{\Lambda}
is surjective. The surjectivity of
\CB P_{\Lambda}
allows us to find
f_{*}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M_{+})
such that
\left\langle f_{*},e_{\lambda}\right\rangle=0
if
\lambda\neq\lambda_{*}
and
\left\langle f_{*},e_{\lambda_{*}}\right\rangle=1
. For this
f_{*}
, we have

\displaystyle\big\langle\Theta f_{*},\frac{\Pi_{\Lambda}}{\Delta_{g}+1}f_{*}\big\rangle_{L^{2}(M)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda\leqslant\Lambda}\left\langle f_{*},\Theta e_{\lambda}\right\rangle\frac{1}{\lambda+1}\left\langle f_{*},e_{\lambda}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-\left\langle f_{*},e_{\lambda_{*}}\right\rangle\frac{1}{\lambda_{*}+1}\left\langle f_{*},e_{\lambda_{*}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\lambda_{*}+1}<0\,,

which concludes the proof.
∎

The Regularized
\Phi_{3}^{4}
Measure on
\mathbb{R}^{3}

We conclude by giving a counterexample on flat space. To do so, given
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we write

(\rho\Pi_{\Lambda}\Phi)(f)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x)(\Pi_{\Lambda}\Phi)(x)\rho(x)\mathrm{d}x\,.

We would like to summarize in one key lemma the central idea behind our counterexample.

Lemma 3.3.3
.

Let
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a closed subset with nonempty interior and

B\subset\mathbb{R}^{d*}
a closed compact ball in Fourier space. Denote by
L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the subspace of
L^{2}
functions whose supported is contained in
A
.
Then the Fourier restriction map defined as

\CB R_{B}:L^{2}_{A}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\ni\varphi\longmapsto\CB F{\varphi}\upharpoonright{B}\in L^{2}(B)

has everywhere dense image.

The idea of proof is very similar to the one of Theorem
3.3
. The simple but powerful idea is that any function on the ball
B
can be approximated by the Fourier transform of some function supported over
A
.

Proof.

By contradiction, if
\text{Ran}(\CB R_{B})
were not dense, then
\text{Ran}(\CB R_{B})^{\perp}
would be a non-empty closed vector subspace and there would be some non-null element
g\in L^{2}(B)
such that for all
f\in L^{2}_{A}

\displaystyle 0=\big\langle\CB F{f},g\big\rangle_{L^{2}(B)}=\big\langle\CB F{f},1_{B}g\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\left\langle f,\CB{F}^{-1}\left(1_{B}g\right)\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},
