from Plancherel identity. The function
\CB{F}^{-1}\left(1_{B}g\right)
is therefore not supported on
A
hence vanishes on some open subset. This contradicts the fact that
\CB{F}^{-1}\left(1_{B}g\right)
is a non-trivial analytic function by the Paley-Wiener theorem.
∎

We now fix
d=3
, since it is the most interesting case. Recall the definition (
3.1.4
) of the approximate
\Phi_{3}^{4}
measure
\nu_{\rho,\Lambda}
. We emphasize here the dependence of the measures
\nu_{\rho,\Lambda}
on the coupling constant
c
by writing
\nu(c,\rho,\Lambda)
, and we set

G_{c,\rho,\Lambda}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}e^{-c\|\rho\Phi_{\Lambda}\|^{4}_{L^{4}}-ca_{\Lambda}\|\rho\Phi_{\Lambda}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}}\leqslant 1.

Denoting by
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
the canonical coordinates of a point
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we write
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})
to say that
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and
\text{supp}(f)\subset\{x_{1}>0\}
.

Theorem 3.4
.

For
c>0
small enough, there exists a function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\nu(c,\rho,\Lambda)}\big[\overline{(\rho\Pi_{\Lambda}\Phi)(\Theta f)}\,(\rho\Pi_{\Lambda}\Phi)(f)\big]<0.

(3.3.2)

This implies that the regularized
\Phi_{3}^{4}
measures
\nu(c,\rho,\Lambda)
are not reflection positive for
c>0
small enough, depending on
\Lambda
and
\rho
.

Proof.

Since
0\leqslant G_{c,\rho,\Lambda}(\varphi)
goes to
1
as
c\downarrow 0
for every distributions
\varphi
, one has by dominated convergence

\mathbb{E}_{\nu(c,\rho,\Lambda)}\big[\overline{(\rho\Pi_{\Lambda}\Phi)(\Theta f)}\,(\rho\Pi_{\Lambda}\Phi)(f)\big]\overset{c\downarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}_{\text{GFF}}\big[\overline{(\rho\Pi_{\Lambda}\Phi)(\Theta f)}\,(\rho\Pi_{\Lambda}\Phi)(f)\big].

(3.3.3)

To justify the use of the dominated convergence, at some fixed cut-offs
\rho
and
\Lambda
, we use the lower bound on the interaction

c\|\rho\Phi_{\Lambda}\|^{4}_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+ca_{\Lambda}\|\rho\Phi_{\Lambda}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\geqslant c\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\rho^{4}\Phi_{\Lambda}^{4}\,\mathrm{d}x-c\frac{|a_{\Lambda}|\delta^{2}}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Phi_{\Lambda}^{4}\rho^{4}\,\mathrm{d}x-c\frac{|a_{\Lambda}|}{2\delta^{2}}\vol(\text{supp}(\rho)),

for any
\delta>0
, using Young’s inequality and the compactness of the support of
\rho
. Choosing
\delta
small enough yields a lower bound of the form

c\|\rho\Phi_{\Lambda}\|^{4}_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+ca_{\Lambda}\|\rho\Phi_{\Lambda}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\geqslant c\frac{2}{3}\|\rho\Phi_{\Lambda}\|^{4}_{L^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}-cK

for some
K>0
.

From the convergence result in (
3.3.3
) it suffices to find
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})
such that

\mathbb{E}_{\text{GFF}}\big[\overline{(\rho\Pi_{\Lambda}\Phi)(\Theta f)}\,(\rho\Pi_{\Lambda}\Phi)(f)\big]=\left\langle\Pi_{\Lambda}(\rho\overline{\Theta f})\,,\,(\Delta+1)^{-1}\Pi_{\Lambda}(\rho f)\right\rangle_{L^{2}}<0

and choose
c>0
small enough. Set the Fourier restriction map

\displaystyle\CB R_{\Lambda}:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})\ni f\longmapsto\CB F(\rho f)1_{B_{0}(\Lambda)}\in L^{2}(B_{0}(\Lambda))\,,

where on the right hand side we consider the restriction of the Fourier transform
\CB F(\rho f)
to the centered Fourier ball of radius
\Lambda
. By a similar argument as in Lemma
3.3.3
, the key idea is to prove that the image of
\CB T
is dense in
L^{2}(B_{0}(\Lambda))
. If it were not true, then
\text{Ran}(\CB R_{\Lambda})^{\perp}
would be a non-empty closed vector subspace and there would be some non-null element
g\in L^{2}(B_{0}(\Lambda))
such that for every
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})

\displaystyle 0=\big\langle\CB F(\rho f),g\big\rangle_{L^{2}(B_{0}(\Lambda))}=\big\langle\CB F(\rho f),1_{B_{0}(\Lambda)}g\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}=\left\langle f,\rho\CB{F}^{-1}(1_{B_{0}(\Lambda)}g)\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})},

from Plancherel identity. The function
\rho\CB{F}^{-1}(1_{B_{0}(\Lambda)}g)
is therefore supported outside the half-space
\{x_{1}>0\}
. Since
\rho>0
on some non-empty region of
\{x_{1}>0\}
this implies that the function
\CB{F}^{-1}(1_{B_{0}(\Lambda)}g)
vanishes in some open subset contained in the half-space
\{x_{1}>0\}
. This contradicts the fact that
\CB{F}^{-1}(1_{B_{0}(\Lambda)}g)
is a non-trivial analytic function by the Paley-Wiener theorem.

Therefore, the provisional conclusion is that there exists a sequence
f_{n}
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})
such that the sequence
\CB R_{\Lambda}f_{n}
converges in
L^{2}(B_{0}(\Lambda))
to
\xi_{1}1_{B_{0}(\Lambda)}(\xi)
. Therefore for this sequence
(f_{n})_{n\geqslant 0}
we find that

\displaystyle\left\langle\Pi_{\Lambda}(\rho\overline{\Theta f_{n}}),(\Delta+1)^{-1}\Pi_{\Lambda}(\rho f_{n})\right\rangle_{L^{2}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3*}}1_{[0,\Lambda]}(|\xi|^{2})\langle\xi\rangle^{-2}\,\overline{\CB R_{\Lambda}f_{n}}(-\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})\CB R_{\Lambda}f_{n}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})\mathrm{d}\xi

converges as
n\uparrow\infty
to

-\int_{{B_{0}(\Lambda)}}1_{[0,\Lambda]}(|\xi|^{2})\langle\xi\rangle^{-2}\xi_{1}^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi<0\,.

This shows that the heat regularized (and space localized) Gaussian free field measure is not reflection positive. Of course the Gaussian free field measure itself is reflection positive and the above proof breaks down when
\Lambda\uparrow\infty
as the function
\xi_{1}
alone is not an element of
L^{2}
.
∎

Note that Theorem
3.4

does not exclude
the fact that
\nu(c,\rho,\Lambda)
may be reflection positive for some large enough coupling constant
c
.

Chapter 4
Entanglement Entropy and Cauchy-Hadamard Renormalization

This chapter is adapted from
[
EL25
]
joint with Benoit Estienne.

Introduction

An (Euclidean) Conformal Field Theory (CFT) is a theory which, in one way, emerges as the
scaling limit
of a lattice statistical mechanics model with
critical parameters
(at phase transition), as the lattice spacing goes to zero. Due to its conformal covariance properties, such a theory can, in two dimensions, naturally be defined on a surface with its conformal structure (
=
complex structure for oriented surfaces).
Broadly speaking and for the purposes of this paper, the data of such a theory consists of assigning to each (closed) Riemann surface
\Sigma
with metric
g
a number
\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma,g)
(the partition function) and a collection of correlation functions defined on
\Sigma^{n}_{\neq}
(subspace of the
n
-fold product
\Sigma^{n}
with non-coincident points), which satisfy well-defined transformation rules under conformal changes of the metric
g
(see definition
4.2.1
). In comparison to the original lattice problem, the number
\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma,g)
corresponds to the normalization constant for the Gibbs measure, while the functions on
\Sigma^{n}_{\neq}
describe the scaling limit of the probabilistic correlation functions of
n
local observables of the spin configurations under the Gibbs measure.

It has long been recognized in the physics literature that the presence of
conical singularities
in the underlying metric alters the conformal covariance properties of the partition function
\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma,g)
in a way that mimics the behavior of correlation functions. This paper aims to provide a rigorous framework for these observations, with a particular emphasis on partition functions on branched coverings, motivated by considerations of entanglement entropy (which we introduce in Section
4.3
). We remark that a CFT in the sense of the previous paragraph makes sense,
a priori
, only for smooth metrics and smooth conformal changes. The first task of this paper thus lies in defining in a simple and natural manner the number
\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma,\widetilde{g})
when the metric
\widetilde{g}
admits finitely many isolated
conical singularities
. Then, applying this definition to metrics coming from pull-backs of ramified holomorphic maps, we observe that certain ratios of these numbers behave exactly like what is expected of correlation functions on the target surface where the variables are the critical values. More precisely, the main result is the following.

Theorem 4.1

(proposition
4.6.3
)
.
1
1

1

See also “Riemann surface terminologies” on the next page. Moreover, we will assume that all Riemann surfaces in this paper are connected, unless otherwise specified.

Let
\Sigma_{d}
,
\Sigma
be closed Riemann surfaces, with a smooth conformal metric
g
on
\Sigma
, and
f:\Sigma_{d}\longrightarrow\Sigma
a ramified
d
-sheeted holomorphic map, whose critical values are
w_{1}
, …,
w_{p}
. Consider a conformal field theory with central charge
c
whose partition function is denoted
\mathcal{Z}
. Pick
h\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
on
\Sigma
. Then under the definition
4.2.8
for
\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma_{d},f^{*}e^{2h}g)
and
\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma_{d},f^{*}g)
we have

\frac{\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma_{d},f^{*}e^{2h}g)}{\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma,e^{2h}g)^{d}}=e^{-\sum_{j}h(w_{j})\Delta_{j}}\frac{\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma_{d},f^{*}g)}{\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma,g)^{d}},

(4.1.1)

where

\Delta_{j}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\frac{c}{12}\sum_{z\in f^{-1}(w_{j})}\Big(\ord_{f}(z)-\frac{1}{\ord_{f}(z)}\Big)

(4.1.2)

are the
conformal weights
or
scaling dimensions
. Here
\ord_{f}(z)
denotes the
order
or
multiplicity
of
f
at
z\in\Sigma_{d}
.

The relation between CFT and conical singularities has been explored in various physical contexts. Early investigations, particularly within string theory, focused on the construction of orbifold CFTs
[
DFMS87
]
. In this framework, Knizhnik
[
Kni87
]
examined the behavior of CFTs defined on flat branched coverings of
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
. More generally, the emergence of universal logarithmic divergences in the free energy due to conical singularities was recognized by Cardy and Peschel
[
CP88
]
. Motivated by both black hole entropy and quantum information theory, entanglement entropy in CFTs was explored in seminal works
[
HLW94
]
and
[
CC09
]
, where it was shown that the universal contributions from conical singularities play a crucial role in understanding the scaling behavior of entanglement entropy in one-dimensional quantum critical systems. In this context, the Rényi entropy was identified as the free energy on
d
-sheeted branched covering of certain flat Riemann surface (typically a cylinder or a torus). Further studies, such as in
[
CW17
]
, extended the analysis of conical singularities to hyperbolic surfaces, with applications in the context of the quantum Hall effect
[
KMMW17
]
.

Conical singularities is also a relatively well-studied subject mathematically. They are one of the simplest types of singularities that can appear on a Riemann surface. The main motivations include spectral geometry (hearing the shape of a drum)
[
Che83
]
and the Berger-Nirenberg problem of finding metrics with prescribed curvature
[
HT92
,
Tro91
]
. Motivated by its higher dimensional analogue in complex geometry called Kähler-Einstein edge metrics, people have also studied the Ricci flow on surfaces with such singularities
[
MRS15
]
. Particularly relevant to the present work are recent investigations on the
\zeta
-determinant of the Laplacian under these conical metrics and the Polyakov formulas
[
AKR20
,
Kal21
]
, on which we shall make a more detailed comment in remarks
4.6.4
and
4.6.5
. We would also like to point out the interesting recent work
[
JV23
]
on Coulomb gas and the Grunsky operator where conical singularities (more precisely, “corners” on the boundary) also play a role. For more literature from these various perspectives we refer to the introductions of
[
AKR20
,
Kal21
]
, section 1.2 of
[
JV23
]
, and section 2.E of
[
MRS15
]
. Finally, one precise relation between conical singularities and the probabilistically constructed Liouville CFT has been discussed in
[
LRV22
,
TNA24
]
.

One important feature of the present work lies in its
simplicity
and
naturalness
. Essentially, the method involves only a close look at the geometry near the cone points and integration by parts (Green-Stokes formula). Consequently, the geometric meaning of each term that shows up in the result is transparent (see also remark
4.6.2
). Moreover, we only need rather weak regularity to be imposed on the regular metric potential at the cone points compared to other related works in the literature (e.g. to
[
Kal21
]
definition 2.1), and this is basically the assumption adopted in
[
Tro91
]
(see also remark
4.4.1
).

Organization.

In section
4.2
we define the three main objects dealt with by this paper: the CFT correlation functions, conical singularities, and the renormalized Polyakov anomaly; these are accompanied by a few pivotal lemmas. Section
4.3
explains the physical motivation which leads us to (
4.1.1
). In these sections (and hence the whole paper) no prior knowledge of QFT, CFT or statistical mechanics is assumed, as this paper serves also to introduce these ideas to the mathematics community. Only some notions of quantum mechanics are required to make sense of entanglement entropy. Then in section
4.4
, we collect a few known facts about conical metrics (subsection
4.4.1
), prove a crucial scaling lemma (subsection
4.4.2
), and compute the asymptotics of several logarithmically divergent integrals using integration by parts (subsection
4.4.3
). They are important as pointed out by remarks
4.2.4
,
4.2.6
, and also in obtaining the final result. In section
4.5
we tie up some loose ends around the definitions of the renormalized anomaly and partition function for conical metrics. In subsection
4.6.1
we prove the main result and apply it to entanglement entropies, and in
4.6.2
we comment on the two closely related work
[
AKR20
,
Kal21
]
. Finally in appendix
C.3
we recall a few things around the so-called “Poincaré-Lelong lemma” concerning the Laplacian of the log of the distance function on a Riemannian surface.

Future work.

In the present version of the work we have focused on the simplest case dealing only with partition functions to illustrate our (already simple) methods. It is clear that the same arguments could apply with minor modifications to obtain analogous results for Segal’s amplitudes on surfaces with boundary (see
[
Gaw99
]
section 2.6) when the metric is “flat-at-the-boundary”, and for the case of general boundaries with possible presence of “corners” (i.e. “polygons”) by including the boundary term in the anomaly, as considered for example by
[
AKR20
]
. These considerations may be included in a future update of the present manuscript. However, another main aim of that work will be to present an equivalent rigorous construction of the quantities
\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma_{d},f^{*}g)/\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma,g)^{d}
in (
4.1.1
) for the GFF by constructing the so-called “twist fields” in the physics literature. This is related to a singular version of the “twisted Laplacians” used e.g. in Phillips and Sarnak
[
PS87
]
. Lastly, less apparent but interesting future investigations include obtaining the precise relation between this work and
[
Kal21
]
in light of Segal’s gluing axioms, and exploring further connections with the Quantum Hall Effect and Coulomb gas in a rigorous manner.

Riemann surface terminology and asymptotic notations.

Let
f:\Sigma^{\prime}\longrightarrow\Sigma
be a holomorphic map of closed (connected
2
2

2

We will assume that all Riemann surfaces in this paper are connected, unless otherwise specified.
) Riemann surfaces. If at
z\in\Sigma^{\prime}
we have
\mathrm{d}f|_{z}=0
we say that
z
is a
critical point
of
f
and
w=f(z)
is a
critical value
of
f
.
If in local holomorphic charts around
z
and
w
respectively
f
looks like a
k
-th power, then we say
k
is the
order
of
f
at
z\in\Sigma^{\prime}
(sometimes referred to as the
local degree
or
multiplicity
), denoted
k=:\ord_{f}(z)
.
We say that
f
is a
ramified
(or
branched
) holomorphic map when we want to emphasize that it is not a strict covering map.
The number
\sum_{z\in f^{-1}(w)}\ord_{f}(z)=:d
, which is same for every
w\in\Sigma
, is called the
number of sheets
of
f
and we say
f
is
d
-sheeted
. Finally, the notation
u\asymp v
under some limit process means
u/v\to C>0
in that limit, and we write
u\sim v
for the case
C=1
.

Definition of Main Objects

Conformal Field Theory and Correlation Functions

Definition 4.2.1
.

In this paper, by a
2d Conformal Field Theory
we mean a rule that associates to each compact Riemannian surface
\Sigma
with metric
g
(a priori smooth) a complex number
\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma,g)
called the
partition function
, and a family
\{\left\langle\phi_{\alpha_{1}}(\cdot)\cdots\right\rangle_{\Sigma,g}\}
of functions of finite tuples of non-coincident points on
\Sigma
, called
correlation functions of primary fields
(labelled by the
\alpha
’s), such that the following two conditions hold:

1.

diffeomorphism invariance:
if
\Psi:\Sigma^{\prime}\longrightarrow\Sigma
is a diffeomorphism of smooth surfaces, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma^{\prime},\Psi^{*}g)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma,g),

(4.2.1)

\displaystyle\left\langle\phi_{\alpha_{1}}(\Psi(x_{1}))\cdots\phi_{\alpha_{n}}(\Psi(x_{n}))\right\rangle_{\Sigma,g}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\phi_{\alpha_{1}}(x_{1})\cdots\phi_{\alpha_{n}}(x_{n})\right\rangle_{\Sigma^{\prime},\Psi^{*}g},

(4.2.2)

for any
x_{1}
, …,
x_{n}\in\Sigma
non-coincident, and as well

2.

local scale (conformal) covariance:
if
\sigma\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
then

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma,\textrm{e}^{2\sigma}g)

\displaystyle=\exp\Big(\frac{c}{24\pi}\int_{\Sigma}(|\nabla_{g}\sigma|_{g}^{2}+2K_{g}\cdot\sigma)\mathrm{d}V_{g}\Big)\cdot\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma,g),

(4.2.3)

\displaystyle\left\langle\phi_{\alpha_{1}}(x_{1})\cdots\phi_{\alpha_{n}}(x_{n})\right\rangle_{\Sigma,\textrm{e}^{2\sigma}g}

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\textrm{e}^{-\sigma(x_{j})\Delta_{\alpha_{j}}}\left\langle\phi_{\alpha_{1}}(x_{1})\cdots\phi_{\alpha_{n}}(x_{n})\right\rangle_{\Sigma,g},

(4.2.4)

where
K_{g}
denotes the Gauss curvature of
g
(half the scalar curvature), the constant
c\in\mathbb{R}
is called the
central charge
, charateristic of the specific theory at hand, and constants
\Delta_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}
the
conformal weights
, charateristic of the theory as well as the fields
\phi_{\alpha}
.

Definition 4.2.2
.

The quantity

A_{\Sigma}(\textrm{e}^{2\sigma}g,g)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\frac{1}{24\pi}\int_{\Sigma}(|\nabla_{g}\sigma|_{g}^{2}+2K_{g}\cdot\sigma)\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}

(4.2.5)

for
\sigma\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
, that appears in the exponential in (
4.2.3
) is usually called the
Weyl
or
Polyakov Anomaly
of
\textrm{e}^{2\sigma}g
against
g
.

Remark 4.2.1
.

The quantities
A_{\Sigma}
has the so-called
cocycle property
, that is, if
g_{3}=\textrm{e}^{2h}g_{2}
and
g_{2}=\textrm{e}^{2\sigma}g_{1}
where
h
,
\sigma\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
, then

A_{\Sigma}(g_{3},g_{2})+A_{\Sigma}(g_{2},g_{1})=A_{\Sigma}(g_{3},g_{1}),

(4.2.6)

as one can check using the relations (
4.4.1
) — (
4.4.4
). One essential ingredient of this work is to define a “renormalized” version of the anomaly of a conically singular metric against a smooth one (definition
4.5.1
). With these definitions the cocycle property will be modified accordingly when involving the conical metrics, see proposition
4.6.1
.

Example 4.2.1

(Gaussian Free Field)
.

Let
(\Sigma,g)
be a Riemannian surface with smooth metric
g
whose Laplacian is denoted
\Delta_{g}
(negative). Consider

\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma,g)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\detz^{\prime}(-\Delta_{g})^{-\frac{1}{2}},

(4.2.7)

where

\detz^{\prime}(-\Delta_{g})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\exp\Big(-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\Big|_{s=0}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{j}^{-s}\Big),

(4.2.8)

with
0=\lambda_{0}<\lambda_{1}\leqslant\lambda_{2}\leqslant\cdots
being the eigenvalues of
-\Delta_{g}
counted with multiplicity, called the
\zeta
-regularized determinant

[
RS71
]
. Then
\mathcal{Z}
satisfies (
4.2.3
) (and (
4.2.1
) trivially) with
c=1
, by the
Polyakov formula
(see
[
PW24
]
appendix B). This
\mathcal{Z}
corresponds to the “total mass” of the formal measure
\exp(-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}|\nabla_{g}\phi|^{2}\mathrm{d}V_{g})\mathrm{d}\mathcal{L}(\phi)
on the space of zero-average distributions
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{0}(\Sigma)
on
\Sigma
, with
\mathcal{L}
being the non-existent Lebesgue measure there. In this case, an actual Gaussian probability measure
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{\Sigma}=:\mu
can indeed be defined on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{0}(\Sigma)
that corrresponds to
\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma,g)^{-1}\exp(-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}|\nabla_{g}\phi|^{2}\mathrm{d}V_{g})\mathrm{d}\mathcal{L}(\phi)
, called the (massless)
Gaussian Free Field

[
PW21
]
. This is characterized by the formal covariance property (pretending that the point values
\phi(x)
,
x\in\Sigma
, are legitimate real random variables)

\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\big[\phi(x)\phi(y)\big]=G_{\Sigma}(x,y),\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\big[\phi(x)\big]\equiv 0,\quad\quad x,y\in\Sigma,

(4.2.9)

with
G_{\Sigma}
being the Green function which is the integral kernel of
(-\Delta_{g})^{-1}P_{\ker\Delta_{g}}^{\perp}
. Now, after an appropriate renormalization process which we do not detail here (related to obtaining a finite value for
G_{\Sigma}(x,x)
), denoted “
\mathcal{R}
”, the quantities

\big\langle\phi_{\alpha_{1}}(x_{1})\cdots\phi_{\alpha_{n}}(x_{n})\big\rangle_{\Sigma,g}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\textrm{``}\mathcal{R}\textrm{''}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\big[\textrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\alpha_{1}\phi(x_{1})}\cdots\textrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\alpha_{n}\phi(x_{n})}\big],

(4.2.10)

where now
\alpha_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
, transform according to (
4.2.4
) with
\Delta_{\alpha_{j}}:=\alpha_{j}^{2}/4\pi
.

The rules (i), (ii) actually determine, say, the two-point function up to a constant for some simple geometries.

Lemma 4.2.1
.

Consider a CFT defined on the Riemann sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2}=\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\}
, equipped with the Fubini-Study metric
g_{\mathrm{FS}}(z):=4(1+|z|^{2})^{-2}|\mathrm{d}z|^{2}
. Take two primary fields
\phi_{1}
,
\phi_{2}
with conformal weights
\Delta_{1}
,
\Delta_{2}
. Then

\big\langle\phi_{1}(u)\phi_{2}(v)\big\rangle_{\mathbb{S}^{2},g_{\mathrm{FS}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}C\sin\big(\frac{1}{2}d_{\mathrm{FS}}(u,v)\big)^{-2\Delta},&\textrm{when }\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{2}=\Delta,\\
0&\textrm{otherwise,}\end{array}\right.

(4.2.11)

where
u\neq v
and
C
is a non-zero constant that cannot be determined from the rules (i) and (ii) alone.

Proof.

Without loss of generality we suppose
u
,
v\neq\infty
. Given a Möbius transformation
\psi\in\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})
that sends
0\mapsto u
,
\infty\mapsto v
, we have by (
4.2.2
) and (
4.2.4
),

\displaystyle\big\langle\phi_{1}(u)\phi_{2}(v)\big\rangle_{\mathbb{S}^{2},g_{\mathrm{FS}}}

\displaystyle=\big\langle\phi_{1}(0)\phi_{2}(\infty)\big\rangle_{\mathbb{S}^{2},\psi^{*}g_{\mathrm{FS}}}

\displaystyle=\textrm{e}^{-\Delta_{1}\sigma(0)-\Delta_{2}\sigma(\infty)}\big\langle\phi_{1}(0)\phi_{2}(\infty)\big\rangle_{\mathbb{S}^{2},g_{\mathrm{FS}}},

(4.2.12)

where
\psi^{*}g_{\mathrm{FS}}=\textrm{e}^{2\sigma}g_{\mathrm{FS}}
. The above must hold for
all
such Möbius maps. These are of the form

\psi(z)=\frac{zv-z_{0}u}{z-z_{0}}.

(4.2.13)

where
z_{0}=\psi^{-1}(\infty)\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
parametrizes the preimage of infinity.
This gives

(\psi^{*}g_{\mathrm{FS}})(z)=\frac{4|\psi^{\prime}(z)|^{2}}{(1+|\psi(z)|^{2})^{2}}|\mathrm{d}z|^{2}=\frac{(1+|z|^{2})^{2}}{(1+|\psi(z)|^{2})^{2}}\frac{|v-u|^{2}|z_{0}|^{2}}{|z-z_{0}|^{4}}g_{\mathrm{FS}}(z).

(4.2.14)

Therefore, according to (
4.2.12
),

\big\langle\phi_{1}(u)\phi_{2}(v)\big\rangle_{\mathbb{S}^{2},g_{\mathrm{FS}}}=C\Big(\frac{1+|u|^{2}}{|u-v|}\Big)^{\Delta_{1}}\Big(\frac{1+|v|^{2}}{|u-v|}\Big)^{\Delta_{2}}|z_{0}|^{\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{1}}

(4.2.15)

where
C=:\left\langle\phi_{1}(0)\phi_{2}(\infty)\right\rangle_{\mathbb{S}^{2},g_{\mathrm{FS}}}
.
Since the correlation function should not depend on
z_{0}
, one finds

\textrm{either }\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{2}\quad\textrm{or}\quad C=0.

(4.2.16)

Summing up,

\big\langle\phi_{1}(u)\phi_{2}(v)\big\rangle_{\mathbb{S}^{2},g_{\mathrm{FS}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}C\left(1+|u|^{2}\right)^{\Delta}\left(1+|v|^{2}\right)^{\Delta}|u-v|^{-2\Delta},&\textrm{when }\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{2}=\Delta,\\
0&\textrm{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

(4.2.17)

where the constant
C\in\mathbb{C}
is
C=\big\langle\phi_{1}(0)\phi_{2}(\infty)\big\rangle_{\mathbb{S}^{2},g_{\mathrm{FS}}}
.
We note here that the quantity

d_{\mathrm{ch}}(u,v)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\frac{2|u-v|}{\sqrt{(1+|u|^{2})(1+|v|^{2})}}

(4.2.18)

is the so-called
spherical chordal distance
between
u
and
v
(see
[
Ahl21
]
page 20), and is related to the actual spherical distance
d_{\mathrm{FS}}(u,v)
by

d_{\mathrm{ch}}(u,v)=2\sin\Big(\frac{d_{\mathrm{FS}}(u,v)}{2}\Big).

(4.2.19)

This gives us the result.
∎

Conical Metrics

In this section we describe precisely our geometric set-up by adopting some of the terminologies of Troyanov
[
Tro91
]
.

Definition 4.2.3
.

Let
\Sigma
be a closed Riemann surface. A
generalized conformal metric
on
\Sigma
is a distributional Riemannian metric
\widetilde{g}
on
\Sigma
such that for any local complex coordinate
z_{U}:U\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
defined on
U\subset\Sigma
we have

\widetilde{g}=\varrho_{U}(z_{U})\,|\mathrm{d}z_{U}|^{2}

(4.2.20)

for some positive measurable function
\varrho_{U}
on
U
. We say
\widetilde{g}
is a
smooth conformal metric
if the functions
\rho_{U}
are all smooth and
0<c_{U,z}\leqslant\varrho_{U}\leqslant C_{U,z}
, for some constants
c_{U,z}
,
C_{U,z}
depending on
U
and the coordinate.

Smooth conformal metrics exist on any Riemann surface by simple constructions using partitions of unity.

Definition 4.2.4
.

A
real divisor
on a closed Riemann surface
\Sigma
is a finite formal sum

D=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\gamma_{j}z_{j},\quad\quad\gamma_{j}\in\mathbb{R},\penalty\ z_{j}\in\Sigma,\penalty\ z_{i}\neq z_{j}\textrm{ for }i\neq j.

(4.2.21)

The number
|D|:=\sum_{j}\gamma_{j}
is the
degree
of
D
. The set
\supp D:=\{z_{j}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \gamma_{j}\neq 0\}
is the
support
of
D
.

Definition 4.2.5
.

Let
\Sigma
be a closed Riemann surface. We say that a generalized conformal metric
\widetilde{g}
has an (admissible, isolated)
conical singularity
(or
cone point
) of
order

d>0
or
exponent

\gamma:=d-1>-1
at
z_{0}\in\Sigma
, if
z_{0}
has a neighborhood
U\subset\Sigma
, such that

\widetilde{g}|_{U\setminus z_{0}}=d_{g}(\bullet,z_{0})^{2\gamma}\cdot\textrm{e}^{2\varphi_{U,g}}g,

(4.2.22)

for some smooth conformal metric
g
on
\Sigma
and some function
\varphi_{U,g}
on
U
, called the
(regular) metric potential
(against
g
), which is continuous on
U
, smooth on
U\setminus z_{0}
, and
\Delta_{g}\varphi_{U,g}\in L^{1}(U,g)
. Moreover, we require that the Gauss curvature of
\widetilde{g}
on
U\setminus z_{0}
is bounded.

Remark 4.2.2
.

If
\gamma=0
(resp.
d=1
) then the point is called
regular
. In a neighborhood
U
of a regular point, the regularity of the metric potential
\varphi_{U,g}
is closely related to that of the Gauss curvature (given by (
4.4.3
) as an
L^{1}(U,g)
function) of
\widetilde{g}
on
U
. For example, if the Gauss curvature is smooth then one could deduce that
\varphi_{U,g}
is also smooth. For details see
[
Tro90
]
proposition 1.2. Our results will be consistent with the presence of regular points upon setting the corresponding exponents to zero, as one could check.

Definition 4.2.6
.

Let
\Sigma
be a closed Riemann surface,
D=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\gamma_{j}z_{j}
be a real divisor with
\gamma_{j}>-1
. We say that a generalized conformal metric
\widetilde{g}

represents
the divisor
D
if it has an isolated conical singularity of exponent
\gamma_{j}
at
z_{j}
respectively for each
j
, and smooth on
\Sigma\setminus\supp D
.

Remark 4.2.3
.

Our presentation differs from what usually happens in the literature where the background metric
g
is the local flat one coming from some compatible complex coordinate on the neighborhood
U
. We are faced with the natural question of whether
\widetilde{g}
would have the same form (
4.2.22
) with the same regularity on the metric potential if we switched to another smooth conformal background metric
g_{1}=\textrm{e}^{2h}g
,
h\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
. Indeed, in this case

d_{g}(\bullet,z_{0})^{2\gamma}\cdot\textrm{e}^{2(\varphi_{U,g}-h)}g_{1}=d_{g_{1}}(\bullet,z_{0})^{2\gamma}\Big(\frac{d_{g}(\bullet,z_{0})}{d_{g_{1}}(\bullet,z_{0})}\Big)^{2\gamma}\cdot\textrm{e}^{2(\varphi_{U,g}-h)}g_{1},

(4.2.23)

and the ratio of distances
d_{g}(\bullet,z_{0})/d_{g_{1}}(\bullet,z_{0})
is continuous on
U
with a limit
\textrm{e}^{-h(z_{0})}
at
z_{0}
, smooth on
U\setminus z_{0}
and
\Delta_{g_{1}}\log d_{g}-\Delta_{g_{1}}\log d_{g_{1}}
is in
L^{1}(U,g_{1})
as a distribution by lemma
C.3.2
. In the traditional setting if both
g
and
g_{1}
are locally
|\mathrm{d}z|^{2}
and
|\mathrm{d}w|^{2}
for some complex coordinates
z
and
w
, then the regular metric potentials differ by a harmonic function, as is well known.
In particular, if we do test against such a coordinate metric then our conditions on the regular metric potential satisfy what Troyanov calls that of an
admissible metric
in
[
Tro90
]
.

The following lemma describes the source of the conical singularities that the main application of the main result of this article tries to target.

Lemma 4.2.2
.

Let
\Sigma^{\prime}
,
\Sigma
be closed Riemann surfaces and
f:\Sigma^{\prime}\longrightarrow\Sigma
a holomorphic map. Let
z_{0}\in\Sigma^{\prime}
be a critical point of order
k
and
w_{0}=f(z_{0})\in\Sigma
the corresponding critical value. Let
g
be a smooth conformal metric on
\Sigma
. Then
f^{*}g
has a conical singularity at
z_{0}\in\Sigma^{\prime}
with order
k
.

Proof.

There exists holomorphic charts
(U,z)
and
(V,w)
around
z_{0}
and
w_{0}=f(z_{0})
in which
f
is represented by
z\mapsto z^{k}
. The metric
g
is locally of the form
g\big|_{V}=e^{2h}|\mathrm{d}w|^{2}
for some
h\in C^{\infty}(V)
, and

f^{*}g\big|_{U}=e^{2f^{*}h}\,|\mathrm{d}f|^{2}=e^{2f^{*}h}\,k^{2}|z|^{2(k-1)}|\mathrm{d}z|^{2}=d_{g^{\prime}}(\bullet,z_{0})^{2(k-1)}\,k^{2}e^{2f^{*}h}\,g^{\prime}\big|_{U}

(4.2.24)

where
g^{\prime}
is any smooth conformal metric on
\Sigma^{\prime}
whose restriction to
U
is
g^{\prime}\big|_{U}=|\mathrm{d}z|^{2}
(such a metric can be manufactured using smooth bump functions, and taking
U
smaller if necessary). Now (
4.2.24
) is of the form (
4.2.22
) with metric potential
\varphi=f^{*}h+\log k
. Note that
\varphi
is actually smooth across
U
in this situation.
∎

Renormalized Anomaly

Definition 4.2.7
.

Let
\Sigma
be a closed Riemann surface and
\widetilde{g}
a generalized conformal metric representing
D=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\gamma_{j}z_{j}
with
\gamma_{j}>-1
. Suppose
g
is a smooth conformal metric on
\Sigma
and
\widetilde{g}=\textrm{e}^{2\sigma}g
for some
\sigma\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma\setminus\supp D)
. We define the
renormalized Polyakov anomaly
against metric
g
to be

\mathcal{R}A_{\Sigma}(\widetilde{g},g)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\frac{1}{24\pi}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\Big[\int_{\Sigma\setminus\bigcup_{i=1}^{p}B_{\varepsilon}(z_{i},\widetilde{g})}(|\nabla_{g}\sigma|_{g}^{2}+2K_{g}\sigma)\mathrm{d}V_{g}+2\pi\sum_{i=1}^{p}\frac{\gamma_{i}^{2}}{1+\gamma_{i}}\log(\varepsilon)\Big],

(4.2.25)

where
B_{\varepsilon}(z_{i},\widetilde{g})
is the metric disk of radius
\varepsilon
centered at
z_{i}
under the metric
\widetilde{g}
.

Remark 4.2.4
.

To show that
\mathcal{R}A_{\Sigma}(\widetilde{g},g)<\infty
we first show that it is true for
g
locally equal to
|\mathrm{d}w_{j}|^{2}
near each
z_{j}
with the coordinate
w_{j}
coming from lemma
4.4.1
(Troyanov form). In this case the result follows from corollary
4.4.7
. Then we use lemma
4.5.1
with
g_{0}
being the special Troyanov form and
g_{1}
generic, to extend to the case of a generic smooth
g
.

Remark 4.2.5
.

This method of renormalization dates back to Cauchy and Hadamard as they defined “principal values” of divergent integrals.

Definition 4.2.8
.

Consider a conformal field theory with central charge
c\in\mathbb{R}
on the Riemann surface
\Sigma
and let
\widetilde{g}
be a generalized conformal metric representing
D=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\gamma_{j}z_{j}
with
\gamma_{j}>-1
. We define the
renormalized partition function
of
(\Sigma,\widetilde{g})
to be

\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma,\widetilde{g})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\exp\big(c\mathcal{R}A_{\Sigma}(\widetilde{g},g)\big)\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma,g),

(4.2.26)

where
g
is any smooth conformal metric on
\Sigma
such that
\widetilde{g}=\textrm{e}^{2\sigma}g
for some
\sigma\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma\setminus\supp D)
.

Remark 4.2.6
.

Lemma
4.5.1
ensures that
\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma,\widetilde{g})
is independent from the choice of reference metric
g
.

Physical Interpretation

In this section we explain in detail the so-called
entanglement entropies
of quantum systems and a technique for computing them using
path integrals
, called the
replica trick
in the physics community
[
CC09
,
Hea19
,
RT17
,
Wit24
]
. This leads us to consider precisely the ratios of the form shown in (
4.1.1
) and to expect that they behave like correlation functions. We remark that what we present here is more of a conjectural, heursitic framework than rigorous proofs.

Partial Trace and Entanglement

In this subsection we discuss some generalities on describing entanglement between quantum systems. We start with a generic quantum system associated to a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. Recall that a
(quantum) statistical ensemble
(or
statistical state
) on
\mathcal{H}
is represented by a nonnegative trace class operator
\rho
on
\mathcal{H}
with
\ttr_{\mathcal{H}}(\rho)=1
. This is usually called a
density operator
. Denote by
\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
,
\mathcal{J}_{\infty}(\mathcal{H})
and
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
the trace class, compact and bounded operators on
\mathcal{H}
.

Now suppose the quantum system can be decomposed into two subsystems
A
and
B
. In other words, assume that the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
is a tensor product:

\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}.

(4.3.1)

In order to describe the relation of the subsystems to the whole system, a useful operation is called the
partial trace
. This corresponds to taking trace “over one component” and one is left with an operator acting on the “remainder component”. It could be defined rigorously as follows.
For each
\rho\in\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
, consider the linear functional

C\longmapsto\ttr_{\mathcal{H}}(\rho(C\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{B}))

(4.3.2)

for
C\in\mathcal{J}_{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
. We have

\big|\ttr_{\mathcal{H}}\big(\rho(C\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{B})\big)\big|\leqslant\big\lVert\rho\big\rVert_{\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mathcal{H})}\big\lVert C\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{B}\big\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})}=\big\lVert\rho\big\rVert_{\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mathcal{H})}\big\lVert C\big\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{A})}.

(4.3.3)

Thus (
4.3.3
) defines a bounded linear functional on
\mathcal{J}_{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
with norm
\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mathcal{H})}
. Since
\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
is the Schatten dual of the ideal
\mathcal{J}_{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
we obtain a unique representative
\ttr_{B}(\rho)\in\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
.

Definition 4.3.1
.

Let the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
be a tensor product as in (
4.3.1
). Then the
partial trace over
\mathcal{H}_{B}
is the linear map

\left.\begin{array}[]{rcl}\ttr_{B}:\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mathcal{H})&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mathcal{H}_{A}),\\
\rho&\longmapsto&\ttr_{B}(\rho),\end{array}\right.

(4.3.4)

such that
\ttr_{B}(\rho)
is the unique operator on
\mathcal{H}_{A}
satisfying

\ttr_{\mathcal{H}_{A}}\big(\ttr_{B}(\rho)C\big)=\ttr_{\mathcal{H}}\big(\rho(C\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{B})\big)

(4.3.5)

for all bounded operators
C\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
.

Remark 4.3.1
.

For more details see Simon
[
Sim77
]
pages 269-270, and also
[
Sim05
]
theorem 3.2. In particular, from (
4.3.3
) one can see that the partial trace is continuous with respect to the
\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
- and
\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
-norms (trace norms), and by taking
C=|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|
,
\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{A}
, in (
4.3.5
) one could also see that
\ttr_{B}(\rho)
is nonnegative if
\rho
is.

Remark 4.3.2
.

Restricted to rank-one operators, we have

\ttr_{B}\big(|\varphi_{A}\otimes\varphi_{B}\rangle\langle\psi_{A}\otimes\psi_{B}|\big)=\langle\psi_{B}|\varphi_{B}\rangle|\varphi_{A}\rangle\langle\psi_{A}|,

(4.3.6)

with
|\varphi_{A}\rangle
,
|\psi_{A}\rangle\in\mathcal{H}_{A}
,
|\varphi_{B}\rangle
,
|\psi_{B}\rangle\in\mathcal{H}_{B}
. Here the notation
|w\rangle\langle v|
for
w
,
v\in\mathcal{H}
denotes the operator mapping any
u\in\mathcal{H}
to
\left\langle v,u\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}w
. See also example
4.3.1
.

Definition 4.3.2
.

Suppose
\rho
is a density operator over
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}
. Then the
reduced density operator over
A
is defined by

\rho_{A}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\ttr_{B}(\rho).

(4.3.7)

It follows from remark
4.3.1
that
\rho_{A}
is a density operator over
\mathcal{H}_{A}
.

Definition 4.3.3
.

Suppose
\rho
is a density operator over
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}
. We define

\displaystyle\textrm{{the entanglement entropy (over }}A\textrm{{)}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{A}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}-\ttr_{\mathcal{H}_{A}}(\rho_{A}\log\rho_{A}),

(4.3.8)

\displaystyle\textrm{{the }}n\textrm{{-th R\'{e}nyi entropy (over }}A\textrm{{)}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{(n)}_{A}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\frac{1}{1-n}\log\ttr_{\mathcal{H}_{A}}(\rho_{A}^{n}),\quad\textrm{for }n>1.

(4.3.9)

Remark 4.3.3
.

In general the quantities
\mathcal{S}_{A}
and
\mathcal{S}_{A}^{(n)}
defined above do not equal
\mathcal{S}_{B}
and
\mathcal{S}_{B}^{(n)}
, the corresponding quantities defined the other way round by first taking partial trace over
A
. However,
\mathcal{S}_{A}=\mathcal{S}_{B}
and
\mathcal{S}_{A}^{(n)}=\mathcal{S}_{B}^{(n)}
do hold if
\rho
is a
pure state
, namely a rank-one projector written as
\rho=|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|
for some
|\psi\rangle\in\mathcal{H}
. Therefore, for pure states it makes sense to define
the
entanglement and Rényi entropies
\mathcal{S}:=\mathcal{S}_{A}=\mathcal{S}_{B}
and
\mathcal{S}^{(n)}:=\mathcal{S}_{A}^{(n)}=\mathcal{S}_{B}^{(n)}
. In the general case, one could then consider another quantity called the
mutual information
, defined as

\mathcal{I}_{A,B}^{(n)}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathcal{S}_{A}^{(n)}+\mathcal{S}_{B}^{(n)}-\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{H}}^{(n)},

(4.3.10)

where
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{H}}^{(n)}
is defined the same way as (
4.3.9
) by just removing the subscript
A
.

Remark 4.3.4
.

If
\rho
is trace class, then
\rho_{A}
is trace class and the complex power
\rho_{A}^{z}
is well-defined for
\mathfrak{Re}(z)>1
. If moreover
\mathcal{S}_{A}<\infty
, one then has the relation

\mathcal{S}_{A}=\lim_{z\to 1^{+}}\mathcal{S}_{A}^{(z)}=-\lim_{z\to 1^{+}}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\ttr_{\mathcal{H}_{A}}(\rho_{A}^{z}).

(4.3.11)

However, for continuum QFTs the tensor product decomposition
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}
is generally not well-defined, and the only approach available which produces reasonable answers seems to be employing the so-called
replica
interpretation to be explained in the next subsection, or the equivalent approach by
twist fields
(both avoid defining the tensor product). These concerns the quantities
\ttr_{\mathcal{H}_{A}}(\rho_{A}^{n})
for positive integral
n
and is also what we will focus on for this paper.

Example 4.3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{H}=\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}=:\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}
, in this order. Consider the standard basis
e_{1}\otimes e_{1}
,
e_{1}\otimes e_{2}
,
e_{2}\otimes e_{1}
and
e_{2}\otimes e_{2}
(in this order) of
\mathcal{H}
where
e_{1}
,
e_{2}
are standard base vectors of
\mathbb{C}^{2}
. Then for
\rho\in\End(\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2})
written

\rho=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}a_{11}&a_{12}&a_{13}&a_{14}\\
a_{21}&a_{22}&a_{23}&a_{24}\\
a_{31}&a_{32}&a_{33}&a_{34}\\
a_{41}&a_{42}&a_{43}&a_{44}\end{array}\right),

(4.3.12)

we have

\ttr_{B}(\rho)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a_{11}+a_{22}&a_{13}+a_{24}\\
a_{31}+a_{42}&a_{33}+a_{44}\end{array}\right),\quad\quad\ttr_{A}(\rho)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a_{11}+a_{33}&a_{12}+a_{34}\\
a_{21}+a_{43}&a_{22}+a_{44}\end{array}\right).

(4.3.13)

Also, if
\rho=\rho_{A}\otimes\rho_{B}
is a Kronecker product, we have
\ttr_{B}(\rho)=\ttr(\rho_{B})\rho_{A}
,
\ttr_{A}(\rho)=\ttr(\rho_{A})\rho_{B}
.

Path Integrals and Replica

Now let us describe the geometric framework where the quantities
\mathcal{S}_{A}
and
\mathcal{S}_{A}^{(n)}
get represented by
path integrals
, as presented in
[
CC09
]
. Here we shall describe the pictures heuristically and
not attempt at any rigor
. For this paper we focus on
1+1
dimensional field theories where the space is either the real line or a circle with a specific perimeter (as a Riemannian manifold), denoted generally by
X
, and space-time a 2-dimensional Riemannian surface with or without boundary denoted
\Sigma
(for example,
\Sigma=X\times[0,T]
or
X\times\mathbb{R}
). Each field theory comes with specified
field configuration spaces
over space and space-time, denoted
\conf(X)
and
\conf(\Sigma)
, as well as over their sub-regions. Typically
\conf(X)=\mathrm{Map}(X,\mathscr{V})
, the space of
maps
from
X
to a
spin value space
/
target space

\mathscr{V}
. Such configuration spaces should allow

1.

restriction of a configuration onto a subregion
A\subset X
(or
\Sigma
), heuristically a map

\left.\begin{array}[]{rcl}\conf(X)&\longrightarrow&\conf(A),\\
\phi&\longmapsto&\phi|_{A}.\end{array}\right.

(4.3.14)

In other words one is allowed to
localize
the field;

2.

each configuration
\phi\in\conf(X)
to be recovered from the pair
(\phi|_{A},\phi|_{A^{c}})
of its restrictions onto
A
and
A^{c}=X\setminus A
, and moreover any pair of configurations on complementary subregions should combine into a global configuration; this is to say heuristically,

\conf(X)\cong\conf(A)\times\conf(A^{c}).

(4.3.15)

Remark 4.3.5
.

The above statements are rigorous in the case
X=\Lambda
is a discrete lattice, and
\conf(\Lambda)=\mathrm{Map}(\Lambda,\mathscr{V})
. Here “subregions” correspond to subsets of lattice sites.

Remark 4.3.6
.

Suppose
\Sigma=X\times[0,T]
. Then
\conf(\Sigma)
could be considered as the space of “paths” through which a configuration over
X
evolves across the time interval
[0,T]
. Indeed, if we have taken
\conf(\Sigma)
to be
\mathrm{Map}(\Sigma,\mathscr{V})
, then we would have simply
\mathrm{Map}(X\times[0,T],\mathscr{V})\cong\mathrm{Map}([0,T],\mathrm{Map}(X,\mathscr{V}))
. The integral of (
4.3.18
) below is thus an integral over a space of “paths”.

We now describe heuristically the Hilbert space and the time evolution. For the Hilbert space one takes

\mathcal{H}_{X}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}L^{2}(\conf(X),\mathcal{L}),

(4.3.16)

where
\mathcal{L}
denotes the non-existent
Lebesgue measure
on the configuration space
\conf(X)
. Under the path-integral formalism (Euclidean
3
3

3

See the first paragraph of subsection
4.3.3
.
) time evolution across
\Sigma=X\times[0,T]
, namely over time
T
, is represented by the integral operator

\left.\begin{array}[]{rcl}U_{T}:\mathcal{H}_{X}&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{H}_{X},\\
F&\longmapsto&(U_{T}F)(\psi)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\displaystyle\int_{\conf(X)}\mathcal{A}_{T}(\psi,\varphi)F(\varphi)\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{L}(\varphi),\end{array}\right.

(4.3.17)

with the integral kernel

\mathcal{A}_{T}(\psi,\varphi)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\int_{\left\{\phi\in\conf(\Sigma)\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ \begin{subarray}{c}\phi|_{X\times\{0\}}=\varphi,\\
\phi|_{X\times\{T\}}=\psi\end{subarray}\right\}}\textrm{e}^{-S_{\mathrm{EQFT}}(\phi)}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{L}(\phi),

(4.3.18)

where now we integrate against the still non-existent Lebesgue measure on
\conf(\Sigma)
(with the indicated boundary conditions), and where
S_{\mathrm{EQFT}}
is the
action functional
(“E” stands for “Euclidean”) of the specific theory at hand.

The above recipe for time evolution does not involve the fact that
\Sigma
is
X\times[0,T]
, and indeed it makes sense for space-times
\Sigma
having any kind of geometry as long as
\partial\Sigma=\partial_{\mathrm{ini}}\Sigma\sqcup\partial_{\mathrm{ter}}\Sigma
and
\partial_{\mathrm{ini}}\Sigma\cong\partial_{\mathrm{ter}}\Sigma\cong X
, namely its boundary has two components (called
initial
and
terminal
) both isometric to
X
. Generalizing still further, Segal
[
Seg04
]
proposed a set of axioms that defines a QFT abstractly as a rule that associates Hilbert spaces to space manifolds and evolution operators to space-time manifolds that “connects” them (cobordisms).

Now denote by
U_{\Sigma}
and
\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}
the evolution operator and its integral kernel corresponding to the space-time piece
\Sigma
. Two important axioms of Segal (and Atiyah) are written as

(composition)

if one has two space-time pieces
\Sigma_{1}
,
\Sigma_{2}
and one glues them together by identifying
\partial_{\mathrm{ter}}\Sigma_{1}
with
\partial_{\mathrm{ini}}\Sigma_{2}
via
X
, obtaining the piece
\Sigma_{2}\circ\Sigma_{1}
, then we have

U_{\Sigma_{2}\circ\Sigma_{1}}=U_{\Sigma_{2}}\circ U_{\Sigma_{1}};

(4.3.19)

(trace)

if one glues the space-time
\Sigma
with itself by identifying
\partial_{\mathrm{ini}}\Sigma
and
\partial_{\mathrm{ter}}\Sigma
via
X
, obtaining
\check{\Sigma}
, then

\ttr_{\mathcal{H}}(U_{\Sigma})=\mathcal{Z}(\check{\Sigma}),

(4.3.20)

where the number

\mathcal{Z}(\check{\Sigma})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\int_{\conf(\check{\Sigma})}\textrm{e}^{-S_{\mathrm{EQFT}}(\phi)}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{L}(\phi)

(4.3.21)

is the
partition function
(same as what appears in definition
4.2.1
).

Remark 4.3.7
.

Comparing (
4.3.21
) with (
4.3.18
), one sees that the trace axiom corresponds formally to the fact that the “trace” of an integral operator is the integral along the diagonal of its kernel. For rigorous results concerning this statement see Simon
[
Sim15
]
section 3.11.

Now we try to incorporate considerations of subregions
A\subset X
into the framework described above. For simplicity we will take
A
to be a finite interval (remember
X
is either a circle or the real line). First of all, following (
4.3.15
), we have formally

\mathcal{H}_{X}\overset{\mathrm{heu}}{\cong}L^{2}\big(\conf(A)\times\conf(A^{c}),\mathcal{L}_{\conf(A)}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{\conf(A^{c})}\big)\overset{\mathrm{heu}}{\cong}\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{A^{c}}.

(4.3.22)

To include the partial trace, the trace axiom is now slightly extended by adding that taking the partial trace (over
A^{c}
) corresponds to gluing “partially” (along
A^{c}
but not
A
), leaving out a “slit” with two sides denoted
A_{-}
and
A_{+}
. In terms of the integral kernels, if we denote by
\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}^{A}
the kernel of
\ttr_{A^{c}}(U_{\Sigma})
acting on
\mathcal{H}_{A}
, then we have

\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}^{A}(\psi_{A},\varphi_{A})\overset{\mathrm{heu}}{=}\int_{\conf(A^{c})}\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}(\psi_{A},\sigma_{A^{c}},\varphi_{A},\sigma_{A^{c}})\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{L}(\sigma_{A^{c}}).

(4.3.23)

Here
\ttr_{A^{c}}(U_{\Sigma})
as an operator acting on
\mathcal{H}_{A}
, the Hilbert space associated to an interval, is represented by a surface (space-time) with a “slit” (or
branch cut
/
defect line
) where the two “sides” of the slit are identified with two copies of the interval. Accordingly, we must also extend the composition axiom to incorporate this situation, namely to allow gluing of surfaces with slits along sides which represents composition of operators acting on Hilbert spaces over intervals. We shall assume that this has been done in the obvious manner.

Finally we arrive at an interpretation of a quantity of the form
\ttr_{\mathcal{H}_{A}}(\ttr_{A^{c}}(U_{\Sigma})^{n})
which appears in the expression for the Rényi entropy (
4.3.9
). Indeed, one starts with the surface
\Sigma
and glue its two ends “partially” along the parts corresponding to
A^{c}
in
X
. We denote the resulting “surface with slit” by
\check{\Sigma}\setminus A
.
Then the two sides of the slit gets identified with two copies of
A
, which we denote by
A_{\pm}
, that corresponds to approaching
A
from the two sides within
\check{\Sigma}\setminus A
. Next, we take
n
copies of
\check{\Sigma}\setminus A
and glue them in a
cyclic manner
, that is, we glue
A_{+}^{(j)}
on the
j
-th copy to
A_{-}^{(j+1)}
on the
(j+1)
-th copy,
1\leqslant j<n
, and finally
A_{+}^{(n)}
to
A_{-}^{(1)}
. We denote the surface thus obtained by
\check{\Sigma}_{n}
, called the
n
-th
replica
.
Importantly,
\check{\Sigma}_{n}
comes equipped with a
metric
which is induced from the metric on the original space-time
\Sigma
. Equivalently, there is an obvious map
f_{n}:\check{\Sigma}_{n}\longrightarrow\check{\Sigma}
, sending the end points of
A
to themselves, and any other point to its counterpart on the original copy.
Then
f_{n}
is a branched
n
-sheeted cyclic covering with critical points being the two end points of the copies of
A
, and the metric on
\check{\Sigma}_{n}
is the pull-back under
f_{n}
of the original metric on
\check{\Sigma}
(on
\Sigma
). This will be a metric with
conical singularities
at the two end points of
A
which are not duplicated. If we assume the
extended
versions of the composition and trace axioms discussed above, then we find

\ttr_{\mathcal{H}_{A}}\big(\ttr_{A^{c}}(U_{\Sigma})^{n}\big)=\mathcal{Z}\big(\check{\Sigma}_{n}\big).

(4.3.24)

The geometric correspondence is summarized in the table below.

Operation

Picture

evolution operator
U_{\Sigma}

\ttr_{\mathcal{H}}(U_{\Sigma})

\ttr_{A^{c}}(U_{\Sigma})

\ttr_{A}\big(\ttr_{A^{c}}(U_{\Sigma})^{n}\big)

Density Operators Represented by Imaginary-time Evolution

We are left with the question: which density operators are also evolution operators across some space-time? To begin with, we reemphasize: our space-time metrics all have Euclidean signature and we work with
Wick rotated
/
Euclidean
QFT. More precisely, the action functional
S_{\mathrm{EQFT}}(\phi)
, which usually involves itself the underlying space-time metric, is real-valued and the exponential weight
\exp(-S_{\mathrm{EQFT}}(\phi))
positive in (
4.3.18
) and (
4.3.21
). The evolution operators defined in this way is said to be in
imaginary time
. For actual Lorentzian QFT one must use
\exp(\mathrm{i}S_{\mathrm{QFT}})
instead of
\exp(-S_{\mathrm{EQFT}})
, where the action
S_{\mathrm{QFT}}
is also written in terms of the Lorentzian metric.

Next, which imaginary-time evolution operators are trace class and nonnegative, serving as candidates for a density operator? In this regard it would be instructive to introduce the third axiom of Segal’s:

(adjoint)

let
\Sigma^{*}
denote the same space-time as
\Sigma
but with the identification of the initial and terminal boundaries
reversed
, then

U_{\Sigma^{*}}=U_{\Sigma}^{\dagger},

(4.3.25)

where
\dagger
denotes the operator adjoint
4
4

4

More precisely, this is a requirement for
unitary
QFTs, and we restrict to this case throughout our discussion.
.

From this, together with the two previous axioms, one could see that Euclidean evolution across a cobordism naturally corresponds to
Hilbert-Schmidt
operators. Indeed,

\ttr_{\mathcal{H}}\big(U_{\Sigma}^{\dagger}U_{\Sigma}\big)=\ttr_{\mathcal{H}}\big(U_{\Sigma^{*}\circ\Sigma}\big)=\mathcal{Z}\big((\Sigma^{*}\circ\Sigma)^{\vee}\big)\quad(<\infty),

(4.3.26)

where
(\Sigma^{*}\circ\Sigma)^{\vee}
is the “double” of
\Sigma
. This “shows” that
U_{\Sigma}
is Hilbert-Schmidt. Immediately from the same expression one also sees that a cobordism of the form
\Sigma^{*}\circ\Sigma
naturally gives a nonnegative trace class operator which is what we wanted. Such a cobordism has, in other words, a
reflection symmetry
exchanging its initial and terminal boundaries, and whose invariant set is exactly isomorphic to a copy of
X
as a Riemannian submanifold.

Remark 4.3.8
.

A somewhat technical requirement is that the boundaries of
\Sigma
be geodesic so that its double would be smooth.

Remark 4.3.9
.

For certain models the (ordinary) Segal axioms have been fully and rigorously constructed. See, for example, the work
[
Lin24
]
of the second author and also
[
GKRV21
]
. In these cases
U_{\Sigma}
is rigorously shown to be Hilbert-Schmidt. The main difficulty lies in defining (
4.3.18
) and (
4.3.21
) rigorously, in which case it implies
\mathcal{Z}(\check{\Sigma})<\infty
, and also in showing the generic composition axiom out of these definitions.

Now we give some examples.

Example 4.3.2
.

Assume that one is given a
Hamiltonian operator

H
acting on
\mathcal{H}_{X}
, and let
\beta>0
. Then the so-called
thermal state
(or
Gibbs state
) at
inverse temperature

\beta
is given by the density operator

\rho_{\beta}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\frac{\textrm{e}^{-\beta H}}{\ttr_{\mathcal{H}}(\textrm{e}^{-\beta H})}.

(4.3.27)

The operator
\textrm{e}^{-\beta H}
gives the evolution over imaginary time
\mathrm{i}\beta
and could be represented by a path integral (
4.3.18
) over the cylinder
\Sigma_{\beta}:=X\times[0,\beta]
with a specific action related to
H
. Now we denote by
\check{\Sigma}_{n,\beta}
the closed surface obtained by gluing
n
copies of
\check{\Sigma}_{\beta}\setminus A
cyclicly along the slit
A
, as explained at the end of the last subsection. Then the replica trick yields

\ttr_{A}\big(\ttr_{A^{c}}(\rho_{\beta})^{n}\big)=\frac{\mathcal{Z}(\check{\Sigma}_{n,\beta})}{\mathcal{Z}(\check{\Sigma}_{\beta})^{n}}.

(4.3.28)

We remark again that
\check{\Sigma}_{n,\beta}
comes equipped with the “replica metric” which is induced from
\Sigma_{\beta}
.

Example 4.3.3
.

Here we consider cobordisms which are disconnected,
\Sigma=\Sigma_{1}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}
, with
\partial\Sigma_{1}\cong\partial\Sigma_{2}\cong X
. We mark
\partial\Sigma_{1}
as initial and
\partial\Sigma_{2}
as terminal. In this case

\conf(\Sigma)=\conf(\Sigma_{1})\times\conf(\Sigma_{2}),\quad\quad\mathcal{L}_{\conf(\Sigma)}=\mathcal{L}_{\conf(\Sigma_{1})}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{\conf(\Sigma_{2})},

(4.3.29)

and assume (a very weak form of)
locality
of the action (no interaction between disjoint space-times), that is,

S_{\Sigma}(\phi)=S_{\Sigma_{1}}(\phi_{1})+S_{\Sigma_{2}}(\phi_{2}),

(4.3.30)

with
\phi=(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})
, then we have formally

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}(\psi,\varphi)

\displaystyle=\iint_{\left\{\begin{subarray}{c}\phi_{1}\in\conf(\Sigma_{1}),\\
\phi_{1}|_{\partial\Sigma_{1}}=\varphi\end{subarray}\right\}\times\left\{\begin{subarray}{c}\phi_{2}\in\conf(\Sigma_{2}),\\
\phi_{2}|_{\partial\Sigma_{2}}=\psi\end{subarray}\right\}}\textrm{e}^{-S_{\Sigma_{1}}(\phi_{1})}\textrm{e}^{-S_{\Sigma_{2}}(\phi_{2})}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{L}_{\conf(\Sigma_{1})}(\phi_{1})\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{L}_{\conf(\Sigma_{2})}(\phi_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma_{1}}(\varphi)\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma_{2}}(\psi),

(4.3.31)

where
\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma_{1}}
and
\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma_{2}}
are the amplitudes associated to
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
individually, defined by the same formula (
4.3.18
) in the case of having just one boundary component (sometimes (
4.3.31
) itself is taken as an axiom, see
[
Gaw99
]
page 766). We see that in this case
U_{\Sigma}
is a rank-one operator. Written more suggestively,

U_{\Sigma}=\big|\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma_{2}}\big\rangle\big\langle\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma_{1}}\big|.

(4.3.32)

We normalize by putting
\rho_{\Sigma}:=U_{\Sigma}/\ttr(U_{\Sigma})
, then following the replica trick we have

\ttr_{A}\big(\ttr_{A^{c}}(\rho_{\Sigma})^{n}\big)=\frac{\mathcal{Z}(\check{\Sigma}_{n})}{\mathcal{Z}(\check{\Sigma})^{n}},

(4.3.33)

where
\check{\Sigma}
is the closed surface obtained by gluing
\Sigma_{1}
with
\Sigma_{2}
along
X
, and
\check{\Sigma}_{n}
by gluing
n
copies of
\check{\Sigma}\setminus A
along the slit
A
, equipped with the induced metric. In particular, if
\Sigma_{1}=\Sigma_{2}=\mathbb{D}
, the unit disk, equipped with a flat-at-the-boundary metric (namely it could be written
|z|^{-2}|\mathrm{d}z|^{2}
in some complex coordinate on an annulus around
\partial\mathbb{D}
), then
\rho_{\Sigma}
defined as above is metric independent (provided flat at the boundary) and represents (projection onto) the
vacuum state
, usually written
|0\rangle\langle 0|
(see
[
Gaw99
]
page 768).

Geometric Lemmas

To begin with, we record some basic conformal relations that will be used repeatedly throughout the paper. These relations are local in nature as they concern only what happens in an arbitrarily small neighborhood around each point. Namely, let
\Sigma
be a smooth
surface
and denote by
\mathrm{d}V_{g}
,
\nabla_{g}
,
K_{g}
,
\Delta_{g}
and
\nabla_{v}^{g}
respectively the volume (area) form, gradient, Gauss curvature (half the scalar curvature), Laplacian and the Levi-Civita covariant derivative in the direction
v\in T_{z}\Sigma
under the Riemannian metric
g
, and let
h\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
, then we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}V_{\textrm{e}^{2h}g}

\displaystyle=\textrm{e}^{2h}\mathrm{d}V_{g},

(4.4.1)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\textrm{e}^{2h}g}

\displaystyle=\textrm{e}^{-2h}\nabla_{g},

(4.4.2)

\displaystyle K_{\textrm{e}^{2h}g}

\displaystyle=\textrm{e}^{-2h}\left(-\Delta_{g}h+K_{g}\right),

(4.4.3)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\textrm{e}^{2h}g}

\displaystyle=\textrm{e}^{-2h}\Delta_{g}.

(4.4.4)

\displaystyle\nabla_{v}^{\textrm{e}^{2h}g}X

\displaystyle=\nabla_{v}^{g}X+(vh)X+(Xh)v-\left\langle v,X\right\rangle_{g}\nabla_{g}h.

(4.4.5)

The relation (
4.4.3
) is also called
Liouville’s equation
.

Technical Remarks on Conical Singularities

The principal local regularity result around a conical singularity in our sense (definition
4.2.5
) is the following obtained by Troyanov
[
Tro90
]
.

Lemma 4.4.1

(
[
Tro90
]
proposition 3.2)
.

Let
\Sigma
be a closed Riemann surface and
\widetilde{g}
a generalized conformal metric on
\Sigma
with a conical singularity at
z_{0}\in\Sigma
of exponent
\gamma
. Then there exists a complex coordinate
w
defined on a neighborhood
U\ni z_{0}
, with
w(z_{0})=0
, such that

\widetilde{g}|_{U\setminus z_{0}}=|w|^{2\gamma}\textrm{e}^{2\varphi_{U,w}}|\mathrm{d}w|^{2},

(4.4.6)

such that
\varphi_{U,w}
satisfies the conditions of definition
4.2.5
as well as

\varphi_{U,w}(z)=\varphi_{U,w}(z_{0})+\mathcal{O}(|w|^{2\gamma+2}),\quad\textrm{and}\quad\partial_{z}\varphi_{U,w},\penalty\ \partial_{\overline{z}}\varphi_{U,w}=\mathcal{O}(|w|^{2\gamma+1}).

(4.4.7)

In particular,
\partial_{r}\varphi_{U,w}=\mathcal{O}(|w|^{2\gamma+1})
where
r
is the radial coordinate
r(z)=|w(z)|
under
|\mathrm{d}w|^{2}
.
\Box

Troyanov showed further that a neighborhood (still denoted
U
) of the conical singularity could in fact be equivalently and more intrinsically described by a set of
polar coordinates
. That is, there is a map

h:[0,\varrho]\times\mathbb{S}_{\Theta}^{1}\longrightarrow U,

(4.4.8)

where
\mathbb{S}_{\Theta}^{1}
denotes the Riemannian circle with perimeter
\Theta=2\pi(\gamma+1)
, such that

1.

h(r,\theta)=z_{0}
iff
r=0
;

2.

h|_{(0,\varrho]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\Theta}}
is a locally bi-Lipschitz homeomorphism onto
U\setminus z_{0}
;

3.

we have

h^{*}(\widetilde{g}|_{U\setminus z_{0}})=\mathrm{d}r^{2}+\omega(r,\theta)^{2}\mathrm{d}\theta^{2},

(4.4.9)

where
\omega:(0,\varrho]\times\mathbb{S}_{\Theta}^{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a function such that
0<c_{1}\leqslant\omega(r,\theta)\leqslant c_{2}
for some constants
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
for all
(r,\theta)
and
\lim_{r\to 0}\omega(r,\theta)/r=1
for all
\theta\in\mathbb{S}_{\Theta}^{1}
.

By the last requirement we have

\Theta(r)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\omega(r,\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\theta\asymp r\Theta=2\pi r(\gamma+1),\quad\quad r\to 0,

(4.4.10)

and thus
2\pi(\gamma+1)
is sometimes called the
cone angle
. Moreover, the regularity of the function
\omega
as well as
h
itself could be deduced from the regularity of the curvature of
\widetilde{g}
on
U\setminus z_{0}
(
[
Tro90
]
theorem 4.1). There are certain recent works in the literature which begin naturally with this latter point of view (
[
AKR20
]
for example). But we stick to definition
4.2.5
throughout this article although utilization of polar coordinates may (or may not) aide with certain proofs.

Remark 4.4.1
.

In the literature people have considered more restrictive classes of allowable conical metrics by assuming more regularity on the regular metric potential (see
[
Kal21
]
section 2.1). The principle is to respect the correct scaling property under dilations centered at the cone points. Especially for the conical metrics with
constant curvature
, the corresponding regular metric potentials (against coordinate metrics) are shown to be
dilation analytic
. This translates, in our notations, roughly into saying that sufficiently near
z_{0}
,
\varphi_{U,w}
would be a real analytic function of
|w|^{\gamma+1}
, extendable over a neighborhood of zero, in each direction respectively (this is clearly an enhancement of (
4.4.7
)). However, due to the simplicity of our method we only need a very rough scaling property that is already deducible from lemma
4.4.1
, which we treat in the section below.

Dilation Properties at the Cone Point

Lemma 4.4.2
.

Let
\Sigma
be a closed Riemann surface and
\widetilde{g}
a generalized conformal metric on
\Sigma
with a conical singularity at
z_{0}\in\Sigma
of exponent
\gamma
. Then

\widetilde{r}(z)=\frac{\textrm{e}^{\varphi(z_{0})}}{\gamma+1}r(z)^{\gamma+1}+\mathcal{O}\left(r^{3\gamma+3}\right),\quad\quad z\to z_{0},

(4.4.11)

where
\widetilde{r}(z):=d_{\widetilde{g}}(z,z_{0})
,
r(z):=|w(z)|
,
z\neq z_{0}
,
\varphi:=\varphi_{U,w}
as in lemma
4.4.1
and
w
is the complex coordinate from lemma
4.4.1
. In particular, we see that
\widetilde{r}(z)\asymp r(z)^{\gamma+1}
as
z\to z_{0}
.

Proof.

By (
4.4.7
) we have

0<\textrm{e}^{\varphi(z_{0})}-\alpha r(z)^{2\gamma+2}\leqslant\textrm{e}^{\varphi(z)}\leqslant\textrm{e}^{\varphi(z_{0})}+\alpha r(z)^{2\gamma+2}

(4.4.12)

for some
\alpha>0
, in some smaller neighborhood
U_{1}\subset U
. Now fix
z\in U_{1}
, let
c(s)=w^{-1}(sw(z)/|w(z)|)
be defined for
s\in[0,r(z)]
(the unit speed geodesic under
|\mathrm{d}w|^{2}
). Then
\widetilde{r}(z)
is majorized by the length of
c
under
\widetilde{g}
, namely

\widetilde{r}(z)\leqslant\int_{0}^{r}|c^{\prime}(s)|_{\widetilde{g}}\,\mathrm{d}s=\int_{0}^{r}s^{\gamma}\textrm{e}^{\varphi(c(s))}\,\mathrm{d}s\leqslant\frac{\textrm{e}^{\varphi(z_{0})}r^{\gamma+1}}{\gamma+1}+\alpha\frac{r^{3\gamma+3}}{3\gamma+3},

(4.4.13)

by (
4.4.12
). On the other hand, the length under
\widetilde{g}
of any curve inside
U_{1}
is bounded below by its length under the metric

g_{1}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}r^{2\gamma}\big(\textrm{e}^{\varphi(z_{0})}-\alpha r^{2\gamma+2}\big)^{2}\cdot|\mathrm{d}w|^{2}=f(r)^{2}\cdot|\mathrm{d}w|^{2}.

(4.4.14)

defined on
U_{1}
. By (
4.4.5
) we know that
c
would now reparametrize into a geodesic under
g_{1}
(minimizing since
g_{1}
is radial with respect to the geodesic coordinates of
g
). Therefore we obtain

\widetilde{r}(z)\geqslant\int_{0}^{r}|c^{\prime}(s)|_{g_{1}}\,\mathrm{d}s=\frac{\textrm{e}^{\varphi(z_{0})}r^{\gamma+1}}{\gamma+1}-\frac{\alpha r^{3\gamma+3}}{3\gamma+3}.

(4.4.15)

This gives the result.
∎

Remark 4.4.2
.

In particular, (
4.4.13
) also shows
\widetilde{r}(z)<\infty
, which is not necessarily true
a priori
.

From this lemma it follows immediately the next two corollaries.

Corollary 4.4.3
.

With the same set-up and notation as lemma
4.4.2
, we have

r(z)=(\gamma+1)^{\frac{1}{\gamma+1}}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{\varphi(z_{0})}{\gamma+1}}\widetilde{r}(z)^{\frac{1}{\gamma+1}}+\mathcal{O}(\widetilde{r}^{2+\frac{1}{\gamma+1}}),

(4.4.16)

as
z\to z_{0}
.

Proof.

This follows from a simple order analysis using Newton’s binomial formula.
∎

Corollary 4.4.4
.

Pick
h\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
and denote by
\widetilde{r}_{h}(z)
the distance from
z
to
z_{0}
under the scaled metric

\widetilde{g}_{h}|_{U}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}d_{g}(\bullet,z_{0})^{2\gamma}\textrm{e}^{2(\varphi+h)}\cdot g|_{U}.

(4.4.17)

Denote by
\delta(\varepsilon)
and
\delta_{h}(\varepsilon)
metric radii under
g
of the
\varepsilon
-metric disks under
\widetilde{g}
and
\widetilde{g}_{h}
respectively centered at
z_{0}
. Then we have

\frac{\delta(\varepsilon)}{\delta_{h}(\varepsilon)}\longrightarrow\exp\left(\frac{h(z_{0})}{\gamma+1}\right),

(4.4.18)

as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
.
\Box

Log-divergent Integrals

In this section we collect some basic computations of “toy integrals” involving functions with log-divergent singularities, which will nevertheless play a fundamental role in the proofs of the main results of this paper.

Throughout this section, we assume
(\Sigma,g)
is a Riemannian surface with smooth metric
g
, let
z_{0}\in\Sigma
and let
\sigma\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma\setminus\{z_{0}\})
such that in a neighbourhood
U
of
z_{0}
,

\sigma(z)=\gamma\log d_{g}(z,z_{0})+\varphi(z)

(4.4.19)

for some
\gamma>-1
, where
\varphi
is a function satisfying the requirements for the metric potential in definition
4.2.5
as well as (
4.4.7
) with
|\mathrm{d}w|^{2}
replaced by
g
. Formula (
4.4.27
), however, needs much weaker assumptions. We start by recalling the following basic fact.

Lemma 4.4.5
.

In the set-up as above, the function
\Delta_{g}\log d_{g}(\bullet,z_{0})
is smooth and uniformly bounded near but not coincident with
z_{0}
.

Proof.

See appendix
C.3
.
∎

Remark 4.4.3
.

A related classical result says that the perimeter
\ell_{g}(\partial B_{r}(z_{0}))
of a geodesic circle centered at
z_{0}\in\Sigma
of radius
r
has an asymptotics

\ell_{g}(\partial B_{r}(z_{0}))=2\pi r-\frac{\pi}{3}r^{3}K_{g}(z_{0})+o(r^{3}),\quad r\to 0^{+}.

(4.4.20)

Here as above,
K_{g}(z_{0})
is the Gauss curvature of
g
at
z_{0}
. See, for example,
[
Car16
]
page 296.

Lemma 4.4.6
.

In the set-up as above, let
\delta>0
. Then we have

\int_{\Sigma\setminus B_{\delta}(z_{0},g)}|\nabla_{g}\sigma|_{g}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}=-2\pi\gamma^{2}\log(\delta)-2\pi\gamma\varphi(z_{0})-\int_{\Sigma\setminus B_{\delta}}\sigma\Delta_{g}\sigma\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}+\mathcal{O}(\delta^{2\min\{\gamma,0\}+2}\log\delta)

(4.4.21)

as
\delta\to 0^{+}
, where
B_{\delta}(z_{0},g)
denotes the geodesic disk around
z_{0}
of radius
\delta
under the metric
g
.

Proof.

Denote
r(z):=d_{g}(z,z_{0})
. Integrating by parts (Green-Stokes formula) we have

LHS

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial B_{\delta}}\sigma(-\partial_{r}\sigma)\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{g}-\int_{\Sigma\setminus B_{\delta}}\sigma\Delta_{g}\sigma\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}

\displaystyle=-\gamma^{2}\underbrace{\int_{\partial B_{\delta}}\log r(\partial_{r}\log r)\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{g}}_{A}-\gamma\underbrace{\int_{\partial B_{\delta}}\varphi(\partial_{r}\log r)\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{g}}_{B}-\gamma\underbrace{\int_{\partial B_{\delta}}\log r(\partial_{r}\varphi)\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{g}}_{C}

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\underbrace{\int_{\partial B_{\delta}}\varphi(\partial_{r}\varphi)\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{g}}_{D}-\int_{\Sigma\setminus B_{\delta}}\sigma\Delta_{g}\sigma\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}.

By the assumptions (
4.4.7
) on
\varphi
and by (
4.4.20
), we have

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=\log\delta\cdot\frac{1}{\delta}(2\pi\delta+\mathcal{O}(\delta^{3}))=2\pi\log\delta+\mathcal{O}(\delta^{2}\log\delta),

(4.4.22)

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{\partial B_{\delta}}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{g}=2\pi\varphi(z_{0})+\mathcal{O}(\delta^{2}),

(4.4.23)

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=\log\delta\int_{\partial B_{\delta}}\partial_{r}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{g}=\mathcal{O}(\delta^{2\gamma+2}\log\delta),

(4.4.24)

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(\delta^{2\gamma+2}).

(4.4.25)

Adding them all up, we obtain the result. Note
2\gamma+2
may be small but is positive, so
\mathcal{O}(\delta^{2\gamma+2}\log\delta)=o(1)
.
∎

Corollary 4.4.7
.

In the set-up as above, let
\widetilde{g}
be a generalised conformal metric representing
D=\gamma z_{0}
with
\gamma>-1
, written in the form (
4.2.22
). Suppose
g
is a smooth conformal metric on
\Sigma
and
\widetilde{g}=\textrm{e}^{2\sigma}g
for some
\sigma\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma\setminus\{z_{0}\})
, so that
\sigma
is of the form (
4.4.19
). Then

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(z_{0},\widetilde{g})}|\nabla_{g}\sigma|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2\pi\gamma^{2}}{\gamma+1}\big[\log(\varepsilon)+\log(\gamma+1)-\varphi(z_{0})\big]-2\pi\gamma\varphi(z_{0})-\int_{\Sigma\setminus z_{0}}\sigma\Delta_{g}\sigma\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}+o(1)

(4.4.26)

as
\varepsilon\to 0
, where
B_{\varepsilon}(z_{0},\widetilde{g})
denotes the geodesic ball around
z_{0}
of radius
\varepsilon
under the metric
\widetilde{g}
.

Proof.

This is because
B_{\varepsilon}(z_{0},\widetilde{g})
has radius
\delta(\varepsilon)\sim((\gamma+1)\varepsilon\textrm{e}^{-\varphi(z_{0})})^{1/(\gamma+1)}
under the metric
g
by lemma
4.4.2
.
∎

Lemma 4.4.8
.

In the set-up as lemma
4.4.6
, pick further
h\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
. Then we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}\int_{\Sigma\setminus B_{\delta}(z_{0},g)}\big(\left\langle\nabla_{g}h,\nabla_{g}\sigma\right\rangle_{g}+(\Delta_{g}h)\sigma\big)\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}

\displaystyle=0,

(4.4.27)

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}\int_{\Sigma\setminus B_{\delta}(z_{0},g)}\big(\left\langle\nabla_{g}h,\nabla_{g}\sigma\right\rangle_{g}+h(\Delta_{g}\sigma)\big)\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}

\displaystyle=-2\pi\gamma h(z_{0}).

(4.4.28)

Here for (
4.4.27
) we only need the weaker assumption that
\varphi
be bounded over
U
in the expression (
4.4.19
) for
\sigma
. Moreover, the 2-form
(\left\langle\nabla_{g}h,\nabla_{g}\sigma\right\rangle_{g}+h(\Delta_{g}\sigma))\mathrm{d}V_{g}
is conformally invariant, that is,

\big(\langle\nabla_{\textrm{e}^{2\varphi}g}h,\nabla_{\textrm{e}^{2\varphi}g}\sigma\rangle_{\textrm{e}^{2\varphi}g}+h(\Delta_{\textrm{e}^{2\varphi}g}\sigma)\big)\mathrm{d}V_{\textrm{e}^{2\varphi}g}=\big(\left\langle\nabla_{g}h,\nabla_{g}\sigma\right\rangle_{g}+h(\Delta_{g}\sigma)\big)\mathrm{d}V_{g}

(4.4.29)

for any
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma\setminus\{z_{0}\})
. Therefore we have in particular

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\int_{\Sigma\setminus B_{\delta}(z_{0},g)}\big(\langle\nabla_{\widetilde{g}}h,\nabla_{\widetilde{g}}\sigma\rangle_{\widetilde{g}}+h(\Delta_{\widetilde{g}}\sigma)\big)\,\mathrm{d}V_{\widetilde{g}}=-2\pi\gamma h(z_{0}),

(4.4.30)

where
\widetilde{g}
is as defined in corollary
4.4.7
.

Proof.

We apply Green-Stokes. The first integral boils down to

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B_{\delta}}(-\partial_{r}h)\sigma\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{g}

\displaystyle=\gamma\int_{\partial B_{\delta}}(-\partial_{r}h)\log r\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{g}+\int_{\partial B_{\delta}}(-\partial_{r}h)\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{g}=\mathcal{O}(\delta\log\delta)+\mathcal{O}(\delta),

and the second to

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B_{\delta}}h(-\partial_{r}\sigma)\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{g}

\displaystyle=-\gamma\int_{\partial B_{\delta}}h\cdot\frac{1}{r}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{g}+\int_{\partial B_{\delta}}h(-\partial_{r}\varphi)\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{g}

\displaystyle=-2\pi\gamma h(z_{0})+\mathcal{O}(\delta^{2\min\{\gamma,0\}+2}),

as
\delta\to 0^{+}
, by (
4.4.7
) and (
4.4.20
). Equality (
4.4.29
) follows directly from (
4.4.1
), (
4.4.2
) and (
4.4.4
) and we obtain the rest of the lemma.
∎

The following lemma is not used in the main proof, but is important regarding remark
4.6.2
.

Lemma 4.4.9
.

Under the assumptions at the beginning of this section, let
0<\delta_{1}(\varepsilon)<\delta_{2}(\varepsilon)
be two positive functions of
\varepsilon
such that
\delta_{i}(\varepsilon)\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
,
i=1
,
2
, and
\delta_{2}/\delta_{1}\to Q>0
. Then

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\int_{B_{\delta_{2}(\varepsilon)}(z_{0},g)\setminus B_{\delta_{1}(\varepsilon)}(z_{0},g)}|\nabla_{g}\sigma|_{g}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}=2\pi\gamma^{2}\log Q.

(4.4.31)

Proof.

We abbreviate the disks
B_{\delta_{i}(\varepsilon)}(z_{0},g)
as
B(\delta_{i})
,
i=1
,
2
. Again integrating by parts we have,

\int_{B(\delta_{2})\setminus B(\delta_{1})}|\nabla_{g}\sigma|_{g}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}=-\int_{B(\delta_{2})\setminus B(\delta_{1})}\sigma\Delta_{g}\sigma\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}+\int_{\partial B(\delta_{2})}\sigma(\partial_{r}\sigma)\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{g}-\int_{\partial B(\delta_{1})}\sigma(\partial_{r}\sigma)\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{g}.

(4.4.32)

Since
\sigma
is locally integrable and
\Delta_{g}\sigma
is bounded on
\Sigma\setminus\{z_{0}\}
, we have

\int_{B(\delta_{2})\setminus B(\delta_{1})}\sigma\Delta_{g}\sigma\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}\quad\xlongrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\quad 0.

(4.4.33)

Next by (
4.4.19
) and (
4.4.7
),

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B(\delta_{2})}\varphi(\partial_{r}\varphi)\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{g},\penalty\ \int_{\partial B(\delta_{1})}\varphi(\partial_{r}\varphi)\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{g}=\mathcal{O}(\delta^{2\gamma+2})

\displaystyle\xlongrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\quad 0,

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B(\delta_{2})}\varphi(\partial_{r}\log r)\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{g},\penalty\ \int_{\partial B(\delta_{1})}\varphi(\partial_{r}\log r)\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{g}

\displaystyle\xlongrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\quad 2\pi\varphi(z_{0}).

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B(\delta_{2})}\log r(\partial_{r}\varphi)\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{g},\penalty\ \int_{\partial B(\delta_{1})}\log r(\partial_{r}\varphi)\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{g}=\mathcal{O}(\delta^{2\gamma+2}\log\delta)

\displaystyle\xlongrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\quad 0.

Therefore the only thing left is

\int_{\partial B(\delta_{2})}\frac{\log r}{r}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{g}-\int_{\partial B(\delta_{1})}\frac{\log r}{r}\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{g}\sim 2\pi\log\Big(\frac{\delta_{2}}{\delta_{1}}\Big)\quad\xlongrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\quad 2\pi\log Q.

(4.4.34)

Adding up all the above, we obtain the result.
∎

Renormalization Procedure

Consistency

As we could see from definitions
4.2.7
and
4.2.8
that a particular reference smooth metric
g
was chosen to make the definition. Now we show that a different conformal reference metric would in fact give the same partition function for the target metric
\widetilde{g}
and hence
\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma,\widetilde{g})
is invariantly defined.

Lemma 4.5.1
.

Consider a conformal field theory with central charge
c\in\mathbb{R}
on the Riemann surface
\Sigma
and let
\widetilde{g}
be a generalized conformal metric representing
D=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\gamma_{j}z_{j}
with
\gamma_{j}>-1
. Also let
g_{1}
and
g_{0}
be two smooth conformal metrics such that
g_{1}=\textrm{e}^{2h}g_{0}
for some
h\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
, and
\widetilde{g}=\textrm{e}^{2\sigma}g_{1}
for
\sigma\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma\setminus\supp D)
. Then if one of
\mathcal{R}A_{\Sigma}(\widetilde{g},g_{0})
and
\mathcal{R}A_{\Sigma}(\widetilde{g},g_{1})
defined by (
4.2.25
) is finite, so is the other, and we have

\mathcal{R}A_{\Sigma}(\widetilde{g},g_{0})-\mathcal{R}A_{\Sigma}(\widetilde{g},g_{1})=A_{\Sigma}(g_{1},g_{0}),

(4.5.1)

where
A_{\Sigma}(g_{1},g_{0})
is defined by (
4.2.5
). Hence definition (
4.2.26
) is independent of the reference metric chosen.

Proof.

Without loss of generality suppose
D=\gamma z_{0}
with
\gamma>-1
. Denote, for simplicity, by
\nabla_{i}
,
|\cdot|_{i}
,
K_{i}
and
\mathrm{d}V_{i}
respectively the gradient, metric norm, Gauss curvature and area form under the metric
g_{i}
,
i=0
,
1
. Then by definition and the relations (
4.4.1
) — (
4.4.3
), valid on
\Sigma\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(z_{0},\widetilde{g})
for any
\varepsilon>0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}A_{\Sigma}(\widetilde{g},g_{1})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{24\pi}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\Big[\int_{\Sigma\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(z_{0},\widetilde{g})}(|\nabla_{1}\sigma|_{1}^{2}+2K_{1}\sigma)\mathrm{d}V_{1}+\frac{2\pi\gamma^{2}}{\gamma+1}\log(\varepsilon)\Big]

(4.5.2)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{24\pi}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\Big[\int_{\Sigma\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(z_{0},\widetilde{g})}(|\nabla_{0}\sigma|_{0}^{2}-2(\Delta_{0}h)\sigma+2K_{0}\cdot\sigma)\mathrm{d}V_{0}+\frac{2\pi\gamma^{2}}{\gamma+1}\log(\varepsilon)\Big],

(4.5.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}A_{\Sigma}(\widetilde{g},g_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{24\pi}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\Big[\int_{\Sigma\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(z_{0},\widetilde{g})}\big(|\nabla_{0}(h+\sigma)|_{0}^{2}+2K_{0}(h+\sigma)\big)\mathrm{d}V_{0}+\frac{2\pi\gamma^{2}}{\gamma+1}\log(\varepsilon)\Big].

(4.5.4)

Therefore

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}A_{\Sigma}(\widetilde{g},g_{0})-\mathcal{R}A_{\Sigma}(\widetilde{g},g_{1})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{24\pi}\int_{\Sigma}(|\nabla_{0}h|_{0}^{2}+2K_{0}\cdot h)\,\mathrm{d}V_{0}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\frac{1}{12\pi}\underbrace{\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\int_{\Sigma\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(z_{0},\widetilde{g})}\big(\left\langle\nabla_{0}h,\nabla_{0}\sigma\right\rangle_{0}+(\Delta_{0}h)\sigma\big)\,\mathrm{d}V_{0}}_{=\penalty\ 0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{24\pi}\int_{\Sigma}(|\nabla_{0}h|_{0}^{2}+2K_{0}\cdot h)\,\mathrm{d}V_{0}

by (
4.4.27
) since
\sigma
has the form (
4.4.19
) with
\varphi
bounded with respect to
g_{0}
.
∎

Regularized Curvature and Anomaly

In this subsection we note another kind of regularized anomaly which concerns smooth (bounded) scaling of a singular metric, rather than singular scaling of a smooth metric which was in some sense what we did above. This quantity will also play a role in the main result proposition
4.6.1
.

Lemma 4.5.2
.

Let
\Sigma
be a closed Riemann surface and
\widetilde{g}
a generalized conformal metric representing
D=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\gamma_{j}z_{j}
with
\gamma_{j}>-1
. Now suppose
h\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
and consider the scaled metric
\textrm{e}^{2h}\widetilde{g}
on
\Sigma\setminus\supp D
. Then

\mathcal{R}A_{\Sigma}(\textrm{e}^{2h}\widetilde{g},\widetilde{g})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\frac{1}{24\pi}\int_{\Sigma\setminus\supp D}\big(|\nabla_{\widetilde{g}}h|_{\widetilde{g}}^{2}+2K_{\widetilde{g}}h\big)\,\mathrm{d}V_{\widetilde{g}}<\infty.

(4.5.5)

Proof.

Choose a smooth background conformal metric
g
and write
\widetilde{g}=\textrm{e}^{2\sigma}g
,
\sigma\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma\setminus\supp D)
. As in lemma
4.4.8
, the 2-form
|\nabla_{\bullet}h|_{\bullet}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}V_{\bullet}
is conformally invariant, and the Gauss curvature transforms as

K_{\widetilde{g}}=\textrm{e}^{-2\sigma}(-\Delta_{g}\sigma+K_{g}),\quad\textrm{on }\Sigma\setminus\supp D.

(4.5.6)

Therefore

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma\setminus\supp D}(|\nabla_{\widetilde{g}}h|_{\widetilde{g}}^{2}+2K_{\widetilde{g}}h)\,\mathrm{d}V_{\widetilde{g}}=\int_{\Sigma\setminus\supp D}(|\nabla_{g}h|_{g}^{2}+2(-\Delta_{g}\sigma+K_{g})h)\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}<\infty,

(4.5.7)

because of lemma
4.4.5
.
∎

Remark 4.5.1
.

As a by-product, we also see that the RHS of (
4.5.7
), which is expressed in terms of the background smooth metric
g
, is independent of
g
.

Definition 4.5.1
.

In the situation as lemma
4.5.2
, we call (
4.5.5
) the
regular Polyakov anomaly
of
\textrm{e}^{2h}\widetilde{g}
against
\widetilde{g}
.

Remark 4.5.2
.

The distribution (or current, more precisely) on
\Sigma
which we denote by
K_{\widetilde{g}}\mathrm{d}V_{\widetilde{g}}
, defined by setting

\left\langle K_{\widetilde{g}}\mathrm{d}V_{\widetilde{g}},\psi\right\rangle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\int_{\Sigma\setminus\supp D}\psi K_{\widetilde{g}}\,\mathrm{d}V_{\widetilde{g}}

(4.5.8)

for all
\psi\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
, is usually called the
regularized Gauss curvature
. This is in
L^{1}(\Sigma,g)\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}
for any smooth conformal metric
g
for just the same reason as in (
4.5.7
).

Comparison with Cut-off Methods

There are existing methods in the literature that essentially define the same quantity as (
4.2.25
), but instead of removing the balls
B_{\varepsilon}(z_{i},\widetilde{g})
entirely, they introduce a regularized metric inside. Such ideas are found in the incomplete notes of Zamolodchikov et al.
[
ZZ
]
(page 86), as well as in Eskin, Kontsevich, and Zorich
[
EKZ14
]
, section 3.6. The cut-off method used in the latter is more mathematically rigorous, being smooth, and it avoids generating additional singularities along each
\partial B_{\varepsilon}
for
\nabla\sigma
. In this subsection, we note that our definition
4.2.8
