yields the same result as in
[
EKZ14
]
, and arguably, it also aligns with other regularization methods discussed in
[
EKZ14
]
, section 3.6.

For each fixed
\delta>\delta^{\prime}>0
we consider a smooth function
\sreg_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(r)
such that

\sreg_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(r)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\log r&\textrm{for }r\geqslant\delta,\\
\log\delta&\textrm{for }0\leqslant r\leqslant\delta^{\prime},\end{array}\right.

(4.5.9)

as well as

\log\delta^{\prime}\leqslant\sreg_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(r)\leqslant\log\delta,\quad\quad\textrm{for }0\leqslant r\leqslant\delta.

(4.5.10)

and

\left|\partial_{r}\sreg_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(r)\right|\leqslant C\delta^{\prime-1},\quad\quad\textrm{for }0\leqslant r\leqslant\delta,

(4.5.11)

for some constant
C
independent of
\delta,\delta^{\prime}
.
Such a function can be constructed using a smooth cut-off function
f:\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
for which
f(t)\equiv 0
for
t\leqslant 0
,
0<f(t)<1
for
0<t<1
,
f(t)\equiv 1
for
t\geqslant 1
, and posing

\sreg_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(r)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\log\delta+f\Big(\frac{r-\delta^{\prime}}{\delta-\delta^{\prime}}\Big)\log\frac{r}{\delta}.

(4.5.12)

Now given a closed Riemann surface
\Sigma
and
\widetilde{g}
a generalized conformal metric representing
D=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\gamma_{j}z_{j}
with
\gamma_{j}>-1
, as well as a smooth background conformal metric
g
such that around each singularity
z_{j}
holds (
4.2.22
), and the regular metric potentials
\varphi_{j}
satisfy the Troyanov conditions (
4.4.7
), we introduce the regularized smooth metric
g_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}
(for
\varepsilon>\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
sufficiently small)

g_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\widetilde{g}&\textrm{on }\Sigma\setminus\bigcup_{1}^{p}U_{j},\\
\exp\big(2\gamma_{j}\cdot\sreg_{\delta_{j},\delta^{\prime}_{j}}(r_{j})\big)\textrm{e}^{2\varphi_{j}}g&\textrm{on }U_{j},\end{array}\right.

(4.5.13)

where
r_{j}=d_{g}(\bullet,z_{j})
, the
U_{j}
’s given in definition
4.2.5
, and we choose
\delta_{j}=\delta_{j}(\varepsilon)
to be the radius of the ball
B_{\varepsilon}(z_{j},\widetilde{g})
under the metric
g
, and likewise
\delta^{\prime}_{j}=\delta_{j}(\varepsilon^{\prime})
.

Lemma 4.5.3
.

In the setting as above, write
g_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}=\textrm{e}^{2\sigma_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}}g
now with
\sigma_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
for each
\varepsilon>0
, and assuming
\varepsilon^{\prime}\sim\varepsilon
as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
, we have

\frac{1}{24\pi}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\Big[\int_{\Sigma}(|\nabla_{g}\sigma_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}|_{g}^{2}+2K_{g}\sigma_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}})\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}+2\pi\sum_{i=1}^{p}\frac{\gamma_{i}^{2}}{1+\gamma_{i}}\log(\varepsilon)\Big]=\mathcal{R}A_{\Sigma}(\widetilde{g},g).

(4.5.14)

Proof.

Since
g_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}=\widetilde{g}
on
\Sigma\setminus\bigcup_{i=1}^{p}B_{\varepsilon}(z_{i},\widetilde{g})
we just need to show that for each
j
,

\int_{B_{\varepsilon}(z_{j},\widetilde{g})}(|\nabla_{g}\sigma_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}|_{g}^{2}+2K_{g}\sigma_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}})\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}\quad\xlongrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\quad 0.

(4.5.15)

We suppress the subscript
j
as we treat each ball individually. Now

\sigma_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(r)=\gamma\cdot\sreg_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(r)+\varphi,

(4.5.16)

with
r=d_{g}(\bullet,z_{j})
. From the fact that

\log\delta^{\prime}\leqslant\sreg_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(r)\leqslant\log\delta

(4.5.17)

one finds that

\sigma_{\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}}(r)=\mathcal{O}(\log\epsilon)

(4.5.18)

and hence the integral of
K_{g}\sigma_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}
over the ball of radius
\epsilon
goes to zero. Since
\varphi
satisfies the Troyanov conditions (
4.4.7
) we have

\int_{B_{\varepsilon}(z_{j},\widetilde{g})}|\nabla_{g}\varphi|_{g}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}\lesssim\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\delta}r^{4\gamma+2}\cdot r\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}\theta=\mathcal{O}(\delta^{4\gamma+4})\quad\xlongrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\quad 0,

(4.5.19)

taking
(r,\theta)
to be the geodesic polar coordinates with respect to
g
. Now it is straightforward to check that

\sup_{r\leqslant\delta}\big|\nabla_{g}\sreg_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(r)\big|_{g}=\mathcal{O}(\delta^{-1}),\quad\quad\delta\to 0^{+}.

(4.5.20)

provided
\delta^{\prime}\sim\delta
. Now together with (
4.4.7
) we have

\int_{B_{\varepsilon}(z_{j},\widetilde{g})}\left\langle\nabla_{g}\sreg_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}},\nabla_{g}\varphi\right\rangle_{g}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}\lesssim\delta^{-1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\delta}r^{2\gamma+1}\cdot r\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}\theta=\mathcal{O}(\delta^{2\gamma+2})\quad\xlongrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\quad 0,

(4.5.21)

and finally, again by the fact that
\delta^{\prime}\sim\delta
,

\int_{B_{\varepsilon}(z_{j},\widetilde{g})}\big|\nabla_{g}\sreg_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}(r)\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}\lesssim\frac{1}{\delta^{2}}\int_{\{\delta^{\prime}\leqslant r\leqslant\delta\}}\mathrm{d}V_{g}=\frac{1}{\delta}\mathcal{O}(\delta-\delta^{\prime})\quad\xlongrightarrow{\delta\to 0^{+}}\quad 0.

(4.5.22)

which concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.5.3
.

This lemma specifically explains why no boundary terms are included in the anomaly formula from definition
4.5.1
. We are working over
\Sigma\setminus\bigcup_{i=1}^{p}B_{\varepsilon}(z_{i},\widetilde{g})
, which is a surface with boundary, and the boundary is generally not geodesic. The reason we don’t include boundary terms is that we could have alternatively opted for the method described in this subsection, which only considers the closed surface
\Sigma
, and still arrived at the same outcome. Moreover, as mentioned in the "future work" section of the introduction, we intend to investigate various settings involving surfaces with boundary in a future revision of the manuscript.

Remark 4.5.4
.

Imaginably, one could allow even more flexibility in the choice of the cut-off function
\sreg_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}
to gain even better convergence in (
4.5.22
). Clearly one just needs to make the infinitesimal annuli with nonzero gradient
\nabla\sigma_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}
thin enough. Nevertheless our choice suffices.

Main Proof

Proof

Proposition 4.6.1
.

Let
\Sigma
be a closed Riemann surface and
\widetilde{g}
a generalized conformal metric representing
D=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\gamma_{j}z_{j}
with
\gamma_{j}>-1
. Now suppose
h\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
and consider the scaled metric
\textrm{e}^{2h}\widetilde{g}
on
\Sigma\setminus\supp D
. Then

\mathcal{R}A_{\Sigma}(e^{2h}\widetilde{g},g)-\mathcal{R}A_{\Sigma}\left(\widetilde{g},g\right)=\mathcal{R}A_{\Sigma}(e^{2h}\widetilde{g},\widetilde{g})-\frac{1}{12}\sum_{j}\frac{\gamma_{j}(\gamma_{j}+2)}{\gamma_{j}+1}h(z_{j}),

(4.6.1)

where
\mathcal{R}A_{\Sigma}(e^{2h}\widetilde{g},\widetilde{g})
is defined by (
4.5.5
) and
\mathcal{R}A_{\Sigma}(e^{2h}\widetilde{g},g)
,
\mathcal{R}A_{\Sigma}\left(\widetilde{g},g\right)
by definition
4.2.7
.

Remark 4.6.1
.

From this we deduce that if we consider a conformal field theory with central charge
c
defined on
\Sigma
equipped with the metric
\widetilde{g}
as above, whose partition function
\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma,\widetilde{g})
is defined in definition
4.2.8
, then we have

\frac{\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma,e^{2h}\widetilde{g})}{\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma,\widetilde{g})}=\exp\Big(c\,\mathcal{R}A_{\Sigma}(\textrm{e}^{2h}\widetilde{g},\widetilde{g})-\sum_{j=1}^{p}h(z_{j})\Delta_{j}\Big),

(4.6.2)

where

\Delta_{j}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\frac{c}{12}\frac{\gamma_{j}(\gamma_{j}+2)}{\gamma_{j}+1}

(4.6.3)

are the
conformal weights
or
scaling dimensions
associated to conical singularities.

First we point out (directly from corollary
4.4.7
)

Lemma 4.6.2
.

Let
g
be a smooth conformal metric on
\Sigma
so that near the cone points
z_{j}
the regular metric potentials
\varphi_{j}
of
\widetilde{g}
against
g
satisfy (
4.4.7
). Then

\mathcal{R}A_{\Sigma}(\widetilde{g},g)=\frac{1}{12}\Big[-\sum_{j=1}^{p}\frac{\gamma_{j}^{2}\log(\gamma_{j}+1)}{\gamma_{j}+1}+\sum_{j=1}^{p}\Big(\frac{\gamma_{j}^{2}}{\gamma_{j}+1}-\gamma_{j}\Big)\varphi_{j}(z_{j})\Big]+\frac{1}{24\pi}\int_{\Sigma\setminus\supp D}\sigma(-\Delta_{g}\sigma+2K_{g})\,\mathrm{d}V_{g},

(4.6.4)

where
\widetilde{g}=\textrm{e}^{2\sigma}g
with
\sigma\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma\setminus\supp D)
.
\Box

Proof of proposition
4.6.1
.

It follows from lemma
4.5.1
that it is sufficient to prove (
4.6.1
) for a specific reference smooth metric
g
. Choose a smooth conformal metric
g
with the same conditions as in lemma
4.6.2
. Write
\widetilde{g}=\textrm{e}^{2\sigma}g
then
\textrm{e}^{2h}\widetilde{g}=\textrm{e}^{2(h+\sigma)}g
,
\sigma\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma\setminus\supp D)
. Using lemma
4.6.2
and relation (
4.5.7
) one obtains immediately

\mathcal{R}A_{\Sigma}(e^{2h}\widetilde{g},g)-\mathcal{R}A_{\Sigma}(e^{2h}\widetilde{g},\widetilde{g})-\mathcal{R}A_{\Sigma}\left(\widetilde{g},g\right)=-\frac{1}{12}\sum_{j}\frac{\gamma_{j}}{\gamma_{j}+1}h(z_{j})+\frac{1}{24\pi}\int_{\Sigma\setminus\supp D}\left(h\Delta_{g}\sigma-\sigma\Delta_{g}h\right)\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}.

(4.6.5)

What we desire follows then from applying lemma
C.3.1
to
\sigma
which has the local form (
4.4.19
).
∎

Remark 4.6.2
.

In view of the Poincaré-Lelong lemma it would be instructive to compare the result with a “naive guess” for the Polyakov formula by pretending that the conical singularities did no more than just putting a log term into the metric potential and hence creating a delta function in the curvature. This is to say we suppose

\log\Big(\frac{\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma,e^{2h}\widetilde{g})}{\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma,\widetilde{g})}\Big)\textrm{``}=\textrm{''}\frac{c}{24\pi}\int_{\Sigma}(|\nabla_{\widetilde{g}}h|_{\widetilde{g}}^{2}+2K_{\widetilde{g}}h)\,\mathrm{d}V_{\widetilde{g}}.

(4.6.6)

Now taking into account the delta functions produced by
-\Delta_{g}\sigma
in (
4.5.7
) over
\supp D
, we find

\int_{\Sigma}(|\nabla_{\widetilde{g}}h|_{\widetilde{g}}^{2}+2K_{\widetilde{g}}h)\,\mathrm{d}V_{\widetilde{g}}=\int_{\Sigma\setminus\supp D}(|\nabla_{\widetilde{g}}h|_{\widetilde{g}}^{2}+2K_{\widetilde{g}}h)\,\mathrm{d}V_{\widetilde{g}}-4\pi\sum_{j=1}^{p}\gamma_{j}h(z_{j}).

(4.6.7)

Therefore the extra piece that we get in the actual formula is the quadratic term
\sum_{j}\frac{2\pi\gamma_{j}^{2}}{\gamma_{j}+1}h(z_{j})
. Going back to the definitions, we could see that this term originates as the asymptotic Dirichlet energy of the metric potential
\sigma
on a shrinking “scaled annulus”, which we singled out as lemma
4.4.9
above (cf. corollary
4.4.4
).

Proposition 4.6.3
.

Let
\Sigma_{d}
,
\Sigma
be closed Riemann surfaces, with a smooth conformal metric
g
on
\Sigma
, and
f:\Sigma_{d}\longrightarrow\Sigma
a ramified
d
-sheeted holomorphic map, whose critical values are
w_{1}
, …,
w_{p}
. Consider a conformal field theory with central charge
c
whose partition function is denoted
\mathcal{Z}
. Pick
h\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
on
\Sigma
. Then under the definition
4.2.8
for
\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma_{d},f^{*}e^{2h}g)
and
\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma_{d},f^{*}g)
we have

\frac{\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma_{d},f^{*}e^{2h}g)}{\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma,e^{2h}g)^{d}}=e^{-\sum_{j}h(w_{j})\Delta_{j}}\frac{\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma_{d},f^{*}g)}{\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma,g)^{d}},

(4.6.8)

where

\Delta_{j}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\frac{c}{12}\sum_{z\in f^{-1}(w_{j})}\Big(\ord_{f}(z)-\frac{1}{\ord_{f}(z)}\Big)

(4.6.9)

is the
conformal weight
or
scaling dimension
associated to the point
w_{j}
.

Proof.

Following proposition
4.6.1
, the only thing we need to check is

\mathcal{R}A_{\Sigma_{d}}\big(f^{*}\textrm{e}^{2h}g,f^{*}g\big)=d\cdot A_{\Sigma}\big(\textrm{e}^{2h}g,g\big),

(4.6.10)

where we recall that the left hand side is defined by (
4.5.5
). Indeed, let
\{z_{1},\dots,z_{q}\}
denote the preimage of the critical values
\{w_{1},\dots,w_{p}\}
of
f
. Then on
\Sigma_{d}\setminus\{z_{1},...,z_{q}\}
the map
f
is an unbranched
d
-fold covering, we have

\int_{\Sigma_{d}\setminus\{z_{1},...,z_{q}\}}\big(|\nabla_{f^{*}g}f^{*}h|_{f^{*}g}^{2}+2K_{f^{*}g}\cdot f^{*}h\big)\,\mathrm{d}V_{f^{*}g}=d\cdot\int_{\Sigma\setminus\{w_{1},...,w_{p}\}}\big(|\nabla_{g}h|_{g}^{2}+2K_{g}\cdot h\big)\,\mathrm{d}V_{g},

(4.6.11)

and we finish the proof.
∎

Remark 4.6.3
.

Note that
\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma_{d},f^{*}g)/\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma,g)^{d}
is diffeomorphism invariant as a function of the critical values of
f
because
\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma_{d},f^{*}(\Psi^{*}g))/\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma,\Psi^{*}g)^{d}=\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma_{d},(\Psi\circ f)^{*}g)/\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma,g)^{d}
if
\Psi:\Sigma\longrightarrow\Sigma
is a diffeomorphism, for the ordinary
\mathcal{Z}(\Sigma,g)
is so invariant by definition.

Corollary 4.6.4
.

Consider a quantum system in the sense of subsection
4.3.2
defined on a circle
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{L}
of perimeter
L
, with central charge
c
. Let
A\subset\mathbb{S}^{1}_{L}
be an interval of length
\ell
and let
\rho
be the
vacuum state
associated to this system. Then under the
replica interpretation
we have

\ttr_{\mathcal{H}_{A}}\big(\ttr_{A^{c}}(\rho)^{d}\big)=C\Big(\frac{L}{\pi}\sin\frac{\pi\ell}{L}\Big)^{-\frac{c}{6}\left(d-\frac{1}{d}\right)},

(4.6.12)

where
C
is the same constant as in lemma
4.2.1
.

Proof.

We adopt the interpretation of example
4.3.3
. Then the surface
\check{\Sigma}
corresponds to a re-scaled Fubini-Study Riemann sphere (of radius
L/2\pi
) where
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{L}
embeds isometrically as the equator. By proposition
4.6.3
we could first assume
L=2\pi
. Suppose the end points of
A
correspond to
0
and
z_{0}\in\widehat{\mathbb{C}}
. Then the surface
\check{\Sigma}_{d}
is topologically still
\widehat{\mathbb{C}}
but equipped with the pull-back of the Fubini-Study metric under a holomorphic map
f_{d}:\widehat{\mathbb{C}}\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathbb{C}}
which ramifies exactly at
0
and
z_{0}
both with order
d
. Such a map could be given by (but not related to final result)

f_{d}(z)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\frac{z_{0}\big(\frac{z}{z-z_{0}}\big)^{d}}{\big(\frac{z}{z-z_{0}}\big)^{d}-1}=\frac{z_{0}z^{d}}{z^{d}-(z-z_{0})^{d}}.

(4.6.13)

Now we apply a smooth scaling
\textrm{e}^{2h}
with
h=\log(L/2\pi)
on the equator, while making
\textrm{e}^{2h}g_{\mathrm{FS}}
flat in a thin neighborhood of the equator, in accordance with the requirement for vacuum state explained in example
4.3.3
. The latter is always possible for Fubini-Study since
4(1+|z|^{2})^{-2}=|z|^{-2}+\mathcal{O}((1-|z|)^{2})
. Then by proposition
4.6.3
, lemma
4.2.1
(and remark
4.6.3
) we obtain

\displaystyle\ttr_{\mathcal{H}_{A}}\big(\ttr_{A^{c}}(\rho)^{d}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathcal{Z}(\check{\Sigma}_{d})}{\mathcal{Z}(\check{\Sigma})^{d}}=C\textrm{e}^{-h(0)\Delta_{d}}\textrm{e}^{-h(z_{0})\Delta_{d}}\sin\Big(\frac{1}{2}d_{\mathrm{FS}}(0,z_{0})\Big)^{-2\Delta_{d}}

\displaystyle=C\Big(\frac{L}{\pi}\sin\frac{\pi\ell}{L}\Big)^{-2\Delta_{d}},

with
\Delta_{d}=\frac{c}{12}(d-\frac{1}{d})
, giving us the result.
∎

Comments on Relation to Literature

Remark 4.6.4

(comparison with a result of V. Kalvin
[
Kal21
]
)
.

We note here that V. Kalvin has obtained a closely related result in
[
Kal21
]
corollary 1.3(1) for the
\zeta
-determinant of the Friederichs Laplacian under the conical metric with zero “boundary condition” at the cone points. As far as the Polyakov formula is concerned, the
\zeta
-determinant is no different from a general CFT partition function in the sense of definition
4.2.1
, except for a constant. So our proposition
4.6.1
coincides with the case of his result when the two divisors coincide. If they don’t coincide, one could readily derive the corresponding result out of our method following lemma
4.6.2
. However, in that case, the constants (last term in
[
Kal21
]
eqn. (1.8)) would be different, as our lemma
4.6.2
only agrees with
[
Kal21
]
eqn. (1.1) upto the integral term and the term involving the regular metric potential. Supposedly, the extra constants in
[
Kal21
]
come from the specific Friederichs extension.

On a deeper level, the proof of
[
Kal21
]
proceeds also by removing disks but then using the BFK decomposition formula. One key point is to show that the blow-up order of
\log\detz\Delta_{D_{\varepsilon}(z_{j})}
of the Friederichs Dirichlet Laplacian
\Delta_{D_{\varepsilon}(z_{j})}
on the shrinking disks
D_{\varepsilon}(z_{j})
centered at the cone points with the singular metric, added up, matches exactly with the blow-up order of the
\log\detz
of the Dirichlet Laplacian on their complement, as the disks shrink (as the contributions from D-to-N maps cancel). In this regard we keenly observe that the cancellation mechanism as shown in
[
Kal21
]
eqn. (2.28) is
different
from ours. The radii of Kalvin’s disks were measured under the background smooth metric. For us, on the other hand, it is important to align the rate of shrinking with the singular metric itself (i.e. measure the disks using the singular metric) in two respects. First to obtain the invariance of the definition of the renormalized partition function as in lemma
4.5.1
, and secondly to obtain the correct scaling dimensions in the final result. A more detailed understanding of the relation of the two methods seems desirable, especially considering that the BFK formula is closely related to Segal’s gluing axioms for QFT/CFT
[
Lin24
,
GKRV21
]
, and that the correlation functions are (conjecturally) the “limits” of propagators associated to the complements of the shrinking disks (
[
KS21
]
section 3).

Remark 4.6.5

(relation to Aldana, Kirsten and Rowlett
[
AKR20
]
)
.

As the authors have themselves remarked in relation to
[
Kal21
]
, they also obtained the same result as above for the
\zeta
-determinant (
[
AKR20
]
eqn. (1.5)). Their method is based on asymptotic analysis of the heat trace for which related results were obtained earlier
[
MR15
]
. For the latter, they employed a blow-up (geometric) technique and the cone contribution term (main interest of this article) seems to ultimately come from computations done to an infinite exact cone which has a longer history (see their appendix A for further references). Last but not least, we note that
[
AKR20
]
seems to have restricted their cone angles to
(0,2\pi)
, which is in the complement of the main concern of this article (proposition
4.6.3
).

Chapter 5
Zeta Determinants of Laplacians on Cyclic Covers

This Chapter is based on joint work with Nguyen Viet Dang and Frédéric Naud.

Introduction

In Euclidean Quantum Field Theory over some compact Riemannian
manifold
M
, for
m\geqslant 0
, the partition function of the massive Gaussian free field is formally denoted by

\displaystyle Z_{M}=\int_{\mathrm{Map}(M,\mathbb{R})}\exp\Big(-\int_{M}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}+m^{2}\varphi^{2}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}\Big)\mathrm{d}\mathcal{L}(\varphi)

where
\mathrm{Map}(\mathbb{S}^{1},\mathbb{R})
denotes loosely functions on which we deliberately do not specify the regularity, and
\mathcal{L}
is non-existent Lebesgue measure on
\mathrm{Map}(\mathbb{S}^{1},\mathbb{R})
, see section
1.4.1
. This formula is analogous to the case of discrete Gaussian free field which is some ferromagnetic spin system (cf. example
1.2.7
) on a discrete box
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
of finite size whose partition function
reads

Z_{\Lambda}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}}\exp\Big(-\sum_{i\sim j}|\sigma_{i}-\sigma_{j}|^{2}-\sum_{i\in\Lambda}m^{2}\sigma_{i}^{2}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}^{\Lambda}\sigma.

In the discrete case, the Dirichlet action functional
S(\sigma)
is a quadratic form on
\mathbb{R}^{\Lambda}
,
S(\sigma)=\sum_{i\in\Lambda}\sigma_{i}(\Delta_{\Lambda}\sigma)_{i}+m^{2}\sigma_{i}^{2}
where
\Delta_{\Lambda}
is the discrete Laplacian and therefore, the partition function
Z_{\Lambda}
of the discrete GFF is a simple Gaussian integral which is easily calculated and equals

\displaystyle Z_{\Lambda}=\det(\Delta_{\Lambda}+m^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}

where
\Delta_{\Lambda}
is the discrete Laplacian for discrete functions on
\Lambda
.
On the manifold
M
, since the space of functions is infinite and the Laplace Beltrami operator is no longer a finite dimensional matrix, so one has to regularize the determinant or equivalently one has to regularize the infinite product
\prod_{\lambda\in\sigma(\Delta)\setminus\{0\}}(\lambda+m^{2})
.
Inspired by the seminal work of Ray–Singer
[
RS71
]
and also by the more physically oriented paper
of Hawking
[
Haw77
]
,
a common choice in theoretical physics is to use zeta regularization to give a mathematical meaning to free fields partition functions. So
Z_{M}
is defined to be

\displaystyle Z_{M}:=\text{det}_{\zeta}\left(\Delta_{g}+m^{2}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}

where we refer to paragraph
5.1
for a precise definition of
zeta determinants.

From the point of view of statistical physics,
the free energy
\log Z_{M}
is expected to grow as the size of the system, here the natural system being our Riemannian manifold
M
. So the natural
intensive quantity
should be the
density of free energy defined as
[
Sch13
, eq (24.28) p. 144]

F\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\frac{\log(Z_{M})}{\text{Vol}(M)}.

(5.1.1)

It is expected, as the volume of our Riemannian manifold gets larger in a certain limiting sense, that
the above quantity converges as the volume of
M
goes to infinity.
It means there exists a
density of free energy in the thermodynamic limit
, which is also interpreted as the energy density of the ground state of a quantum system described by the GFF.
Inspired by the recent results of the third author
[
Nau23
]
as well as several studies on the spectral theory of Laplacians on random surfaces
[
MM24
]
and motivated by problems of taking large volume limits in
quantum field theory
[
Lin24
]
,
the goal of the present paper is to establish the existence of a limit for the quantity
F
defined by equation (
5.1.1
) for suitable sequences of cyclic covers of large degree that
converge
in some appropriate sense to some
\mathbb{Z}^{p}
-periodic Riemannian manifolds obtained as abelian cover of some initial closed compact Riemannian manifold
M
.

Geometric Setting

Our geometric set-up here will be a direct generalization of that in section
2.7.1
to general dimensions.
Let
(M,g)
denotes a smooth, closed, compact Riemannian manifold.
We shall start by giving some way to manufacture towers of abelian covers of
M
following
[
JNS20
]
.
Consider any group homomorphism of the form

\rho:\pi_{1}(M)\longrightarrow H_{1}(M,\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}

where the last arrow
H_{1}(M,\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}
has infinite elements in its image.
Then
\ker(\rho)
is a subgroup of the fundamental group

\pi_{1}(M)
. We denote by
\widehat{M}

the universal cover of
M
,
\ker(\rho)
is a subgroup of the deck group of
\widehat{M}\longrightarrow M
(deck group are automorphisms of the cover which descend trivially on the base) and the quotient

M_{\infty}:=\widehat{M}/\ker(\rho)

is an abelian cover of
M
with deck group
\mathbb{Z}
, in fact the quotient

\widehat{M}/\ker(\rho)
is a periodic Riemannian manifold endowed with an
isometric action of
\mathbb{Z}
.
We denote by
\rho_{N}:\pi_{1}(M)\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}

the composition of
\rho
with mod
N
reduction.
Then the quotient
M_{N}:=\widehat{M}/\ker(\rho_{N})
is an abelian cover of
M
with deck group
\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}
and when
N\rightarrow+\infty
we can think of the sequence
(M_{N})_{N}
as an increasing sequence of abelian covers that
converges
to the periodic cover
M_{\infty}
. Denoting by
\gamma
the generator of the
\mathbb{Z}
-action on
M_{\infty}
, one can also think of
M_{N}
as the quotient
M_{\infty}/\left\langle\gamma^{N}\right\rangle
where
\left\langle\gamma^{N}\right\rangle
denotes the subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}
generated by
N
.

Like in
2.7.1
, we now make a
crucial assumption
which will also related our set-up to Segal’s composition picture as described in
2.7.1
. Let us concretely describe how the abelian cover appears from the manifold
M
we started with.
We suppose a closed hypersurface
\Sigma\subset M
whose dual class

[\Sigma]\in H^{1}(M,\mathbb{Z})
is non-trivial and non-torsion which means that the class
[\Sigma]\in H^{1}(M,\mathbb{R})
with coefficients over the reals is non-trivial.
The dual class is defined by the intersection of cycles

[\Sigma]:=[p]\in H_{1}(M)\longmapsto\left\langle[p],[\Sigma]\right\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}

which we assume to be
surjective
.
The intersection pairing defines a morphism

\rho:\pi_{1}(M)\longrightarrow H_{1}(M,\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}

whose kernel
\ker(\rho)
generates a
\mathbb{Z}
-cover of
M
as
\widehat{M}/\ker(\rho)
. Geometrically,
cut the manifold
M
along
\Sigma
, this creates a smooth manifold with even number of boundary components, identify one part as ingoing and the other part as outgoing yielding a cobordism
U
. Then make an infinite iterate

U\circ U\circ\dots\circ U
of this cobordism generates the
\mathbb{Z}
-cover
\widehat{M}/\ker(\rho)
.
To generate cyclic covers, just repeat the previous operation
and compose the previous
\rho
with reduction mod
N
.

Statement of the Main Result

In this chapter we will focus on the limits as
N\to+\infty
of quantities (
5.1.1
)
in the case

m=0
. The case
m>0
was in a sense (for surfaces) obtained in subsection
2.7.4
by exploiting the composition axiom in the context of a Segal QFT. Moreover the
m>0
case may also be proved following a similar argument as in subsection
5.2.2
of the present chapter without resorting further to the lemmas. However for
m=0
the accumulation of the eigenvalues near
0
of the Laplacian on the cover as the degree grows large creates an extra difficulty, and a main part of the present chapter, therefore, is to deal with this difficulty.

We keep the notations from the previous paragraph, denote by
\Delta_{N}
(resp.
\Delta_{\infty}
) the Laplace-Beltrami operator on
M_{N}
(resp.
M_{\infty}
) we can state the main results of our note. We consider
M_{N}\longrightarrow M
a sequence of cyclic cover of degree
N
as defined in the introduction
2.7.1
. One would like to think that the sequence of covers
M_{N}
converges in a suitable sense to a limit cover
M_{\infty}
. We denote by
\Delta_{N}
the natural Laplace-Beltrami operator on the cover
M_{N}
,
\det_{\zeta}(\Delta_{N})
denotes the zeta regularized determinant of
\Delta_{N}
defined
as in
4.2.1
and also
[
RS71
]
,

\displaystyle\text{det}^{\prime}_{\zeta}(\Delta_{M_{N}})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\exp(-\zeta^{\prime}(0)),\quad\quad\zeta_{\Delta_{N}}(s)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sum_{\lambda\in\sigma(\Delta_{N})\setminus\{0\}}\lambda^{-s}

(5.1.2)

where the spectral zeta function
\zeta_{\Delta_{N}}(s)
has holomorphic continuation near
s=0
.
We show in the present note the following:

Theorem 5.1
.

Under the above assumptions, we find that the sequence

\displaystyle\boxed{\frac{\log\left(\det^{\prime}_{\zeta}\left(\Delta_{M_{N}}\right)\right)}{\mathrm{vol}(M_{N})}}

(5.1.3)

has a limit when
N\rightarrow+\infty
and we give a formula for the limit in terms of the heat kernel on
M_{\infty}
.

Main Strategy and Proof

We start by recalling the Mellin transform formula for the zeta function of a positive semi-definite elliptic differential operator
P
, with strictly positive principal symbol
1
1

1

This means the principal symbol
\sigma_{P}(x,\xi)
is positive definite for
\xi\neq 0
(
[
Gil95
]
section 1.6.2).
, acting on a Hermitian vector bundle over a Riemannian manifold (
[
Gil95
]
section 1.12):

\zeta_{P}(s)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{s-1}\big(\ttr_{L^{2}}(\textrm{e}^{-tP})-\dim\ker P\big)\,\mathrm{d}t.

(5.2.1)

We denote by
\textrm{e}^{-tP}(x,y)
the heat kernel which is smooth on
M\times M
. In the first part of this paper we consider
P=\Delta_{\infty}
,
\Delta_{N}
, or
\Delta
, the Laplacians on
M_{\infty}
,
M_{N}
or
M
respectively, acting on functions.

By definition of the
\zeta
-determinant, to prove the existence of the limit in theorem
5.1
it suffices to prove that
\{\zeta_{\Delta_{N}}(s)/\vol(M_{N})\}
is a sequence of holomorphic functions converging in the compact-open topology on a neighborhood of
s=0
as
N\to+\infty
. To do this, we use formula (
5.2.1
) and then express the heat trace as the integral along diagonal of the heat kernel.
We use the crucial fact that the heat kernels on
M_{N}
and
M_{\infty}
are related in a simple way by summing over the deck orbits (
[
Bus10
]
section 7.5).
Applying a Li-Yau type estimate on the heat kernel over
M_{\infty}
, we obtain the convergence of
N^{-1}\ttr_{L^{2}(M_{N})}(\textrm{e}^{-t\Delta_{N}})
as
N\to+\infty
for fixed
t>0
.
The main technical part then boils down to obtaining a bound on the heat trace in
t
, uniform in
N
, to justify dominated convergence.

Relation of Heat Kernels and a Li-Yau Estimate

We will use in a crucial way the
\mathbb{Z}
-cover
M_{\infty}\longrightarrow M_{N}\longrightarrow M
which covers all the degree
N
cyclic covers
M_{N}
.
The group
\mathbb{Z}
acts on
M_{\infty}
by isometries (the reader should think translations), the generator is denoted by
\gamma:M_{\infty}\longrightarrow M_{\infty}
that we identify with an isometry of
M_{\infty}
.
The infinite cover
M_{\infty}
endowed with the metric induced from
(M,g)
is a geodesically complete Riemannian manifold by the Hopf-Rinow Theorem since every closed bounded subset in it is compact.

Denote by
\Delta_{\infty}
the Laplacian on
M_{\infty}
,
\pi_{N}:M_{\infty}\longrightarrow M_{N}
. The geodesic completeness implies that
\Delta_{\infty}:C^{\infty}_{c}(M_{\infty})\subset L^{2}(M_{\infty})\longrightarrow L^{2}(M_{\infty})
has a self-adjoint extension by the work of Gaffney.
Then observe we have the identity

\displaystyle\left(\pi_{N}\times\pi_{N}\right)^{*}e^{-t\Delta_{N}}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-t\Delta_{\infty}}(\cdot,\gamma^{kN}\cdot).

We make the crucial observation that

\displaystyle\ttr_{L^{2}(M_{N})}(e^{-t\Delta_{N}})=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{\Omega_{N}}e^{-t\Delta_{\infty}}(x,\gamma^{kN}x)\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}(x)\,=N\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{\Omega}e^{-t\Delta_{\infty}}(x,\gamma^{kN}x)\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}(x)\,

where
\Omega
,
\Omega_{N}
are the respective fundamental domains of
M,M_{N}
on the cover
M_{\infty}
, we also used the invariance equation
e^{-t\Delta_{\infty}}(x,x)=e^{-t\Delta_{\infty}}(\gamma(x),\gamma(x))
.

In the sequel, we will make an extensive use of the following Lemma
which is a particular case of Milnor-Schwarz
[
BH13
, Prop 8.19 p. 140]
:

Lemma 5.2.1

(Milnor-Schwarz type Lemma)
.

Let
(M,g)
be a smooth compact Riemannian manifold and
M_{\infty}\longrightarrow M
a
\mathbb{Z}
cover of
M
. Then
there exists
L>0
such that for all
x\in M_{\infty}
:

\textbf{d}(x,\gamma^{p}x)\geqslant pL.

A similar kind of estimate holds true in the case of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
covers.

The second ingredient we shall need are Gaussian bounds on the heat kernel
e^{-t\Delta_{\infty}}
which are due to Li-Yau
[
SY94
, Thm 4.6 p. 169]
:

Theorem 5.2

(Li-Yau heat kernel bounds)
.

Let
(M_{\infty},g)
be a complete Riemannian manifold of dimension
d
with
\mathrm{Ric}(M)\geqslant-K
,
K\geqslant 0
. Then for all
0<t\leqslant 1
,
\delta\in(0,1)
and
(x,y)\in M_{\infty}\times M_{\infty}
, the heat kernel
e^{-t\Delta_{\infty}}

satisfies the bound of the form

\displaystyle|e^{-t\Delta_{\infty}}(x,y)|\leqslant C_{1}(\delta,d)t^{-\frac{d}{2}}e^{-\textbf{d}^{2}(x,y)/(4+\delta)t}\textrm{e}^{C_{3}K\delta t}

(5.2.2)

where
d
is the Riemannian distance and
C_{1}(\delta,d)\rightarrow+\infty
when
\delta\rightarrow 0
, and
C_{3}
depends only on the dimension.

Dominated Convergence

Here we prove theorem
5.1
while leaving details on the estimate of the heat trace to section
5.3
.

Proof.

(Proof of theorem
5.1
.) Using (
5.2.1
), we decompose

\frac{\zeta_{\Delta_{N}}(s)}{\vol(M_{N})}=\frac{1}{\Gamma(s)\vol(M_{N})}\Big(\underbrace{\int_{0}^{1}}_{A}+\underbrace{\int_{1}^{\infty}}_{B}\Big)\big(\ttr_{L^{2}(M_{N})}(e^{-t\Delta_{N}})-1\big)t^{s-1}\,\mathrm{d}t.

(5.2.3)

For part
A
we have

\frac{1}{N}\int_{0}^{1}\big(\ttr_{L^{2}(M_{N})}(e^{-t\Delta_{N}})-1\big)t^{s-1}\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{1}\Big[\int_{\Omega}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\textrm{e}^{-t\Delta_{\infty}}(x,\gamma^{kN}x)\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}(x)-\frac{1}{N}\Big]t^{s-1}\,\mathrm{d}t.

(5.2.4)

We observe by the Milnor-Schwarz lemma and Li-Yau estimate (and positivity of the heat kernel) that

\displaystyle|\mathcal{T}_{\neq 0}(t,s)|

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\bigg|t^{s-1}\int_{\Omega}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}e^{-t\Delta_{\infty}}(x,\gamma^{kN}x)\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}(x)\bigg|\leqslant|t^{s-1}|\,\int_{\Omega}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}e^{-t\Delta_{\infty}}(x,\gamma^{kN}x)\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}(x)

\displaystyle\lesssim t^{\mathfrak{Re}(s)-1-\frac{d}{2}}\int_{\Omega}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}e^{-C_{2}\mathbf{d}^{2}(x,\gamma^{kN}(x))/t}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}(x)

\displaystyle\lesssim\vol(M)\cdot t^{\mathfrak{Re}(s)-1-\frac{d}{2}}\Big(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}\\
k\neq 0\end{subarray}}e^{-C_{2}(kNL)^{2}/t}\Big)\leqslant\vol(M)\cdot t^{\mathfrak{Re}(s)-1-\frac{d}{2}}\Big(2\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}e^{-C_{2}kNL/t}\Big)

\displaystyle=\vol(M)\frac{2e^{-C_{2}NL/t}}{1-e^{-C_{2}NL/t}}\cdot t^{\mathfrak{Re}(s)-1-\frac{d}{2}}\lesssim e^{-C_{2}NL/t}\cdot t^{\mathfrak{Re}(s)-1-\frac{d}{2}}.

(#)

Since
e^{-\frac{C}{t}}t^{\mathfrak{Re}(s)-1-\frac{d}{2}}=\mathcal{O}(t^{\infty})
for
C>0
as
t\downarrow 0
, the r.h.s. of the above is obviously integrable on
(0,1)
, regardless of
s
. This shows firstly
\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{T}_{\neq 0}(t,s)\,\mathrm{d}t
in fact defines an entire function of
s
, and secondly from a simple change of variables we have
\int_{0}^{1}e^{-C_{2}NL/t}\cdot t^{\mathfrak{Re}(s)-1-\frac{d}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}t\lesssim N^{\mathfrak{Re}(s)-1-\frac{d}{2}}
, and we conclude that

\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{T}_{\neq 0}(t,s)\,\mathrm{d}t\lesssim N^{\mathfrak{Re}(s)-1-\frac{d}{2}}\quad\xlongrightarrow{N\to+\infty}\quad 0

for
\mathfrak{Re}(s)\ll 0
and hence as entire functions of
s
(Montel-Vitali theorem). Since

\frac{1}{N\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{1}t^{s-1}\,\mathrm{d}t=\frac{1}{N\Gamma(s+1)}\quad\xlongrightarrow{N\to+\infty}\quad 0,

(5.2.5)

we deduce

\frac{1}{N\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{1}\big(\ttr_{L^{2}(M_{N})}(e^{-t\Delta_{N}})-1\big)t^{s-1}\,\mathrm{d}t\quad\xlongrightarrow{N\to+\infty}\quad\frac{1}{\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{1}t^{s-1}\int_{\Omega}\textrm{e}^{-t\Delta_{\infty}}(x,x)\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}(x)\,\mathrm{d}t,

(5.2.6)

the latter defining a meromorphic function of
s
which is holomorphic near
s=0
by the asymptotic expansion of the heat kernel (
[
BGV04
]
theorem 2.30) and that
\vol(\Omega)=\vol(M)<\infty
.

To treat part
B
, we first note that by the same estimates (#) we have shown

\lim_{N\to+\infty}\frac{1}{N}\big(\ttr_{L^{2}(M_{N})}(e^{-t\Delta_{N}})-1\big)=\int_{\Omega}\textrm{e}^{-t\Delta_{\infty}}(x,x)\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}(x)

(5.2.7)

for each fixed
t>0
. Now by lemma
5.3.1
and dominated convergence we obtain

\lim_{N\to+\infty}\frac{1}{N\Gamma(s)}\int_{1}^{\infty}\big(\ttr_{L^{2}(M_{N})}(e^{-t\Delta_{N}})-1\big)t^{s-1}\,\mathrm{d}t=\frac{1}{\Gamma(s)}\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{s-1}\int_{\Omega}\textrm{e}^{-t\Delta_{\infty}}(x,x)\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}(x)\,\mathrm{d}t,

(5.2.8)

as holomorphic functions of
s
for
\mathfrak{Re}(s)<1/2p
and hence in particular near
s=0
. Combining the two parts we obtain the result with limit

\lim_{N\to+\infty}\frac{\zeta_{\Delta_{N}}(s)}{\vol(M_{N})}=\frac{\zeta_{\Delta_{\infty},\Omega}(s)}{\vol(M)}

(5.2.9)

where

\zeta_{\Delta_{\infty},\Omega}(s)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\frac{1}{\Gamma(s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{s-1}\int_{\Omega}\textrm{e}^{-t\Delta_{\infty}}(x,x)\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}(x)\,\mathrm{d}t.

(5.2.10)

Note that we know a posteriori after (
5.2.7
) and lemma
5.3.1
that
\zeta_{\Delta_{\infty},\Omega}(s)
is a holomorphic function of
s
near
s=0
as the sum of two such functions, one being a meromorphic continuation.
∎

Accumulation of Eigenvalues and Long-time Behavior of Heat Trace

The goal of this section is to prove

Lemma 5.3.1
.

There exists a positive integer
p
, a number
\varepsilon_{0}>0
, and constants
C_{4}
,
C_{5}>0
independent of
N
, such that

\frac{1}{N}\big(\ttr_{L^{2}(M_{N})}(e^{-t\Delta_{N}})-1\big)\leqslant C_{4}t^{-1/2p}+C_{5}\textrm{e}^{-t\varepsilon_{0}},

(5.3.1)

for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
and all
t\geqslant 1
.

Proof.

We obtain from corollary
5.3.11
the threshold
\varepsilon_{0}>0
,
p
and
C_{4}
independent of
N
so that

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\sigma(\Delta_{N})\\
0<\lambda<\varepsilon_{0}\end{subarray}}\textrm{e}^{-t\lambda}\leqslant C_{4}t^{-1/2p}.

(5.3.2)

Now since
\vol(M_{N})=N\vol(M)
, and
\mathrm{Ric}(M_{N})\geqslant\mathrm{Ric}(M)
, by lemma
5.3.13
we have some
C_{6}
independent of
N
such that

\sharp\{\lambda\leqslant\Lambda|\lambda\in\sigma(\Delta_{N})\}\leqslant C_{6}N\Lambda^{d/2}.

(5.3.3)

Now pick
\varepsilon_{0}\ll\Lambda_{0}
, and put
\Lambda_{j}:=\Lambda_{0}+j
. Then we have for
t\geqslant 1
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\sigma(\Delta_{N})\\
\lambda\geqslant\varepsilon_{0}\end{subarray}}\textrm{e}^{-t\lambda}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sharp\big([\varepsilon_{0},\Lambda_{0})\cap\sigma(\Delta_{N})\big)\,\textrm{e}^{-t\varepsilon_{0}}+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\sharp\big([\Lambda_{j},\Lambda_{j+1})\cap\sigma(\Delta_{N})\big)\,\textrm{e}^{-t\Lambda_{j}}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{6}N\Lambda_{0}^{d/2}\textrm{e}^{-t\varepsilon_{0}}+\textrm{e}^{-t\Lambda_{0}}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}C_{6}N\Lambda_{j+1}^{d/2}\textrm{e}^{-tj}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{6}N\Lambda_{0}^{d/2}\textrm{e}^{-t\varepsilon_{0}}+C_{6}N\textrm{e}^{-t\Lambda_{0}}\underbrace{\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(\textrm{poly. in }j)\cdot\textrm{e}^{-j}}_{\textrm{converge}}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{5}N\textrm{e}^{-t\varepsilon_{0}}.

We obtain the result.
∎

Twisted Laplacians and Decomposition of Spectra

The next step is to identify the action of the Laplace operator
\Delta_{N}
on
M_{N}
with the action of some family of Laplacians acting on functions on the base manifold
M
.

Fourier Transform in the Fibers

Consider
M_{N}\longrightarrow M
the
N
-th degree cyclic cover, we may think of the cover
M_{N}\longrightarrow M
as a
\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}
–principal bundle over
M
so that we can use the discrete Fourier transform over the fibers. Then we
observe that
given any function
f
on
M_{N}
, the deck group of
M_{N}
acts on the function
f
as some discrete rotation of the fibers. Therefore,
we can define the
fiberwise Fourier transform

\widehat{\bullet}:C^{\infty}(M_{N})\longrightarrow C^{\infty}(M_{N})^{N}
by

\displaystyle\widehat{f}_{p}(x)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sum_{k=1}^{N}f(\gamma_{0}^{k}.x)e^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}\frac{p}{N}k},\quad\quad\textrm{for }0\leqslant p\leqslant N-1.

where
\gamma_{0}
is the
generator
of the deck shifts. The Fourier inversion formula subsequently reads

\displaystyle f(x)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{p=1}^{N}\widehat{f}_{p}(x),\quad\quad x\in M_{N}.

(5.3.4)

Now we define several function spaces as follows.

\displaystyle L^{2}_{p,N}(M_{N})

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\{u\in L^{2}(M_{N})\penalty\ |\penalty\ \gamma_{0}^{*}u=\textrm{e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}\frac{p}{N}}u\},

(5.3.5)

\displaystyle C^{\infty}_{p,N}(M_{N})

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\{u\in C^{\infty}(M_{N})\penalty\ |\penalty\ \gamma_{0}^{*}u=\textrm{e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}\frac{p}{N}}u\},

(5.3.6)

\displaystyle C^{\infty}_{p,N}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\{u|_{\Omega}\penalty\ |\penalty\ u\in C^{\infty}_{p,N}(M_{N})\},

(5.3.7)

where
\Omega
is the fundamental domain fixed in the introduction. Observe that
\widehat{f}_{p}\in L_{p,N}^{2}(M_{N})
and

Lemma 5.3.2
.

We have
L^{2}_{p,N}(M_{N})\perp L^{2}_{q,N}(M_{N})
in
L^{2}(M_{N})
for
p\neq q
.

Proof.

We have for
u\in L^{2}_{p,N}
and
v\in L^{2}_{q,N}
, since the deck shifts are transitive,

\displaystyle\left\langle u,v\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M_{N})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\int_{\Omega}\overline{u}(\gamma^{k}.x)v(\gamma^{k}.x)\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}(x)\,

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{N}e^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}\frac{p}{N}k}e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}\frac{q}{N}k}\int_{\Omega}\overline{u}(x)v(x)\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}(x)=0,

by the automorphy condition and since
\sum_{k=1}^{N}e^{2\pi\mathrm{i}\frac{p-q}{N}k}=0
.
∎

The Fourier inversion formula thus gives the orthogonal decomposition

L^{2}(M_{N})=\bigoplus_{p=0}^{N-1}L_{p,N}^{2}(M_{N}).

(5.3.8)

Note that since
\gamma_{0}
acts by isometry we have
\Delta_{N}:C^{\infty}_{p,N}(M_{N})\longrightarrow C^{\infty}_{p,N}(M_{N})
for each
p
. Since
\Delta_{N}
is essentially self-adjoint on
C^{\infty}(M_{N})
, we have also decomposed

\Delta_{N}=\bigoplus_{p=0}^{N-1}\Delta_{N}|_{L_{p,N}^{2}(M_{N})}.

(5.3.9)

Next we observe that by the automorphy condition, the action of
\Delta_{N}
on each
L_{p,N}^{2}(M_{N})
boils down to its action on
L^{2}(\Omega)
with a specified boundary condition. More precisely, given
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
, define its
(quasi-)periodization

\mathcal{P}_{p,N}f\in L^{2}(M_{N})
, such that as a distribution

\big\langle\mathcal{P}_{p,N}f,\varphi\big\rangle_{L^{2}(M_{N})}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\big\langle f,\widehat{\varphi}_{p}|_{\Omega}\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\Omega)},

(5.3.10)

for test functions
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(M_{N})
. One can subsequently verify that indeed
\mathcal{P}_{p,N}f\in L^{2}_{p,N}
by resorting to the adjoint of
\gamma_{0}^{*}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{P}_{p,N}:C^{\infty}_{p,N}(\Omega)\longrightarrow C^{\infty}_{p,N}(M_{N})
. We arrive at the main observation of this subsection.

Lemma 5.3.3
.

For each integer
0\leqslant p\leqslant N-1
we have the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}_{p,N}(\Omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{2}(\Omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}_{p,N}(M_{N})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{2}_{p,N}(M_{N}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{\Omega}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{p,N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{p,N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{N}}

(5.3.11)

Denote by
\Delta_{p,N}
the self-adjoint extension of the Laplacian on
L^{2}(\Omega)
with core
C^{\infty}_{p,N}(\Omega)
. Then
\Delta_{p,N}
is unitarily equivalent to
\Delta_{N}|_{L_{p,N}^{2}(M_{N})}
through
\frac{1}{N}\mathcal{P}_{p,N}
for each
0\leqslant p\leqslant N-1
.

Proof.

We only point out that in this case the existence and uniqueness of the self-adjoint extension
\Delta_{p,N}
can be obtained thanks to the unitary map
\frac{1}{N}\mathcal{P}_{p,N}
and the corresponding result for
\Delta_{N}|_{L_{p,N}^{2}(M_{N})}
. A more general dituation is considered in lemma
5.3.5
.
∎

Characters and Unitary Equivalence

We remember that our cover
M_{\infty}
was constructed to correspond to the normal subgroup of
\pi_{1}(M)
which is the kernel of the map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho:\pi_{1}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Ab}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I(-{,}{[}\Sigma{]})}

(5.3.12)

where the first is Abelianization and
I(-,[\Sigma])
is the
oriented intersection number
with a compact oriented hypersurface
\Sigma
, which is surjective.

By Poincaré duality and Hodge theory on the closed oriented manifold
M
, there exists a unique harmonic 1-form
\alpha_{\Sigma}
such that

I([\gamma],[\Sigma])=\int_{\gamma}\alpha_{\Sigma}

(5.3.13)

for all (smooth) loops
\gamma
in
M
. By the construction of the cover
\pi_{\infty}:M_{\infty}\longrightarrow M
, there is the relation

(\pi_{\infty})_{*}(\pi_{1}(M_{\infty},x_{0}))=\ker\rho,

(5.3.14)

upon picking a base point
x_{0}\in M_{\infty}
and interpreting
\pi_{1}(M)
as
\pi_{1}(M,\pi_{\infty}(x_{0}))
. This translates into saying that

\int_{(\pi_{\infty})_{*}\widetilde{\gamma}}\alpha_{\Sigma}=\int_{\widetilde{\gamma}}\pi_{\infty}^{*}\alpha_{\Sigma}=0

(5.3.15)

for all loops
\widetilde{\gamma}
in
M_{\infty}
based at
x_{0}
. This means
\pi_{\infty}^{*}\alpha_{\Sigma}\in\Omega^{1}(M_{\infty})
is exact (
M_{\infty}
is connected), and a primitive is given by

\psi_{\Sigma}(x)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\int_{x_{0}}^{x}\pi_{\infty}^{*}\alpha_{\Sigma}.

(5.3.16)

Observe moreover that
\psi_{\Sigma}
is harmonic since
\alpha_{\Sigma}
is harmonic (thus co-closed). The most important property of
\psi_{\Sigma}
for us is the following which we single out as a lemma.

Lemma 5.3.4
.

For
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(M)
, use the same notation to denote the deck action of the class
\overline{\gamma}\in\pi_{1}(M)/\ker\rho
on
M_{\infty}
. Then we have

\psi_{\Sigma}(\gamma.x)=I([\gamma],[\Sigma])+\psi_{\Sigma}(x)=\rho(\gamma)+\psi_{\Sigma}(x),

(5.3.17)

for any
x\in M_{\infty}
.

Proof.

This comes from lifting loops, (
5.3.16
) and the property (
5.3.13
).
∎

Now we introduce several helpful functions. Let
\theta\in[-\pi,\pi)
(identified with the torus) be a parameter. Define the
character

\left.\begin{array}[]{rcl}\chi_{\theta}:\pi_{1}(M)&\longrightarrow&\mathbb{C},\\
\gamma&\longmapsto&\textrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta\int_{\gamma}\alpha_{\Sigma}}=\textrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta\cdot\rho(\gamma)}.\end{array}\right.

(5.3.18)

Next we define for
\theta\in[-\pi,\pi)
the simple automorphic function
G_{\theta}:M_{\infty}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
,

G_{\theta}(x)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\textrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta\psi_{\Sigma}}.

(5.3.19)

By (
5.3.17
) we find that

G_{\theta}(\gamma.x)=\chi_{\theta}(\gamma)G_{\theta}(x),

(5.3.20)

for deck shifts
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(M)
,
x\in M_{\infty}
. Now we restrict
G_{\theta}
to the fundamental domain
\Omega
. Similarly as before we define

C_{\theta}^{\infty}(\Omega)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\left\{\widetilde{f}|_{\Omega}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ \widetilde{f}\in C^{\infty}(M_{\infty}),\penalty\ \widetilde{f}(\gamma.x)=\chi_{\theta}(\gamma)\widetilde{f}(x)\textrm{ for }\gamma\in\pi_{1}(M)\right\}.

(5.3.21)

Denote by
\Delta_{\Omega,\theta}
the self-adjoint Laplacian acting on
L^{2}(\Omega)
with core
C_{\theta}^{\infty}(\Omega)
, sometimes called a
Born-von Kármán Laplacian

[
Lew22
]
. Note that
C_{0}^{\infty}(\Omega)
is identified with
C^{\infty}(M)
, and trivially
L^{2}(\Omega)
with
L^{2}(M)
.

Lemma 5.3.5
.

The Laplacian is indeed essentially self-adjoint on
C_{\theta}^{\infty}(\Omega)
. The map
G_{\theta}\mathbf{\cdot}:L^{2}(\Omega)\longrightarrow L^{2}(\Omega)
,
f\mapsto G_{\theta}f
is a unitary isomorphism. The operator
\Delta_{\theta}
on
L^{2}(M)
defined by
\Delta_{\theta}f:=G_{\theta}^{-1}\Delta_{\Omega,\theta}(G_{\theta}f)
is essentially self-adjoint on the core
C^{\infty}(M)
and unitarily equivalent to
\Delta_{\Omega,\theta}
.

Note that if we identify
M\setminus\Sigma
with
\Omega^{\circ}
, then
G_{\theta}
has monodromy crossing the cut
\Sigma
. However, for
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
it follows from local computations with
\Delta=-\ddiv\nabla
that

\Delta_{\theta}f=\Delta_{M}f-2\mathrm{i}\theta\alpha_{\Sigma}(\nabla f)+\theta^{2}|\alpha_{\Sigma}|_{g}^{2}f,

(5.3.22)

where
\alpha_{\Sigma}=\mathrm{d}\psi_{\Sigma}
is the harmonic 1-form on
M
obtained in (
5.3.13
). Thus we do have
\Delta_{\theta}f\in C^{\infty}(M)
.

Proof.

(Proof of lemma
5.3.5
.)
We point out that
\Delta_{\Omega,\theta}
will have domain

\dom(\Delta_{\Omega,\theta})=\left\{f\in H^{2}(\Omega)\penalty\ \middle|\left.\begin{array}[]{l}f|_{\Sigma_{+}}=\textrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta}f|_{\Sigma_{-}},\\
(\partial_{\nu_{+}}f)|_{\Sigma_{+}}=\textrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta}(\partial_{\nu_{-}}f)|_{\Sigma_{-}}\end{array}\right.\right\},

(5.3.23)

where
H^{2}(\Omega)
denotes the second order Sobolev space (
[
Lew22
]
page 85 for the torus case). We also see from (
5.3.22
) that
\dom(\Delta_{\theta})=H^{2}(M)
. Consequently it’s clear that
G_{\theta}\cdot\dom(\Delta_{\theta})=\dom(\Delta_{\Omega,\theta})
. The rest of the lemma follows.
∎

Combining lemmas
5.3.3
and
5.3.5
, we obtain

\sigma(\Delta_{N})=\bigcup_{p=0}^{N-1}\sigma(\Delta_{2\pi p/N})=\bigcup_{p=0}^{N-1}\sigma(\Delta_{p,N}).

(5.3.24)

Such operators already appeared in the works
[
PS95
,
KS90
]
whose purpose was to count geodesics with homological constraints, as well as in the more recent works
[
Ana03
, Appendix]
,
[
Ana10
, Remark 1.10 p. 600]
where
\Delta_{p,N}
is called
twisted Laplacians

[
Ana10
, p. 599, 610]
.

Vanishing of Bottom of Spectrum

Lemma 5.3.6
.

Let
\Delta_{\theta}
be the operator defined in lemma
5.3.5
with
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
. Then
\ker(\Delta_{\theta})\neq\{0\}
iff
\theta\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

If
\theta=2\pi k
, then
G_{\theta}^{-1}=\textrm{e}^{-2\pi\mathrm{i}k\psi_{\Sigma}}\in C^{\infty}(M)\subset\dom(\Delta_{\theta})
. One could verify directly with (
5.3.22
) that in this case
G_{\theta}^{-1}\in\ker(\Delta_{\theta})
. Conversely, suppose now
\ker(\Delta_{\theta})\neq\{0\}
. Here it turns out more convenient to work with the original operators
\Delta_{\Omega,\theta}
on the fundamental domain
\Omega
. Indeed, for general
s\in H^{2}(\Omega)
we have by Green-Stokes formula (
\overline{s}
denoting the complex conjugate of
s
)

\int_{\Omega}\overline{s}\Delta_{\Omega}s\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}=\int_{\Omega}\langle\overline{\nabla s},\nabla s\rangle_{g}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}-\int_{\Sigma_{+}}\overline{s}(\partial_{\nu_{+}}s)\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}-\int_{\Sigma_{-}}\overline{s}(-\partial_{\nu_{-}}s)\,\mathrm{d}V_{g},

(5.3.25)

where we remember that
\nu_{+}
is
outward
pointing whereas
\nu_{-}
is
inward
pointing. In particular, for
s
satisfying the boundary conditions in (
5.3.23
), the boundary terms of (
5.3.25
) vanish. This shows that, under
these boundary conditions
,
s\in\ker(\Delta_{\Omega,\theta})
iff
\nabla s=0
, which implies
s
is a constant. However, looking at the boundary conditions again, this could only happen with
s\neq 0
if
\theta\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}
, proving the result.
∎

From (
5.3.25
) and the boundary conditions one can also see that
\Delta_{\Omega,\theta}
are all nonnegative. On the other hand, from the expression (
5.3.22
) for
\Delta_{\theta}
we see that it is a second order elliptic differential operator which by the usual methods has compact resolvent and hence discrete spectrum. Thus we denote the spectrum of
\Delta_{\theta}
by
0\leqslant\lambda_{0}(\theta)\leqslant\lambda_{1}(\theta)\leqslant\cdots
, counting multiplicity. Lemma
5.3.6
then translates into saying that
\lambda_{0}(\theta)=0
iff
\theta\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}
, and
\lambda_{0}(\theta)>0
otherwise.

Kato Perturbations and Uniform Spectral Gap

Our goal is to control the bottom of the spectrum of the sequence
\Delta_{N}
uniformly in
N
when
N
gets large enough. We have followed the approach of Phillips-Sarnak
[
PS95
]
and obtained a family of operators
\Delta_{\theta}
indexed by the parameter
\theta\in[-\pi,\pi)
on the “Jacobian torus”, which are related to the spectrum of
\Delta_{N}
by (
5.3.24
).

We have also shown that lowest eigenvalue
\lambda_{0}(\theta)
of
\Delta_{\theta}
vanishes exactly when
\theta=0
for
\theta\in[-\pi,\pi)
. In this subsection we will apply Kato’s perturbation theory to see that
\lambda_{0}(\theta)
in fact depends analytically on
\theta
near
\theta=0
and
\lambda_{0}(0)=0
is a
strict minimum
.

The Family of Twisted Laplacians Parametrized by the Angle

Definition 5.3.1
.

An (unbounded) operator-valued function
T(\beta)
on a complex (resp. real) domain
\mathcal{R}
is called an
analytic family (in the sense of Kato)
if

1.

for every
\beta\in\mathcal{R}
,
T(\beta)
is closed and has non-empty resolvent set
\mathbb{C}\setminus\sigma(T(\beta))
;

2.

for every
\beta_{0}\in\mathcal{R}
there exists
z_{0}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\sigma(T(\beta_{0}))
and
\delta>0
such that
z_{0}
remains in the resolvent of
T(\beta)
for
|\beta-\beta_{0}|<\delta
, and
(T(\beta)-z_{0})^{-1}
is a (resp. real) analytic operator-valued function of
\beta
for
|\beta-\beta_{0}|<\delta
.

Lemma 5.3.7
.

The function
\theta\mapsto\Delta_{\theta}
is a real analytic family on
\mathbb{R}
.

For every
s\in\mathbb{R}
, we will denote by
\Psi^{s}(M)
the pseudodifferential operators of order
s
acting on distributions on the manifold
M
.

Proof.

Essentially, we see from the expression (
5.3.22
) that
H^{2}(M)
is a
common domain
for all of
\Delta_{\theta}
,
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
. We could verify with that expression also that for
u\in H^{2}(M)
,
\Delta_{\theta}u
is a
L^{2}(M)
-valued (real) analytic function of
\theta
. These ensure that
(\Delta_{\theta})_{\theta}
is an
analytic family of type (A)
in the sense of
[
RS78
]
page 16 and is consequently a Kato-analytic family (see
[
RS78
]
and Kato
[
Kat95
]
pages 375-381).

Nevertheless, we indicate the argument for our case to make the paper more self-contained. We would like to prove, say, the real analyticity near a fixed
\theta_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. Clearly for any
L>0
,
-L\notin\sigma(\Delta_{\theta})
for all
\theta
since
\Delta_{\theta}:H^{2}(M)\longrightarrow L^{2}(M)
are self-adjoint with nonnegative spectrum. By definition of the operators
\Delta_{\theta}
, we have an identity of the form

\displaystyle\Delta_{\theta}=\Delta_{M}-\mathrm{i}\theta X+\theta^{2}V\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\Delta_{M}+A_{\theta}

where
X
is a smooth vector field (differential operator of order
1
) and
V
a smooth potential on
M
. We would have

\displaystyle(\Delta_{\theta}+L)^{-1}=(\mathbbm{1}+(L+\Delta)^{-1}A_{\theta})^{-1}(L+\Delta)^{-1}

provided
\|(L+\Delta)^{-1}A_{\theta}\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}<1
. Indeed, by the composition theorem for pseudodifferential operators,
(L+\Delta)^{-1}\circ A_{\theta}
is bounded in
\Psi^{-1}(M)
uniformly in
\theta
near
\theta_{0}
and therefore as operators in
\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(M))
since
\Psi^{-1}(M)\subset\Psi^{0}(M)\subset\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(M))
by the Calderón-Vaillancourt theorem. Thus by choosing
L
large enough (possibly depending on
\theta_{0}
), we can make sure that

\|(L+\Delta)^{-1}A_{\theta}\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
, uniformly in
\theta
near
\theta_{0}
and the result follows by explicit power series expansion of
(\Delta_{\theta}+L(\theta_{0}))^{-1}
in
\theta
near
\theta_{0}
.
∎

Analytic Vanishing of Bottom of Spectrum

We use the following two results which can be found in
[
RS78
]
theorems XII.8 and XII.13.

Proposition 5.3.8

(Kato-Rellich, partial statement)
.

Let
T(\beta)
be an analytic family in the sense of Kato on a domain
\mathcal{R}
. Pick
\beta_{0}\in\mathcal{R}
and let
E(\beta_{0})
be an isolated
simple
eigenvalue of
T(\beta_{0})
. Then there exists
\eta(\beta_{0})>0
such that for
|\beta-\beta_{0}|<\eta(\beta_{0})
, there is exactly one point
E(\beta)
of
\sigma(T(\beta))
near
E(\beta_{0})
which is an isolated simple eigenvalue. Moreover
E(\beta)
is an analytic function of
\beta
in the neighborhood
|\beta-\beta_{0}|<\eta(\beta_{0})
.

Proposition 5.3.9

(Kato-Rellich, finite multiplicity version)
.

Let
T(\beta)
be an real analytic family of
self-adjoint operators
in the sense of Kato on a domain
\mathcal{R}\subset\mathbb{R}
. Pick
\beta_{0}\in\mathcal{R}
and let
E(\beta_{0})
be an isolated eigenvalue of
T(\beta_{0})
with finite multiplicity
m
. Then there exists
\eta^{\prime}(\beta_{0})>0
and
m
not necessarily distinct (real) analytic functions
E^{(1)}(\beta)
, …,
E^{(m)}(\beta)
, with
E^{(k)}(0)=E_{0}
, such that for
|\beta-\beta_{0}|<\eta^{\prime}(\beta_{0})
,
E^{(1)}(\beta)
, …,
E^{(m)}(\beta)
are the only eigenvalues of
T(\beta)
near
E_{0}
.

Lemma 5.3.10
.

Let
\Delta_{\theta}
be the analytic family of operators defined in lemma
5.3.5
. There exists a threshold
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and an integer
p>0
such that

1.

[0,\varepsilon_{0})
contains no
\lambda_{i}(\theta)
for any
i\geqslant 1
and
\theta\in[-\pi,\pi]
;

2.

there exists
a>b>0
as well as
\eta>0
such that
|\lambda_{0}(\theta)|=|\lambda_{0}(\theta)-\lambda_{0}(0)|<\varepsilon_{0}
implies

b\theta^{2p}\leqslant\lambda_{0}(\theta)=a\theta^{2p}+\mathcal{O}(\theta^{2p+1}),

(5.3.26)

as well as
|\theta|<\eta
, with
\lambda_{0}(0)=0
being a strict minimum of
\lambda_{0}(\theta)
.

Proof.

First of all we remark that
\lambda_{0}(\theta)=\inf\sigma(\Delta_{\theta})
is continuous in
\theta
since at every
\theta_{0}
we can choose
L>0
large enough so that
-L
is in the resolvent set of
\Delta_{\theta}
for all
\theta
near
\theta_{0}
and, as each
(\Delta_{\theta}+L)^{-1}
is compact,
(\lambda_{0}(\theta)+L)^{-1}=\sup\sigma((\Delta_{\theta}+L)^{-1})=\left\lVert(\Delta_{\theta}+L)^{-1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}
, the latter being continuous in
\theta
since
\Delta_{\theta}
is a Kato-analytic family. This tells us that in fact

\inf_{\theta\in[-\pi,\pi]\setminus(-\delta,\delta)}\lambda_{0}(\theta)=\varepsilon_{1}>0

(5.3.27)

for any
0<\delta<\pi
since otherwise it would contradict lemma
5.3.6
.
Since we know that
\lambda_{0}(0)=0
is a simple eigenvalue, each
\Delta_{\theta}
is nonnegative and we have lemma
5.3.6
, we deduce by the Kato-Rellich theorem
5.3.8
that in some neighborhood
|\theta|<\eta_{1}
,
\theta=0
is a strict minimum of the function
\theta\mapsto\lambda_{0}(\theta)
, which is analytic in the neighborhood.
Therefore in this neighborhood
\lambda_{0}(\theta)
will have the local form on the r.h.s. of (
5.3.26
). Consequently for any
b<a
the inequality on the l.h.s. of (
5.3.26
) will be satisfied when
|\theta|<\eta_{3}
for some
\eta_{3}<\eta_{1}
.
Next, since we also know that
\lambda_{1}(0)>0
is an isolated eigenvalue of finite multiplicity, by the finite multiplicity version of Kato-Rellich
5.3.9
we know that for some other
\eta_{2}>0
,
\lambda_{1}(\theta)
is continuous in
\theta
for
|\theta|<\eta_{2}
. Summing up,
by necessarily choosing some

0<\varepsilon_{0}<\min\Big\{\varepsilon_{1},\inf_{|\theta|\leqslant\eta_{2}}\lambda_{1}(\theta)\Big\},\quad\textrm{and}\quad\eta=\min\{\eta_{2},\eta_{3}\},

(5.3.28)

we will have (i) and (ii) satisfied for some
p>0
and we obtain the result.
∎

The spectral decomposition (
5.3.24
) together with lemma
5.3.10
tells us that for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
, every
\lambda\in[0,\varepsilon_{0})\cap\sigma(\Delta_{N})
must be
\lambda_{0}(2\pi k/N)
for some integer
k<\frac{1}{2}N
. This will give us the following important corollary.

Corollary 5.3.11
.

Let
\varepsilon_{0}
and
p
be the numbers obtained in lemma
5.3.10
. Then there exists
C_{4}>0
independent of
N
, such that

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\sigma(\Delta_{N})\\
0<\lambda<\varepsilon_{0}\end{subarray}}\textrm{e}^{-t\lambda}\leqslant C_{4}t^{-1/2p}

(5.3.29)

for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
and all
t>0
.

We point out the following formula related to the Gamma function:

Lemma 5.3.12
.

Let
\alpha
,
\beta>0
then we have

\int_{0}^{\infty}\textrm{e}^{-\beta x^{\alpha}}\,\mathrm{d}x=\frac{1}{\alpha}\Gamma\Big(\frac{1}{\alpha}\Big)\beta^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}.

(5.3.30)

Proof.

(Proof of corollary
5.3.11
.)
By (ii) of lemma
5.3.10
and the above formula, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\sigma(\Delta_{N})\\
0<\lambda<\varepsilon_{0}\end{subarray}}e^{-t\lambda}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{2\pi}{N}\sum_{0<2\pi|k|\leqslant\eta N}e^{-tb(2\pi k/N)^{2p}}\leqslant 2\int_{0}^{\eta}e^{-tb\theta^{2p}}\,\mathrm{d}\theta\leqslant\frac{1}{p}\Gamma\Big(\frac{1}{2p}\Big)(tb)^{-\frac{1}{2p}},

the second inequality holds true since
e^{-u}
is decreasing. We obtain the result.
∎

Rough Eigenvalue Counting

The author sincerely acknowledge Junrong Yan for suggesting the following elegant estimate.

Lemma 5.3.13
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a smooth compact Riemannian manifold of dimension
d
with
\mathrm{Ric}(\mathcal{M})\geqslant-K
,
K\geqslant 0
. Let
\Delta_{\mathcal{M}}
be the Laplacian on
\mathcal{M}
. Then for all
\Lambda\geqslant 1
we have

\sharp\{\lambda\leqslant\Lambda|\lambda\in\sigma(\Delta_{\mathcal{M}})\}\leqslant C_{6}\textrm{e}^{1+C_{3}K/2}\vol(\mathcal{M})\Lambda^{d/2},

(5.3.31)

where
C_{6}
is a constant depending only on dimension, and
C_{3}
the same constant appearing in (
5.2.2
).

Proof.

Indeed, we have,

\sharp\{\lambda\leqslant\Lambda|\lambda\in\sigma(\Delta_{\mathcal{M}})\}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\sigma(\Delta_{\mathcal{M}})\\
0\leqslant\lambda\leqslant\Lambda\end{subarray}}1\leqslant\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\sigma(\Delta_{\mathcal{M}})\\
0\leqslant\lambda\leqslant\Lambda\end{subarray}}\textrm{e}^{1-\frac{1}{\Lambda}\lambda}\leqslant\textrm{e}\cdot\ttr_{L^{2}(\mathcal{M})}(\textrm{e}^{-\Delta_{\mathcal{M}}/\Lambda}).

(5.3.32)

Thus the result follows by integrating the Li-Yau estimate (
5.2.2
) choosing
\delta=1/2
.
∎

Appendix A
Probability and Gaussian Measures

In this appendix we collect some more background materials in probability theory and Gaussian measures. Let us start with the following remark.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an infinite dimensional vector space equipped with a translation invariant metric
d_{\mathcal{X}}
. In most practical situations, since
\mathcal{X}
is infinite dimensional, there exists a sequence
\{x_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
on the unit ball
\mathbb{B}(0,1)
such that
d_{\mathcal{X}}(x_{i},x_{j})\geqslant\frac{1}{2}
for any
i\neq j
. This implies that
\mathbb{B}(0,2)
contains infinitely many disjoint balls of radii
\frac{1}{6}
, namely
\mathbb{B}(x_{n},\frac{1}{6})
. Therefore, if we would like a translation invariant Borel measure
\mu
on
\mathcal{X}
such that
\mu(\mathbb{B}(0,\frac{1}{6}))>0
, then
\mu(\mathbb{B}(0,2))=\infty
! By a scaling, we see that if the balls were to have positive measure, then necessarily
\mu(U)=\infty
for all open set
U
. So a
working
Lebesgue measure cannot really exist in infinite dimensions! This creates the basic problem for expressions such as (
1.2.66
). The substitute for a nicely behaved measure in infinite dimensions turns out to be a
Gaussian probability measure
, which we have discussed in subsection
1.3.4
and continue to discuss in this appendix.

\sigma
-algebras and Conditioning

This appendix discuss some basic probability notion centered around the concept of
conditioning
. We refer to Dudley
[
Dud02
]
chapter 10 for more details.

A
probability space

(Q,\mathcal{O},\mathbb{P})
consists of a set
Q
, a family
\mathcal{O}
of subsets of
Q
called the
\sigma
-algebra
, and a function
\mathbb{P}:\mathcal{O}\longrightarrow[0,1]
called the
probability measure
. The
\sigma
-algebra is required by definition, roughly speaking, to preserve the “natural” set operations (union, intersection, etc.) and hence could be thought of as an algebra made of subsets. A function
X:Q\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
(or
\mathbb{C}
) is called
\mathcal{O}
-measurable
if
X^{-1}(U)\in\mathcal{O}
for all
U
open, and such is also called a
random variable
on
(Q,\mathcal{O},\mathbb{P})
. With
X
, we could always let it
generate
a sub-
\sigma
-algebra
\sigma(X)\subset\mathcal{O}
which is the smallest one containing every
X^{-1}(U)
. We give below a basic example illustrating the intuition behind the
\sigma
-algebra.

Example A.1.1
.

We drop a pin randomly onto the square
[0,1]\times[0,1]
and assume that it always hits a unique point. In this model we take
Q=[0,1]\times[0,1]
, which is the “collection of outcomes” of the experiment, and let
\mathcal{O}
be the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the open sets in
[0,1]^{2}
. In the usual circumstance where one has no trouble in observing precisely the outcome of the experiment, one can, in principle, “measure” or estimate the probability
\mathbb{P}(A)
that the pin falls into any open set
A\subset[0,1]^{2}
via a large number of trails.

Now let us divide the square
[0,1]^{2}
into 4 parts
[0,\frac{1}{2}]\times[0,\frac{1}{2}]
,
[0,\frac{1}{2}]\times[\frac{1}{2},1]
,
[\frac{1}{2},1]\times[0,\frac{1}{2}]
, and
[\frac{1}{2},1]\times[\frac{1}{2},1]
, and label them by
1
,
2
,
3
and
4
. Suppose now that somebody has placed this square at the bottom of a dark well so that we could no longer see ourselves the results of our pin-dropping experiment, but there is a robot at the bottom telling us which of the 4 smaller squares our pin has fallen into. The “robot observer” thus defines a random variable
X_{\mathrm{Rob}}:Q\longrightarrow\{1,2,3,4\}\subset\mathbb{R}
. One could see in this case that the
\sigma
-algebra
\sigma(X_{\mathrm{Rob}})
generated by
X_{\mathrm{Rob}}
would then consist only of unions of the 4 smaller squares as subsets of
Q
, and nothing more. In a sense, therefore, a
\sigma
-algebra represents a certain “resolution” at which one observes the experiment: a “finer”
\sigma
-algebra gives higher resolution, and a more “coarse” one, lower resolution.

Let
(Q,\mathcal{O},\mathbb{P})
be a probability space and let
X\in L^{1}(Q,\mathcal{O},\mathbb{P})
, that is, assume
X
is integrable against
\mathbb{P}
. Let
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{O}
be a sub-
\sigma
-algebra. Then the
conditional expectation
of
X
with respect to
\mathcal{A}
is an
\mathcal{A}
-measurable (integrable) random variable denoted
\mathbb{E}[X|\mathcal{A}]
, defined uniquely by the property that

\mathbb{E}[XY]=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[X|\mathcal{A}]Y]

(A.1.1)

for any other
\mathcal{A}
-measurable bounded random variable
Y
. For
B\in\mathcal{O}
we define
\mathbb{P}(B|\mathcal{A}):=\mathbb{E}[1_{B}|\mathcal{A}]
.

Example A.1.2

(continued)
.

If
X:[0,1]^{2}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a random variable then one can show

\mathbb{E}[X|\sigma(X_{\mathrm{Rob}})]=\sum_{j=1}^{4}\frac{\mathbb{E}[X1_{A_{j}}]}{\mathbb{P}(A_{j})}1_{A_{j}},

(A.1.2)

where
A_{j}
,
1\leqslant j\leqslant 4
, are the 4 smaller sub-squares constructed previously. Here
\mathbb{E}[X1_{A_{j}}]/\mathbb{P}(A_{j})
is in other words just the average value of
X
on
A_{j}
.

Lemma A.1.1

(
[
Dud02
]
page 338)
.

Let
(Q,\mathcal{O},\mathbb{P})
be a probability space and
\mathcal{A}_{1}\subset\mathcal{A}_{2}\subset\mathcal{O}
two sub-
\sigma
-algebras. Then for any
X\in L^{1}(Q,\mathcal{O},\mathbb{P})
one has

\mathbb{E}[\penalty\ \mathbb{E}[X|\mathcal{A}_{2}]\penalty\ |\mathcal{A}_{1}]=\mathbb{E}[X|\mathcal{A}_{1}].

(A.1.3)

The above lemma is sometimes called the
law of total probability
. The conditional expectations of
L^{2}
random variables will be important for us in the context of the Itô-Wiener-Segal theorem (proposition
1.3.2
).

Lemma A.1.2
.

Let
(Q,\mathcal{O},\mathbb{P})
be a probability space and
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{O}
a sub-
\sigma
-algebra. Then
L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{A},\mathbb{P})
is a closed subspace of
L^{2}(Q,\mathcal{O},\mathbb{P})
and for
X\in L^{2}(Q,\mathcal{O},\mathbb{P})
,
\mathbb{E}[X\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{A}]
is the orthogonal projection of
X
onto
L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{A},\mathbb{P})
.

Corollary A.1.3

(
[
Jan97
]
theorem 4.9)
.

Let
(Q,\mathcal{O},\mathbb{P})
be a probability space and
\mathcal{H}
,
\mathcal{K}\subset L^{2}(Q,\mathcal{O},\mathbb{P})
two Gaussian Hilbert spaces. Denote by
P_{\mathcal{K}|\mathcal{H}}
the restriction of the orthogonal projection
L^{2}(Q,\mathcal{O},\mathbb{P})\to\mathcal{K}
to
\mathcal{H}
, and by
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H})
,
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{K})
the
\sigma
-algebras generated by variables in
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{K}
respectively. Then

\left.\begin{array}[]{rcl}\Gamma(P_{\mathcal{K}|\mathcal{H}}):L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H}),\mathbb{P})&\longrightarrow&L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{K}),\mathbb{P}),\\
X&\longmapsto&\mathbb{E}\big[X\big|\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{K})\big],\end{array}\right.

(A.1.4)

where
\mathbb{E}\big[X\big|\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{K})\big]
is the conditional expectation of
X
with respect to
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{K})
.

Cameron-Martin Space

Definition A.2.1
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a real separable Fréchet (or Banach) space, and
\gamma
a Borel Gaussian measure on
\mathcal{X}
. Then the closure of
\mathcal{X}^{*}
in
L^{2}(\mathcal{X},\gamma)
is called the
Gaussian Hilbert space of
\gamma
, denoted
\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be reflexive and equipped with a Borel Gaussian measure
\gamma
. Starting from the Gaussian Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}
we define the
covariance operator

\left.\begin{array}[]{rcl}C:\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{X}^{**}=\mathcal{X},\\
f&\longmapsto&\big(C(f):h\longmapsto\mathbb{E}_{\gamma}[\phi(f)\phi(h)]\big).\end{array}\right.

(A.2.1)

In other words
C(f)
is the linear functional
\left\langle f,-\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X},\gamma)}
on
\mathcal{X}^{*}
. Then the image
C(\mathcal{H}_{\gamma})\subset\mathcal{X}
, which is a Hilbert space with norm denoted
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{C(\mathcal{H}_{\gamma})}
, is called the
Cameron-Martin space
. It does the following job. For two measures
\mu
and
\nu
, we write “
\mu\approx\nu
” to mean
\mu
and
\nu
are
mutually absolutely continuous
, and “
\mu\perp\nu
” to mean
\mu
and
\nu
are mutually singular.

Proposition A.2.1

(Cameron-Martin-Girsanov,
[
Bog98
]
corollary 2.4.3)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a real separable Fréchet space,
\gamma
a Gaussian measure on
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
, and
C(\mathcal{H}_{\gamma})\subset\mathcal{X}
the Cameron-Martin space. For any
\phi_{0}\in\mathcal{X}
, define the measure
(\phi_{0})_{*}\gamma
on
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
by setting

(\phi_{0})_{*}\gamma(B)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\gamma(B-\phi_{0}),

(A.2.2)

where
B-\phi_{0}=\{\phi-\phi_{0}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \phi\in B\}
. Then

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\phi_{0})_{*}\gamma\approx\gamma,&\textrm{if }\phi_{0}\in C(\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}),\\
(\phi_{0})_{*}\gamma\perp\gamma,&\textrm{if }\phi_{0}\not\in C(\mathcal{H}_{\gamma}).\end{array}\right.

(A.2.3)

Moreover, in the first case, one has the Radon-Nikodym density

\frac{\mathrm{d}(\phi_{0})_{*}\gamma}{\mathrm{d}\gamma}(\phi)=\textrm{e}^{(C^{-1}\phi_{0})(\phi)-\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\phi_{0}\right\rVert_{C(\mathcal{H}_{\gamma})}^{2}},

(A.2.4)

where
C
is the operator defined in (
A.2.1
).

More Wick Calculus and Feynman Graphs

This appendix is a sequel to subsection
1.3.2
. We list more Feynman rules, define Feynman diagrams and give an example of computation using Feynman diagrams.

Lemma A.3.1

(
[
Sim74
]
propositions I.2, I.3, I.4,
[
Jan97
]
theorems 1.28, 3.9, 3.19)
.

1.

For
X_{1}
, …,
X_{n}\in\mathcal{H}
(not necessarily distinct) jointly Gaussian random variables,

\mathbb{E}[X_{1}\cdots X_{n}]=\sum_{\mathscr{P}}\prod_{k}\mathbb{E}[X_{i_{k}}X_{j_{k}}],

(A.3.1)

where the sum is over all partitions
\mathscr{P}
of the set
\{1,\dots,n\}
into disjoint pairs
\{i_{k},j_{k}\}
,
1\leqslant k\leqslant n/2
. In particular, the result is zero if
n
is odd.

2.

For
X\in\mathcal{H}
,

{:}X^{n}{:}=\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\frac{(-1)^{j}n!}{(n-2j)!j!2^{j}}\mathbb{E}[X^{2}]^{j}X^{n-2j}=\mathbb{E}[X^{2}]^{\frac{n}{2}}h_{n}\big(X\big/\mathbb{E}[X^{2}]^{\frac{1}{2}}\big).

(A.3.2)

3.

Let
X_{1}
, …,
X_{n}\in\mathcal{H}
and
Y_{1}
, …,
Y_{m}\in\mathcal{H}
be jointly Gaussian random variables. Then

\mathbb{E}\big[{:}X_{1}\dots X_{n}{:}\penalty\ {:}Y_{1}\dots Y_{m}{:}\big]=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[X_{i}Y_{\sigma(i)}],&&m=n,\\
&\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ 0,&&m\neq n.\end{aligned}\right.

(A.3.3)

In particular,
\mathbb{E}[{:}X^{n}{:}\penalty\ {:}Y^{m}{:}]=\delta_{nm}n!\mathbb{E}[XY]^{n}
.

There is a general way of associating random variables (or numbers) to
Feynman diagrams
. A
Feynman diagram
consists of vertices, legs (segments with only one end attached to a vertex), and edges (contracted legs). Two legs are
contracted
means they are connected to form an edge. A Feynman diagram is called
fully contracted
if there is no unconnected legs. Given
n
random variables
X_{1}
, …,
X_{n}
, a Feynman diagram
labelled by

(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
is simply any Feynman diagram whose vertices are in bijection with
\{X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\}
. A Feynman diagram labelled by
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
can now be associated with
either
a random variable
or
a number using the following rules:

1.

for each leg attached to a vertex
j
, write down the corresponding random variable
X_{j}
, and multiply them all together;

2.

whenever two legs are contracted, enclose the corresponding two random variables by
\mathbb{E}[\bullet]
.

Thus fully contracted diagrams are always associated with numbers. If
\gamma
is a Feynman diagram labelled by
X_{1}
, …,
X_{n}
, denote by
v(\gamma)
the associated object following the above rules.

Example A.3.1

(
[
PS95
]
section 4.4)
.

In a physical context Feynman diagrams are used in rather formal calculations. We would like to find

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}\big[\phi(x)\phi(y)\textrm{e}^{-\frac{\lambda}{4!}\int_{M}\phi(x)^{4}\mathrm{d}V_{M}(x)}\big]

(A.3.4)

by Taylor expanding
\exp(-\frac{\lambda}{4!}\int_{M}\phi(x)^{4}\mathrm{d}V_{M}(x))
. This gives

\displaystyle(\textrm{\ref{eqn-feynman-calc}})

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{heu}}{=}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}\big[\phi(x)\phi(y)\big]+\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}\Big[\phi(x)\phi(y)\Big(-\frac{\lambda}{4!}\Big)\int\phi(z)^{4}\mathrm{d}V_{M}(z)\Big]

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}\Big[\phi(x)\phi(y)\frac{1}{2}\Big(-\frac{\lambda}{4!}\Big)^{2}\iint\phi(z)^{4}\phi(w)^{4}\mathrm{d}V_{M}(z)\mathrm{d}V_{M}(w)\Big]+\cdots

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}\big[\phi(x)\phi(y)\big]-\frac{\lambda}{4!}\underbrace{\int\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}\big[\phi(x)\phi(y)\phi(z)^{4}\big]\mathrm{d}V_{M}(z)}_{A}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\frac{1}{2}\Big(-\frac{\lambda}{4!}\Big)^{2}\iint\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}\big[\phi(x)\phi(y)\phi(z)^{4}\phi(w)^{4}\big]\mathrm{d}V_{M}(z)\mathrm{d}V_{M}(w)+\cdots

Treating
\phi(x)
,
\phi(y)
,
\phi(z)
as (jointly Gaussian!) random variables, by (i) of lemma
A.3.1
and the above rules we write

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{heu}}{=}3\int\mathrm{d}V_{M}(z)v\bigg(\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\includegraphics[height]{fig-feynman-1.jpg}}\bigg)+12\int\mathrm{d}V_{M}(z)v\bigg(\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\includegraphics[height]{fig-feynman-2.jpg}}\bigg)

\displaystyle=3\int G(x,y)G(z,z)^{2}\mathrm{d}V_{M}(z)+12\int G(x,z)G(y,z)G(z,z)\mathrm{d}V_{M}(z),

where the factors correspond to the number of ways of getting the same contraction starting from 6 legs (4 on
z
, 1 on
x
,
y
each), and
G
denotes the Green function (of
\Delta+m^{2}
). One can represent higher order terms using these diagrams in a similar manner. More than that, the diagrams also represent actual physical processes. See Peskin and Schroeder
[
PS95
]
.

Remarks on
Q
-spaces for Random Fields

A
Q
-space
is in other words a probability sample space. The GFF on
M
, say, can be seen (abstractly) as a way of associating a Gaussian random variable
\phi(f)
to each
f\in W^{-1}(M)
so that (
2.2.4
) holds. This says nothing about the sample space on which these random variables are actually defined, and naturally there exist many choices. For example,

1.

the Bochner-Minlos construction, mentioned as proposition
2.2.1
;

2.

the formal Fourier series construction, mentioned in remark
2.2.1
; this can be identified with the previous one by appealing to the condition under which a formal Fourier series represents an actual distribution, see Shubin
[
Shu01
]
page 92 proposition 10.2;

3.

the
abstract Wiener space
construction. While this is not used essentially in this paper, it is a way of constructing (recovering) a separable Banach
Q
-space
\mathcal{X}
starting from (knowing) the Cameron-Martin space, and taking closure with respect to a carefully defined norm weaker than
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{C(\mathcal{H}_{\gamma})}
. See Sheffield
[
She07
]
and Bogachev
[
Bog98
]
section 3.9 for details.

Some other models are discussed in Simon
[
Sim74
]
section I.2, to which we refer for details in general. We shall discuss the question of in what sense two models of
Q
-spaces are equivalent, though all the three examples above could eventually by realized in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
. This is useful concerning the decomposition (
2.5.11
), and we give the precise sense in which the original
\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M}
could be recovered from the decomposed measure
\mu_{\DN}^{\Sigma,M}\otimes\mu_{\mathrm{GFF}}^{M\setminus\Sigma,D}
.

Definition A.4.1

(
[
Sim74
]
page 4)
.

Two probability measure spaces
(Q,\mathcal{O},\mu)
and
(Q^{\prime},\mathcal{O}^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
are called
isomorphic
if there is an isomorphism of
measure algebras

T:\mathcal{O}/\mathcal{I}_{\mu}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{O}^{\prime}/\mathcal{I}_{\mu^{\prime}},

(A.4.1)

here
\mathcal{I}_{\mu}
,
\mathcal{I}_{\mu^{\prime}}
being the ideals of measure zero sets of
\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
, such that
\mu^{\prime}(T(A))=\mu(A)
for all
A\in\mathcal{O}/\mathcal{I}_{\mu}
(we do not distinguish an event
A
from its class in
\mathcal{O}/\mathcal{I}_{\mu}
).

Remark A.4.1
.

Mutually absolutely continuous measures
\mu
on
\mathcal{O}
will define the same measure algebra
\mathcal{O}/\mathcal{I}_{\mu}
as they have the same measure zero sets.

Definition A.4.2

(
[
Sim74
]
page 5)
.

If
(Q,\mathcal{O},\mu)
and
(Q^{\prime},\mathcal{O}^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
are isomorphic under
T
, then two random variables
f:Q\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
f^{\prime}:Q^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}

correspond under the isomorphism
if

T(f^{-1}(B))=(f^{\prime})^{-1}(B)

(A.4.2)

for all Borel sets
B\subset\mathbb{R}
.

For a real Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, a (centered)
Gaussian process indexed by

\mathcal{H}
is a family of centered Gaussian random variables
\{\phi(f)\penalty\ |\penalty\ f\in\mathcal{H}\}
so that

\mathbb{E}[\phi(f)\phi(h)]=\left\langle f,h\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

(A.4.3)

for all
f
,
h\in\mathcal{H}
. Such a process is
defined on
the probability space
(Q,\mathcal{O},\mu)
if each
\phi(f)
is a random variable from
Q
and
\{\phi(f)\penalty\ |\penalty\ f\in\mathcal{H}\}
generates
\mathcal{O}
.

Proposition A.4.1

(
[
Sim74
]
theorem I.6)
.

Let
\{\phi(f)\}
and
\{\phi^{\prime}(f)\}
be two Gaussian processes indexed by
\mathcal{H}
defined respectively on
(Q,\mathcal{O},\mu)
and
(Q^{\prime},\mathcal{O}^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
. Then there is an isomorphism between the two probability spaces so that
\phi(f)
corresponds to
\phi^{\prime}(f)
under the isomorphism for each
f\in\mathcal{H}
.

Remark for proof.

Note for probability spaces
L^{2}(Q,\mathcal{O},\mu)\supset L^{\infty}(Q,\mathcal{O},\mu)\supset\{\textrm{indicators}\}
. Similarly for
(Q^{\prime},\mathcal{O}^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
. Thus
\Gamma(U)
where
U
takes each
\phi(f)
to
\phi^{\prime}(f)
gives the isomorphism (corollary
1.3.3
).
∎

Proposition A.4.2

(
[
Sim74
]
proposition I.7)
.

Let
\{\phi_{1}(f)\}
and
\{\phi_{2}(f)\}
be Gaussian processes, respectively, indexed by
\mathcal{H}_{1}
and
\mathcal{H}_{2}
, defined on
(Q_{1},\mathcal{O}_{1},\mu_{1})
and
(Q_{2},\mathcal{O}_{2},\mu_{2})
. Then a Gaussian process
\{\phi(h)\}
indexed by
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2}
can be defined on
Q:=Q_{1}\times Q_{2}
equipped with
\mathcal{O}_{1}\widehat{\otimes}\mathcal{O}_{2}
and
\mu_{1}\otimes\mu_{2}
, by putting

\phi(f_{1}\oplus f_{2})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\phi_{1}(f_{1})+\phi_{2}(f_{2}),

(A.4.4)

for all
f_{1}\in\mathcal{H}_{1}
,
f_{2}\in\mathcal{H}_{2}
.

Appendix B
Functional Analysis

Operator Ideals

Let
A:\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow\mathcal{K}
be a compact operator and
A^{*}:\mathcal{K}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}
its adjoint. Then
A^{*}A
is a compact self-adjoint nonnegative operator whose spectrum consists of positive eigenvalues
\mu_{0}(A)^{2}\geqslant\mu_{1}(A)^{2}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant 0
with the only possible accumulation point being zero, which may or may not be in the spectrum or an eigenvalue. The numbers
\mu_{n}(A)
are called
singular values
of
A
. Define

\displaystyle\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathcal{J}_{p}}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\Big(\sum_{n}\mu_{n}(A)^{p}\Big)^{1/p},

\displaystyle\quad\textrm{for }1\leqslant p<\infty,

(B.1.1)

\displaystyle\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathcal{J}_{\infty}}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\left\lVert A\right\rVert,

\displaystyle\quad\textrm{for }p=\infty.

(B.1.2)

Put
\mathcal{J}_{p}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K}):=\{A\textrm{ compact}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathcal{J}_{p}}<\infty\}
. We shall be concerned only with
\mathcal{J}_{1}
