, where the norm is also denoted
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}
, called the
trace class
operators and
\mathcal{J}_{2}
, called the
Hilbert-Schmidt
operators, where the norm is also written
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}
. When
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{H}
, define also

\ttr_{\mathcal{H}}(A)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sum_{n}\left\langle e_{n},Ae_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},

(B.1.3)

where
\{e_{n}\}
is an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}
. Its convergence and (in)dependence on basis is discussed below. When
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{K}
we use the notation
\mathcal{J}_{p}(\mathcal{H})
.

Lemma B.1.1

(
[
Sim05
]
theorems 2.7, 2.8, 2.14, 3.1,
[
GJ87
]
page 132-133)
.

We have

1.

whenever
A:\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}
is bounded and self-adjoint nonnegative, the sum (
B.1.3
) valuing in
[0,+\infty]
is independent of bases; it is finite iff
A\in\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
in which case
\ttr_{\mathcal{H}}(A)=\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}
;

2.

A\in\mathcal{J}_{p}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K})
iff
(A^{*}A)^{p/2}\in\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
, in which case
\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathcal{J}_{p}}=\ttr_{\mathcal{H}}((A^{*}A)^{p/2})^{1/p}
. In particular,
A
is Hilbert-Schmidt iff
A^{*}A
is trace class.

3.

Whenever
A
,
C\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
and
B\in\mathcal{J}_{p}(\mathcal{H})
we have
\left\lVert ABC\right\rVert_{\mathcal{J}_{p}}\leqslant\left\lVert A\right\rVert\left\lVert C\right\rVert\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{\mathcal{J}_{p}}
; thus each
\mathcal{J}_{p}
is a two-sided ideal in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
;

4.

if
A\in\mathcal{J}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
then the sum (
B.1.3
) converges absolutely, is independent of bases, and
|\ttr_{\mathcal{H}}(A)|\leqslant\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathrm{tr}}
;

5.

whenever
p^{-1}=q^{-1}+r^{-1}
and
A\in\mathcal{J}_{q}(\mathcal{H})
,
B\in\mathcal{J}_{r}(\mathcal{H})
then
\left\lVert AB\right\rVert_{\mathcal{J}_{p}}\leqslant\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathcal{J}_{q}}\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{\mathcal{J}_{r}}
and
AB\in\mathcal{J}_{p}(\mathcal{H})
; in particular, the product of two Hilbert-Schmidt operators is trace class.

Hilbert-Schmidt Operators on
L^{2}

Proposition B.2.1

(
[
RS72
]
page 52)
.

Let
(Q_{1},\mu_{1})
and
(Q_{2},\mu_{2})
be measure spaces so that
L^{2}(Q_{1},\mu_{1})
,
L^{2}(Q_{2},\mu_{2})
are separable. Then the map

\left.\begin{array}[]{rcl}L^{2}(Q_{1},\mu_{1})\times L^{2}(Q_{2},\mu_{2})&\longrightarrow&L^{2}(Q_{1}\times Q_{2},\mu_{1}\otimes\mu_{2}),\\
(f,g)&\longmapsto&fg,\end{array}\right.

(B.2.1)

with
(fg)(x,y):=f(x)g(y)
extends to a unique isomorphism
L^{2}(Q_{1},\mu_{1})\otimes L^{2}(Q_{2},\mu_{2})\cong L^{2}(Q_{1}\times Q_{2},\mu_{1}\otimes\mu_{2})
.

Proposition B.2.2

(
[
RS72
]
page 220,
[
Sim15
]
theorem 3.8.4)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
,
\mathcal{K}
be Hilbert spaces. Then the map

\left.\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathcal{I}:\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{K}&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{J}_{2}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K}),\\
(v,w)&\longmapsto&\left\langle v,-\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}w\end{array}\right.

(B.2.2)

extends to a unique isometric isomorphism
\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{K}\cong\mathcal{J}_{2}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K})
. In particular, for every
\eta\in\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{K}
there exist orthonormal sets
\{\varphi_{n}\}\subset\mathcal{H}
,
\{\psi_{n}\}\subset\mathcal{K}
and real numbers
\lambda_{n}>0
so that

\eta=\sum_{n}\lambda_{n}(\varphi_{n}\otimes\psi_{n}),\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathcal{I}(\eta)(v)=\sum_{n}\lambda_{n}\left\langle\varphi_{n},v\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\psi_{n},

(B.2.3)

and vice versa.

Corollary B.2.3

(
[
Sim15
]
theorem 3.8.5)
.

Let
(Q_{1},\mu_{1})
and
(Q_{2},\mu_{2})
be measure spaces so that
L^{2}(Q_{1},\mu_{1})
,
L^{2}(Q_{2},\mu_{2})
are separable. Then there is an isometric isomorphism

\left.\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathcal{I}:L^{2}(Q_{1}\times Q_{2},\mu_{1}\otimes\mu_{2})&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{J}_{2}(L^{2}(Q_{1},\mu_{1}),L^{2}(Q_{2},\mu_{2})),\\
K(x,y)&\longmapsto&\Big[f\longmapsto\displaystyle\int K(x,-)f(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(x)\Big].\end{array}\right.

(B.2.4)

In particular, there exist orthonormal families
\{\varphi_{n}\}\subset L^{2}(Q_{1},\mu_{1})
,
\{\psi_{n}\}\subset L^{2}(Q_{2},\mu_{2})
so that

K(x,y)=\sum_{n}\mu_{n}(\mathcal{I}(K))\overline{\varphi_{n}(x)}\psi_{n}(y),

(B.2.5)

with the latter series converging absolutely in
L^{2}(Q_{1},\mu_{1})
for almost every fixed
y
, and
\mu_{n}(\mathcal{I}(K))
denote the singular values of
\mathcal{I}(K)
.

Now (ii) of lemma
B.1.1
implies that

Corollary B.2.4
.

Let
L^{2}(Q_{1},\mu_{1})
,
L^{2}(Q_{2},\mu_{2})
be as above and
A:L^{2}(Q_{1},\mu_{1})\longrightarrow L^{2}(Q_{2},\mu_{2})
a Hilbert-Schmidt operator, represented via (
B.2.4
) by the integral kernel
K_{A}(x,y)
. Then

\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^{2}=\ttr_{L^{2}(Q_{1})}(A^{*}A)=\int|K_{A}(x,y)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}(y).\quad\Box

(B.2.6)

Remark B.2.1
.

For general
A\in\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(Q))
or
\mathcal{J}_{2}(L^{2}(Q))
, the fact that
\int|K_{A}(x,x)|\mathrm{d}\mu(x)<\infty
does not imply
A
is trace class, and nor does the trace equal
\int K_{A}(x,x)\mathrm{d}\mu(x)
when
A
is trace class (since the diagonal has measure zero in
Q\times Q
, in reality one could let
K_{A}(x,x)
be arbitrary without affecting
A
, thus an important condition is that
K_{A}
be continuous in “some sense”). See Simon
[
Sim15
]
section 3.11, also Vershik, Petrov and Zatitskiy
[
VPZ14
]
section 3.3.

Nuclear Spaces

We say that a topological vector space
\mathcal{H}_{\infty}
is a
countably Hilbert space
if it is a Fréchet space whose topology is generated by a countable family
(\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
of
inner products
(thus
\left\langle v,v\right\rangle=0
implies
v=0
) whose norms
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{n}:=\sqrt{\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{n}}
form an increasing sequence
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\cdots
. By completeness of
\mathcal{H}_{\infty}
in its topology it follows that

\mathcal{H}_{\infty}=\bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{H}_{n},\quad\textrm{with}\quad\mathcal{H}_{n}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\overline{\mathcal{H}}_{\infty}^{\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{n}}.

(B.3.1)

Each
\mathcal{H}_{n}
is a Hilbert space with
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{n}
, and
\mathcal{H}_{1}\supset\mathcal{H}_{2}\supset\cdots
. We denote by
\iota_{n,m}:\mathcal{H}_{n}\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{m}
the natural inclusion for
m\leqslant n
. It follows from the definitions that
\iota_{n,m}
is continuous with respect to the
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{n}
and
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{m}
norms and has dense image.

Definition B.3.1
.

A countably Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\infty}
with inner products
(\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
is called
nuclear
if for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
n>m
so that the injection
\iota_{n,m}:\mathcal{H}_{n}\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{m}
is Hilbert-Schmidt.

By definition of the topology of
\mathcal{H}_{\infty}
, a bilinear form
\mathcal{C}:\mathcal{H}_{\infty}\times\mathcal{H}_{\infty}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
is continuous if and only if there exist
C>0
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that

|\mathcal{C}(f,h)|\leqslant C\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{m}\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{m}

(B.3.2)

for all
f
,
h\in\mathcal{H}_{\infty}
. Denote by
\mathcal{H}_{-\infty}:=\mathcal{H}_{\infty}^{\prime}
the topological dual of
\mathcal{H}_{\infty}
. Countably Hilbert spaces are reflexive.

Appendix C
Global Analysis

Sobolev Spaces over Domains

In this paper we make essential use of the usual
L^{2}
Sobolev spaces over Riemannian manifolds. First let
(M,g)
be a closed Riemannian manifold and
s\in\mathbb{R}
. Then the Sobolev space
W^{s}(M)
of order
s
is defined generally as the closure of
C^{\infty}(M)
under a norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{W^{s}(M)}
, where the norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{W^{s}(M)}
could be defined in various equivalent ways. We refer to Taylor
[
Tay23
]
chapter 4 for a general discussion. For us,
s=\pm 1
,
\pm\frac{1}{2}
. We rely heavily on the following fact.

Lemma C.1.1
.

Let
\Lambda_{2s}
be an elliptic
strictly positive
formally self-adjoint pseudodifferential operator on
M
with order
2s
. Then the inner product

\left\langle-,-\right\rangle_{W^{s}}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\left\langle-,\Lambda_{2s}-\right\rangle_{L^{2}}

(C.1.1)

induces an equivalent norm for
W^{s}(M)
.

In particular, the real power
(\Delta_{M}+m^{2})^{s}
of the Helmholtz operator (massive Laplacian)
\Delta_{M}+m^{2}
provides such a candidate for
\Lambda_{2s}
.
Convention:
whenever we use the space
W^{s}(M)
, the inner product (
C.1.1
) with
\Lambda_{2s}=(\Delta_{M}+m^{2})^{s}
is understood, unless otherwise specified.

Remark C.1.1
.

Various regimes of functional calculus can be used to define
(\Delta_{M}+m^{2})^{s}
. One of them is presented in section
2.2.2
which in fact defines
complex
powers. We also mention a smooth functional calculus presented in Sogge
[
Sog17
]
theorem 4.3.1.

Next we discuss important subspaces of
W^{s}(M)
. Let
A\subset M
be a closed set and
U\subset M
an open set. Define

\displaystyle W^{s}_{A}(M)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\{u\in W^{s}(M)\penalty\ |\penalty\ \supp u\subset A\textrm{ as a distribution}\},

(C.1.2)

\displaystyle W^{s}_{U}(M)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\textrm{closure of }C_{c}^{\infty}(U)\textrm{ inside }W^{s}(M),

(C.1.3)

\displaystyle W^{s}(U)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}W^{s}_{M\setminus U}(M)^{\perp}\subset W^{s}(M).

(C.1.4)

These are closed subspaces of
W^{s}(M)
.

Remark C.1.2
.

We point out right away that by definition, then,

W^{s}(U)\cong W^{s}(M)/W^{s}_{M\setminus U}(M),

(C.1.5)

the latter equipped with the quotient norm, which is a more familiar characterization of
W^{s}(U)
, see Taylor
[
Tay23
]
page 339. Our definition as in (
C.1.4
) poses the obvious problem that in general
C_{c}^{\infty}(U)\not\subset W^{s}(U)
, at least for
s\not\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. We emphasize therefore that what is important in this definition is not the space
W^{s}(U)

per se
but the following choice for its inner product:

\big\langle f,h\big\rangle_{W^{s}(U)}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\big\langle P_{M\setminus U}^{\perp}f,P_{M\setminus U}^{\perp}h\big\rangle_{W^{s}(M)},

(C.1.6)

for any
f
,
h\in W^{s}(M)
, in particular for
f
,
h\in C_{c}^{\infty}(U)
, which produces a norm equivalent to the quotient norm, where
P_{M\setminus U}^{\perp}:W^{s}(M)\longrightarrow W^{s}(U)
denotes the orthogonal projection.

Remark C.1.3
.

Clearly
W^{s}_{U}(M)\subset W^{s}_{\overline{U}}(M)
by definition. In general the inclusion is strict (certainly if
U\neq(\overline{U})^{\circ}
!). See Taylor
[
Tay23
]
page 339 and section 4.7 for interesting discussions on conditions for
s
and
U
for which equality holds. In particular,
W^{k}_{\Omega}(M)=W^{k}_{\Omega^{\circ}}(M)=W^{k}_{\overline{\Omega}}(M)
if
\Omega\subset M
is a domain with smooth boundary
\partial\Omega
(a closed Riemannian manifold with one dimension less) and
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. In this case, we use these notations interchangeably.

The rest of this appendix could be read along with section
2.5.1
. Let
s=-1
. Although
C_{c}^{\infty}(U)\not\subset W^{-1}(U)
, we have

Lemma C.1.2
.

Let
U\subset M
be an open set. Then
P_{M\setminus U}^{\perp}(C_{c}^{\infty}(U))
is dense in
W^{-1}(U)
.

Proof.

We note
\Delta_{M}+m^{2}
is local and therefore
(\Delta+m^{2})(C_{c}^{\infty}(U))\subset C_{c}^{\infty}(U)
. It follows from lemma
2.5.1
and our definition of
W_{U}^{1}(M)
that
(\Delta+m^{2})(C_{c}^{\infty}(U))\subset W^{-1}_{M\setminus U}(M)^{\perp}
and is dense there, proving the result.
∎

Remark C.1.4
.

Clearly, the map
P_{M\setminus U}^{\perp}
is also injective on
C_{c}^{\infty}(U)
; together with lemma
C.1.2
this shows
P_{M\setminus U}^{\perp}
is a good embedding of
C_{c}^{\infty}(U)
in
W^{-1}(U)
. In fact, this is the same as the embedding of
C_{c}^{\infty}(U)
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(U)
, by remark
2.5.1
. Nevertheless, the smaller class
(\Delta+m^{2})(C_{c}^{\infty}(U))
, as it is already dense in
W^{-1}(U)
, suffices as a class of test functions to define the GFF with Dirichlet condition over a domain (see remark below lemma
2.2.2
). This reflects the fact that the Cameron-Martin pairing
\left\langle-,-\right\rangle_{W^{1}}
is more natural than
\left\langle-,-\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
in treating the GFF (see remark
2.2.3
). We have stuck to
\left\langle-,-\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
only because this is more practical with functional analysis.

Remark C.1.5
.

For general
s\in\mathbb{R}
, one could also define

W^{s}_{0}(U)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\textrm{closure of }C_{c}^{\infty}(U)\textrm{ under (\ref{eqn-def-inner-prod-sobo-open})}.

(C.1.7)

Then
W^{s}_{U}(M)\subset W^{s}_{0}(U)
. But it cannot generally be compared with
W^{s}_{\overline{U}}(M)
(to the author’s knowledge). See the exercises in Taylor
[
Tay23
]
pages 343-344 for more information.

Next we state the duality results for the various spaces. Recall that
\left\langle-,-\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M)}
denotes both the inner product of
L^{2}(M)
and the distributional pairing between
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
and
C^{\infty}(M)
. Below, we extend it to denote also the pairing between dual Sobolev spaces (see (i) of the lemma below).

Lemma C.1.3
.

Let
M
be a closed Riemannian manifold,
U\subset M
an open set,
A\subset M
a closed set, and
s\in\mathbb{R}
.

1.

W^{-s}(M)
is the dual Banach space, denoted
W^{s}(M)^{*}
, of
W^{s}(M)
under
\left\langle-,-\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
;

2.

the
annihilator
of
W^{s}_{U}(M)
under
\left\langle-,-\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
is
W^{-s}_{M\setminus U}(M)
, that is,

W^{-s}_{M\setminus U}(M)=\{u\in W^{-s}(M)\penalty\ |\penalty\ \left\langle u,f\right\rangle_{L^{2}}=0\textrm{ for all }f\in W^{s}_{U}(M)\};

(C.1.8)

the
annihilator
of
W^{s}_{A}(M)
is accordingly
W^{-s}_{M\setminus A}(M)
;

3.

W^{s}(U)^{*}\cong W^{-s}_{U}(M)
,
W^{s}_{U}(M)^{*}\cong W^{-s}(U)
, these spaces being therefore reflexive.

Finally, when
\Omega\subset M
is a domain with smooth boundary
\partial\Omega
, we define, in view of lemma
2.5.1
, the
Dirichlet Green operator

(\Delta_{\Omega,D}+m^{2})^{-1}:=(\Delta+m^{2})^{-1}P_{M\setminus\Omega^{\circ}}^{\perp}:W^{-1}(\Omega^{\circ})\longrightarrow W_{\Omega^{\circ}}^{1}(M)
. Clearly this agrees with the usual definition. In terms of quadratic forms,

Lemma C.1.4

(
[
Sim74
]
theorem VII.1)
.

Let
\Omega\subset M
be a domain with smooth boundary
\partial\Omega
. We have

\big\langle f,(\Delta_{\Omega,D}+m^{2})^{-1}h\big\rangle_{L^{2}}=\big\langle P_{M\setminus\Omega^{\circ}}^{\perp}f,P_{M\setminus\Omega^{\circ}}^{\perp}h\big\rangle_{W^{-1}},

(C.1.9)

for
f
,
h\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega^{\circ})
.

Symbol Convergence Lemma and Heat Kernel

Proof of lemma
2.3.1
.

By coordinate invariance of the definition of
\Psi^{m}(M)
it suffices to pick
x\in M
and prove the result for a chart around
x
and
\chi(x)=1
. Denote the kernel of
\chi E_{\varepsilon}\chi
by
E_{\chi,\varepsilon}
then in this chart we could write

E_{\chi,\varepsilon}(x,y)=\widetilde{E}_{\chi,\varepsilon}(x,h)=\frac{1}{F_{\varepsilon}(x)}\widetilde{\psi}\left(\frac{h}{\varepsilon}\right)\widetilde{\chi}(h),

(C.2.1)

where
h=x-y
. Indeed, by definition of our function
\psi
and freedom of choosing
\chi
we could further assume that for small enough
\varepsilon
one has
\widetilde{\chi}(h)\equiv 1
on the support of
\widetilde{\psi}(\cdot/\varepsilon)
. Thus under this condition

\sigma_{\chi E_{\varepsilon}\chi}(x,\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\textrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}h\cdot\xi}\frac{1}{F_{\varepsilon}(x)}\widetilde{\psi}\left(\frac{h}{\varepsilon}\right)\mathrm{d}h=\frac{\varepsilon^{d}}{F_{\varepsilon}(x)}\underbrace{F_{1}(x)\sigma_{\chi E_{1}\chi}(x,\varepsilon\xi)}_{\textrm{indep. of }x}.

(C.2.2)

Note that
\sigma_{\chi E_{1}\chi}(x,\eta)
is Schwartz in
\eta
and
\sigma_{\chi E_{1}\chi}(x,0)=1
. On the other hand clearly
\sigma_{\chi\mathbbm{1}\chi}(x,\xi)\equiv 1
. Thus for some
U^{\prime}\subset U
depending only on the chart and
\chi
, one has

\sup_{x\in K\Subset U^{\prime}}\sup_{\xi}\frac{|\sigma_{\chi(E_{\varepsilon}-\mathbbm{1})\chi}(x,\xi)|}{\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{\delta}}\leqslant\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle C\sup_{x\in K\Subset U^{\prime}}\sup_{|\xi|\leqslant R}\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{-\delta}|\sigma_{\chi E_{1}\chi}(x,\varepsilon\xi)-1|\leqslant C_{K,\chi}\sqrt{\varepsilon},\\
\displaystyle C\sup_{x\in K\Subset U^{\prime}}\sup_{|\xi|\geqslant R}(\cdots)\leqslant C_{K,\chi}\varepsilon^{\delta/2}\sup_{\eta}|\sigma_{\chi E_{1}\chi}(x,\eta)|,\end{array}\right.

(C.2.3)

with
R=\varepsilon^{-1/2}
. Next we deal with derivatives. Note that by (
C.2.2
) all the
x
-derivatives fall on
1/F_{\varepsilon}(x)
and all
\xi
-derivatives fall on
\sigma_{\chi E_{1}\chi}(x,\varepsilon\xi)
. Indeed, one has
|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}(1/F_{\varepsilon}(x))|\leqslant C_{\alpha}\varepsilon^{-d}
(see Dyatlov and Zworski
[
DZ16
]
page 28), and so when there are only
x
-derivatives we obtain the same bounds as (
C.2.3
) only with new constants depending on
\alpha
. When there is at least one
\xi
-derivative,

|\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\left(\sigma_{\chi E_{1}\chi}(x,\varepsilon\xi)\right)|=|\varepsilon^{|\beta|}(\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\sigma_{\chi E_{1}\chi})(x,\varepsilon\xi)|\leqslant C_{\beta,K,\chi}\varepsilon^{|\beta|}\left\langle\varepsilon\xi\right\rangle^{\delta-|\beta|},\quad|\beta|\geqslant 1.

(C.2.4)

Hence, on account of (
C.2.2
) again,

\sup_{x\in K\Subset U^{\prime}}\sup_{\xi}\frac{|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\sigma_{\chi(E_{\varepsilon}-\mathbbm{1})\chi}(x,\xi)|}{\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{\delta-|\beta|}}\leqslant C\varepsilon^{d}C_{\alpha}\varepsilon^{-d}C_{\beta,K,\chi}\varepsilon^{|\beta|}=C_{\alpha,\beta,K,\chi}\varepsilon^{|\beta|}.

(C.2.5)

Consequently, all the
\mathcal{S}^{\delta}_{1,0}
seminorms of
\sigma_{\chi(E_{\varepsilon}-\mathbbm{1})\chi}
goes to zero as
\varepsilon\to 0
. We obtain the result.
∎

In what follows we sum up some properties of the
heat operator

\textrm{e}^{-t(\Delta+m^{2})}
of the massive Laplacian (Helmholtz operator) and its Schwartz kernel
p_{t}(x,y)
called the
heat kernel
.

Lemma C.2.1

(
[
BGV04
]
theorems 2.30, 2.38 and pages 92-94)
.

We have

1.

p_{t}(x,y)\in C^{\infty}((0,\infty)\times M\times M)
;

2.

we have

(\Delta+m^{2})^{-1}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\textrm{e}^{-t(\Delta+m^{2})}\mathrm{d}t.

(C.2.6)

In particular, the kernel
G_{t}
of
\textrm{e}^{-t(\Delta+m^{2})}(\Delta+m^{2})^{-1}=(\Delta+m^{2})^{-1}\textrm{e}^{-t(\Delta+m^{2})}
is

G_{t}(x,y)=\int_{t}^{\infty}p_{s}(x,y)\mathrm{d}s,

(C.2.7)

for
x
,
y\in M
.

3.

Let
\dim M=n
. There are asymptotic expansions

\displaystyle p_{t}(x,y)

\displaystyle\sim\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}\textrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{4t}d(x,y)^{2}}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}f_{i}(x,y)t^{i},

(C.2.8)

\displaystyle\ttr_{L^{2}(M)}\big(\textrm{e}^{-t(\Delta+m^{2})}\big)

\displaystyle\sim\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}a_{i}t^{i}

(C.2.9)

as
t\to 0+
, for some real numbers
a_{i}
and functions
f_{i}\in C^{\infty}(M\times M)
,
i=0
,
1
,
2
, ….

4.

For
t
large and each
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,

\left\lVert p_{t}(x,y)\right\rVert_{C^{\ell}}\leqslant C_{\ell}\textrm{e}^{-tm^{2}/2}

(C.2.10)

for some constant
C_{\ell}
.

Poincaré-Lelong Lemmas

Proof of lemma
4.4.5
.

We denote by
\nabla_{v}
the Levi-Civita covariant derivative in the direction
v
. Since we are concerned with only one metric
g
we omit it from the notations. Take the geodesic polar coordinates
(r,\theta)
around
z_{0}
. Then

g=\mathrm{d}r^{2}+\omega^{2}(r)\mathrm{d}\theta^{2}
where
\omega(r)=|\partial_{\theta}|
. From the expression of
\Delta
in polar coordinates we have

\displaystyle\Delta\log r=\frac{1}{\omega(r)}\partial_{r}\left(\omega(r)\partial_{r}\log r\right)=-\frac{1}{r^{2}}+\frac{\partial_{r}\omega(r)}{r\,\omega(r)}.

Either noting the fact that
\partial_{\theta}
is a Jacobi field along
\theta=\textrm{const}
connecting
z_{0}
to
z
(
[
Car92
]
page 115), or otherwise, we have a Taylor expansion

|\partial_{\theta}|=r+\mathcal{O}(r^{3}),\quad r\to 0.

(C.3.1)

This gives

\frac{\partial_{r}|\partial_{\theta}|}{|\partial_{\theta}|}=\frac{1}{r}+\mathcal{O}(r),\quad r\to 0,

(C.3.2)

yielding the result.
∎

Lemma C.3.1
.

Let
(\Sigma,g)
be a Riemannian surface with smooth metric
g
, and fix
z_{0}\in\Sigma
. Denote
r(z):=d_{g}(z,z_{0})
. Then for any
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(U)
where the neighborhood
U
lies within the injectivity radius of
z_{0}
,

\int_{\Sigma}\log r(-\Delta_{g}\psi)\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}=\int_{\Sigma\setminus z_{0}}\psi(-\Delta_{g}\log r)\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}-2\pi\psi(z_{0}).

(C.3.3)

Proof.

Pick the metric disk
B_{\varepsilon}(z_{0})
with radius
\varepsilon>0
at
z_{0}
. Then Green’s formula gives

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(z_{0})}\big[\log r(\Delta_{g}\psi)-\psi(\Delta_{g}\log r)\big]\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\partial B_{\varepsilon}}\big[\log r(\partial_{r}\psi)-\psi(\partial_{r}\log r)\big]\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{g}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon\log\varepsilon)+\psi(z_{0})\frac{1}{\varepsilon}2\pi\varepsilon+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon),

as
\varepsilon\to 0
, since
\psi
is smooth and (
4.4.20
).
∎

Remark C.3.1
.

More precisely we have the equality of currents
\Delta_{g}\log r\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}=\Delta_{g}\log r|_{U\setminus z_{0}}\mathrm{d}V_{g}+2\pi\delta_{z_{0}}
on
U
.

Lemma C.3.2
.

Fix
r(z):=d_{g}(z,z_{0})
and now let
g_{1}=\textrm{e}^{2h}g
,
h\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
, and
r_{1}(z):=d_{g_{1}}(z,z_{0})
. Then

\Delta_{g_{1}}\log r-\Delta_{g_{1}}\log r_{1}\in L^{1}(U,g_{1}).

(C.3.4)

Namely, as a distribution, it agrees with an integrable function when paired with
\mathrm{d}V_{g_{1}}
.

Proof.

The point is that the current
\Delta_{g_{1}}\log r\,\mathrm{d}V_{g_{1}}
agrees with
\Delta_{g}\log r\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}
, because of their definitions and conformal covariances (
4.4.1
), (
4.4.4
). Therefore the delta functions cancel out exactly and we are left with the regular parts
\Delta_{g_{1}}\log r|_{U\setminus z_{0}}-\Delta_{g_{1}}\log r_{1}|_{U\setminus z_{0}}
which is in fact
L^{\infty}(U,g_{1})
.
∎
